CALL FOR PAPERS DEADLINE· 25 March 2002


Abstract Submission:


ISTFA 2002 will only consider abstracts in English (two-page maximum) including representative images. figures and references. At the end of the abstract, describe in 25 words or less the significance of the work discussed (conclusions and benefits) and three (3)


keywords (for indexing purposes). It is highly advisable to include sufficient detail (at least one full page) to allow for fair evaluation of the work discussed. Also, include the following information for all authors, presenting author listed first: complete name, title,


affiliatioo with complete mailing address, lelephooe and fax numbers, and email address. This information must be included with the abstract Of it will not be considered for evaluation. Please go to www.edfas.orglistfa 10 submit your abstract. The deadline for


final submission is 25 MARCH 2002.


Manuscript Suhmission:


All authors accepted into the ISTFA 2002 Symposium are required to submit a full manuscript for review by 15 JULY 2002. A final manuscript for publication will be required in electronic formal by 26 AUGUST 2002.


*Pre-approval to presenl your work at tSTFA 2002 is recommended. All costs associaled with your participation will be at your e~pensc: (travel. housing. registration).

Preface


These are interesting limes for our industry.


There is an ancient saying: "May you live in


interesting times". This was supposedly


originally meant as a curse, since in those days.


illlerestiflg times were often filled with conflict,natural disasters, famines, or other events which disrupted the safe. routine existence of the


populace. A case can certainly be made thallhe imeresling times we are living through in the


electronics industry faJl into this connotatioo.


From a booming economic position in much of 2000, we are seeing book-to-bill ratios, revenues,and profits fall in near-historic fashion in 2001.


Yel, I believe that the case can be made that we are living in interesting limes in a positive sense.


The technological developments in our industry as we enter the third millennium are certainly


ill1erestillg. Copper metallurgy, lOW-I(


dielectrics. and exotic polymer packaging


materials are among our illlerestillg daily


challenges, We stand 00 the imeresting


threshold of sub-lOO nm lithographies, of


increasingly more widespread applicatioos of


Silicon-on-lnsulalor (Sal). compound


semiconductors (e,g. SiGe), and


Microelectromechanical Systems (MEMS); of


communicatioos chips running at hundreds of


GHz: of System-OIl-Chip (SOC) integration


beyood our wildest dreams only a decade ago.


In the not too distant future, we can expect to be dealing with interesting concepts like srrainedlattice silicon struclUres. carbon nanotubes and


perhaps even molecular or single-electron


devices.


The constant running through this torrent of


inreresling developments is the constant need to improve our Failure Analysis tools, techniques,and skills. The International Symposium for


Test and Failure Analysis (lSTFA) is the preeminent opportunity for the practitioners of our art to gather. share new ideas. discuss new


challenges, and learn of the new developments


across our community. It is the most costeffective means available to provide for technical interchange, skills training, and an opportunity to view new lools in the Failure Analysis arena.


These proceedings reflect the cUtling edge of FA research. development. and practice across our


industry. If the past is prologue. this volume will


xi


become a part of the reference libraries in FA


labs and offices around the world. It represents the best of a record number of submissions for


consideration at this year's event. I hope that


you who attend ISTFA 2001 will find the event in all of its individual parts imeresring to you


and that all readers of these proceedings will


share them with co-workers to encourage both


involvement with ISTFA in the future and to


improve the performance of each of the


organizatioos which the readers represent


The entire ISTFA 2001 Organizing Committee and the Electronic Device Failure Analysis


Society (EDFAS), which sponsors this event. are excited about the opportunities in our industry


and the FA community. We look forward to


being with you for the many inreresling times


ahead.


IBM Microelectrooics Division

Conclusions


Looking to the future, for synchrotron work, the key  issue is the reduction of the scan times through direct  imaging techniques. One may attempt to achieve a  purely incoherent beam as implemented first in  Gottingen or one may use phase retrieval techniques  with a highly coherent beam. For industrial  applications, it is necessary to bring the technology  inside the factory.  X-Radia, a company founded in  part by former employees of Argonne National  Laboratories, is dedicated to this mission.13(Identification of a commercial entity does not imply  recommendation or endorsement.)

X-ray Nanotomog raphy (XRMT) Tool for Non-destructive High-Resolution Imaging of ICs


Wenbing Yun, Steve Wang, David Scott, Kenneth W. Nill


Xradia, Inc., 4075A Sprig Drive, Concord, CA 94520  Waleed S. Haddad


2245 South Vasco Road, Livermore, CA 94520


Abstract


A high-resolution table-sized x-ray  nanotomography (XRMT) tool has been  constructed that shows the promise of nondestructively imaging the internal structure of a  full IC stack with a spatial resolution better than  100 nm.  Such a tool can be used to detect, localize, and characterize buried defects in the  IC.  By collecting a set of X-ray projections  through the full IC (which may include tens of  micrometers of silicon substrate and several  layers of Cu interconnects) and applying  tomographic reconstruction algorithms to these  projections, a 3D volumetric reconstruction can  be obtained, and analyzed for defects using 3D  visualization software.  XRMT is a powerful  technique that will find use in failure analysis  and IC process development, and may facilitate  or supplant investigations using SEM, TEM, and  FIB tools, which generally require destructive  sample preparation and a vacuum environment.


Introduction


X-rays are well suited for imaging  semiconductor ICs.  Their short wavelength does  not limit spatial resolution because it is orders of  magnitude smaller than the 30-100-nm spatial  resolution to be obtained in XRMT   Because xray absorption is highly material dependent (especially between mostly low-Z dielectric  materials such as organic and Si-based dielectric  materials, and high-Z conducting materials such  as copper and tantalum), x-ray images often  show high intrinsic contrast between  interconnect and dielectric materials.  In  addition, x rays are weakly absorbed by ambient  air and the silicon substrate, so that an  electrically functional IC with its Si substrate  locally-thinned to about 10 micrometers can be  imaged in an ambient air environment, unlike


other high resolution imaging tools such as  SEM, TEM, and FIB. These capabilities make it  possible to develop powerful and convenient  XRMT tools for non-destructive imaging of the  internal structure of semiconductor ICs.


Early work on x-ray “nanotomography” demonstrated 3D resolution well below 1 micron


[1].  More recently, biological specimens have  been imaged with a 25-nm resolution in 2D [2], and 80-100 nm resolution in 3D [3, 4].  The  application of these techniques to 3-D imaging  of microchips was demonstrated through  computer simulations that revealed great  potential for non-destructive visualization of  internal structures [5].  Following  demonstrations of 3D imaging of simple metal  structures in silicon [6, 7], high-resolution x-ray  imaging of microchips using x-rays capable of  penetrating a full IC stack has been recently  demonstrated [8-9] .  However, until now, sub100 nm resolution x-ray imaging has been  carried out using large and expensive  synchrotron x-ray radiation sources that are not  appropriate for laboratory environments, or with  laboratory sources at energies insufficient to  penetrate the requisite thickness of silicon [10]. Recent advances in the key components makes it  possible to develop a table-sized XRMT tool  with a spatial resolution better than 100 nm. The  XRMT tool for ICFA is configured for imaging  unpackaged ICs without affecting their electrical  functionality by locally thinning the silicon  wafer to a few tens of micrometers over a local  area of about 100 x 100 micrometers.  This  XRMT tool produces 2D stereo images of IC  chips with a field of view of 20 micrometers in  an image time of about 20 minutes.  A data set  for 3D imaging is acquired by taking a series of  2D projection images of the IC at multiple tilt


angles relative to the x-ray beam (acquisition of  a full data set takes several hours).  These  projection images are then used to obtain a 3D  tomographic reconstruction of the IC stack. To  date, this tabletop system has been used to  obtain a tomographic data set with better than  300nm resolution, which will be discussed  below.


X-ray Image Contrast of Important Materials  in IC Fabrications


X rays in the 1.5-12 keV energy range offer a  combination of good image contrast between  many important materials in ICs, and adequate  penetration power for non-destructive imaging of


either part of or the whole IC stack.  In  particular, the interconnect metallic materials  such as Cu, W, and Ta or Ta-containing  compounds used in the new Cu technology have  strong contrast with Si and organic dielectric  materials.  Depending on specific applications, the performance of an x-ray imaging system can  be optimized in terms of spatial resolution and  image contrast.


Absorption contrast has been used in essentially  all commercial x-ray imaging equipment, including printed circuit board (PCB) inspection  tools and medical x-ray computed tomography (CT) scanners.  The linear attenuation length (the thickness through which x-ray transmission  is reduced by 1/e or about 37%) varies with  atomic number, density, and x-ray energy, so that  variations in sample composition can lead to  large differences in x-ray absorption contrast


[11].  As can be seen in Fig. 1, the tabulated [12] x-ray linear attenuation lengths of Cu and Ta are  significantly smaller than Si, and provide  absorption contrast for imaging Cu and Ta  features with Si thicknesses of up to about 10


microns.

Tabletop X-Ray Nanotomography Tool and  Key Components


The major challenges in producing a tabletop xray nanotomography tool for ICFA applications  include: (1) achieving sub-100 nm spatial  resolution to match the 2004 ITRS target over a  field of view greater than 20 micrometers; (2) obtaining an acceptable exposure time for  obtaining 2D images and a complete data set for  tomographic reconstruction using a laboratory xray source; (3) developing tomographic  reconstruction algorithms and software  specifically designed for reconstructing the 3D  object from 2D images taken over a limited set  of viewing angles; (4) designing a mechanical  system that is thermally and vibrationally stable  over the data collection time; and (5) producing  a tool with an acceptably small footprint.


One enabling x-ray optical component for  achieving the high spatial resolution is an x-ray  zone plate objective lens.  Zone plates (see Fig. 3) are diffractive optics consisting of concentric  rings (the Fresnel zones) with alternating  absorption and/or phase-shifting properties.  The  zone width decreases with increasing radius.  The maximum theoretical spatial resolution  according to the Rayleigh criteria is equal to


1.22δr, where δr is the width of the narrowest, outermost zone [13].  Zones must be correctly  placed to about one third of their width [14] (a  precision of 10’s of nanometers over a diameter  of 10’s of micrometers).  The focusing efficiency  of a zone plate lens depends on the materials  constructing the zones, and zone thickness and  profile [15,16].  The theoretical maximum  focusing efficiency is ~10% for an amplitude  zone plate of which alternative zones are  completely opaque, and ~40% for a phase zone  plate whose alternative zones produce a relative π phase shift to the x-rays with negligible  attenuation.  Because the outermost zones must  be narrow to obtain high spatial resolution, yet  be thick along the x-ray beam direction in order  to obtain high efficiency via significant  absorption or a phase shi    absorption or a phase shift of π, high aspect


ratios must be achieved when fabricating zone  plates. Zone plate objectives have been used  successfully in x-ray microscopy with 20-30 nm  spatial resolution in the soft x-ray spectral region (energy less than 1 keV) with about 10% focusing efficiency [17-20], and better efficiency


(~20%) but somewhat lower resolution (~60-120  nm) with higher energy x rays [9,21-23].  In fact, the spatial resolution obtained with zone plate  lenses is the highest in microscopes employing  electromagnetic waves.


To keep exposure times to a minimum both for  2D imaging and for tomographic data set  acquisition, a bright laboratory x-ray source  providing a desired x-ray spectral line is  required.

Figure 2: Schematic representation of  computed tomography.  Images of the  projected optical density through the object  are taken from several viewing directions. These images can then be used to build up the  Fourier transform representation of the object  for reconstruction by inverse transform, or  other algorithms such as those of algebraic  reconstruction tomography (ART) can be  used to reconstruct a complete view of the  object.


In addition, all optical components including the  zone plate lens objective, a condenser, and a  suitable position sensitive detector must have the  highest possible efficiency. The zone plate must  be quasi-monochromatically illuminated with  suitable convergence to achieve the high spatial  resolution, therefore beam-conditioning optics  for obtaining the required monochromaticity is


required.


Results From a Prototype X-Ray  Nanotomography Tool


A prototype x-ray tomography tool was used to  test and demonstrate our x-ray imaging design  concept and tomographic reconstruction  algorithms. The specifications of the prototype  are given in table 1 below.


Table 1, Specifications of the prototype x-ray  microscope


|Spatial resolution |<400 nm |
|---|---|

|Field of view |60 μm|

|Angular range for 3D data collection |<150º |

|2D image collection time for 20% contrast |<20 minutes |

|Footprint |<2 meters |

This prototype tomography tool was used to  image several test samples to characterize its  performance.  For demonstrating 3D  tomographic imaging, a test object having sharp  edges was constructed. It consisted of two small  pieces of copper SEM grids, one of a 1000 mesh  grid, and the other of a 2000 mesh grid, which  were glued onto separate silicon nitride windows


of ~1-μm thickness.  The two windows were  then placed back to back and aligned so that one  of the grid pieces lay directly above the other, and the separation is ~21 microns.  The windows  were then glued together to form the 3-D test  object.  The thickness of both the Cu grids is  about 4 microns and produces an absorption  image contrast of ~20% at the 5.4 keV x-ray  energy used for imaging.  The grid sections are  approximately 50 microns x 60 microns.


Fig.4 shows a 2D image taken at zero degree tilt  angle, i.e., when the x-ray direction is  perpendicular to the planes containing the grids.  A data set containing 53 images was collected at  a 2.5-degree interval from –62.5 to +70 degrees  of tilt angle. The images were recorded on a  digital CCD detector at an exposure time of 20  minutes at lower tilt angles; longer exposure  times were used for higher tilt angles to


Figure 3: Zone plates as x-ray focusing optics.  Left: schematic view of a zone plate focusing a  plane wave incident from the left (not shown) to a point focus on the right.  The narrower, outermost zones diffract x-rays through a larger angle than the wider, innermost zones do, producing a point focus in this configuration.  Right: photograph of a zone plate.


compensate for increased attenuation by the  object. The prototype unit was configured with


8.5X of x-ray magnification and 20X of optical  magnification between the x-ray scintillator and  the CCD optical detector.  Consequently, the  measured resolution of about 300 nm  corresponds to 4 pixels of the 13-micrometer  wide CCD camera.  The resolution of the image  was calculated using two different methods:  1) estimating the full width at half maximum (FWHM) of the line spread function (LSF) from  the derivative of the x-ray intensity across an  edge, and 2) measuring the length of the  intensity change from 10% to 90% of the peakto-bottom intensity value across the edge. Using  the two methods, the resolution of image in Fig. 4 was determined to be 300-nm.


A 3D tomographic reconstruction of the imaged  object is created from a set of 2D transmission  images (projections) acquired at angles up to 70  degrees from the normal to the Cu grids.  The  reconstruction is complicated by two factors that  become significant at large angles.  First, the  transmission of x-rays falls rapidly at high angles  since the effective thickness of the object  increases inversely as the sine of the incident


angle.  Second, ever-wider areas of the objects  fall into the field of view as the incident angle  increases, so 2D images at high angles contain  features that do not appear in the 2D images at  normal incidence (non-compact support).  Furthermore, the substantial attenuation of x-rays  by an extended sample such as an IC chip at tilt  angles greater than 70 degrees makes it  impossible to acquire images at these high  angles with reasonable exposure times, and  results in an incomplete data set.  The problem  of limited data may render common  reconstruction methods such as filtered back  projection as inadequate due to the artifacts in  the reconstruction. Special 3D tomographic  reconstruction algorithms have been developed  to deal with these complications.

Discussion and Conclusions


X-ray nanotomographic imaging shows promise  for use in IC failure analysis, including the nondestructive imaging of defects and the evolution  of failure mechanisms such as those induced by  electromigration during operation.  The  performance of a prototype XRMT tool realizing  300 nm resolution in 2D and 500 nm voxel  resolution in 3D points the way to the first  generation of <100 nm resolution laboratory  tools expected to become available in 2002.  In  addition to the use of these new capabilities for  imaging defects, we anticipate that XRMT


imaging will prove useful in guiding the use of  destructive imaging techniques such as TEM.

Single Point PICA Probing with an Avalanche Photo-Diode


Mike Bruce, Rama R. Goruganthu, Shawn McBride, David Bethke


Advanced Micro Devices, Inc., Austin, TX, USA


J.M. Chin


Advanced Micro Devices (Singapore) Pte. Ltd., Singapore


For time resolved hot carrier emission from the backside, an alternate approach is demonstrated termed single point PICA. The single point approach records time resolved emission from an individual transistor using time-correlated-single-photon counting and an avalanche photo-diode. The avalanche photo-diode has a much higher quantum efficiency than micro-channel plate photo-multiplier tube based imaging cameras typically used in earlier approaches. The basic system is described and demonstrated from the backside on a ring oscillator circuit.


Tsang and Kash1,2 have demonstrated a non-invasive


Efficiency)(% Quantum


backside timing diagnostic technique termed PICA(Picosecond Imaging Circuit Analysis). PICA measures time-dependent hot carrier induced light emission from the integrated circuit (IC) both spatially and temporally,thus enabling failure analysis and timing evaluation of a device3-5.  In the PICA approach of Tsang and Kash1,2,an imaging micro-channel plate photo-multiplier tube(MCP-PMT) is used. Within the field of view of the objective, this allows for parallel acquisition of time resolved emission from many nodes at once.


Unfortunately, the MCP-PMT detector has low quantum efficiency, especially in the near infrared region. In particular, the detector loses virtually all sensitivity for wavelengths above 900nm.  For backside acquisition,considering the spectral characteristics of hot carrier emission6-11 and the optical transmission characteristics of doped silicon12-15 through the backside, most of the transmitted photons will be in the 900-1500nm range;hence, the MCP-PMT will detect few of the available photons.  This can lead to lengthy acquisition times for long loop time test from the backside.


In this paper, another approach is showcased, referred to as single point PICA, which utilizes the higher quantum efficiency of a solid state detector; namely, a single


element avalanche photo-diode (APD). The APD has a much higher quantum efficiency than the imaging MCPPMT, especially in the near infrared region (see Fig. 1 for a comparison); hence, acquisition times should be improved on an individual transistor basis.  Although developed independently, this approach is similar to that presented in Stelleri et al16.

Single Point PICA Technique


The Apparatus


A schematic illustration of the prototype system is shown in Fig. 2.  The system utilizes a fast silicon based APD that is built into a commercially available Single Photon Counting Module (SPCM). The SPCM includes Peltier temperature controller, active quench circuit,discriminator and high voltage supply.  The dark count rate is less than 100 counts per second.   The APD is coupled to an aperture through a fiber optic cable. The aperture is placed in the back imaging plane of the objective lens. A 90/10 beam-splitter sends 10% of the light to a navigation camera while the remaining 90% is

Application Of Focused Ion Beam System As A Defect Localization And  Root Cause Analysis Tool


C.C. Ooi, K.H. Siek and K.S. Sim


Quality & Reliability, Intel Technology Sdn. Bhd.


Bayan Lepas FIZ, 11900 Penang, Malaysia


Phone: (604) 859-6485   Fax: (604) 859-6479   Email: chong.chin.ooi@intel.com


1. Abstract


Focused ion beam system has been widely used as a critical  failure analysis tool as microprocessor technology advances  at a ramping speed. It has become an essential step in failure analysis to reveal physical defects post electrical fault isolation. In this highly competitive and challenging environment prevalent today, failure analysis throughput time is of utmost important. Therefore quick, efficient and reliable physical failure analysis technique is needed to avoid  potential issues from becoming bigger. This paper will discuss the applications of FIB as a defect localization and root cause determination tool through the passive charge contrast technique and pattern FIB analysis.


2. Introduction


In the past decade, focused ion beam (FIB) systems have made significant impact to the semiconductor industry primarily in the failure analysis and device debug area. A FIB system typically uses a gallium liquid metal ion source (Ga LMIS) to generate a very focused ion beam that will then  be accelerated from 5 – 50kV. A series of electrostatic lenses  will focus the ion beam onto the substrate.  When the incident ion beam bombards on the substrate, electrons and atoms are knocked off from the target. This is described as sputtering. Sputtering, or more often known as micromachining is one of the most common applications of focused ion beam in failure analysis field [1]. The focused ion beam system has the ability to form sub-micron beams with current density of ~1A/cm2 for modification, cross section and repair of semiconductor devices with high aspect  ratio [1]. Therefore FIB has become an essential tool in microprocessor failure analysis to aid in viewing post electrical isolation process physical defects. Conventional failure analysis technique uses FIB extensively to view the three-dimensional image formation from a multi layers structures by cross sectioning. A cross sectional image of a microprocessor chip allows a failure analyst to view the profile of the metal interconnect at the failing location to look for via/metal defects. In addition, depth profiling and TEM sample preparation are also a few of many FIB applications. However FIB is not limited to its current level  of excellent performance. The increasing complexity of integrated circuitry and the shrinking dimension of transistor  size demand more technologically important failure analysis  techniques.  As the failure analysis turn around time is of utmost important, fast and efficient method is indeed needed  to shorten the process between electrical fault isolation to closure. This paper will discuss two unique applications


where focused ion beam system is also used as a defect localization and root cause determination tool. The first technique uses the passive charge contrast technique to localize an open circuitry or a shorting path [2].  The second  technique utilizes a reactive gas to selectively etch away material to look for extremely small particle such as a metallic stringer.


3. Outline Of Failure Analysis Flow


It is important to provide a brief description of a failure analysis process flow to understand when does FIB come into the picture during failure analysis (Fig. 1). When a reject  unit is submitted for in depth failure analysis, the failure will  first be verified using a tester. By applying the microprocessor’s architecture knowledge during macro and micro electrical fault isolation simulation, the failing region can be narrowed down to less than one percent of the total transistors. Microcode analysis will then further localize to a  smaller failing area, within a few tenths of transistors. A detail schematic and layout analysis using layout database and viewer will seek opportunity to narrow down the long failing signal consisting of multiple nodes to a single node or  shorter failing path. After electrical fault isolation has been successfully completed, physical failure analysis will be performed until physical defects are identified. Conventional  physical failure analysis uses reverse processing technique and optical investigation to inspect the failing path which consists of multiple metal layers and interconnects.  FIB cross section will be performed on the spot where abnormalities are observed to reveal the cause of defect.


4. FIB Charge Contrast Technique


The use of FIB in physical failure analysis has led to significant gains in time and tedious effort compared to conventional de-processing/polishing method. This complimentary approach is able to extend the localization diagnostic to a smaller area based on its unique charge contrast mechanism acquired on the failing circuitry. FIB charge contrast mechanism is based on topographic,materials, voltage and channelling effect  [3]. By using ionbeam solid interaction and collecting the secondary electrons  generated, the voltage contrast of the metal lines can provide  information to localize the defect. Principally, a grounded metal line will appear to be very bright whereas oxide surface or floating metal line will appear to be dark. This is  because when a metal is grounded or connected to other conductive paths, the positive ions introduced by the incident  ion beam during ion bombardment can be neutralized. If a


4


metal is floating or not grounded, positive ions will be trapped on the sample surface and these trapped charge will  build up a potential barrier above the sample preventing low  energy secondary electrons from reaching the detector. A“binary search” method is usually associated to localize the  defect if the failing signal involves very long interconnects.


This technique is able to determine whether the failure is due  to a high resistive path or a particle short that could be buried  underneath the upper metal layers. Examples of the usage of  the passive voltage contrast mechanism will be presented below to validate the existence of open metal path and via defect.


Fig. 1: Typical failure analysis flow


5. Application Of FIB Charge Contrast Technique


An advanced microprocessor that failed functional test was sent for in depth failure analysis. HDL signal tracing and architecture study found the error occurred at a particular signal. The failing logical signal path was finally isolated down to a buffer as shown in Fig. 2. Post fault isolation work suggested an open circuit along the failing signal line. From  the layout study, this failing signal ran through several metal  layers and tungsten interconnects and involved a total of


~14,000 micron in length (Fig. 3). Physical failure analysis started by investigating the top most metal layer which is a very long metal 6 on the signal path. A binary search method  was used whereby a point at the middle of the metal 6 was  exposed to determine whether the open path fell to the left or  to the right of the exposed area. A bright metal 6 indicated a  conductive path whereas a floating metal 6 appeared as dark. This has helped to narrow down fifty percent of the investigated area.  The binary search step was repeated until  a floating metal 6 was detected indicating an open circuitry at  the path between the two points exposed, labelled as A as shown in Fig. 4.

6. FIB As A Root Cause Analysis Tool


Another important aspect of FIB during physical failure analysis is to identify physical defect and to reveal cause of  failure mechanism. The technique used here is called pattern  FIB analysis. Basically this technique uses gas assisted etching (GAE) to etch high aspect ratio holes, even though in  a tight spaces with very dense circuitry. Straight sputtering without gases always leaves a rough and uneven floor uniformity which leads to an interlayer shorts. The process of  removing material from a surface with FIB can be greatly enhanced if the milling is performed in the presence of a reactive gas. Most importantly, the etchant gas must be properly chosen to give high selectivity to dielectric and with  minimal impact to metal lines. By understanding the sensitivity of the relative flux rate of the gas and the ions,xenon difluoride  (XeF2) has been selected to perform pattern  FIB analysis to review extremely small particle such as metallic stringer [2]. When this reactive gas is leaked into the  chamber, the gas molecule is adsorbed on the sample surface. Energy introduced by the incident focused ion beam stimulates a chemical reaction between the reactive gas and sample surface. A volatile by-product is generated and will be pumped out from the chamber. As XeF2 has an enhancement of 7 – 10x on SiO2, and together with a properly characterized milling parameters, it will selectively  etch the dielectric material with minimum impact to the metal lines [4]. By performing pattern FIB analysis, metallic  stringer is easily discovered at the SRAM cell within a few  microns square area.


7. Application Of Pattern FIB Analysis


Electrical fault isolation has confidently narrowed down the  failing microprocessor unit to a single bit failure at a cache  area. Physical failure analysis started with layer strip back until metal 1 with dielectric intact. Base on the layout study, the failing cell was identified. As this type of single bit failure is usually caused by a short and it could occur anywhere within the cell, cross section of the failing cell might not be able to pin point the stringer precisely in addition to a long throughput time. Conventional physical failure analysis technique using reverse processing such as polishing or plasma etching can damage or remove the evidence. Pattern FIB analysis can be used to pin point the


exact failing spot and determine the cause of the failure. A larger area of metal below the dielectric was first exposed using GAE. Pattern FIB analysis was then performed on the  failing cell only using the repair file established earlier. XeF2 was purged into the chamber while milling. The selectivity of  XeF2 allowed dielectric to be etched down at a much more faster speed compared to metal sputtering rate. Image contrast was well controlled and the image was monitored closely while the dielectric was getting thinner. The final results revealed the root cause of the failure to be a metallic  stringer which shorted the metal 1 to the adjacent tungsten plug, as shown is Fig. 9. This unique technique is the best technique that can reveal the metallic stringer defect in SRAM cell precisely and clearly. It can also be applied to reveal any potential horizontal short between two metal lines. Fig. 10 shows another example of how the pattern FIB analysis is used to identify the cause of a failure. In the case  where no defect was detected, the FIB hole can be back-filled with dielectric to proceed with further failure analysis at the  underlying circuitry.


Fig. 9: Pattern FIB analysis showing a metallic stringer  shorting between metal 1 and adjacent tungsten plug


7


Fig. 10: Another example of  Pattern FIB analysis  showing a metallic stringer shorting between


metal 1 and adjacent tungsten plug


8. Conclusion


The use of focused ion beam system has become significantly important in device repair and failure analysis world. Despite the high electrical fault isolation competency, the final logical failing signal path usually involves long metal lines with multiple metal layers and interconnects.


Moreover due to the decreasing dimension of transistor size, turn around time for physical failure analysis is indeed a challenge. An effective and time efficient technique is needed to extend the localization capability of the defects.


The two approaches described in this paper, the passive charge contrast technique and the pattern FIB analysis method has proven its capability to help to improve the failure analysis success rate and throughput time in finding subtle defects with reliable results. These techniques have expanded the failure analysis world to the role of defect localization and root cause identification. With the increasing  degree of integration and scaling, the need of such techniques  has become more prominent to the failure analysis world.

[5] Jon Orloff, “Liquid MetalIon Source and Limits on Foucsed Ion Beam Imaging Resolution, course notes in  The Essential Of Focused Ion Beam Technology,University of Maryland, October 1997.


[6] M. Abramo, L. Hahn and L. Moszkowicz, “Gas Assisted  Etching : An Advanced Technique for Focused Ion Beam Device Modification”, Proc. Of the 20th ISTFA,p.439-446.

X-Ray Tomography of Integrated Circuit Interconnects:  Past and Future


Zachary H. Levine and Steven Grantham


National Institute of Standards and Technology, Gaithersburg, MD 20899-8410


A scanning transmission x-ray microscope was used  to perform x-ray tomography of integrated circuit  interconnects. Reconstructions of test circuits were  made with 140 nm 3D resolution in the best case.


The modern era of x-ray tomography began around  1960 with a modest measurement of two metal  cylinders with a resolution of 7 mm. Within two  decades, CAT scanners (computerized axial  tomography) were a major new diagnostic tool in  medicine. Resolution improved gradually; however, in medicine it is necessary to limit the dose to the  patient, which limits the resolution.  The constraint of  dosage is relaxed by orders of magnitude for  inorganic samples. This insight launched synchrotron  based microtomography in 1987,1 with a resolution of  15 μm, improving to 3 μm by 1992.2  The use of  Fresnel zone plates permitted a dramatic  improvement in resolution in synchrotron-based x-ray  tomography.3  A test pattern of gold bars on a silicon  nitride window was imaged with a resolution of 50  nm. A similar resolution was achieved in a biological  sample in 1997.4  These data are summarized in  Figure 1.


Avalanche

2


XY stage


We performed tomographic reconstructions of  integrated circuit interconnects. Integrated circuit  interconnects are three-dimensional structures of  considerable complexity. Three-dimensional images


of integrated circuits are of potential interest both to  process development and failure analysis groups  within the semiconductor industry. X-ray tomography  offers three-dimensional imaging with better  resolution than optical microscopy, the ability to view  optically hidden structures, and is less invasive than  the thinning required for transmission electron  microscopy (TEM).


To achieve reasonable efficiency in x-ray  tomography, the sample should transmit about 14% of the radiation and the sample size should be 100-1000 resolution units.  For integrated circuit  interconnects (which are composed primarily of  silica, aluminum, tungsten and copper) operating at a  photon energy just below the silicon K-edge at 1839  eV and the tungsten MV edge at 1809 eV, but above  the aluminum K-edge of 1559 eV was deemed best  for 2-3 layer circuits.5  At these photon energies, the  x-rays have an attenuation length (i.e., thickness  yielding 1/e transmission) of about 8 μm in silica. Transmitting through a standard wafer thickness of  300 μm was out of the question.  We opted to retain  only the silica, removing the silicon substrate  altogether.  Two methods were used: a focused ion  beam (FIB) opened a window of approximately 300 μm on a side, leaving the silica intact; alternatively, all of the silica was removed on some samples by  etching in CsOH. The etched samples were much  easier to prepare, but they were more fragile.  All  samples were attached to a standard slotted 3.05 mm  TEM ring.  We found that mounting the wiring  directions roughly 45 degrees from the rotation axis (which is orthogonal to the long axis of the slot in the  TEM ring) permitted good reconstructions under the  actual operating conditions.6

Goal


Monochromatic light from the beamline was focused  onto the sample using a Fresnel zone plate.  We  achieved 400 nm resolution in the initial experiment5 which improved to 140 nm more recently.7 Our  technique involved using scanning transmission x-ray  tomography at the 2-ID-B beamline of the Advanced  Photon Source.8  A schematic of the experiment is  shown in Figure 2.  The intensity of the transmitted xrays was recorded with a single-channel avalanche  photodetector.  The sample was rastered through the  beam.  Images were acquired at several angles.


The scanned images had to be aligned; moreover, they suffered a linear distortion due to a lack of  ideality in the motion of the scan stage. Special  techniques were developed which were specific to the  problem of aligning integrated circuit interconnects.6,9 In particular, the wiring directions in the sample are  engineered to be orthogonal to a high degree of  accuracy. An analysis of the two dimensional  projections (i.e., the scanned images) allowed a fit of  a six parameter model which yielded a three  parameters describing the orientation of the sample  relative to the laboratory coordinates (parameters  equivalent to Euler angles) and three additional  parameters describing linear distortion (other than  overall scale) of the actual motion of the scanning  stage relative to nominal co-ordinates. By considering  the Radon transform of the images, it was possible to  automate the identification of the location and  orientation of wire edges and improve the accuracy of  such identification by a factor of 4.9


We considered two algorithms for the tomographic  analysis, the Simultaneous Iterative Reconstruction  Technique10 and a Bayesian method called the  Generalized Gaussian Markov Random Field (GGMRF) method.11 In practice, the image  reconstruction problem is underconstrained because  there are several times more degrees of freedom in  the image than the number of observations.  Hence, it  is necessary to make assumptions. The SIRT  algorithm makes assumptions about the  unconstrained data implicitly: it never modifies any  linear combination of the data which does not affect a  constraint equation.12 If one starts, as we did, from an  initial guess of zero, this is equivalent to assuming a  zero value for the missing data.


Under a Bayesian method, the reconstructed image  depends both upon the measured data and explicit  assumptions about the missing data.  Bouman and  Sauer introduced the GGMRF method in part to  handle abrupt changes in density in materials  inspection problems in tomography, while retaining


much of the analytic simplicity of the earlier  Gaussian Markov Random Field (GMRF) method. The materials inspection problem is characterized by  the presence of a few edges, i.e., discontinuous  changes in the density of the material. The  discontinuous changes tend to be blurred or noisy


Figure 4 schematically shows the acquisition scheme in PICA mode. Photons emitted by the DUT are


collected and imaged onto the PMT, which consists of a photo-cathode (to convert the photon to an electron), a multi-channel plate (MCP, to amplify each electron by 105-106 times), and a resistive anode(to measure the position of the photon). Timing information is obtained from the voltage pulse generated by the electrons as they exit the MCP. The MCP is position preserving, allowing the incident photon position, and, hence, the photon emission point in the DUT, to be determined.

FIGURE 4. PICA acquisition geometry. PMT is


image preserving so x, y, and t of photon emission from DUT can be determined.


Timing resolution of this scheme is limited by the PMT jitter (or transit time spread, TTS) which is typically ≈100 ps. The position resolution of the detector is limited by noise in the resistive anode and is ≈50 μm, corresponding to ≈0.3 μm at the DUT for the 100x objective lens. The detector’s active area is≈25 mm in diameter.


The optical system consists of objective lenses and a≈1.5x relay lens system. Using a 100x objective lens gives a 160 μm diameter field of view in PICA mode.


20x and 5x lenses give fields of view of 800 μm and 3200 μm diameter, respectively. Photons emitted anywhere within the field of view at the DUT can be detected. Thus, data from many transistors are collected in parallel and the need for careful navigation and probe placement is eliminated.


Raw PICA emission data consists of a list of all detected events, tagged with the x, y position and the time, t, with respect to the start of the test loop. Each event is then sorted and placed into the appropriate position and time bin within a 3-dimensional x, y, t


matrix. Once the data is collected and sorted, it can


be analyzed in a variety of ways: time resolved


emission images, movies, and time histograms.

Movies are a series of emission images played sequentially in time. The time-bin size for each frame,and the time-span of the movie, are user defined.

LVP/PICA Comparison


a


Invasiveness: LVP


LVP using lasers with 1.17 eV photon energy,slightly higher than the silicon band gap energy of


1.11 eV, to actively probe the DUT. As a result,significant numbers of electron-hole pairs are generated in the reverse biased junction being probed.


This can generate a relatively large photo-current that


q(


may affect device operation, particularly rise and fall times which can affect timing in subsequent stages


[26].


The photo-current can, in principle, affect the measured LVP waveforms. However, even when probing the smallest transistor structures in logic gates in 0.18 μm technology devices, no timing shifts of the waveforms were found over a wide range of laser powers [9], up to a resolution of 10 ps.


Another potential effect of the photo-current is a modification of the propagating electrical waveform in the IC. As expected, this has been found to depend on the size of the probed transistor, which is proportional to the capacitance of the node. For example, at the highest laser power, delay shifts of only 4  ps were seen when sweeping the mode locked laser pulse in time over the rising or falling edges of the signal in a (10 μm)2 transistor. Delay shifts of over 100 ps were seen when probing the most sensitive transistors in a domino logic circuit. In practice, test failure induce by LVP has not been a problem.


Accurate LVP waveforms can be acquired despite this electrical disturbance in the DUT because equivalent time sampling is used [4]. In equivalent time sampling, the test pattern must be repetitive. At the end of each loop, the DUT is reset to its state at the beginning of the loop and any electrical disturbances are terminated. This means that each measurement made by the mode-locked laser is on an undisturbed signal. Since the mode-locked laser makes its measurement within 35 ps, the LVP measurement is valid because the measurement is performed before the voltage has changed significantly.


This argument implicitly assumes the 150 ns CWL pulse does not perturb the circuit significantly. The test loop is not reset after the CWL measurement so any disturbance that it causes can propagate in the DUT, affecting the MLL measurement. The photocurrent generated by the CWL in a gate protection diode in a 0.25 μm geometry device has been measured [7]. The reported maximum value was <5 nA of average current. This was taken using a 30 μs loop length, giving a duty cycle of 150 ns/30 μs =1/200. Therefore, the peak current generated was <1μA over 150 ns. For most logic circuits, this level of photo-current has not been a problem. However,when probing floating nodes such as dynamic memory cells, invasiveness in the measured LVP waveforms has been observed [7]. 2 - 3x reductions in peak current may be possible in the future by reducing CWL power and using correspondingly longer pulses to maintain constant pulse energy.


Invasiveness: PICA


PICA is non-invasive; it uses the naturally occurring photon emission from switching FETs.


SOI Probing: LVP


Partially depleted SOI devices gain much of their performance advantage through the reduction of junction capacitance [27]. This reduction is achieved by using a buried oxide (BOX) layer to isolate the drain and source diffusions from the substrate, thus reducing the area of the p/n junction formed [28]. The difference between bulk CMOS and SOI is illustrated in Figure 7.


In addition to reducing the junction capacitance, the BOX layer isolates the FET body from the substrate.


If the FET body is left floating, it can charge up to some potential during LVP probing due to the photocarrier generation discussed above. Body charging can affect transistor operation via the history effect and threshold voltage variability [29]. Even if body


potentials are well controlled, by using body contacts,for example, invasiveness may be more problematic in SOI devices due to reduced node capacitance.


Testing for invasiveness [9] should be performed when evaluating LVP for SOI probing capability.


p-substrate


n+


Depletion region


Bulk CMOS


p-body


n+ nn+


SOI


BOX


STI


FIGURE 7. Bulk versus SOI nFET. BOX layer


reduces capacitance by reducing p-n junction area of drain/source diffusions.


Besides the potential for invasiveness, two other problems can arise when probing SOI devices using LVP. The first potential problem is caused by the strong back-reflections from the BOX layer interfaces. This reflection is caused by the large index mismatch between oxide and silicon. Assuming indices of refraction of 1.5 and 3.5 for the BOX layer and silicon, respectively, reflections at each interface can be as large as 16% [23]. For comparison,reflection from the drain contacts used to retro-reflect the beam (Figure 2) can be as low as 3 - 5%. Since the BOX interfaces are very close to the drain contacts, a significant fraction of the light reflected by them is collected by the objective lens and makes it back through the LSM optics for detection. This produces a larger DC offset to the LVP signal,reducing the relative signal strength. This degradation in SNR results in longer acquisition times and/or poorer waveform quality, as compared to bulk CMOS probing. One possible, partial solution is to etch the substrate down to the BOX layer, as has been suggested for PICA probing of SOI devices [11].


Then index matching fluid and a transparent diamond heatsink can be applied to handle power dissipation[30]. This scheme would eliminate the scattering from the box/substrate interface.


The second, more serious, problem is the reduction of the active area that can be probed. As discussed above (Figure 7) the drain/substrate p-n junction has been eliminated in SOI devices. Only the drain/body junction remains, which has a much smaller crosssection. This reduction in cross-section directly reduces the LVP signal modulation, further contributing to degraded waveform quality.


Some preliminary testing on 0.35 μm SOI devices(with body contacts to control body potential)confirms the expected reduction in LVP waveform


quality. Instead of the 1000 - 2000 ppm that is typical of 0.35 μm bulk CMOS devices, only a few hundred ppm could be obtained.


SOI Probing: PICA


PICA is passive and is, therefore, better suited for SOI probing. SOI may actually improve PICA acquisition times over bulk CMOS in one of two ways [11].  First, the BOX layer provides a convenient means for controlling etch depth.


Therefore, the substrate may be easily removed by etching to improve transmission of the shorter wavelength photons which would normally be absorbed by the substrate. With current PMT technology, the NIR sensitivity cuts off very sharply above ≈0.9 μm so improving shorter wavelength transmission greatly reduces acquisition times. (Note:Aggressive thinning to less than 20 μm of remaining silicon substrate by mechanical polishing has also been demonstrated [24].) Second, the use of undoped silicon for SOI substrates helps improve longer wavelength NIR transmission [25] which may become more important when higher IR sensitivity PMTs become available.


Acquisition Time: LVP


Acquisition times for LVP depends on the test pattern loop length, the ambient noise level, the modulation strength of the signal, the edge transition time, and the required timing accuracy. In a test floor environment, with the LVP system docked to a high-speed tester, DUT vibration amplitudes are typically ≈0.2 μm. This level of DUT vibration modulates the reflected beam by ≈200,000 ppm p-p, or ≈40,000 ppm rms. Using the dual laser scheme, this modulation/noise can be reduced by 20x to ≈2000 ppm, rms. To achieve this level of noise, a cable interface must be used between the test head and the DUT to reduce the amount of vibrations coupled in through the tester.


LVP signal levels have decreased by ≈2x for each process generation. To make up for this reduction,phase detection was incorporated into the system [5].


Currently, signal levels of ≈500 ppm are easily achievable, even for 0.13 μm geometry devices.


Test pattern loop lengths of 10-100 μs are typical for most users. This has remained fairly constant over the last three process generations (0.25-0.13 μm). LVP waveforms have been acquired using loop lengths as long as 1 ms, but sample drift, caused predominantly by temperature fluctuations, must be well controlled to achieve good results.


Assuming a 100 μs loop length, 500 pixels per waveform, 500 ppm signal strength, and 2000 ppm initial noise level, the total time needed to acquire a waveform with SNR of 10 is 80s, from (3). Since there is a ≈20% overhead in the system, the acquisition time is actually ≈1.5 minutes. In real debug situations the user must also navigate to the probe point and determine the best probe location and focus. On average, experienced users can acquire ≈1-2 waveforms/hour, once the tester is set up and phaselocked to the LVP system.

transistor. If we measure the signal from a transistor≈5 μm wide, the acquisition time required to achieve an SNR of 10 would be about 11 minutes for a 1 μs long loop, according to (8). It would be ≈18 hrs for a 100 μs long loop.


Note that PICA acquisition time is proportional to the loop length, loopT , and is inversely proportional to the photon detection rate, phS . Data has been published


on the emission rate from several recent device technologies. This clearly shows that the emission rate, and, hence, phS , is an exponential function of ccV  [31]. Therefore, loop lengths as short as possible and bias voltages as high as possible should be used to help decrease acquisition times.


The TTS of the PICA system also affects acquisition times because of its effect on SNR. Like the LVP case (Figure 8), SNR for PICA can be expressed as t


TTSΔ


, where tΔ  is the required timing accuracy of the measurement and


Δ  is equivalent


to the rise time. Thus, PICA acquisition time is proportional to the square of the detector’s TTS.


PICA acquisitions can last several hours or even days.


The PICA system was designed to be mechanically stable and can automatically compensate for system drift, making these long acquisition times practical.


Acquisition Mode: LVP


LVP waveforms are acquired serially. The operator must be intimately familiar with the device design, or be closely aided by someone who is. CAD alignment and navigation is normally required to help locate probe points. The LVP system also requires that the user define a time span of interest before the


acquisition begins. Events outside this time window are not recorded, and the system only records 500 pixels within this window. The fixed number of recorded pixels in each waveform can limit the timing resolution of the measurement. For example, for a small time span of 10 ns, the time per pixel will be 10 ns/500 = 20 ps. In this case, the waveform resolution is limited by the 35 ps MLL pulse width. However, if a wider time span of 100 ns is desired, the time per pixel is 200 ps, which then becomes the limiting factor for the measurement.


In LVP, the mode-locked laser pulses must be synchronized to the DUT tester. This requirement puts certain restrictions on the test pattern loop length for a given tester frequency. In practice, this is not a severe limitation and full details are given in [4].


However, the need to synchronize the MLL clock to the tester clock puts strict requirements on the tester clock accuracy and stability. The laser clock frequency is fixed at 100MHz ±1KHz (±10ppm)which puts similar requirements on the tester clock.


In addition, tester clock jitter and drift during an acquisition should be <<1ppm.


Acquisition Mode: PICA


PICA uses an entirely different acquisition scheme that detects photons throughout the test loop. Every photon detected by the PICA system is stamped with its position and arrival time, up to a hardware resolution of 2.5 ps, regardless of the test loop length.


This feature provides greater flexibility in data analysis and post-processing, compared to LVP. The PICA system also does not have to be actively synchronized to the tester, although it can be for extra timing stability when using long test patterns.


PICA offers parallel data acquisition. Data from all transistors within the image field of view are acquired. The operator only needs to know roughly where to probe, and there is no probe placement required. As a result, DUT setup and acquisition are greatly simplified for the operator. Analysis of the acquired data can be performed off-line or remotely.


Movies of chip operation can be generated from PICA data. This is a unique and powerful capability of PICA, and its impact on device debug is only beginning to be explored.


One potential concern with PICA is that, typically,only nMOS emission is acquired because of the longer time needed to detect pMOS signals. This limitation means that only one edge of a signal is measured. Thus, interpreting PICA data may require new simulation tools.


≤0.10 μm Probing: LVP


The optical resolution of LVP is currently ≈0.7 μm,using a 100x, 0.85 NA lens. Despite this relatively large spot size, LVP is currently being used to probe


0.13 μm geometry devices. A number of things enable this sub-resolution probing. First, there are resolvable features in the IC that can be used to help guide probe placement. Second, the LSM galvomirror resolution is ≈0.04 μm so the probe can be placed very accurately. Third, transistors are spaced far enough apart in many devices that cross-talk is not yet an issue.


The reduction in beam overlap with the FET drain/substrate p-n junction, however, can reduce the LVP signal, increasing acquisition times and reducing the SNR. Currently, the LVP noise floor is ≈20ppm,rms. As signal strengths drop, achievable SNRs also drop because the noise can not be decreased below this limit. In addition, cross-talk may eventually become an issue. Consider the extreme case of having the n- and p- FET drains of a CMOS inverter spaced<<0.7 μm apart. Since their p-n junctions are oppositely biased, the beam modulations from one will tend to cancel those from the other. This cancellation can prevent a signal from being acquired.


It is possible to reduce the LVP laser spot size. The physics of the LVP interaction with the DUT and the IR transmission window of silicon do not allow the use of shorter wavelengths, but spot size can be improved by increasing the NA (numerical aperture)of the objective lens. An immersion lens with NA >1 has been developed for this purpose [9]. This will give better imaging resolution and should improve signal strength. Immersion lens testing is ongoing.


≤0.10 μm Probing: PICA


Since the PICA and LVP systems currently use the same 100x lens, PICA image resolution is also limited to ≈0.7μm. Emission from devices spaced much closer than this can not be separated spatially.


However, unlike the LVP situation, the emission signal from two close devices can not cancel each other. Thus, a signal from each device will still be detectable. If the devices switch at different times,simulations may allow peaks in the histogram from the combined devices to be sorted. If they switch at the same time, then the peak area in the histogram may allow this situation to be identified.


To improve PICA spatial resolution, it is also possible to use an immersion lens. Unlike the immersion lens for LVP, the PICA immersion lens must be optimized for a range of wavelengths to


match the wide emission spectrum from the DUT.


This makes lens design and fabrication more difficult and expensive.


PICA signal strength is also expected to decrease as geometries shrink. Two factors that contribute to this decrease are the decrease in emission per unit gate width due to the reduction in supply voltage [31], and the decrease in gate width. From [31], emission appears to drop by 2x for each new process generation. Another 40% decrease can be expected due to decreasing gate widths. Improvements in position sensitive PMT technology is expected to help overcome this expected ≈3x drop in signal strength. Next-generation InGaAs photo-cathodes would dramatically improve detection efficiency due to improved IR sensitivity. (Note: New superconducting detector technology is also being developed for single-element (non-imaging) timeresolved emission measurements [32].)


LVP and PICA are complementary tools that use very different underlying technologies to make timing measurements from internal nodes of ICs.


Laser voltage probing is a mature tool that has been used to debug many generations of CMOS ICs. For the experienced user, it excels at making measurements quickly, even when using long loop lengths on the latest generation of logic devices.


However, it can be difficult to use and has the potential for invasiveness. Invasive effects preclude it use on devices with floating nodes, such as dynamic memory, and raises questions about its usefulness for probing SOI devices.


PICA, on the other hand, is a non-invasive technique that allows timing measurements to be made in parallel. It’s parallel acquisition and "movie" features are unprecedented and may have unanticipated uses for both device debug and failure analysis. Several case studies have been published by IBM researchers.


However, current detector technology, at times,necessitates long acquisition times and/or short loop lengths. Advances in NIR-PMT and other detector technologies are expected to help relax this requirement in the future.

Figure 2  Schematic of Single Point PICA system.


In the single point PICA approach, an individual transistor is selected using the aperture. The aperture serves to “mask off” light from unwanted transistors and transmit light from the desired transistor.  The size and shape of the aperture can be varied to match different size transistors.  Alignment of the aperture and transistor can be accomplished by sending light through the fiber from the APD end (must disconnect APD) and focusing the spot on the DUT (Device-Under-Test). The projected image of the aperture will be clearly visible in the navigation camera image (See Fig. 3).


Fiber


Spot


Figure 3  100x Backside image of Ring Oscillator circuit from the navigation camera. The arrow indicates the light spot (near the center) illuminated by the fiber during alignment. Emission will be recorded only from the area under the spot.


The aperture spot position remains fixed with reference to the image.  The DUT, mounted on an x-y-z stage, is physically moved to place the desired transistor under the aperture viewing location.  The field of view (FOV)of the image, and hence viewing area of the aperture,can also be adjusted using different magnification lenses.  A selection of lenses (mounted on a turret) are available ranging from 2.5X to 100X.


Timing Electronics Description


Timing analysis is accomplished using the TimeCorrelated Single Photon Counting (TCSPC) method19 which utilizes a TAC (Time-to-Amplitude Converter),ADC (Analog-to-Digital Converter) and MCA (MultiChannel Analyzer).  A schematic of the electronics is


*


also shown in Fig. 2.  Pulses from the SPCM and DUT trigger are used to “Start” and “Stop” the TAC,respectively.  The TAC generates an output pulse whose amplitude is directly proportional to the time difference between the detected photon (“Start”) and DUT trigger(“Stop”) pulse. The TAC output pulse is digitized by the ADC which is interfaced to the MCA via a personal computer (PC). By sampling over many cycles in PulseHeight-Analysis (PHA) mode, the MCA/PC records a histogram of photon count vs. arrival time differences between the photons (i.e., SPCM) and DUT trigger pulses. The TAC/ADC is capable of 1.2ps channel resolution; however, timing jitter from the associated electronics and the detector will limit the photon pulse resolution. This will be discussed further in the next section.


It should be noted, in this scheme using the photon to“Start” the TAC, the actual histogram data is recorded as an inverted time spectrum (i.e., left to right the time axis moves from last pulse to first pulse); however, all plots presented in this paper have been corrected to present time as increasing (i.e., left to right the time axis moves from first pulse to last pulse). This is necessary since the trigger pulse rate can be too high for the TAC to keep up with it due to dead time considerations. On the other hand, starting the TAC with the photon signal lowers the TAC dead time as the photon count rate is much lower than the trigger rate.   Thus the TAC can“keep up” with the photons except for unusually high photon count rates.


Results from a Ring Oscillator Circuit


Ring Oscillator Description


A ring oscillator circuit was used to demonstrate the single point PICA approach.  The ring oscillator was mounted in a flip chip package for backside access. The die were thinned and polished to approximately 100μm.


An anti-reflective coating was applied to the backside of the die, as described in Davis and Chi20.  The ring oscillator circuit consisted of a chain of 101 CMOS inverters. The transistor gate widths were based on a


0.25μm technology.


Time Response of the System


The time response of the system is the fastest time that


can be practically resolved between two pulses nearby in


time and is determined from the full-width-halfmaximum (FWHM) of a time limited response. The


actual measured time response results from the1


convolution of jitter from the electronics, the transittime-spread (TTS) of the APD, and the time response of


the source (i.e., photoemission from a transistor in this


instance). In the case of using an inverter in a ring


oscillator configuration for the source, at sufficiently


high Vdd (~2.5V) the gate-to-gate delay times will be


under 100ps. However, the actual switching time while


the transistor is in saturation (when most of the hot


carrier photoemission is expected to be emitted) will be


much less than the delay time. The jitter contribution


from the electronics is also expected to be very low (a


few picoseconds). In this situation, most of the time


response profile will be due to the TTS in the detector.


To record the time response, time-resolved


photoemission from the output buffer of the ring


oscillator was monitored.  A typical time resolved


emission signal from the output inverter buffer of the


ring oscillator is shown in Fig. 4.  The aperture was


placed directly over the n-channel transistor of the


inverter. As will be discussed further in the next subsection, emission from the 1−>0 transition (output of the


inverter) of the n-channel transistor is much stronger


than emission from the 0−>1 transition.  Since theS inverter switches very fast in time and the timing jitter ofnu the electronics is very low, the measured time profile0O corresponds closely to the inherent TTS of the APD.


o c u


The time response of the system, defined as the FWHMd4 of the peak in Fig. 4, is 262ps. This is slower than the


hmnherdWwhnd


time response reported using the imaging MCP-PMT1,2(~100ps).  However, it should be noted that single photon APDs have been demonstrated in the literature with timing responses well below 100ps (FWHM)21,22.

n-channel vs. p-channel


The high resolution capability of single point PICA allows us to discriminate emission between n-channel and p-channel emission. Time measurements were taken while the aperture was placed directly over an n-channel transistor of an inverter, and then another time measurement was recorded while the aperture was placed over the p-channel of the same inverter. The aperture was placed at the far edges of each transistor to ensure there was very little overlap in the emissions detected from the two transistors. This resulted in a slightly weaker signal than normal. Results are shown in Fig. 5.  The largest peak represents the 1−>0 transition(output of the inverter) of the n-channel transistor while the 0−>1 peak of the p-channel transistor is approximately 10 times weaker. Similar intensity ratios have been noted by Tsang and Kash1,2.  Perhaps more surprising is that the 0−>1 transition is observable from the n-channel transistor and the 1−>0 transition(approximately same intensity as the 0−>1 transition) is observable from the p-channel transistor.  This is primarily understood as resulting from the “shortcircuit”, or “crowbar”, current that occurs during the switching transient when both transistors in the inverter are on momentarily23.

Delay measurements were made from nearby inverters in the ring oscillator circuit.  Time resolved emission was recorded while the aperture viewing area was placed over the n-channel transistors of the inverters.

Single Point PICA Signal with a Leaky Transistor


During the course of this study, an inverter with a leaky transistor was also observed. The circuit remained functional but had unusually high standby current and performed more poorly than a normal oscillator. Single point PICA measurements were made on an n-channel transistor. Results are shown in Fig. 8.


Single Point PICA: Leaky transistor


9


Figure 8  Single point PICA signal from an inverter with a leaky n-channel transistor. Note that photons are collected throughout the high cycle (output).


In Fig. 8, it is noted that photons are collected throughout the high cycle (output). This gives the appearance of a waveform-like time profile.  However,the strong peak emission is still observed during the 1−>0 transition (along with the weak 0−>1 transition)indicating the circuit remains functional, as was observed.  These results indicate that the n-channel transistor leaks during the high cycle (output) while the p-channel transistor is on.


Pros and Cons


In this section, some of the pros and cons between the single element APD and the global imaging MCP-PMT approach are listed. Although a direct comparison has not been conducted in this study, a few general comments are possible.


Single Point PICA with APD


Pros:


• Higher quantum efficiency: In fact, quantum efficiencies even higher than Si at wavelengths beyond 1μm may be achievable with other APD’s types, e.g. InGaAs, as has been demonstrated in the literature24,25.


• Shorter acquisition times expected (on an individual transistor basis), mainly due to higher quantum efficiency.


• Lower cost than imaging MCP-PMT’s.


• Simple to operate.


Cons:


• Slower temporal response: However, as indicated earlier, faster APD’s have been demonstrated21,22.


• Acquire a single node at a time.


Global Imaging MCP-PMT PICA


• Faster temporal response.


• Global acquisition of all nodes within the field of view. At some point, it will be more beneficial to collect multiple nodes at once,albeit for longer times, than to collect them individually.


nPhoto Counts


• Poorer quantum efficiency, especially for wavelengths above 900nm.


• Longer acquisition times expected.


• Higher cost: Besides the more costly detector,also requires additional electronics such as HV supplies, multi-parameter ADC/MCA’s,position analyzers, cooled housings, amplifiers,discriminators, etc.


• More complex to operate.


Conclusion


A single point PICA approach has been demonstrated on a ring oscillator circuit from the backside. In the single point PICA approach, time-resolved emission is observed from a single transistor element. The technique uses a single element APD detector which has higher quantum efficiency in the near infrared region than typical MCP-PMT imaging cameras. This can lead to shorter acquisition times on an individual transistor basis.  Single point PICA is particularly suitable for backside analysis and very high-resolution work where only a few transistors of interest are visible in the field of view.

Advanced Techniques 2

p 33-42

William Lo, Steven Kasapi, and Kenneth Wilsher


Schlumberger Technology, San Jose, CA, USA


Two optical techniques are currently available for making at-speed timing measurements through the backside of CMOS integrated circuits: Laser Voltage Probing and Picosecond Imaging Circuit Analysis.


These techniques differ significantly in many ways,including potential for invasiveness, Silicon-OnInsulator probing capability, acquisition times versus test pattern loop length, data acquisition mode, and prospects for probing at the ≤0.1 μm nodes. This paper compares and contrasts these techniques so that their relative merits and limitations can be better understood.


Debugging new IC designs frequently requires obtaining at-speed, internal-node timing information from a packaged IC. Electron-beam based probing systems have been widely used for design debug of conventionally packaged chips that allow access to the front side metallization [1]. However, the limited front side access afforded by flip-chip packaging and the increasing number of metal layers in ICs has


688


greatly reduced the usefulness of electron-beam probing for leading edge CMOS logic devices [2, 3].


As a result, new diagnostic techniques for acquiring timing information through the device’s silicon substrate, or backside, had to be developed.


One such technique is laser voltage probing (LVP)[4-9]. It uses fast laser pulses to actively acquire waveforms via equivalent time sampling. Another technique is Picosecond Imaging Circuit Analysis(PICA) [10-12]. It utilizes the naturally occurring


light emission from switching FETs to passively


。


男


perform timing analysis. Backside optical probing with either tool is possible because silicon is partially transparent to ≈0.9 - 2.5 μm wavelength light [8].


The LVP and PICA techniques will be compared and their relative merits and limitations discussed. In particular, we shall discuss invasiveness, Silicon-OnInsulator (SOI) probing, acquisition times versus loop length, data acquisition mode, and ≤0.1 μm probing.


To better understand the origin of these differences,the LVP and PICA operating principles are first reviewed.


LVP Operation


LVP operation is similar to that of a digital sampling scope used in equivalent time sampling mode. Instead of mechanical probes, however, LVP uses light pulses from 1.064 μm wavelength (1.17 eV photon energy)lasers to probe individual transistors or diodes in CMOS ICs [13, 14]. Imaging and CAD overlay capabilities are integrated into the system to aid in navigation and probe-placement. Tester docking capabilities are also provided to facilitate at-speed probing. Full details of the LVP technique have been published [4, 5].


A Confocal Laser Scanning Microscope (LSM) with a 100x, 0.85 NA objective lens is used to deliver the laser beams to the DUT for imaging and for probing.


After reflection from the DUT, the beams are collected through the same optics and are detected using an InGaAs photodiode. In imaging mode,galvo-mirrors in the LSM raster scan the beam across the DUT and the reflected beam intensity is displayed as a function of x and y beam position (Figure 1).


FIGURE 1. (160 μm)2 LSM image of a 0.18 μm


geometry IC taken through the Si substrate (thinned


to 100 μm, polished, and anti-reflection coated).


In waveform acquisition mode, the same galvomirrors statically deflect the beam to a user defined probe point in the image. Waveforms are acquired by


detecting the reflected beam intensity modulations ata)that point, as a function of DUT test loop time.


Reflected beam modulations due to DUT activity can


come from two sources: the Franz-Keldysh Effect andN


女


the Quasi-Plasma Optical Effect [8, 9]. Both effects


produce intensity and phase modulations in theb)otreflected laser beam. To detect these modulations, the probing geometry shown in Figure 2 is used. Probing


of the drain/substrate p-n junction in the nFET of a


witindwaM


CMOS inverter is shown. The laser beams are retroreflected by the drain contact and separated from the incident beam inside the LSM for detection. Both n and p FETs can be probed using this technique.


pFETs tend to give larger amplitude (≈50%) signals,but waveform voltage fidelity is usually better when probing nFETS. The reason for this is not well understood. For explicit phase detection, a Michelson interferometer, with the objective lens and DUT forming one arm, is introduced into the beam path to first convert phase changes into amplitude changes before detection [5, 15].
