FIGURE 2. LVP probing geometry. Incident 1.064μm wavelength laser beams are used to probe


drain/substrate p-n junctions.


LVP waveforms acquired using amplitude detection are shown in Figure 3. As can be seen, these LVP waveforms bear a close resemblance to voltage waveforms. However, since LVP senses electric field and charge density modulations [8, 9], and not voltages directly, waveforms may appear inverted [9].


Waveforms may also invert due to changes in the probing conditions such as focus and probe placement [7].


FIGURE 3. LVP Waveforms from 0.18 μm device shown in Figure 1. 5 min. acquisition, 30 μs loop, 200 ppm/div vertical scale. a): 5 ns/div horizontal scale,b): 200 ps/div horizontal scale.


Since waveforms are sensitive to probe placement and focus, absolute voltage accuracy is difficult to achieve with LVP. However, for a given focus and probe position, LVP waveforms have been found to be linear with respect to the applied voltage [9].


Voltage linearity under these conditions is expected to hold unless the device is damaged or driven improperly. For example, if the drain/substrate p-n junction becomes forward biased during part of the voltage swing, the resultant current flow can contribute an additional modulation to the LVP waveform. Depending on the probing conditions, this additional signal can be inverted and/or have different modulation strength, with respect to the rest of the waveform.


Reflected beam intensity modulations from electrical activity are weak (≈100 – 1000 ppm) compare to those caused by DUT vibrations (≈100,000 - 200,000


↑


ppm). A two-laser probing scheme was developed to reduce the impact of this noise [4, 5].  Within each loop, a 150 ns pulse, generated by chopping the output of a continuous wave laser (CWL) samples the DUT at a fixed time, relative to the supplied tester trigger. During the same loop, a 35 ps wide pulse generated by a mode-locked laser (MLL) samples the DUT at a variable time. Both laser pulses track the slow reflectivity changes caused by DUT vibrations,but only the MLL pulse tracks the fast changes caused by DUT electrical activity. Taking the ratio of MLL to CWL pulses each loop reduces the effects of vibrational noise by ≈20 - 30x, allowing waveforms to be acquired ≈400 - 900 times faster. Acquisition times are typically minutes for test pattern loop lengths of ≈100 μs.


PICA Operation


PICA is, essentially, emission microscopy with picosecond timing resolution. Single photon counting techniques are used to detect the faint emission from switching FETs [16-19]. Like the LVP system, PICA features integrated LSM imaging, CAD overlay, and DUT tester docking capabilities. Switching between LSM imaging mode and PICA data collection mode is under full software control. Images acquired in both modes can be overlaid and are registered to each other using software calibration of the PICA position data. Full details of the PICA system are being published separately [20, 21].


CMOS ICs can emit light when current flows in the channel region of nFETs in saturation [22]. Although the full theory is still under development, a first order model is that the high fields (~105 V/cm) that exist in the pinch-off region of the channels accelerates electrons to high energy (1 eV or more) [18]. These hot electrons have a small, but finite, probability of losing energy in the form photons. pFET emission is weaker and more red shifted because holes have lower mobility than electrons and thus gain less energy before emission occurs [18].


In a normally operating CMOS circuit, the photon emission is synchronous with current flowing in the channel in the presence of high electric fields. In an inverter, the nFET will thus emit light as the output goes from high to low, whereas the pFET will emit light when the output goes from low to high. The PICA signal therefore contains timing information about the circuit. A large part of this emission is in the NIR so timing measurements can be made through the backside of an IC [18].


A time and position-resolved, single-photon-counting photo-multiplier tube (PMT) is used to detect the faint emissions from the IC. The probability of detecting a photon for each switching event is very low, on the order of 10-7 to 10-11 photon/switch perμm of gate width with current detectors, so photons must be accumulated over many repetitions of the DUT test loop. To keep acquisition times reasonable,test patterns are kept short to increase the number of switching events during an acquisition. The DUT is thinned (globally or locally), polished, and AR coated to optimize photon transmission [24]. Thinning minimizes NIR absorption by the substrate [25],especially of the shorter wavelength photons (<1 μm)for which the current PMT has much greater sensitivity.


*Formerly Advanced Micro Devices, Inc.


Resistive Interconnection Localization (RIL) is a new  scanning laser microscope analysis technique that  directly and rapidly localizes defective IC vias, contacts, and conductors from the front side and  backside. RIL uses a scanned laser to produce localized  thermal gradients in IC interconnections during  functional testing.  A change in the pass/fail state with  localized heating of the IC identifies the failing site.  The technique reduces the time to locate a resistive via  from months to minutes.  The sources of defective vias, the physics of RIL signal generation, and examples of  RIL analysis are presented.


Resistive interconnections are a major IC yield, performance, and reliability problem and their impact  increases with the number of interconnection levels and  operating frequency.  The ability to rapidly localize  these defective interconnections is critical to diagnosing  IC failures and implementing corrective action. Resistive interconnections on failing ICs can be  extremely difficult and time consuming to localize  because they normally require functional testing to  produce a failure.  They also require extensive  repetitive probing to identify.  Multiple levels of metal  interconnection obscure lower conductor levels and  flip-chip packaging making IC front side examination  techniques either difficult or impossible to apply.


These challenges stimulated development of several  backside IC analysis techniques.  Unfortunately, the  present suite of backside methods, such as infrared  optical microscopy, light emission microscopy [1, 2], transistor logic state mapping [3], and induced thermal  effect imaging (TIVA/SEI, OBIRCH) [4, 5] are  generally either not always effective or time consuming  for localizing resistive interconnections.


In response, we developed a scanning laser microscopy  analysis technique, Resistive Interconnection  Localization (RIL), that directly localizes failure  producing interconnections in a quick and easy manner.   RIL uses the effect of local heating on resistive  interconnections during functional testing. The infrared  laser wavelength used in RIL enables laser scanning  from the front side or backside of an IC.  The laser  wavelength produces heat without generating unwanted  photocurrents.


RIL can analyze ICs having defective vias or contacts  where metal-metal, metal-poly, or metal-Si structures  are the targets of applied laser energy.  These  interconnections typically have voids or a weak Al-W  interface.


The failure mechanisms that can produce defective vias  and their electrical and reliability behavior are  described.  The physics for producing the RIL signal  and the system used to acquire RIL images are also  described.  RIL imaging examples show the technique’s  ability to quickly detect resistive defects in both simple  and complex ICs.  The RIL imaging results are  corroborated by data obtained from other electrical and  physical failure analysis techniques.


Resistive Interconnections


As IC technologies advance in complexity and  functionality, resistive interconnections become a  significant concern. The rising numbers of vias and  contacts as well as higher performance demands  increase the probability that a “weak” interconnection  will produce a functional failure.


Failing vias occur due to process related defects and  errors and have many sources.  These include:


• Via pushups from stress voiding during


high temperature anneals


• CMP particle contamination


• Improper post-CMP cleans (chemicals that


attack the via liner)


• Small random particles within the via or


larger particles that cause mask defects and


missing vias


• Incomplete etches


• Etch ash polymers left in the via bottom


after reactive ion etch


• Residual moisture in the via that creates


voids when W is deposited leading to


increased resistance and corrosion


• Insufficient W filling of vias


• Poor W plug adhesion to the liner


• Incomplete step coverage of the via liner


(usually sputtered Ti/TiN)


• Chemical reactions in Al deposition that


adversely affect the W/Al interface


adhesion


• Via misalignment that affects connecting


areas and via resistance


• Stress voiding in a line or via


• Patterning defects


Several of these mechanisms are reported in the  literature. Via voiding, particulates, and via pushup  have received special attention due to their widespread  occurrence.  Their properties related to root cause and  detection are summarized below.


Via Voiding and Particulates


An early report of a resistive contact and its  temperature effects was given by Campbell, et al. [6].  Significantly, the initial temperature-dependent  resistive contact failure later electromigrated to a full  open contact upon further testing in the failure analysis  lab under a normal electrical environment.  Takagi and  Kohno reported failure analysis of CMP-related via  failures affecting many ICs [7].  The symptoms were  anomalous power supply current patterns.  Via cross  sections showed foreign material and void space  between metal-1 and the W stud.  Auger analysis  identified organics, aluminum oxide, P, and Si  compounds all traceable to the CMP slurry and brushes.   Fig. 1a shows a floating plug caused by chemicals  attacking the liner and Fig. 1b shows a via whose plug  has popped out [8].  The causes are believed to be weak  liner attachment to the W and stress forces, respectively.


Via Pushup


Fig. 2 shows a via defect where the Al bulges or pushes  up into the W via [9].  The permanent extrusion of Al


into the via space is believed to be stress-induced with  the thermal coefficient of expansion playing an initial  role.  A typical process lines the etched via with Ti and  TiN to decrease resistance and prevent corrosion.  The  TiN layer isolates Al from Ti since a reaction forming  TiAl3 has a 5% volume shrink generating stresses of  hundreds of MPa [10].  This can happen if the TiN  barrier is compromised.


The relation of stress voiding to defective via reliability  was also reported by others.    Shibata et al. reported via  push-up defects formed by stress voiding that occurred  during and after a high temperature cleansing anneal  prior to via metal deposition [11].  The cleansing anneal  removes residual impurities and prevents corrosion.  When the anneal temperature is above the dielectric  deposition temperature, Al thermally expands with  respect to the dielectric.  Al goes into compression  forming a stress gradient that drives Al into the via  space.  The dielectric material and liner metals were  missing at the via hole at that stage of the process and a  stress gradient formed between the via region and a  location in the lower metal that was at least 10 μm from  the via.  The stress migration drove Al into the via  space putting an inelastic strain on the metal.   Most of  the displaced Al atoms do not return upon cooling.  The  via cross-section showed that much of the Al remained  as a pushup extrusion.    The stress gradient generated  small voids in the metal line.  Upon cooling, the reverse


(a)


(b)


Fig. 1. (a) Floating W plug, (b) W plug that  popped out of via [8].


stress gradient tried to drive Al back from the via, but  the process created more voids rather than refill the  original voids.  Stress experiments on test structures  showed this phenomena was a reliability concern.


J. Walls, in experiments similar to Shibato, found via  failures with slightly different results [12].  Stress  gradients and thermal expansion drove Al into empty  vias during the high temperature anneal, but voids  formed rapidly under the bulging Al in the via and not  in the metal lines as Shibato reported [11].  The voids  under the extruded Al in the via did not cause electrical  failures in the completed W via test structures since  adequate continuity and step coverage was preserved, but later these vias failed due to electromigration.  The  significant conclusion was that electromigration failures  occurred rapidly due to these voids beneath the via.  Electromigration enhanced by high tensile stress  gradients caused an early failure mechanism.  Stress  voiding that occurs around the via structures is driven  by different geometries, dielectric constants, and  thermal cycling environments.


Localizing Defective Interconnections


Defect Signal Properties


Typical modern via resistances lie in the 2-5 Ω range.  Resistive vias are those whose resistance is above the  normal distribution.  They have a wide ohmic range and  are an increased reliability risk [6, 9, 11-13].   A via is  defined as defective when the performance demands  put on the via exceed its signal transmission  capabilities.  Therefore the via’s location in the circuit is  as important as the via’s resistance in deciding whether  or not it is defective.


Two studies of resistive via detection with simple speed  test vectors showed that detectable delays did not occur  until via resistance (via in series path of signal)


exceeded about 150 kΩ in the Philips Labs cell library


[9] and 830 kΩ in an Intel simulation [14].  Therefore, simple speed testing is a relatively insensitive detection  method for the lower range of resistive vias.  Production IC testing uses a more sensitive test.  It  measures functionality at two temperatures using delay  fault patterns or other performance-based tests (e.g., propagation delay, FMAX , etc.).   Typically, room  temperature results compared against a test at 80oC or  higher gives sufficient indication of a problem.  Resistive vias have an unusual failure signature in that  devices usually perform correctly at higher  temperatures but fail at lower temperatures.  Most  modern, high performance parts undergo twotemperature testing for just this purpose [14].


Physics of RIL Imaging


Resistive interconnects increase the propagation time, rise time, and/or fall time during transistor switching. The functional failure can be very sensitive to operating  conditions such as die temperature,  power supply  voltage, etc.


Figure 3 illustrates a defective via with voids in its  structure at high and low temperature. When the  temperature is lowered, the metal contracts and the size  of the void increases thereby increasing resistance.  As  the via resistance increases, the signal requires a longer  time to traverse the defective via and can cause a  functional failure at a given temperature.


Metal electrical resistance increases with temperature, but this increase can be insignificant in defective vias  and contacts compared with changes in resistance  resulting from thermal expansion. Localized laser  heating causes the metal to expand and reduces the size  of the void.  The resistance of the via decreases and  heated ICs pass the functional test.


RIL uses a laser with a wavelength of 1.3 μm that scans  the sample in a raster fashion to increase local  temperature.  At 1.3 μm, the laser has insufficient  energy to produce electron-hole pairs when it interacts  with the silicon so that no unwanted photocurrents are  generated in the silicon.  The generation of electronhole pairs in silicon requires a laser with wavelengths  less than or equal to 1.1 μm (Si indirect bandgap  energy).  Silicon is relatively transparent at 1.3 μm and  this permits RIL examination from either the front side  of an IC through the SiO2 layer or from the backside of  an IC through the Si substrate.

W


Fig. 2. Al extrusion into the W via [9].


RIL Imaging System


The RIL imaging system includes a scanning laser  microscope, an IC tester, and a means for detecting  changes in the pass/fail state of the IC being tested (Fig. 4).  During a functional test, the laser scans across the  defective IC.  The localized heating from the laser  changes the resistance of the interconnections.  The  change in resistance with heating, and subsequently the  pass/fail state, depends on the nature of the defect, how  heat affects the defect, the ambient temperature, and the  electrical parameters of the functional test.


The change in the pass/fail state produces an image as  the laser scans the sample.  When the pass/fail  condition changes, contrast variations in the RIL image  are produced that localize resistive interconnection  sites.  Scan times are similar to those used for TIVA.


RIL Imaging Examples


Three examples are given of die with resistive vias.  In  the first example a TIVA system is used to locally heat  a resistive via.  Because of the via’s location in the IC (power to ground path) the TIVA signal localized the  failure site.  This example demonstrates the theory that  temperature elevation decreases the resistance of the  defective via.  The second two examples demonstrate  RIL analysis on two advanced microprocessors.


The first resistive via example is on a 1 μm nonvolatile  memory device designed by Sandia.  Because of the  defect’s position in the IC (power to ground path), the  TIVA approach identified the failing via.

Fig. 4.  RIL imaging system.


Fig. 5.  Resistive via site identified using a  TIVA setup on a memory device from the front  side at low magnification. The reflected light (top) and TIVA (bottom) image pair are of the  same field of view.


The memory IC exhibited a stuck at logic 1 failure.  Stress voiding was suspected, but techniques for  localizing open interconnections (voltage contrast,  CIVA[15], and LIVA[16]) were not successful.  The  failure site was identified using the TIVA approach  from the front side of the IC.  TIVA measures a change  in IC power demand as the laser scans the IC.  The  failing site is shown in Figs. 5-6.


The Sandia design used a precharged word line to  increase the memory’s access speed.  When the  programmed data was a logical 0, the precharged word  line was connected to ground and a logic 0 was read. The suspect M3 to M2 via provided a current path from  power to ground for a word line.  The conductor  beyond the suspect via did not drive any gates, so it  would not be susceptible to CIVA or LIVA analysis.


The failure site was repaired using a FIB "jumper."  Subsequent FIB cross sections of the via revealed the  voiding that generated the resistive (essentially open)


via (Fig. 7). Laser heating of the via thermally  expanded the metal, thereby decreasing the resistance  and providing a better power to ground current path.  The via resistance decrease was detected as a power  demand increase with TIVA.


In general, resistive interconnections that cause  functional failures will not always be detected by the dc  biasing used in TIVA because they conduct a logical  signal (1 or 0) between logic gates and not a defective  power to ground path.


The second resistive interconnect failure analysis study  used backside RIL imaging on an advanced  microprocessor.  The IC was functional at normal  voltage and room temperature but failed as the  operating voltage was reduced, or the temperature  decreased.  Initially, voltage and temperature were  adjusted so that the device was failing a functional test.  The laser heating produced a functional passing result  and the change in the pass/fail status was the location  indicator for the RIL images.  Figure 8 shows backside  RIL and reflected light images of a suspected resistive  interconnection site on an advanced microprocessor.  The arrow indicates the suspect via.  Figs. 9 and 10 are  higher magnification views of the suspect  interconnection at a M2-M3 step.  The "T" shape of the  RIL signal in Fig. 10 reflects the structure of the M2-M3 metallization as heat is conducted into the via along  the metal conductors.


Figure 11 shows the logic waveform of signals before  and after the suspect via.  The waveforms were  acquired using a Schlumberger IDS 2000.  There is an  RC type delay seen after the via in the failing condition.  A FIB cross section of the suspect via is shown in Fig


12.  The via is malformed, with Al extrusions into the  W visible at the bottom of the via.

Resistive interconnections are a major IC challenge that  will grow with future performance demands.  RIL is a


critical tool that provides a new method for resistive via  and contact localization that is effective for front and  backside failure analysis.  It is fast, easy to use, and  physically pinpoints resistive interconnections.   It is a  powerful new addition to the set of failure analysis  tools.

Optical Waveform Probing – Strategies for Non-Flipchip Devices and  Other Applications


Siva Kolachina, Kendall Scott Wills, Tim Nagel, Aswin Mehta, Rand Carawan, Omar Diaz de Leon,  James Grund, Charles Todd, Kartik Ramanujachar,


Sundari Nagarathnam


Texas Instruments, Stafford, USA


Christine Charpentier, , Daniel Gobled


Texas Instruments, Nice, France


Willmar Subido


Texas Instruments, Dallas, USA


Optical waveform probing is a critical component in  flipchip diagnostics. There is a dramatic increase in  the need for backside silicon probing of non-flipchip  packaged devices. The effective way to implement  this strategy is to package the die in a BGA carrier  that allows backside analysis. Optical waveform  probing has been used primarily as a digital  waveform timing analysis tool. The capability of  optical waveform probers can be extended to failsite  isolation and qualitative analog signal analysis.


Optical waveform probing is the technique of choice  to obtain waveforms in flipchip devices [1,2,3]. In the  semiconductor industry optical waveform probing is  performed with the IDS2xxx series of probers [4]. Several developments in this tool technology enabled  improved probing of flip chip devices. The primary  developments have been the addition of phase  interference detection [5] and transparent heat  spreader [6].  Several excellent articles have appeared  discussing the intricacies of optical waveform  probing [7-10].


Success in the use of optical waveform probing for  flipchip devices prompted investigation in the use of  optical waveform probing for non-flipchip devices. This investigation was also motivated by the  increasing difficulty in obtaining waveforms in nonflipchip devices in the conventional topside mode  using electron beam probers.   The principal reasons  for this trend are the increasing number of metal  levels, increased coverage of top layers with bus  metal lines and smaller dimensions of metal lines. The capability to optically probe  non-flipchip


devices is dependent on the  access to the backside  silicon without compromising the electrical integrity  of the package and the die.


The demand for backside analysis of non-flipchip  devices is not confined to optical waveform probing  only. Several failsite isolation techniques can be  implemented from the backside, and developments in  device technologies demand such an implementation. Some examples of such techniques are, Photon  Emission Microscopy (PEM), Optical Beam Induced  Current (OBIC), Light Induced Voltage Alteration (LIVA), Thermally Induced Voltage Alteration (TIVA), Schlieren Thermal Mapping (STM), Fluorescence Microthermal Imaging (FMI). Excellent  descriptions of these techniques are compiled in the  Microelectronics Desk Reference [11]. Limitations of  conventional topside microsurgery with focused ion  beams are also increasing the demand for backside  microsurgery. This broad demand for backside  technique implementation on non-flipchip devices  adds a fundamentally critical step to the failure  analysis process – modification of the non-flipchip  package to enable backside analysis. Jim Colvin's  article [12] at ISTFA99 is the most well known  reference on the subject of modifying packages to  enable backside analysis. In the present article the  applicability of this process for optical waveform  probing of non-flipchip devices will be discussed. A  new rebonding strategy for optical waveform   probing will be presented. This strategy effectively  extends the use of optical waveform probing beyond  diagnostics of flip chip packaged devices.


Obtaining timing data in the waveforms is the  primary application of optical waveform  probers. We will explore how the tool can be utilized in  failure analysis beyond the primary application. Examples of such application will be presented.


Rebonding for Backside Analysis


Rebonding of different types of packages for  backside analysis was discussed by Colvin at  ISTFA99 [12]. In this technique, a non-flipchip  package was lapped to expose the backside silicon (Fig 1(a)). Rebonding  of, either the remaining  wireframe or the bondwire encapsulated in the  package, to the frame of a ceramic carrier was then  carried out (Fig. 1b). An example of such a rebonded  sample is shown in Fig.2.


An advantage of this process is that backside analysis  process can be performed on any package. This  process can also be used for failure analysis of one of  the kind field return units. Comparisons of topside  analysis data can be made with backside analysis data  to draw effective conclusions about the failure. However the use of this rebonding process for  analysis with optical waveform probers is limited for  several reasons. Optical waveform probers demand a  very short working distance between the object plane  and the lens. The working distance in the probers  with the standard 100X objective lens is of the order  of 1mm while that in probers with high-resolution  immersion lens [10] is likely to be lower.


A consequence of using the rebonding process  described above is that the bond wires limit the  ability to navigate across the complete die at such  short working distances.  The sample preparation  process also involves deposition of anti reflective  coating. Under certain conditions the deposited  coating has to be removed for recoating. This process  is potentially damaging for the bondwires.


Additionally, rebonded samples sometimes need to  be thinned further to assist improved probing. This  step necessitates the repetition of the entire rebonding  process.


Rebonding for Optical Waveform   Probing


A method of rebonding non-flipchips that yields a  sample compatible with optical waveform probing is  now discussed. The method discussed is applicable to  bare die as the starting material.  The technique can  theoretically be extended to packaged dice as the  starting material.


In this rebonding process a BGA carrier is first  selected. This BGA carrier should accommodate the  die and have adequate number of contacts for testing  the device and placing it in the fail mode (Fig 3). The  die is then glued to the BGA carrier. Wire bonding is  carried out to bond the wire frame of the BGA carrier  to the bond pads on the bare die (Fig 4). The topside  of the die and the bondwires are then encapsulated in  epoxy (Fig 5). This protects the integrity of the die  and the bond wires. The next step would be to expose  the backside silicon. This can be accomplished by  using a milling tool like chip-unzip (Fig. 6). Care has  to be taken in selecting a suitable BGA carrier so that  the heat sink of the BGA carrier does not limit the  milling process. The backside-thinned sample can  now be coated with antireflective coating for optical  waveform probing (Fig 7).

in an optical waverform prober is shown in Fig. 9.


There are several advantages to this process. First,


the objective lens in the optical waveform probers


can approach the silicon surface to achieve short


working distances without being limited by the bond


wires or any step type artifacts in the package.


Secondly, the sample can be thinned further without


needing to repeat the rebonding process.


The number of intermediate tasks in this process is


not significantly different from that described earlier


and hence can be used for other backside analyses. Techniques like Schlieren Thermal Mapping need  antireflective coating and backside FMI technique  needs a suitable fluorescent film on the silicon. For  techniques that involve backside imaging (LIVA, PEM etc.) an anti-relective coating on the backside is  desirable. This can be easily accomplished in those  facilities that share optical waveform prober  resources, as these are likely to contain an  antireflective coater.


Rebonding for optical waveform probing brings  several constraints into the analysis. Boards for  testing the parts have to be built in advance. Consequently the carrier selection needs to be  initiated early in the pre-analysis preparation process.

Applications of Optical Waveform  Probing


Optical waveform probing is applied in flipchip  devices to obtain waveforms at various junctions in  the devices. The strength of the signal extracted is not  calibrated with respect to voltage. Typical analyses  with optical waveform probers involve the use of the  timing information in the waveforms. We will now  give examples of analyses where optical waveform  probing was used beyond investigating timing in the  waveforms.


Analog Signals


The waveforms obtained with optical waveform  probing are not calibrated with voltage. Consequently  these are used primarily to obtain timing information  and to compute rise and fall times. In this section the  effectiveness of optical waveform probers to perform  qualitative analysis of analog signals will be  discussed. Most of the components in a CMOS  integrated circuit are digital in nature. However  analog circuits exist in these devices. For example, the processes of writing and reading data in a  memory involves analog signals within the memory  decode circuitry. It has been shown [10] that while  the amplitude of waveforms obtained with optical  waveform probers are not calibrated for voltage, the  relation between signal strength and voltage is linear.  Fail site isolation can be obtained by probing analog  circuits with an optical beam assuming that the  analog signals are short in duration.


Fig. 10 shows the results of simulation of a signal of  interest in a microprocessor. The primary intention of


the simulation was to predict the waveforms at the  junction of interest in the presence of via defects. The  waveform expected in the absence of a defect is  indicated by the trace labelled as ‘no via defect’. The  waveform expected in the presence of a defect is  indicated by the trace labelled as ‘via defect’. The  waveform at the transistor of interest obtained with  the IDS2000 is shown in Fig.11. This waveform  depicts the charging profile that was predicted  through simulation. In the absence of this ability of  the IDS2000 to profile the analog characteristic of the  waveform, the defective via would not have been  localized.


The ability to monitor analog signals has assisted the  analysis during various debugging sessions. During  the debugging of a read failure in SRAM memory  probing of several transistors in the read circuit were  carried out. These transistors are transmission gates  carrying analog signals. The waveforms obtained are  weak in nature due to the weak signal in these  transistors. Additionally gates’ switching in the  vicinity couple to these signals and this coupling is  visible in the waveforms. However adequate  information is present in these signals to indicate the  functionality of the transistor. Failures could be  localized in these transistors by monitoring such  signals. An example of such an analysis is shown in  Fig. 12. In the passing condition, one can observe  two waveforms, which are separated at a specific  time as expected. Notice the degree of noise in the  signal. In order to observe this signal, the signal was  considerably averaged. In the failing condition, no  such separation of waveforms was observed. Based  on the circuit behavior, the absence of separation  indicated that the failure is localized at this transistor.


No via defect


Via defect


Fig. 10 Results of simulation of a waveform at a  transistor.


Fig. 11 Waveform obtained with IDS2000 at a  transistor associated with a defective via.


Fig.12 Two sets of analog signals obtained with IDS2000. The signals have adequate information for  successful fail site isolation.


Analysis of Scanchains


Scan chains are an effective methodology in the  design for testing strategy.  With appropriate test  patterns the failing nodes can be localized rapidly. This strategy however can fail when the scan chain  itself is failing. Optical waveform probing can then  be used to localize the failing location. The scan  chain typically consists of thousands of flops.


In the process of debugging the chain with IDS2000 a  sequence of 010101 is first shifted through the chain  at the appropriate input pins. The failure of the chain  is indicated by the absence of the sequence at the  scan chain ouput pin. The IDS2000 is then used to do  a binary search of all the flops within the chain.


Probing is carried out with optical waveform prober  at a transistor halfway through the chain to determine  the presence of the signal at that transistor. If the  signal is present as expected, then the failing location  is in the second half of the scanchain. If the signal is  absent at the midpoint of the chain then the failure  location is in the first half of the chain. Assume that  the expected optical waveform signal is present at the  midpoint of the chain and absent at the end of the  chain. The failure location is then in the second half  of the chain. Optical waveform prober is used again  to observe the signal at three-quarters of the chain. Based on the data at this location the failure location  is localized to either the last quarter or the second last  quarter of the chain. Continuing in this mode of  operation, one can localize the fail location  accurately to a single flop. In the mode described  above, the optical waveform prober is used as a  localization tool instead of a timing analysis tool.


Summary


Optical waveform  probing has been utilized in the  analysis of timing in the waveforms in flipchip  devices. In this article the ability to optically probe  non-flipchip devices is explored. An effective


rebonding strategy tailored to use with optical  waveform probers is presented. Additionally, examples of the use of optical waveform probing in a  non-timing analysis mode are presented. These set of  examples and processes show that optical waveform  probing can be used with both flipchips and nonflipchips for both timing and non-timing analyses of  waveforms.

Advanced LIVA/TIVA Techniques


R. Aaron Falk


OptoMetrix, Inc., Renton, Washington


LIVA (Light Induced Voltage Alterations) and TIVA (Thermally Induced Voltage Alterations) have  demonstrated significant capability for fault isolation. A difficulty with both techniques is their use of a  constant current source, whereas integrated circuits  operate with a constant voltage source. A new  technique that utilizes the constant current sensing of  LIVA/TIVA, while allowing for use of constant  voltage bias on the integrated circuit, has been  developed. As a bonus, the technique is also  significantly more sensitive (at least one order of  magnitude) than the standard LIVA/TIVA approach.


A class of failure analysis techniques has evolved  over the last several years, which utilizes a scanning  laser beam* to activate electrical characteristics of the  test device. OBIC (Optical Beam Induced Current)


[1] was the first of the class. It was later followed by  LIVA [2], TIVA, and SEI (Seebeck Effect Imaging) [3-4]. The utilization of these laser probe techniques  for failure analysis is primarily in fault isolation. TIVA, for example locates ohmic shorts due to the  large resistance change with thermal heating that  occurs at most shorts. SEI locates opens due to the  electrical imbalance of the thermal electric effects at  the open site. A significant feature of these  techniques is their ability to work from both the  topside and backside. Backside analysis is critical for "flip-chip" packages and devices with multiple metal  layers, which obscure visibility from the topside.


One difficulty with most of these fault isolation  techniques is the need for constant current biasing of  the device. Nearly all integrated circuits require  constant voltage source for correct operation. Another issue is noise level, which can obscure weak  signals. A new technique has been developed, which  allows for constant current biasing of the circuit, while still allowing for constant current signal  sensing. A further advantage of the technique is its  ability to reject many noise sources.


Reduced noise means reduced image acquisition  times and higher quality images. Higher quality


* Scanned electron or ion beams can also be used for  activation in some cases.


allows for extraction of image detail, which can  potentially lead to identification of the specific fault.


Advanced LIVA/TIVA - XIVA


General Approach


A basic system for performing these fault isolation  techniques utilizes a laser scanning (confocal) microscope to sequentially scan a focused laser spot  over the integrated circuit. Scanning can be  performed from the front or backside through  selection of the laser wavelength. Some device  preparation for backside scanning is also necessary [5-6]. In addition to unzipping the package, thinning  and polishing of the substrate is generally required. Use of an anti-reflection coating improves image  quality and allows more of the laser beam to  penetrate into the substrate.


As the focussed laser beam passes over an integrated  circuit, it causes changes in the device electrical  characteristics through two effects, generation of  photocarriers and heating. If the laser wavelength is  chosen to be below the semiconductor bandgap†, then  only heating occurs. Shorter wavelengths, above the  bandgap, will produce both photocarriers and  heating. However, photocarrier effects are orders of  magnitude stronger and generally dominate any  thermal signals. Both photocarrier generation and  heating can cause changes in the circuit resistance (photoconductive and thermal-conductive effects) and cause currents to flow (photovoltaic and Seebeck  or thermal couple effects).


For the photovoltaic and Seebeck effects, no external  bias is needed to observe a signal. For the  photoconductive and thermal conductive effects, application of a constant current to the integrated  circuit causes small voltage alterations to occur  across the device in response to the optically induced  changes in the circuit resistance.


The main application of these techniques, to date, has  been fault isolation. In some cases, faults are located  by comparison of an image from a "good" sample to  the image of a failed device. Comparison is not

detailed physics of the laser interaction. The table in

technique.


Sensing Techniques


The original LIVA and TIVA approach utilized a


constant-current source bias on the test device. A


change in the test device impedance results in a


change in the voltage across the device. This change


is then amplified by an AC coupled amplifier, which


rejects the DC voltage on the device.


The alternative to TIVA is exemplified by OBIRCH


(Optical Beam Induced Resistance Change) [7],


which utilizes a constant voltage bias and places a


current sensor in series with the test device. This


approach is more compatible with how integrated Constantcircuits are normally biased. However, experimental  results have shown that TIVA can be orders of


magnitude more sensitive than OBIRCH [3].


Our new approach, which we have designated XIVA*


(Externally Induced Voltage Alterations), allows


constant voltage biasing of the device, while


maintaining the high sensitivity of constant current


sensing. The simultaneous application of constant


voltage bias to the test device coupled with constant


current sensing appears, at first, to be contradictory


functions. The contradiction is resolved by realizing


that the voltage bias is a low frequency "signal" and


* Patent Pending


the constant current sensing is a high frequency  signal. The bias is a constant, whereas, the XIVA  signal only occurs while the scanning laser passes  over the failure sight. An ideal current choke, placed  in the bias lines, produces the desired functional  separation (see Figure 2). At low frequencies, the  current choke has no effect on the circuit and proper  voltage bias can be established. However, when the  scanning laser causes a sudden change in impedance  of the test device, the current choke acts to  temporarily suppress any changes in the DC current  level. Constant current sensing with constant voltage  biasing is thereby achieved.

Figure 2  Block diagram of XIVA head utilizing  current choke to separate bias and sensing functions.


Practical realization of the XIVA circuit can be  obtained by several means. The simplest approach is  the use of a pair of high-inductance choke coils  placed in the voltage bias lines.


Noise Issues


The time it takes to acquire an image of a failure with  a clear signal is proportional to the square of the  system noise. Clearly, decreased noise levels produce  great benefit in image acquisition time. The ability to  pull out fine detail in the image is an additional  benefit of decreased noise.


Noise can occur from a variety of sources:


• Preamplifier


• Test Device


• Environment


• Power Supply


Preamplifier noise represents a fundamental limit. This limit can be brought down into the microvolt  range for typical bandwidths needed for image  acquisition. At these low voltage levels, other noise  sources become rather important.


The test device will produce Johnson noise. This  noise is only an issue at fairly high resistance levels, on the order of 100,000 ohms or higher. However, test devices are not pure resistors. They contain  active transistors, protection diodes, etc., which can  add significantly to the noise levels. Maintaining the  device in a static condition during image acquisition  is also critical.


External pickup can be a major noise problem. Test  devices are typically mounted into a PC board for  supplying power and I/O connections. These boards  are rarely laid out with noise pickup in mind. Pickup  noise due to board layout has been shown  experimentally to increase noise levels by as much as  a factor of 15, even in a relatively benign noise  environment. Shielding can help with pickup noise. However, the pickup is often magnetic, which is very  difficult to shield against. PC board designs should  utilize short connections and avoid large loops in  lines, to obtain optimum performance. An example of  noise pickup for bad and good board design is shown  in Figure 3. The current dependency of the noise  relates to the effective impedance of the test device, which for non-linear devices is current (voltage) dependent.


Power supplies are also a potential noise source at the  microvolt level. Only top quality supplies come close  to meeting these requirements.


The XIVA approach has demonstrated factors of 10-20 improvement in SNR over the standard constant  current approach. The improvement results primarily  from XIVA’s ability to reject a number of these noise  sources. The circuit is clearly balanced with the  signal passing through a differential preamplifier. A  high degree of common noise rejection is achieved  with this technique. A serendipitous aspect of the  circuit is that the current choke doubles as a filter for  the power supply noise at high frequencies. The AC  coupled preamplifier filters out any remaining lowfrequency power supply noise.


Results


Signal Characteristics


Generally, OBIC and LIVA signals are quite large  compared to noise. TIVA and SEI signals can be  quite weak, especially from the backside. Additionally, signal reduction occurs at low  magnification, where the laser spot size is large and  less efficient at heating the device structure. Resistance changes in TIVA can be only a few parts  in 105 for a level 5 short at low magnification, for  example.


A standard test structure was utilized to compare the  XIVA technique to TIVA. The structure consists of  metal serpentines with an insulating layer between  the metal and the substrate. The serpentine structure  is a simple high-resistance path with low resistance  connections. Figure 4 shows two images of a series  pair of serpentines at low magnification (2.5X* and  numerical aperture of 0.06). The first image is a  backside TIVA image and the second image is a  backside thermal XIVA image. Laser power was  identical for both images (~ 50 mW at 1340  nanometers from the objective aperture). The AC  coupled preamplifier and acquisition times were also  identical for both images. The only difference was  the use of a constant current source for the TIVA  image and a constant voltage source with a current  choke for the XIVA image. In fact, the constant  current source utilized the same input voltage  reference as the constant voltage source. The  difference in signal-to-noise ratio is readily apparent. A real world example is shown in Figure 5. The  sample is an SRAM with a level 3 short. The first  thermal XIVA image was obtained at 2.5X, in order  to localize the fault location. Image acquisition time  was less than 1 second. At 10X magnification, the  rather large spot in the previous image is resolved  into three distinct spots. Significant detail is seen in

Diagnostic Potential


To date, the primary use of OBIC, LIVA, TIVA, and  SEI has been fault isolation. The availability of high  quality imagery opens the door for more detailed  diagnostic techniques. The three separate signals (spots) seen in the XIVA thermal image in Figure 5b  are clearly different. The left most signal shows a  vertical white structure around a black dot. A white “tail” can be seen to the right of the black dot. The  signal to the upper right of the group is a single black  dot. The lower right dot is a bit larger and has a white  tail.


The physical interaction of the laser activation and  circuit response is clearly different in each of these  three signals. The vertical white-black-white  structure, for example, is characteristic of a Seebeck  effect. Further evidence for Seebeck effect is the  existence of the signal at zero voltage bias. The tail  structure is demonstrably an effect of the device  physics as well, since one of the three signals shows  no tail.


The tails relate directly to the recovery time of the  fault. A simple model of the device temporal  response is be given by

where ρ is the device response, G is a generation  term due to the laser illumination, and τ is a recovery  time. In the case of photoconductive effects, ρ would  be the carrier density generated by the laser  illumination, G, and τ would be the carrier  recombination time. For thermal effects, ρ is the  temperature and τ is the thermal recovery time. In  both cases the change in device electrical parameters, e.g. resistance, is assumed to be linearly related to ρ.


The effect of the AC coupled amplifier in the sensing  circuit is to produce the derivative of the device  response. The electronics, which includes the

The XIVA images shown in this paper all derive  from a left-to-right raster scan with a pixel dwell time  of ~ 10 microseconds. Thus, left-to-right position in  the images also represents a time response for an  isolated signal. A plot of the response of a portion of  the FET signal (Figure 8) is shown in Figure 10. The  similarity to Figure 9 is quite clear. The tail indicates  a cooling time of the FET of ~ 100 uSec. Figure 11  shows the time response of the level 5 short in Figure


6. Note that the short signal is of the opposite sign as  the FET signal. The thermal recovery time of the  short is sufficiently long (> 1000 uSec) that it is  difficult to estimate. The FET, located within the  substrate, is clearly cooling off much faster than the  level 5 short, located deep within highly thermally  insulating dielectric material. The potential for  estimating the depth of a defect from the thermal  recovery time is clear. Determining the accuracy of  such estimates will require further study. Detailed  thermal modeling of the surrounding geometry will  clearly aid in producing accurate results.


Information on the failure is also available in its  response with laser intensity and current. Cole, et al. have described the effect of signal response versus  laser intensity [4]. Figure 12 shows a plot of the  signal response versus current for a manufactured, metal-to-metal FIB short. A linear signal response  with current would be expected from this simple  ohmic short. Surprisingly, the signal saturates at  fairly low current levels.

Figure 7 shows the reasons behind this non-linear  current response. The device bias voltage is  increasing as the current level increases, which  causes the active elements in the device to “turn on”and pull current (voltage) away from the actual  failure. Clearly, information on the nature of the  circuit failure is contained within the XIVA thermal  signal response versus bias. For complex circuits, this  information may prove difficult to decipher, however.


Photovoltaic (OBIC) and photoconductive (LIVA) failures were not available for XIVA testing at the  time of this writing. However, the appearance of  recovery times relating to carrier recombination and  sweep out can be expected. Signal versus bias level  can also be measured. Effort is needed to determine  the potential value of these measurements.


Summary and Conclusions


An advanced technique for failure localization has  been demonstrated. This XIVA technique allows  constant voltage device biasing, while utilizing  constant current signal sensing. The XIVA technique  allows photovoltaic (OBIC), photoconductive (LIVA), thermal electric (SEI) and thermal  conductive (TIVA) measurements through the power  lines or signal lines with the test device operating  under correct, voltage bias conditions.


An additional, feature of the XIVA technique is  strong rejection of external noise sources. Order of  magnitude noise improvements have been observed. Decreased noise produces deceased image acquisition  times, along with improvements in image quality.


Although it is too early to produce definitive  statements, the potential to utilize XIVA for failure  diagnosis seems apparent. Further effort to correlate  signal appearance, laser power response, and  voltage/current response to specific failure types is  needed.

Session 3:


Packaging


C. Hartfield • Texas Instruments, Inc .• Dallas, Texas


In the current generations of devices the die  and its package are closely integrated to  achieve desired performance and form  factor. As a result, localization of continuity  failures to either the die or the package is a  challenging step in failure analysis of such  devices. Time Domain Reflectometry  [1] (TDR) is used to localize continuity failures. However the accuracy of measurement with  TDR is inadequate for effective localization  of the failsite. Additionally, this technique  does not provide direct 3-Dimenstional  information about the location of the defect. Super-conducting Quantum Interference  Device (SQUID) Microscope is useful in  localizing shorts in packages [2]. SQUID  microscope can localize defects to within  5um in the X and Y directions and 35um in  the Z direction. This accuracy is valuable in  precise localization of the failsite within the  die, package or the interfacial region in flipchip assemblies.


In this paper, the application of SQUID  microscope in resolving multiple failure  modes will be presented. Examples include  use of SQUID from the topside and backside  of the device.  A case study of localization of  a short within a flip chip assembly will be  described.  The potential capability of the  SQUID microscope to analyze IDDQ defects  will be highlighted. The various steps  involved in preparation of the SQUID  analysis will be discussed. The role of  SQUID in a typical failure analysis process  flow will be illustrated.


Background - package


Flip Chip packaging has many advantages  over wire bond designs. In Flip Chip  packaging the semiconductor device is  bonded face down directly to the package  substrate through a bump as shown in  Figure 1. The direct connection to the  package shortens the path length from the  device to the output, reducing the  impedance of the package when compared  to a wire-bonded device. The lower  impedance permits the package device  assembly to operate at higher speeds. The  face down configuration permits the  backside of the device to be directly  accessible for the application of heat  exchangers. The heat exchangers improve  the heat dissipation, which allows higher  power devices with greater performance to  be mounted in the same area. The wire  bonding process of semiconductor devices  puts stress on the underlying silicon  substrate. The stress induced during  bonding restricts the positioning of the wire  bonds to the periphery of the device.  Flip  Chip bonded devices have less stress at the  bond point, bump, thus the bumps can be  placed anywhere on the surface of the  device. By using more of the surface higher  pin count devices can be produced.


The advantages offered by the Flip Chip  package ads new challenges for the Failure  Analysis (FA) Engineer. In the Flip Chip  package localizing the defect becomes more  problematical. The defects are buried deep  in the structure of the assembly. Nondestructive access to the inter-connects is  limited. New techniques are required to  locate the failsite.

Background - SQUID


A new tool, Super-conducting Quantum  Interference Device (SQUID), provides a  useful method of locating shorts within the  three dimensions, X Y Z, of a Flip Chip  assembly.  The SQUID detector used in this  experiment is a highly sensitive magnetic  field detector that works at cryogenic  temperatures, 90 Kelvin.  As shown in  Figure 2. The detector itself is a DC SQUID, which is composed of two super-conducting  tunnel junctions, Joesphson junctions, connected by a super conducting material.  The SQUID system used for the  experiments described here is a MAGMAC10. Figure 3. Is a block diagram of the  SQUID system. Figure 4 is diagram of the  MAGMA-C10 SQUID tool shown in Figure 3. The detector head is shown in Figure 5. positioned over a device under test. The  pins of the device shown here are individual  wired to supply power. When required tester  boards or other hardware needed to drive  the device can be installed under the head. The only requirement is to limit the amount  of magnetic material in the mounted  hardware. Any additional hardware needs to  be demagnetized.


Figure 2. Diagram of the SQUID detector  used in the MAGMA-C10.


Figure 3. Diagram of SQUID tool.


Figure 4. Image of the MAGMAC10 SQUID  tool. The detector is in the cabinet on the  right.

The signal that is sensed by the SQUID  detector is dependent upon the direction of  the magnetic field. Figure 6 indicates the  way the magnetic field might be orientated  with respect to the SQUID. As can be seen  in the image the magnetic field is going  down on the left side of the image. The  magnetic field is parallel to the SQUID in the  center of the image and then goes up on the  right side of the image.  The pencil is  pointing in the direction of the current. To  make an image the SQUID detector moves  across the sample in the direction indicated  in Figure 6.


Figure 6. Drawing of the SQUID detector at  different positions in magnetic field of a wire  where the current is in the direction of the  pencil.


Figure 7 shows that the signal from the  SQUID would be negative for the case


MAGMA-C10


where the magnetic field points down. The


Scanning HTS SQUID Micrtosope


signal from the SQUID would be zero when  the magnetic field is parallel to the SQUID. The signal would be positive for a magnetic  field that is pointing up.


Figure 7. Plot of the direction of the  magnetic field in the SQUID detector by  position in Figure 6.


Graphically the signal is represented by  varying intensity of the red coloration for the  negative case. White represents a zero  signal. A positive signal is represented by a  variation in the intensity of the blue  coloration. The intensity of the red and blue  represents the strength of the magnetic field  at the point. Figure 8 shows a typical  example of the coloration.


Figure 8. False color image representing the  direction and magnitude of the magnetic  field in the SQUID detector by position in  Figure 6.


With the knowledge gained from the SQUID  signal the direction of the current can be  deduced. The width of the white space tells  the distance to the current source. Therefore, the user knows the X, Y, and Z to  the current source.

Background – sample preparation


To get maximum resolution the SQUID  detector must be as close to the current  source as possible. The need to bring the  SQUID close to the current source makes  sample preparation very important. The best  possible sample is one where the surface is  smooth and flat.  A smooth surface allows  the detector to be as close as possible to the  sample. For flip chip die level analysis the  silicon is typically lapped to a thickness of  100um. Figure 9. Shows the best possible  sample.


The worst possible sample is one where the  sample is in a cavity. Working in a tight  space, such as a cavity, limits the ability to  locate the SQUID in XYZ. Tight working  spaces limits the distances that can be  scanned in X and Y. The ability to get close, vertically, to the device is impaired. Because  of the limited ability to locate the SQUID the  sample could be damaged or the SQUID  itself could be destroyed.


An acceptable approach to measuring the  magnetic fields is to raise the SQUID above  the cavity or any objects, which would be  damaged. The trade off is the resolution in  determining the exact location of the current. Figure 11. Shows an acceptable solution.


Figure 9. Diagram of the Best-case (chip) a) Backside: The Chip surface should be  above or planar with local obstacles (eg: caps). b) Frontside:  Wire-bonded chips - area of interest away from wire bonds.


Figure 10. Worst possible case. The SQUID  must be placed in a cavity.


Figure 11. If the scan is on the front side of a  wire-bonded device the SQUID should be  above wire-bonds.


Placement of the SQUID tip is important to  obtain the highest resolution images. To get  the best resolution the tip needs to be  placed close to the window to minimize the  distance from the tip to the sample. The  window must be placed as close to the  sample current source as possible. Figure  12 illustrates the Z positioning of the SQUID  with respect to the backside of a silicon  device.


Figure 12. Diagram of SQUID tip placement  with respect to the sample.

Experiment – case 1


A device was received into the Failure  Analysis (FA) laboratory that failed shorts on  a clock pin after highly accelerated stress  testing (HAST). From past device history the  short was assumed to be in the die. The root   cause was assumed to be ESD. For  electrical testing purposes the device was  powered up by supplying Vcc and ground. When the device was analyzed by photon  emission microscopy the device was found  not to photon emit. Analysis by liquid crystal  indicated the failure was near the corner of  the die. The assumption at this point was the  heating of the die was due to current draw  through the device.


Because the package assembly had been  though HAST testing more caution than  normal was taken to avoid mistakes. The die  was assumed to have failed due to ESD but  packaging is the known weakest link in the  HAST environment.


To test out the hypothesis that the failure  might be in the package the MAGMA-C10  SQUID system was used. The device was  powered up first pass with just Vcc and  ground. To verify the results obtained by  powering up the part in the standard  manner. The clock pin was also connected. The clock pin was brought high when  necessary to verify the analysis. The image  in Figure 13. Shows the magnetic field  image of this defect. When the image is  converted to the current image, Figure 14., the short is clearly seen in the corner of the  die. Figure 15 is an image of the package  from the die attach side.


Careful measurement of the white space in  the image indicated the majority of the  current was 750um from the SQUID  detector. The SQUID was placed 40um from  the silicon of the die, which had been lapped  to 100um. This makes the short 610um from  the backside of the die. The short could not  be in the die. The short must be in the  package. The results did not change when  the clock pin was activated.


A package short or package damage of  some kind would better explain a failure  from the HAST environment. Armed with the  SQUID data implying the defect was in the  package, the package was parallel lapped  layer by layer until the defect was reached.


Figure 15. Image of package from  backside of the die.


Figure13. Magnetic field map of short.


Figure 14. Current image of short.


Figure 16 shows the first indication seen in  the package that the package had failed. Cracking was seen in one of the dielectric  layers. Lapping further into the package  reveled a hole near the failing pin. The hole  is seen in Figure 17


Through the use of the dark field technique  on an optical microscope the underlying  material could be seen. Figure 18. Is an  image as seen through the optical  microscope in dark field mode. Note the  material which seems to span the gap  between the VIA lands. The material seen  between the lands was identified as lead  through energy dispersive x-ray (EDX) analysis. Figure 19 shows a close up dark  field optical image of the lead contamination.


Figure 16. Optical microscope image


showing cracks in the dielectric layer.


Figure 19. Optical microscope dark field  image showing the material between the VIA  lands. The material is lead.


The solder found between the metal lands  was a result of a crack in the solder mask  and the package at the time the solder balls  were attached. The failure was exacerbated  by the HAST environment.

In the previous case the SQUID showed its  usefulness on a short where there was only  one die in the package. Another package  type was submitted for analysis by the  SQUID technique. The package was a Multi  Chip Module (MCM) where the failure had  been isolated electrically to one output, which came from the four dice. The signal  line output three of the required four states.  Electrically the states could not be  differentiated by which die originated the

Experiment – potential


While working with the SQUID system other  failure modes were evaluated. Figure 22  shows an example of a magnetic field image  from an Iddq failure.


The device analyzed in Figure 22 is shown  in Figure 23. Note the extra area around the  die, which has been milled out to permit the  SQUID to move completely over the device.


Figure 22 clearly indicates that the SQUID  can be utilized on device level failures as  well as packages.


Figure 23. Image of a device, which fails  Iddq ready for SQUID testing.


Figure 22. Magnetic field image of


an Iddq failure.


Through the careful application of the  SQUID technique package level defects can  be found. In the cases discussed the use of  the SQUID allowed the analyst to determine  an appropriate course of action. In case 1. The package was lapped to the defect  leaving the device intact for future analysis. In case two the SQUID located the open leg  in a MCM. A failure which could not be  located by TDR or conventional techniques. Experimental work performed on an Iddq  failure showed the potential for device level  failsite location using the SQUID technique.

Rajen Dias, Lars Skoglund, Zhiyong Wang, and David Smith


Intel Corporation, 5000 W. Chandler Blvd, M/S CH5-263, Chandler, AZ 85226


Scanning superconducting quantum interference  device (SQUID) microscopy using high-TC SQUID  sensor has been slowly gaining acceptance in the  failure analysis (FA) community as a number of  silicon device manufacturers are applying the tool  and technique to an ever-broadening spectrum of  silicon technologies for detecting the location of  leakage and short failures by imaging the current path  through the die and package. This paper will present  the application of scanning SQUID microscopy to  short isolation on die and explore the integration of  this technique into the FA flow. From the examples  presented in this paper, it can be seen that die level  short isolation has been possible even when the  separation from SQUID sensor to current is about  800-900μm. Several potentially useful techniques  that will increase the accuracy of locating the die  level short nondestructively are also discussed.


As devices are scaled down to deep submicron  dimensions, switching speed, power consumption, and high density I/Os require flip chip and chip scale  packages. The extensive use of such packages  presents a real challenge to failure analysis of  microelectronics. In particular, new testing and  analytical methods are required to detect fault  locations nondestructively through the flip-chip  mounted silicon and package substrate with finer  pitches and many layers of metallization. Conventional nondestructive methods such as  infrared thermography 1 and liquid crystal 2 have had  limited success in detecting shorts and leakages  buried in multiple layers of metal or flip chip  packages. Scanning SQUID Microscopy has recently  showed promising results for detecting shorts on die, package and MCM (multi chip module)3-7. It works  0


even when other techniques like emission  microscopy and liquid crystal are ineffective. The  ability to sense and localize shorts at large distances  makes scanning SQUID microscopy ideally suited  for nondestructive detection of circuit defects in  buried layers of packages and flip-chip mounted dies  from both the front and backside of the unit. A  number of silicon device manufacturers are applying  the tool and technique to an ever-broadening  spectrum of silicon technologies for detecting the


location of leakage and short failures by imaging the  current path through the die and package.


In this paper, we present the application of scanning  SQUID microscopy to short isolation on die and  explore the integration of this technique into the FA  flow. Several potentially useful techniques that will  increase the accuracy of locating the die level short  nondestructively are also discussed.


Scanning SQUID Microscopy


The scanning SQUID microscope works by raster  scanning the biased sample closely over a SQUID  sensor. The voltage across a SQUID is a periodic  function of the applied magnetic flux. To produce an  output which is linear in the applied flux, it is  necessary to use a null detection method or flux  locked loop. In this technique a set of room  temperature electronics amplifies the voltage across  the SQUID and then integrates the resulting signal to  produce a correction flux which is fed back to the  SQUID. With the SQUID held in a flux locked loop, a computer records the responses of the SQUID as a  function of the sample position and converts the data  into a false color image of the magnetic field strength  across the sample. Then the magnetic field data can  be Fourier transformed to produce current density  maps based on Biot-Savart law 3, 5. This benefits FA  in that the actual functioning circuits can be seen. By  comparing the actual functioning circuit with IC  design, the faults can be isolated.


The heart of the microscope lies in the design and  construction of the SQUID. In the system used (MAGMA-C10), the SQUID is oriented to sense only  the vertical component of the magnetic field from the  sample and is capable of measuring magnetic field as  small as 20 pT (about 2 million times weaker than the  earth’s magnetic field). The best resolution is  obtained when the SQUID is kept as close to the  sample as possible. The system also consists of a  mechanism for micro positioning the sample, a  cryogenic environment for keeping the SQUID at  superconducting temperature (80K) which allows the  sample under investigation to be at room temperature  and in air, feedback electronics to maintain the  SQUID in a flux locked loop and a computer for  controlling the position of the sample and the  corresponding data from the SQUID. Further


description of the scanning SQUID microscopy can  be found in more detail elsewhere 3, 8.


Before operation the SQUID tip must carefully be  lowered from it’s protected park position to its  operational position. The SQUID tip is typically kept  at about 1mm from the delicate sapphire window  when not in use, and lowered to within 25-50μm of  the window for scanning. Sample substrates or  packages requiring a magnetic field scan must first be  carefully wired to the lands, pads or I/O’s. This  process can affect the planarity of the sample with  respect to the SQUID tip as well as the SQUID tip  separation to the sample surface. Once prepared, the  sample can be mounted to the Delrin stage and the  leads attached for supplying the AC/DC current to  energize the circuit path. The SQUID tip must be  electronically tuned before each session because of  the many variables that can affect the sensitivity, gain  and signal to noise ratio.


Short Isolation on Die


Imaging silicon test structures such as heaters, daisy  chains, or serpentines was thought to be nearly  impossible on intact flip chip packages. The distance  from the SQUID sensor to these intact die structures  is typically in the 800-900μm range, while the die  contuinity structures vary around 2μm in width. However, surprisingly when we conducted die level  short isolation using scanning SQUID microscopy, the silicon test structures can be revealed clearly.


Figure 1 shows a serpentine heater structure to metal  line short on a silicon test vehicle. The serpentine  structure has 10 turns as shown in figure 1 (c). By  comparing with the design layout of the serpentine  heater, the short is isolated to bottom of the 9th turn as  shown in figure 1 (a) and (b) with a cross mark.


Figure 1. (a) Magnetic field image of heater to metal  line short. (b) Current image of same short. (c) Package and die design layout with the serpentine  heater of 10 turns highlighted. The short is near the  9th turn at the bottom as marked with the black cross  in (a) and (b).


Figure 2. Daisy chain to daisy chain short isolated by  overlaying the current image on the design layout.


Figure 2 shows a daisy chain to adjacent daisy chain  short. The short is isolated to near the die edge by  overlaying the current image on the design layout. Figure 3(c) shows a daisy chain metal to temperature  sensor metal short through FA validation by backside  thinning of the die. The short is caused by the  diffusion of solder material into die as shown in  figure 3 (c). Usually, this type of short can be isolated  to the vicinity of an individual bump by carefully  comparison with the design layout.


Figure 3. (a) Magnetic field image of temperature  sensor metal 6 to daisy chain metal 6 short. (b) Current image of same short. (c) Physical FA  validates the short location by backside thinning the  die.


In some cases, only DC current can be applied to the  circuit with diodes or active devices. Figure 4 shows


an example of diode to power plane short using DC  current scan. By subtracting the static magnetic field  image without applied current from the energized  magnetic field image, the actual current path can be  revealed as shown in figure 4 (c). The applied DC  current may vary depending on background magnetic  field and the device characteristics. Both good part  and shorting part were scanned at high resolution  showing clear current path difference. This is  illustrated in figure 4 (d) and (e).


(d)


(e)


Figure 4. (a) DC magnetic field image with applied  current. (b) DC magnetic field image without applied  current.  (c) DC magnetic image after subtracting (b) from (a). (d) High resolution scan on good unit. (e) High resolution scan on shorted unit.


The present examples demonstrated the capability of  scanning SQUID microscopy to isolate shorts and  leakages on flip-chip mounted die. In the mean time, all the design features on the die can also be  energized and scanned to build a magnetic field and  current density image library of all the design  elements. In the future whenever a certain design  structure is involved with a short, we can always  refer to the image library for a quick comparison to  identify the difference of current path thus improving  and accelerating the fault isolation process. Figure 5  shows a contact resistance chain structure we scanned  for our image library with design schematics overlaid  on top. Although the resolution of the scanning  SQUID microscopy is insufficient to reveal finer  metal traces (1-2μm) leading to the structure 800-900μm away from the SQUID sensor, the general  shape and geometry of the structure can be observed  as illustrated in figure 5. Figure 6 shows another  library image for an energized metal resistor routed  as a serpentine with less than 1μm separation  between lines. Unlike the heater case showed in  figure 1, none of the finer turns can be seen due to  large Z distance and the canceling effect of close  magnetic fields. However, the heater as a whole is  clearly revealed.


In the next section, the integration of scanning  SQUID microscopy into FA flow is discussed.


Figure 5. An energized contact resistance chain  structure with a schematic of design layout overlaid  on top.


Figure 6. An energized serpentine metal resistor  structure.


Integration into FA Flow


SQUID sensitivity and software enhancements have  been attained allowing correlation of the current  density image with the die or package design  database. Also, recent improvements such as  increased SQUID sensitivity, reduced SQUID to  device separation, advanced signal to noise software  algorithms, improved raster scanning stage motors


short


and optical alignment have all contributed to an  improved SQUID tool and process. As the  semiconductor industry faces new challenges in FA  due to reduced nondestructive accessibility to defects  on flip-chip packages, scanning SQUID microscopy  is continuously demonstrating the capability to  overcome some of the difficulties. It is advisable to  integrate scanning SQUID microscopy into FA flow.     With the aid of die and package design databases, the  layout of the structures could be overlaid on the  magnetic field and current density image. Once a  good match of the die design element and the MFI  current density image has been completed, the exact  short location can be identified and the information  handed off to a tool/technique that can quickly and  accurately reveal the nature of the defect. With this  approach, test, scanning SQUID microscopy for fault  isolation and destructive physical analysis such as  cross section analysis for defect can be done in the  same day.


However, the accuracy of fault isolation is currently  good enough that no present deprocessing tool can  fully exploit the output from scanning SQUID  microscopy. In addition, analysis time at scanning  SQUID microscopy is typically less than one and a  half hours for intact package/die level imaging. Analysis time can be further shortened with a fully  automated CAD navigation and image overlay  system correlated to the design database and the  detected shorts. On the other hand, cross sectioning  or silicon grind back techniques take at best 4-6 hours  for complete analysis. Therefore, tools like, laser  cutting, and CNC type of precision cross sectioning  saw, are required. Without additional developments  and improvements at the cross section and grind back  process, the improved throughput time (TPT) gained  by scanning SQUID microscopy may be lost.


Scanning SQUID microscopy using high-TC SQUID  sensor has been slowly gaining acceptance in the FA  community as a number of silicon device  manufacturers are applying the tool and technique to  an ever-broadening spectrum of silicon technologies  for detecting the location of leakage and short  failures by imaging the current path through the die  and package. From the examples presented in this  paper, it can be seen that die level short isolation has  been possible even when the separation from SQUID  sensor to current is about 800-900μm. In order to  take advantage of the benefit scanning SQUID  microscopy has brought to microelectronic FA, integration into FA flow thru development of design  element extraction and image overlay software for  precise location of short defects is essential. Further,


without additional developments and improvements  at the cross section and grind back techniques, the  improved TPT gained by scanning SQUID  microscopy may be lost.
