p 83-86


DOI: 10.1361/cp2001istfa083


Evaluation of alternative Preparation Methods for


Failure Analysis at modern Chip- and Package Technologies


A. Dübotzky, B. Krüger


Infineon Technologies, Munich, Germany


We evaluated laser ablation and sandblasting as preparation methods for package related failures and for backside analysis of ICs. With laser ablation we uncovered  gold wedges  on an internal  board  of a PLFBGA package without damage of the gold wires and the board metallization. This was possible by optimization of the laser pulse energy and the pulse repetition rate and by limitation of the ablation area.


Sandblasting showed to be a gentle way for backside thinning down to 60 µm silicon thickness. For a surface smoothness sufficient for IR imaging a subsequent planarization treatment is necessary.


Modern package technologies often incorporate internal boards  like substrates for BGA or MCM.


Frequently, nondestructive methods like X-ray microscopy, C-SAM, SQUID or TDR will not allow sufficient localization of defects at these packages. In these cases the molding compound has to be removed. To maintain electrical functionality,sufficient selectivity of the etching process to the organic interposer and the metal lines in board is necessary, e.g. under etching of board metal lines has to be prevented. Metal lines in a board can not be uncovered with the usual wet decapsulation methods using HNO3 or H2SO4 since the lines and/or the board material are etched within seconds. We evaluated the suitability of laser ablation for uncovering wedges and wires of a P-LFBGA 160 package.


For backside analysis, we tested sandblasting for thinning of ceramic packages and silicon dies.


Laser Ablation


Laser ablation for backside sample preparation was proved to be an alternative to micro milling 1. We used laser ablation for front side preparation. The aim was to develop a preparation method to uncover opens or shorts in the wiring too small to be seen in X-ray analysis. The main advantage of laser ablation is that the etching and under etching of board metal lines is avoided.


The mechanism of ablation with an Excimer laser is the instant and very local overheat (laser penetration depth <2 µm) and therefore the evaporation of the plastic in combination with its UV destruction 2.


Experimental  setup


We  used  an Excimer laser with a wave length of 248 nm and  a  pulse length of 30 ns. The spot size was  8-10 mm2. We tested repetition rates in the range  of 1-10 Hz and energy densities per shot between 600 and 2500 mJ/cm2.


Experimental  Results and Discussion


To  remove  the  plastic  at a  sufficient   rate    (about 3 mm3/min) an energy of 2000 mJ/cm2 per shot has to be used. Below an energy of 600 mJ/cm2, we did not observe any material removal. The pulse frequency has to be low (1-10 Hz) to avoid thermal damage of the metal.


Our first attempts in laser ablation (Figures 1, 2)showed a sufficient removal of the molding compound and an anisotropic removal of the board polymer, but also partial melting of the gold wires of the P-LFBGA package. Besides we observed some mold residues (stalagmites). Since the ablation threshold is higher for the SiO2 grains within the molding compound than for its plastic, the observed


stalagmites are presumably the result of masking of the laser beam by not removed SiO2 grains. To prevent the formation of  stalagmites, a laser energy of 2000 mJ/cm2 has to be used. Applying this energy,damages (melting) of the gold wires occur when the pulse rate is too high or the plastic above the wire is too thin.


Figure 1 P-LFBGA wedges and board lines


uncovered with laser ablation. Notice remaining


stalagmites.


Figure 2 P-LFBGA wedges and board lines


uncovered with laser ablation.


Notice flattened gold wires.


Another possibility to prevent the formation of stalagmites might be the variation of the incident laser beam angle during ablation (not yet tested).


Further development of the laser ablation resulted in a process which leaves the gold wires nearly unchanged (s. Figures 3, 4) in comparison with a bonded and not molded sample.


A requirement for nondestructive uncovering of gold wires is, that the area of interest is limited to a few square mm. We used  a metal sheet mask to get a


local abrasion and scanned the laser spot across the mask. We achieved a non-destructive molding removal of  an area covering about 10 wedges(approx. 2 mm2). As the ablation rate is hard to predict, monitoring is essential.

Figure 3 P-LFBGA wedges and board lines uncovered with laser ablation. Final process.


Electrical  measurements  showed  a leakage of 1 MOhm.

Figure 4  P-LFBGA wedges and board lines


uncovered with laser ablation. Final process.


Electrical measurements showed a leakage of


1 MOhm.


Electrical measurements of the uncovered wedges and wires showed a leakage of about 50 kOhm to the adjacent wedges. The origin of this leakage is not known yet. It could be due to carbonized molding compound. We assume that the reason for leakage is not due to sputtered and re-deposited gold since a post-cleaning with Gold Stripper did not show a significant change in leakage.


Besides Gold Stripper we tested sandblasting, HF-dip and dry etching as cleaning methods. We received


best results with a short HF-dip which removes the SiO2 grains incorporated in the molding compound and interrupts the leakage  paths. By performing a subsequent HF-dip we reduced the leakages to a


Original, masked Surface


range of 1-2.5 MOhm.


Sandblasting


Sandblasted Surface


We evaluated sandblasting as a preparation method for backside analysis. A further aim was to get a local pre-thinning method for FIB modifications.


Advantage of sandblasting is a surface parallel,


x100200μ路


SkuBam


homogeneous, mechanically gentle, anisotropic abrasion of hard materials such as ceramics, glass and silicon. Soft materials like adhesive films can easily work as masks.


The mechanism of removing brittle material is the conversion of kinetic energy into “crack broadening”energy. The impact of a grain leads to compressive stress in the surface area which results in shear cracks. Radial tensile stress affects axial cracks in the bulk material. Changing of tensile and compressive stress causes lateral cracks.


In their sum many micro cracks lead to material removal 3.


Experimental  Setup


The blasting material we used was Al2O3 with a grain diameter of about 13 µm. The applied blasting


3SDUnsE


Sku


FA / HCHB23mm


pressure was 6 bar, the diameter of blasting spot was 5 mm. The abrasive rate of silicon was 10 µm/min(sample size 100 cm2). For ceramic it was found to be 200 µm/h (sample size 7.5 cm2).


Although sandblasting of silicon showed the feasibility of local removal of silicon, this method is limited due to the fact that the material has to be removed out of a trench within an angle. This results in a V shaped trench, e.g. a hole 600 µm deep must have a diameter of at least 1 mm (s. Figure 5). Side walls become more vertical when tilting the nozzle.


The devices were thinned down to 100 µm. Surface roughness is shown in Figure 6. The largest cracks in surface were about 7 µm deep. A wafer piece and a die in a partially decapsulated package showed no difference in roughness after sandblasting.


We tested the quality of the sandblasted surface using IR microscope images. With the original rough surface after sandblasting, we achieved an IR image only with a macro lens (8x magnification).


Figure 5 Original and sandblasted silicon surface.


Figure 6 Sandblasted silicon surface. Left side


polished with laser.


For higher magnifications further polishing was necessary. As post planarization methods we tried wet etch polishing (s. Figures 7, 8), laser re-melting(Figure 6), manual polishing and automatic polishing.


We will also apply Anti Reflection Coating in the future 4. Laser polishing was found to be best using 3 pulses with an energy density of 1000 mJ/cm2, but for IR imaging the quality of surface has to be further improved. Optimization is still ongoing.


The wet polishing etch yielded a less rough surface,but polishing is limited by the fact that crystal defects seem to be always etched preferentially and hence a perfect plane is difficult to get. Best results were observed with a HNO3/HF/CH3COOH mixture. The etch rate of silicon is about 1 µm/min.


Figure 7 IR image taken through a sand blasted


surface after wet etch polish (200x magnification).


Area of rectangle shown in Figure 8.


Figure 8 IR image taken through a sand blasted


surface after wet etch polish (1000x magnification).


Perfect IR images were achieved after manual and automatic polishing of the sandblasted devices using 1 µm diamond paste.


A future application of sandblasting might be FIB modification from the backside after local thinning with sandblasting while using smaller grains (down to 3 µm) and a better focused beam.


We have demonstrated that laser ablation is a method to uncover assembly and package related failures with maintenance of electrical functionality of the device.


Sandblasting was found to be a mechanically gentle way of pre-thinning dies from the backside with a promising potential for future applications using smaller grains as sandblasting material.

TDR Analysis of Advanced Microprocessors


David Bethke, Wayland Seifert  Advanced Micro Devices, Inc.


Time Domain Reflectrometry or TDR is an  analytical technique used to determine the impedance  and electrical length of conductors. This relatively  inexpensive technique utilizes a pulse card and digital  oscilloscope whereby the reflected signal amplitude  from an initiating pulse is measured versus time.  The  technique is useful for characterizing the impedance  of a conductor in the time domain, and has  traditionally been employed in board level analysis. More recently, TDR has been shown to be useful in  electrically isolating integrated circuit package  failures1.       Historically, open failures on non-flip  chip devices were resolved through relatively  straight-forward, low risk methods in a failure  analysis lab. Typically, root cause analysis involved  simple verification on a curve tracer, non-destructive  inspection using X-Ray imaging, chemical, thermal  or mechanical decapsulation, optical and electron  microscopy and as necessary, the use of mechanical  probe isolation.  The implementation of advanced flip  chip package technology rendered the traditional  isolation methodologies inadequate. After  verification and X-ray inspection, a decision had to  be made prior to subsequent destructive physical  analysis as to the most probable failure location. Since the board interconnects, board interposer, and  bump locations were not geometrically aligned, isolation of opens through physical cross-sectioning  became risky, tedious and lengthy. These constraints  were overcome through the use of TDR analysis.


The authors have successfully incorporated the  TDR technique into AMD’s microprocessor failure  analysis flow, improving success rate, reducing risk  and decreasing turn-around time.   The paper will  include a brief description of TDR theory and  hardware, technical barriers that the authors  encountered during implementation, sample  preparation as well as details where the technique  was successfully employed in failure isolation.  The  remaining portion of this paper provides illustrative  examples where TDR was effectively utilized in the  analysis of slot A cards, ceramic flip chip PGA pins, and internal package trace failures.


TDR (Time Domain Reflectrometry) is a stimulusecho technique to determine the electrical length of  conductors. It has proven useful for isolating  integrated circuit failures. 1 This paper will discuss  the incorporation of the TDR technique into the FA  flow of continuity failures in  advanced flip chip


microprocessors. With the introduction of high pin  count devices, and complex packaging,  the analysis  difficulty of  parametric continuity failures has


increased. This has led to longer turn-around-time  and a lower success rate. The FA flow for continuity  failures is displayed Figure 1.


Figure 1 FA Flow for continuity failures.


After electrical verification by automated curve  tracer,  inspection of the failure with RTX (Real  Time X-Ray) and SAM (Scanning Acoustic  Microscopy) is done. If the failure is not isolated  after these steps, the next steps are less  straightforward and risk losing the device failure  mechanism. A specific example to isolate the failure  between the circuit board and package of a slot A  card required extensive probing. Some nodes were  inaccessible, so physical analysis proceeded without  a known failure site. The device required a crosssection of the die/bump interface, as well as top down  deprocessing of the die. The failure mechanism was  unresolved in this case after many hours of analysis. These constraints were overcome, in subsequent  analysis,  through the use of TDR, with a  goal to  reduce turn around time, and increase the success rate  of continuity failures of advanced microprocessors.


The implementation of TDR into the FA flow  was  three fold:1) Compare the TDR result of the failed  unit to known good reference units to determine the  failure site, 2) correlate the results to established  failure analysis techniques, 3) easy to use for an open  campus environment. This paper will discuss the  implementation of TDR into the processs flow, including the hardware setup, and method used, the  sample preparation required and the device fixturing, give detailed examples of open, shorts, and resistive  failures in slot A cards, ceramic flip chip PGA  packages and internal package trace related failures  and, the limitations encountered will be discussed.


TDR Setup and Method


The physical setup used and explanation of the TDR  test method employed in our lab will be discussed. The comparative technique has been described  previously. 2 The system hardware setup is shown in  the block diagram in Figure 2. The setup consists of   a  digital sampling oscilloscope, with a 20 GHZ TDR  module. The device fixturing is specialized for the  particular DUT type: slot A card, ceramic PGA, DUT  card, or  internal package failure (not shown). The  oscilloscope runs the Windows operating system. Additional TDR software to catalog the waveforms, and perform additional analytical analysis, if  necessary, is installed. The system is networked so  that the results can easily be distributed in a timely  and


efficient


manner.


Figure 2 Block diagram of the TDR hardware setup.


The TDR system has an inherent resolution limitation  of 1/2 of TDR step rise time, which is about ~3-4 mm  of trace length.3 The method that we used is a  comparative TDR technique that attempts to  overcome this inherent limitation, by comparing the  failure to reference standards that mimic the trace  length of possible continuity failures. It also  eliminates the need for trace layout information, circuit information, or specific device expertise so  that a quick determination of the likely failure site is  possible. For instance, in a standard advanced flip  chip PGA device, likely failure sites are: within the  package, at the die/package interface, and within the  die itself.


The failing pin is probed on the failing unit, and each  reference standard. The TDR waveforms are  compared to determine the most likely failing site (see Figure 3). The TDR waveform displays the  voltage vs. time. The load impedance can be  calculated by Equation 1.4


ZL=Z0((1+ρ)/(1-ρ))                         (1)


For continuity failures, voltage increase to the full  incident step amplitude (500mV) shows an open  circuit termination and a zero voltage shows a short  circuit termination. The waveform shape reveals  impedance information, as well. The TDR results are  then correlated to well known techniques.


Figure 3 An example TDR waveform. The reference  standard waveforms are shown, as well as  a  typical  opens failure.


Sample Preparation


TDR Sample Preparation for Slot A Cards and FlipChip Technology Packages


This section describes various ways to prepare  standards and make modifications to existing test  hardware to assist with the localization of continuity  failures using TDR at the Slot A Cards and package


level.  A brief discussion of TDR probe tips and  cables is also covered.


When using TDR to characterize continuity failures  at the package level, it is essential that one use  identical standards of the failing package type that  have components removed at major interconnect  levels for comparison purposes.  This aids in  distinguishing the minute distances in time that it  takes the TDR signal to travel and return over the  short continuity paths associated with packages.  In  most cases, we have performed tests on flip-chip  ceramic PGA packages or Slot A microprocessor slot  A cards.  In an ideal situation, acquiring the standards  at various levels before they are assembled is most  favorable, however this luxury is not always  available.


TDR Slot A card standards preparation


For Slot A card applications, TDR probing from the  card edge was the only feasible option.  Directly  probing traces (which were accessible) on the  backside of the microprocessor through the circuit  board would have sent the TDR signal down two or  more parallel paths, adding confusion to the  waveforms.  The component under test on the card  was a ceramic LGA flip-chip package with an  interposer between the package and the circuit board.  This configuration presented us with four different  standards that would be necessary to compare all the  possible discontinuity locations (see Figure 4).  The  initial standard required the removal of the flip-chip  die from the package.  Accomplishing this without  smearing the flip-chip solder connections on the  package, and preserving the rest of the components  on the card was a challenge.  A high-speed, diamond  bit on a CNC milling machine was used to slowly cut  the silicon away and eventually expose the solder  connections.


For the next standard, the package was separated  from the interposer by using a de-soldering station  that concentrates the heat to only the component  being removed on the circuit board.  A vacuum is  applied through a spring-loaded suction cup affixed  to the die on the package.  While approaching the reflow temperature of the solder, the interposer is held  down on opposite sides (towards the circuit board) by  a pair of X-acto knives.  When the solder re-flows  between the interposer and the package, the springloaded suction cup pulls the LGA package straight up  and away from the interposer without smearing the  solder balls.


The third standard, which is comprised of the Slot A  circuit board with all the peripheral components  except for the microprocessor, was acquired before  the microprocessor was assembled on it.  Numerous  attempts and techniques were tried to remove the  interposer and microprocessor package from the


board for this standard, but all of them were  unsuccessful at keeping the solder from smearing.


The fourth and final standard needed for TDR  probing of Slot A cards is a functionally good unit.


Figure 4 Sample preparation for the slot A card  reference standards.


TDR ceramic/organic package standards  preparation


The sample preparation necessary for packages is  relatively straightforward as compared to that of Slot  A Cards, and it is basically the same for  various flipchip technology package types such as PGA and  LGA.  With packages, TDR probing is typically  performed directly on the package pins or leads.  This  being the case, only two basic standards are required.  One of the standards would be a package without a  die to simulate an open or short at the die to package  flip-chip connection and the other standard would be  a functionally good unit.  Both of these types of  standards are usually easy to obtain and don’t require  any special preparation unless probing of signals  within the  package is desired.     With these two  standard types it is also possible to identify opens or  shorts within the layers of the package substrate.


TDR "Short" Standards Preparation


The preparation of short reference standards to  simulate a short at the flip chip connection to package


is relatively straightforward.  This can be  accomplished by applying a conductive film, such as  silver conductive paint used for SEM sample  preparation, to connect all of the flip-chip packageto-die pads to one another.  To mimic shorts  elsewhere in the package or die is far more  challenging but could theoretically be performed by  backside ion-milling techniques, CNC milling into  the package and other methods.  Creating these  reference standards would also require an in-depth  knowledge of the package and flip-chip connection  layout scheme and the short would have to be  associated with a failing pin.


A more practical approach to this is to just rely on the  package flip-chip pads shorted reference standard and  make an educated assumption on where the short is  located based on the TDR signature.


Modifying existing test hardware for TDR  probing


Most commercially available TDR probe tips are  extremely expensive; due to this, it may be necessary  to modify existing test hardware or DUT boards to  perform TDR testing.  This becomes especially  significant for increasing throughput when large  volumes of units are tested or if inexperienced  personnel are performing the characterization.  There  are some minor drawbacks that relate to a  degradation in the TDR signal by using DUT  hardware for testing purposes, but these small  performance losses do not outweigh the benefits of  higher throughput and ease of use.


For our purposes, a readily available PGA DUT card  that was used for functional testing of units was  modified, by replacing the female header connectors

TDR probe tips, cables and probing aids


TDR probe tips are generally expensive and  impedance matched to the TDR module, typically 50  Ohms.  We have used fixed pitch probes, which  range from 250um to 1250um in width, and are  available in several sizes in between for Slot A card  characterization. Variable pitch probes were used for  troubleshooting packaged pins, leads and internal  package traces.  The fixed pitch probes are extremely  fragile (definitely not for the casual user) and cost >$500.  The variable pitch probe is more rugged and  can withstand some abuse, however its cost is


>$1,000 for a kit of 3 that includes (1) 1X probe and


(2) 10X probes.


In addition to this, cables should also be impedance  matched, low loss and of a high quality.  Other  helpful equipment for performing TDR probing  would be a micropositioner for ease of tip placement  and a stereo zoom optical microscope


Examples


Examples of slot A cards, ceramic PGA packages, and internal package failures will be discussed. We  have expanded the use of TDR to include all types of  continuity failures. Examples of each will be shown  along with correlation data from other techniques, where appropriate.


Opens Failures  Slot A Card


One of the first challenges was determining the  failure site of an open within a slot A card.  As noted  above there are at least 5 interfaces that are likely  failure sites. In addition, possible multiple trace  branching within the multilayer board complicate the  isolation process. A suitable probing location for   TDR was determined at the card edge. Comparative  TDR was ideal for this case due to the complicated  nature of the board layout, with multiple trace  branching, as well as other components being  connected to the trace. TDR results of one open pin  are shown in Figure 5. The probing was done with a  fixed pitch 1250 um impedance matched coaxial  probe tip, and probe manipulator.


Figure 5 TDR waveform for the failing card. The  failure occurs, in time, after the no die reference  standard, but before the complete module reference  standard, putting the likely failure site at the  die/bump interface.


The TDR results were confirmed with the SAM (Scanning Acoustic Microscope) (see Figure 6). The  SAM data showed a distinct white spot at the suspect  bump. This white spot indicates delamination at the  bump/package interface.


Figure 6 SAM results. The white spot indicates a  point of delamination.


Ceramic PGA


Another open failure analyzed was one ceramic flip  chip PGA device that had failed HBM ESD. Curve  tracing verified the open trace. The number of likely  failure sites was 3 and the  trace lengths  were much  shorter than for the Slot A Card. A variable pitch  coaxial probe was mounted to a manipulator and the  PGA pin was probed directly, and an adjacent ground  pin was contacted to the probe ground. The TDR  results showed that the failure had an open circuit, before the reference standard with no die (see Figure  7). This would physically place the failure within the  PGA package.

Shorts


One ceramic PGA device that had failed to ground  was analyzed. Curve tracing verified the failing pin at  about 5 ohms. The short reference standard was  probed along with the failure. The results of the TDR  analysis are shown in Figure 12. The failure occurred  at the die bump interface. Also, the calculated load  impedance, ZL, from the reflected voltage, agrees  with the curve tracer results.


Figure 12 TDR waveform of the shorted pin.


Resistive


TDR has been  used to observe the resistance change  with temperature of a failure. One device that passed  at room temperature, but failed at cold was analyzed. The TDR waveforms under both conditions are  shown. At cold, the TDR waveform was aligned with  the no die reference standard which indicates an  open. At room temperature, a slight vertical  difference was observed compared to the reference, which indicates a resistance change.


Figure 13 Effect of temperature on the TDR  waveform.


Limitations/Future Direction


Some limitations were encountered while using the   comparative TDR technique. The sensitivity of the  measurement results could be  affected by the quality  of the fixturing (probe pressure, probe placement, interface quality), cabling and impedance matching,  quality of the reference standard, and ambient  temperature. The results obtained could be noisy  waveforms that are not reproducible and  unacceptable for comparison.


Future directions are increased database capability, automation, and data analysis. Presently, each  reference standard is acquired at the time of fail. A  database library would make comparisons easier and  faster. Furthermore, with improvement in fixturing, automation of multiple pin probing could be possible. Finally, measurement sensitivity could be increased  with additional analysis of the raw data.


The TDR technique has been integrated into the FA  flow for continuity failures within our laboratory. The turn-around time has been significantly  decreased due to a more straight forward, predetermined analysis flow. Fixturing for a number of  device types was developed as well, as a method to  create reference standards for comparative TDR.


Correlation with established failure analysis methods  has increased the success rate, and improved the  confidence in this method. Finally, the setup has  matured so that training of additional failure analysts  has increased the analysis throughput.

Backside Etch: A New FA Technique for


Gate Oxide Pinhole and Si Defect Identification for Power IC Devices


Zhaofeng Wang


Failure Analysis Lab


International Rectifier Corp.


222 Kansas St., El Segundo, CA90245


The present paper describes a backside F/A technique  that identifies power IC devices’ Iqcc  (quiescent Vcc  current) failure mechanisms. Choline hydroxide[1, 2] is  used to expose the entire die back, keeping gate oxide  intact. The perspective gained from the backside etch  allows an analyst to quantitatively observe gate oxide  defects as well as Si defects. It is discovered that  either one of them can cause the same Iqcc failure.  More than 60 dice can be prepared on one specimen  in 2-3 hrs. Another advantage of this technique over  conventional top delayering or precision crosssection process is that no SEM work is necessary, only optical microscope is needed to identify defects  with typical size of 0.1 µm.


Iqcc failure has been affecting power IC production  yield, raising reliability issues and causing failures in  customer applications. There are arrays of techniques  such as liquid crystal, photoemission microscope, EBIC, etc. that can readily locate a failure site on a  faulty device. Delayering or precision crosssectioning and subsequent SEM examination can  reveal the details of the defect. However, due to the  fact that only the highest leakage site is detected, these procedures can only lead to the discovery of  one defect.  Because of SEM’s limitation both in  view area and depth of field, it is practically  impossible to scrutinize every transistor gate on one  die, let alone a specimen consisting of multiple dies, within a reasonable amount of time.


Results and Discussion


For both aforementioned dice, the emissions  detected were all within the same sub-structure


- the longest polysilicon capacitor structures of  the PLL block. The physical deprocessing  revealed rupture on the polysilicon and gateoxide of the capacitor at the emission spot, which was in fact the antenna of the capacitor. Therefore, the failure was suspected to be  related to a kind of charging-induced gateoxide breakdown. It was believed to be caused  by the plasma-induced-damage during highdensity-plasma (HDP) CVD inter-layerdielectric (ILD) deposition after the front-end  processes, as only the lots from one HDP-CVD  deposition equipment have a very high  percentage of such PLL functional failure.


To verify this suspicion and address the root  cause of such failures, the process engineer in  the thin-film module performed a machine  commonality check and found non-uniform  ILD thicknesses from this equipment, which  indicated a non-uniform plasma intensity  occurred during the ILD oxide deposition.


Furthermore, investigation showed a gas  shower-head of this system was worn-out, which affected the flow rate, pressure of the  input gas and thus the plasma intensity. The  abnormally high intensity of plasma created  extra charging and caused the PLL polysilicon  capacitor breakdown at the emission sites due  to antenna effect. The longest capacitor  absorbed more charging during such a plasma  intensity variation and its discharging point (emission spot) was under higher electrical  stress, which finally caused the gate oxide  breakdown there. After replacing the gas  shower-head, the PLL failures disappeared and  the yield was back to normal for the lots from  this HDP-CVD ILD deposition equipment.


Based on the analysis done as above, the  plasma intensity variation due to the worn-out  gas shower-head of an ILD deposition  equipment was found as the root cause of the  PLL failures. An effective corrective action  was implemented accordingly and the fab  product yield was improved drastically. The  backside photoemission microscopy was  successfully used in this kind of challenging  PID related failures, and it will become more  and more important for fab yield improvement  with more metallizations and complex design  being adopted for deep submicron ASIC  devices.


The authors would like to acknowledge Mr. S. Gunasagar for coordinating and driving of the  corrective actions. Thanks are also given to  Mr. Rikkie Setiawan for help on some sample  preparations during the analysis.


[1] Ching-Lang Chang and Daniel T. Hurley, Proceedings of the 36th International  Reliability Physics Symposium, P137, 1998


[2] K. Naitoh, T. Ishii and J. I. Mitsuhashi, Proceedings of the 23rd International  Symposium for Testing and Failure  Analysis, P145, 1997.


[3]  H. Shin., K. Noguchi, X. Y. Qian, N. Jha, G. Hill, at el., IEEE Electron Device Lett. 14, P88, 1993.

Failure  Types & Analysis In Cu Process Development of Design-Rule 0.18µm CPU


DongKwon Jeong, JuHyeon Ahn, SangIn Lee, JooHyuk Chung,


ByungLyul Park, JeongLim Nam, SangRok Hah, Kyung-Tae Lee, Soocheol Lee


Samsung Electronics Ltd. Kiheung, Korea


This paper presents the problems, the solutions, and  the development state of the novel 0.18um Cu Metal  Process through failure analysis of the Alpha CPU  under development at Samsung Electronics. The presented problems include : “Via Bottom Lifting”induced by the Cu Via void, “Via Bottom dissociation” due to the IMD stress, “Via side dissociation” due to the poor formation of the Barrier  Metal, “Via short/not-open failure” due to the IMD  lifting, and Cu metal Corrosion/Loss. The analysis  was carried out on the Via Contact Test Chain Patterns,using the “Electron (ION) Charge Up” method. After  carefully analyzing each of the failure types, process  improvement efforts followed. As a result, the pass  rate of the via contact Rc was brought up from a mere  20% to 95%, and the device speed higher than 1.1GHz  was achieved, which surpasses the target speed   of 1  GHz.


In order to achieve speeds exceeding 1 GHz in the  CPU devices requiring the most up-t o-date technologies, studies have been focused in Transistor Performance improvement , Low-K Dielectric Material  development, multi-layer Metal interconnections, and  low-resistance interconnection. In the area of low -resistance interconnections, Cu metal is the most popular candidate at the present. Although Cu metal exhibits low resistance compared to the conventional  Tungsten Plug contacts or Aluminum metal lines, it


presents some process limitations, such as the fact that  Cu must be deposited using Electro-plating method  and that damascene process must be used in place of  direct etching since Cu dry etch scheme has not been  fully developed yet. During the development of Cu  interconnections using Electroplating and Dual Damascene process, various types of failures appeared  in the Stack-type via contact  test patterns. This paper  describes and analyzes the failure types that can arise  in Cu process ,. and The failure types include: “Cu Contact Void”, “Cu Contact Bottom Lifting”,“Dielectric Layer Lift ing & Cu Metal Fail ”, ”Cu Contact Side Void”, “Cu Metal Loss(Corrosion)”, and “Cu Metal Striation”.


Failure Types & Analysis


Charge Up Test


(Passive Voltage Contrast Imaging Test)


In order to pin-point the exact fail point in the TEST  pattern, “Charge-up” method has been used. To find  the physical fail point within the test pattern using the  FIB Charge Up Image, the capping material is


Fig1. Charge Up Image In Via Chain Test Modules


Fig2. Charge Up Mechanism


removed using dry etch process, and “Charge-up(Passive Vpltage Contrast Imging )” test is carried out. <fig. 1> shows an actual FIB Charge UP Image.


Charge Up Inspection is one of the general methods  used in the analysis of a Contact Chain type TEG or a  Device Cell Core. When one or more contacts are  electrically open, the part of the line connected to the  ground is bright in color as shown in <fig. 1>, and the  line becomes dark starting at the point of the disconnection. This can be explained by the following  explanation. When ion beam is injected onto the sample, the metal lines are charged. If one end of the  line is grounded, the line connected to the ground is  electrically neutralized by the electrons supplied by  the ground. The neutralized surface of the line doesn ’t show repulsion against the on-coming ions. But, ions  charge up the line disconnected from the ground, and  subsequent decrease of hit rate causes the sample to  become dark. This mechanism is shown in <fig. 2>. “Charge up ” test has been used to find most of the fail  point explained in this paper.


Via Bottom Lifting


After finding the fail point as explained in <fig. 2>, the exact point was cross-sectioned using FIB, and the  result showed “ Via Contact Bottom Lifting”, as shown  in <fig. 3>. This was found in samples fabricated with  Dual Damascene process, and the “ lifting” was found  between the Barrier Metal and Cu metal. At first, this  appeared to be a result of physical stress, but the  subsequent tests didn’t exhibit any improvement. Thus,


a step -by-step verification of the process was carried  out, and a “Contact Center Void” was found right after  Cu Electroplating, as shown in <fig. 4>


Fig3. Via Bottom Lifting


Fig.4. Via Center Void after Electroplating


It was discovered that the As-EP Void was generated  as a result of the high current used in EP process. The  EP is a “ bottom-up” type deposition process, and in  case of a large -sized via, the top opening of the via is  prone to be blocked before the whole via can be filled.


As a result, the current was controlled to decrease the  bottom-up rate, and the void was reduced which  subsequently decreased the occurrence of the “ Via Contact Bottom Lifting.” The “ Via Contact Bottom  Lifting” is related to the void generated during the EP  process, which undergoes the thermal/physical stress  release during the subsequent processes. Thus, if there  were no void after EP, there wouldn’t be any bottom  lifting


Dielectric Layer Lift & Cu Metal Fail


In another electrical test sample, there was an electrical short failure, and the sample was analyzed. As seen in <fig. 5>, the TEM cross-section of the fail point shows that there is an abnormal layer formed  above the SiN via etch -stopping layer. The substance  forming the layer was analyzed to be Cu, and the layer  was formed between the SiN stopping layer and the  IMD(Inter-Metal Dielectric). It was proved that the  cause of this abnormal layer was the “Lifting”generated by the poor adhesion between the dielectric  layer and the SiN stopping layer and the film stress, which created path for Cu diffusion.


fig. 5. Cu Contact Short By IMD Lifting


In order to solve this problem, a new cleaning process  before IMD deposition and a low -temperature process


fig. 6. Cu Contact Not Open By IMD Lifting


were introduced to increase IMD adhesion and reduce  dielectric stress. Also, as it was found that the “ lifting”is dependent on the dielectric material, the etchstopping layer and the IMD materials were changed to  improve the situation.


As IMD lifting can cause electrical short in the test  pattern, it can also cause open failures. If the lifting  appeared between the stopping layer and the dielectric  below, Electrical test would show open failure as if the  hole wasn’t properly formed. This type of failure is  shown in <fig. 6>. As in the case of short failure, this  type of failure was avoided by introducing a new  cleaning scheme to increase the adhesion between the  stopping layer and the dielectric below, and by optimizing the temperature control scheme to release  thermal stress. In conclusion, if there were adhesion  problems between the IMD and the stopping layer, electrical tests would show either short or open failures.


<fig. 7> shows different contact Rc distributions as  the anneal temperature and time change. From the  graph, it can be seen that the temperature around 200C  yields the best result, and after additional analysis to  guarantee sufficient grain growth, the result has been  applied to the real device process.


fig.7. Anneal Temp/Time Split Test


Cu Contact Side Void


One of the most critical steps in the whole Cu Process


is the Barrier Metal deposition. An optimal barrier  metal should show good adhesion to IMD and seed Cu, and at the same time, should function as an effective  diffusion barrier. Since the barrier metal is deposited  using a PVD (Physical Vapor Deposition) method, it  is very sensitive to the Via Contact and the Metal  Trench Profiles.


Poor via contact or metal trench profiles can cause  local loss of barrier metal, and this can create a void  during the EP process. This, in turn, can result in  increased resistance, which would slow down the transistor operation, and in the worst case, could result  in a device failure. In <fig. 8>, there was an abnormal  trench profile(negative slope) around the stopping layer and the subsequent local loss of barrier metal  resulted in the poor formation of the seed and EP Cu. This type of failure cannot be viewed only as a barrier  metal problem, since it was found that an unstable EP  process or the barrier metal asymmetry within the wafer could cause the same problem.


fig. 8. Cu Contact Side Void, formed by a poor Barrier  Metal deposition


Cu Metal Loss(Corrosion)


After full layers of the dual damascene Cu process, tests have revealed many failure types that had not  been expected in devices with Aluminum interconnections. <fig. 9> shows a top-view SEM of  Cu lines that have metal corrosion. It was found after  TEM inspection of a sample without chemical attack  that this kind of metal loss occurs at various points and


in many forms.


First, in case of photon-induced corrosion, the Cu  metal layer connected to the p-type substrate becomes  excited by photons, and after being exposed to electrolytic wet chemicals, shows rapid corrosion. Metal corrosion was seen during the after-CMP wet  cleaning process, and when the light had been strong  enough, five minutes or less tim e resulted in serious  attack on the metal lines.


To graphically show how light affects the electrical  state of the device, the graph in <fig. 10> shows how  the electrical potential changes when the intensity of  the light on the sample changes. It revealed that the  electrical potential was as much as nine times bigger  when the light was turned on, compared to the no-light situation where no light was allowed to enter the  equipment.


fig. 9. SEM Image of Metal Loss


Fig. 10. Photon Effect


The failure related to the photon-induced corrosion


can be seen in <fig. 11>. This type of failure was  resolved by shielding the light from the equipments


that use wet chemicals. If the light was unavoidable  the time the wafers were exposed to the light was  minimized.


fig. 11. Photon-Induced Cu Metal Corrosion


The second source of metal loss was within the dual  damascene process. When via is etched first and trench etch follows, the under layer below a contact  becomes exposed and in the subsequent wet cleaning  process, metal corrosion could occur.


fig. 12. Metal Loss Caused by Over Etch & Cleaning


As in <fig. 12>, it could be observed in some fail  points that there’s no barrier metal at the bottom of a  via. This means that the under metal had already  undergone corrosion and was subsequently filled by  its upper layer EP.


This problem was solved by controlling the metal etch


recipe. The third type of metal loss is a typical  chemical corrosion as shown in <fig. 13>. It is typical, but the source could be many things, such as contamination in the line, or the void formed in the


Fig. 13Cu Metal Corrosion Caused by Chemicals


contacts below. Tests are still on-going to find the  exact source.


Cu Metal Striation


Any Cu device engineer, using a dual damascene  process, is bound to be concerned with the striation in  the metal lines. <fig. 14> shows a typical metal line in  Cu devices with striations. The striation, when serious, can affect the proper formation of the barrier metal at  the trench sidewall.


fig.14. Cu Metal Striation


In <fig.15>, all Cu metal and IMD are removed to  observe the barrier metal formation. As it can be seen  from the picture, despite the sidewall striations, if it  isn’t very serious, it doesn’t affect the barrier metal  formation.


Fig. 15.   Barrier Metal


After Removing Cu Metal and IMD


However, when the design rule reaches below 0.13um  level, the striation could lead to a serious barrier metal  problem. There should be a continued study on the  subject to deal with it in the future.


In order to reduce Via Contact failures, a new IMD  material was introduced to decrease IMD Stress, and  an optimized anneal condition and a low current EP


fig. 16  Via Rc Pass Rate In Test Pattern


recipe were applied to eliminate Cu void. This resulted in via Rc pass rate above 98%, which had stayed at a  mere 20% before all these changes were applied. This improvement directly helped in achieving the targeted yield and performance of the device. In future studies, topics such as “CMP micro scratch” which is not observed in test patterns but can affect the device  operations, the “local metal loss caused by the CMPinduced barrier metal attack”, “ effect of stress in multilayered devices”, “EM ”, and “BTS” should get continuous focus.

TEM examination of a specified site identified by X-SEM  in microelectronics failure analysis


J.Y. DAI, S.F. Tee, C. L. Tay, S. Ansari, E. Er and S. Redkar


Failure Analysis Group, QRA


Chartered Semiconductor Manufacturing Limited 60 Woodlands, Industrial Park D


Street 2, Singapore 738406


In semiconductor failure analysis, there is a demand that after mechanical polishing and scanning electron microcopy (SEM) examination, the failure site needs to be analyzed by transmission electron microscope(TEM) for a detailed examination to find the root cause. In this paper, a fast and practical TEM sample preparation method for TEM examination of specific site identified by cross-section scanning electron microscope (SEM) is demonstrated for further structural analysis.


As the scale of semiconductor devices shrinks to deep sub half micron regime, TEM is becoming a major failure analysis method for semiconductor industry, such as thin film measurement, transistor profile examination, implant damage, etch profile, via or contact filling et al. However, X-SEM is still a fast and effective method having been heavily used for large area examination and failure sites identification.


In semiconductor failure analysis, there is a demand that after mechanical polishing and SEM examination TEM analysis need to be used for a detailed examination on the suspected failure site for ID of root cause since SEM cannot give clear and conclusive results due to the limitation of spatial resolution. Since the specific site, for example via or contact, is normally polished to the center and even chemically stained, is it still possible for a TEM examination? In this paper, a method for a fast and practical TEM sample preparation method by focused ion beam (FIB) is proposed to be used for TEM examination of a specified site identified by X-SEM.


Experimental procedure


The Cross-section SEM examination sample was prepared by grinding and mechanical polishing until to the center of the particular site. In order to get a


sharp and clear SEM picture, a chemical stain by using buffered oxide etching (BOE) was performed and then followed by Pt coating.  After the suspected failure site was observed by SEM, the sample on the X-SEM holder was put into FIB 200TEM station directly, and ion beam milling was performed to prepare a TEM sample of the site.


In order to protect the interested site surface from ion beam damage during sample preparation by FIB milling, a box (1x10 um2) of Pt coating was deposited by ion beam scanning on top of the site before ion beam milling. Figure 1 illustrates how a TEM sample was prepared by FIB after X-SEM examination.


Initially, a rectangular trench back to the specified site was milled by Ga+ ion beam with higher current of 3000pA. Lower currents of 1000pA, 300pA and 100 pA were subsequently used to cut the membrane containing the specified site to a final thickness of


0.1m. The front side of the site that was examined by SEM also needed to be cleaned by low current ion beam since it was already covered by a layer of Pt.


Finally, the sample was tilted to 45 degree and a “U”shaped frame was cut to leave the sample free for plucking out by a glass needle with static charge.


Details of the X-TEM sample preparation by FIB is described elsewhere [1,2].


Unlike open via contact, high resistance via is normally very hard to isolate by conventional passive voltage contrast (PVC) [3]. Therefore, X-SEM examination was usually carried out to find out abnormal via in via chain structure. Figure 2 is an example X-SEM picture of an abnormal via found inside the via chain that suffered from high contact resistance.  This picture suggested that Al extruded into W plug (here we refer to “mushroom” via). The arc shaped contrast as indicated by dotted lines is suspected to be barrier metal layer of Ti/TiN, and Al expanded into the W plug. In order to understand the root cause for the formation of this mushroom via, it


is essential to identify the Ti/TiN barrier layer relative to W plug and Al metal line. However, due to the resolution limitation, SEM is unable to give a concluding  result.


Since TEM has been thought to be the only technique that can provide enough spatial resolution and chemical information to identify the barrier metal layer, this X-SEM examined sample was further analyzed by TEM. Therefore, a TEM sample containing the interested site of via as shown in Fig. 2 was made by FIB using the method as described above. TEM picture containing detailed structure of the mushroom via is shown in Fig. 3. It can be seen that there is an arc shaped contrast as outlined by the circles labeled 1 to 6. EDX analysis along the areas as indicated by the circles marked as 1 to 6 with electron beam size of about 1 nm confirmed the profile of the barrier metal of Ti/TiN.  Figure 4 is a representation of EDX spectrum from circles 1 to 6 showing presence of Ti. It is also found that there is a breaking point between circles 5 and 6. This suggests that Al extrusion broke the barrier metal during W CVD deposition.


In order to protect the interested site from damage during etching, only very short time of chemical etching can be used. In fact, instead of chemical


etching, plasma enhanced etching and Cr coating for SEM examination can be performed on Gatan precision etching and coating system (PECS). The advantage of this etching technique is that it can reduce the damage thickness compared to the chemical etching and thus provide more leeway for further TEM sample preparation.


In conclusion, a fast and practical TEM sample preparation method for TEM examination of specific site identified by X-SEM is demonstrated for further structural analysis.

Design Debug and Design Fix Verification in a Failure Analysis Lab for a RF/IF Circuit for cellular applications with high Battery Save and Electrostatic Discharge Leakage: A Case Study


Manoj Nair, Paul Sanders, Scott Kiefer, John Steele, Mauri Sutton, Syd Wilson,Richard Ida, Roger Miglore, John Quigley, and Jim Rock


Motorola Semiconductor Products Sector 2100 E. Elliot Road, Tempe, Arizona 85284


A cost-effective, BICMOS mixed signal, RF/IF integrated circuit for cellular phones had high leakage current in “battery save” conditions. It was also susceptible to Electrostatic Discharge (ESD). Our tasks were to identify the source of leakage, to identify the reason for the low Electrostatic Discharge protection, and to fix the design. Design edits of the circuits were accomplished using Focused Ion Beam tool. This approach helped in verifying the design fix on the silicon before committing to expensive masks.


The capabilities of Focused Ion Beam systems are rapidly becoming critical for Failure Analysis and design edits of submicron technology devices.


Time to market windows and expensive mask changes are some of the greatest challenges in the Integrated Circuit development process. We present the results of failure analysis during the development of an advanced dual mode Global System for Mobile Communications (Group Special Mobile or GSM)Integrated Circuit. This integrated circuit is intended to support the needs of the Global System for Mobile Communications/


Digital


Communications Service1800 (DCS1800) / Personal Communications Service 1900 (PCS1900) portable and mobile telephone products. DCS1800 and PCS1900 are the bands in the Global System for mobile communications mode.


Many U.S. personal communications service systems require dual band mode due to lack of personal communications service systems coverage. It is a transceiver chip with multiple accumulators, fractional N synthesizer, reference oscillator with a buffered output and a secondary reference phase lock loop(PLL) with operating frequency of 2 GHz.


This Integrated Circuit is based on a cost-effective graded-channel, BiCMOS technology with three–layer metallization. It integrates MOSFETs, bipolars and passive devices in a low mask count process that


produces an increase in the number of die per wafer compared to its previous generation [1-2].


This Global System for Mobile Communications Integrated Circuit prototype first tested had high leakage current in “battery save” conditions. The current design was also marginal to electrostatic discharge specifications. The goal was to work with the design team and product team to identify the source of leakage. We also used focused ion beam to edit and to verify the functionality of potential design changes before the expensive mask change.  This reduced wafer costs and minimized design iterations.


In this paper we will discuss photon emission microscopy analysis, use of infrared micro thermography and focused ion beam circuit modification to develop and debug a mixed signal RF/IF integrated circuit.


Battery Save Leakage Failure Analysis


The tester, as well as the failure analysis lab test set up, showed battery save leakage varying from 430µA to 4442µA.  The failure analysis lab set up had a custom fabricated board fixture to accommodate the prototype chip and to program it in the battery save mode.  The packaged integrated circuit was decapped and mounted in the socket of the board fixture for photon emission analysis and focused ion beam edits.


The custom board was mounted on a chuck of the probe station. The custom socket cap with an opening to expose the die was focused through a microscope mounted with a photon emission charged coupled device camera. The die exposure through the socket opening also helped in in-circuit probing and measurements. The board fixture was connected to the power supplies and the computer for programming the integrated circuit as shown in Fig. 1.  After programming the circuit into the battery save mode,the excessive leakage was measured from the IF Vcc pins.


In the next step, we used photon emission microscopy analysis to localize the sources of


leakage. The photon emission microscopy tool setup and the test setup are shown in Figure 1.


Figure 1. Photograph showing test and photon emission tool setup for analyzing battery save leakage.


Photon emission analysis, in battery save mode,showed emission sites at all the subVt circuits in both channels of the circuit as shown in Figure 2.


Figure 2. Optical Micrograph showing pre focused ion beam photon emission subVt sites emitting when the battery save average current was at 4.4mA.


After zooming into individual subVt circuit blocks it was observed that photon emissions were not only seen at the subVt circuit, but also from the antenna diodes which provided the sneak paths for the current to follow. Two photon emission sites corresponded to the pbias and pdvbe voltage bias signals (the Vcc path) and the third photon emission site corresponded to an antenna diode connection to ground as shown in Figure 3.


Figure 3. Optical Micrograph showing photon emission subVt sites emitting at the Vcc path and at antenna diode when the battery save average current was at 4.4mA.


The purpose of this work was to investigate failure  mechanism (s) of the Iqcc failure. In order to identify  the root cause, which may lead to the elimination of  the failure, it is of importance to collect data such as  defect size, shape, location, and total number on each  device. In this study, backside analysis is  investigated. Choline hydroxide is selected as the  etchant.


1. Three areas (each has 20-60 dice depending on  device type) - flat, center, and edge of a wafer– are  sampled out from each wafer that has Iqcc rejects.


2. The samples are mounted face down on a glass  slide with chemical resist resin.


3. The mounted specimen is ground down to about  20-30 µm thin (hand grind or on an Allied Techprep  machine <30 min.).


4. Choline hydroxide solution is heated to 90° C on a  hot plate under a chemical hood. The specimen is  suspended in the solution with an alligator clamp. Agitation is provided with a magnetic stirrer.


5. Etching is stopped after backside Si on all the dice  is removed. The specimen is rinsed in running D.I. water for about 5 minutes and blow-dried.


6. The samples are inspected under an optical  microscope.


Result and Discussion


1.Two failure mechanisms are identified:


(a) gate oxide pinhole (Fig. 1)


(b) stacking fault  (Fig. 11, 12)


Fig. 1, 2: An exposed gate oxide pinhole


detected by PEM on a transistor


2. Thanks to the light reflection at the interfaces of  different medium (i.e. different materials of thin films  of the device), various features of power IC devices  can be viewed from die backside after Si etch under  an optical microscope (Fig. 3) by their color contrast: gate oxide/poly, field oxide/poly, field oxide/metal  lines, contacts, etc.


Fig. 3: A die after backside-etch, the yellow


color are gate oxide regions


Gate oxide pinholes stand out in contrast due to the  fact that Si etchant went through the pinhole undercutting the underneath poly, thereby forming a circle  radiated from the center (Fig. 4-7). This contrast is so  obvious that a typical defect size of 0.1μm can be  immediately recognized under magnification as low  as x10 objective.


Fig. 4-7: Examples of gate oxide pinhole viewed


from backside under optical microscope (in red


circles)


3. Pinholes are found on certain p-MOS transistors. Observation over entire area of a device accumulates  data, which eventually led to the successful isolation  of the root cause. It was static charge from an  implanter, which has been eliminated since.


4. When some failed (Iqcc) devices are back etched, no sign of gate oxide pinhole is visible; i.e. the  etchant does not etch through the gate oxide. Closeup examination observes some dark spots on the gate  oxide (Fig. 8,9).


Fig. 8, 9: “dark spots” on gate oxide viewed from  backside


Photoemission analysis, front side delayering and  SEM inspection of hot spots unveils that these are  indeed Si defects (stacking fault, Fig. 10-12) - a  different mechanism that causes the same Iqcc failure. These Si defects are found only in P+ area of all the  failed devices, where boron is heavily doped. This is  a text book example of oxidation introduced stacking  fault (OISF) [3, 4].


Fig. 10: Top view of a failed region after de-layering


and “Wright Etch” stain showing the stack fault


signature


5. It is also discovered under SEM that the dark spot  on the gate oxide (under optical microscope) has the  typical Si single crystal orientation, and the fact that  they were not etched during backside Si etching  indicates that they are partially oxidized (Si defect).


Fig. 11: left: partially oxidized stacking fault on gate  oxide viewed from backside.


Fig. 12: right: “finger print of the stacking fault” - a  defect site viewed from top on Si after HF stripping  from top.


This type of Si defect image has never been  published in literature. The reason probably is that  the backside approach and the extreme selectivity of  choline hydroxide made it possible to preserve the  partially oxidized Si defect at the interface of gate  oxide and Si substrate.


6. The etching rate is calculated at about 0.7 μm/min  for these particular devices.


This back side etch followed by optical microscope  inspection can qualitatively and quantitatively  identify gate oxide pinholes and Si defects with  typical size of 0.1 – 0.2 μm. The discovery of these  defects leads to the failure mechanism isolation, root  cause identification, and hence the elimination of the  problem. It has been proved to be a quick and  accurate analysis tool.

Fabrication Of Inexpensive Decapsulation Fixtures for Small or Unique Plastic Packages


Mark Kimball


Maxim Integrated Products Beaverton, Oregon


A method of fabricating fixtures for chemical decapsulation of plastic packages is presented. Fixtures built using this technique can easily decapsulate very small or unique plastic packages, such as the SC70,SOT123, µMax and others. The method uses materials and tools commonly found in the FA lab.


Moore’s Law has continued to predict the trend in ever decreasing transistor size. A similar evolution has occurred in package technology. The Dual Inline Package has evolved into a plethora of small,sometimes unique, packages such as the SOT, SC70,µMAX, and so on. This explosion of package styles has become a problem for FA labs. Commercially available decapsulation machines have answered the need for consistent chemistry and programmable etch conditions, but decapsulating small packages is difficult if not impossible when using standard decapsulation fixtures. Many decapsulation machine vendors offer a two-piece system, where an etch mask and a package alignment fixture are combined to obtain the desired alignment between the package and etch mask.


However tolerance control is typically poor, requiring some care to achieve repeatable results. When very small packages are attempted, even a great deal of skill is insufficient to provide consistent results. Decap vendors offer custom-made fixtures, for additional cost;but when one factors in the large number of small,unique packages that are commonly encountered today,costs can quickly mount. A new package style can also cause delays in analysis until a new fixture can be purchased.


Making Your Own Decap Fixtures


We found that the solution to the dual problems of cost and analysis delays was to make our own decap fixtures. The method we devised uses inexpensive


tools and materials commonly available in most FA labs, and works with many package styles. Anyone who has sufficient dexterity to pluck bond wires off a die can build the fixtures. Using them, we have found that decapping SOTs, SC70’s etc. is as easy as decapping DIPs.


The Method


The key to making the fixtures was the realization that a package could be used to make its own decap fixture.


A sacrificial package is placed on a plastic disk and heated until it melts the plastic and sinks into the plastic. After the plastic cools the part is pried out of its cavity. The cavity provides precise alignment between the package holder and etch mask, because they are formed from the same piece of material. The fixture is completed by trimming away the plastic that flowed out, and forming the aperture through which the decap chemicals flow. The final step is to shape the fixture so it can fit into the decapsulation machine. The result is a precision decapsulation fixture for a particular package style.


Material Selection


The plastic used to make the fixture was chosen based on two requirements. It had to be resistant to the Nitric/Sulfuric acid mixture used to decap packages;and its melting point needed to be above 80°C. This is the temperature commonly used to decap plastic encapsulated parts. Polypropylene (PP) has been found to satisfy both requirements. We initially chose PP from an examination of chemical resistance tables for various materials1, and the material has worked very well for us. PP is normally used to make wafer boxes,so it should be readily available in most FA labs.


Tools And Materials


The tools you will need are:


1. X-Acto Knife, with small cutting blade (#11 or smaller). If you have it, a scalpel and #11 scalpel blade are better yet. You will also need a flat blade to pry the template package out of its cavity.


2. 15W soldering iron with small tip. Larger packages may require a larger soldering iron to deliver enough heat to the part.


3. A compass that can be fitted with a scribe, or a pair of dividers.


4. Metal cutting shears.


5. Small drill set. Drills with a 1/8” shank are best for hand drilling, although a drill and pin vise will also work.


6. Tweezers.


7. Binocular microscope. The small size of some of the packages makes it necessary to use a low-power microscope to do the clean up work, position the drill bit, and do the final shaping.


In addition to the tools, you will need a source of flat Polypropylene sheet. It should be approximately 2mm thick. The flat sides of wafer boxes have been found to be a source of PP that is the desired thickness. Pieces can be cut out using metal shears. The pieces should be at least 5mm larger than the final size of the fixture, to make it easier to shape the disk to its final size.


Fabrication Details


Set a compass that has been fitted with a scribe (or dividers) to the diameter of the decapsulation machine’s gasket holder. Scribe a circle on the PP sheet. This mark will later be used to cut out the disk so it can be shaped to its final diameter.


Place the sacrificial part, etch side down, on the PP sheet. The part should be placed in the center of the scribed circle. Some SOT packages are packaged“cavity down”, so the package pins will be in contact with the plastic. This is a complicating factor, because the package pins will become embedded in the plastic when melted. It is possible to cut them free, although this requires some extra effort. As an alternative, the pins can be bent out of the way during the decapsulation procedure and bent back after it is decapped. This simplifies the fabrication of the decap fixture, at the risk of breaking package pins. We normally choose to not bend the leads.


After positioning the part, a hot soldering iron is placed in contact with the package. Larger packages will be


unevenly heated unless a heat spreader is used to more evenly distribute the heat. We use a small sheet of copper or brass cut to fit the package as a heat spreader.


See Figure 1 for an example of this technique. A layer of solder on both sides of the heat spreader provides better heat transfer when it melts.


Figure 1 A home-made heat spreader is used to evenly heat the package


When the plastic begins to melt, it will be necessary to use a pair of tweezers to ensure that the part sinks evenly into the plastic. Try not to allow the part to sink any deeper than the package pins, because the parting line of the package is quite variable, protruding varying distances between the package pins. This variation would prevent good registration of other packages.


After the part has sunk to its desired depth, remove the soldering iron and gently hold the part down with the tweezers until the plastic solidifies. Figure 2 shows a part that has been treated in this fashion.


Figure 2 The sacrificial part has been pushed down into the melted plastic.


If the package sinks too deeply into the plastic, all is not lost. Simply cut away the portion of melted plastic which shows the impression of the package pins and parting line.


After the plastic cools, the part must be pried out of its cavity using a flat-bladed knife. If plastic welled up between the package pins, it may be necessary to cut away the plastic with a sharp knife before you are able to free the part. At this point you should see a sharp impression of the package, with a perimeter of melted plastic that was forced out from underneath the package. See Figure 3.


Figure 3 The cavity left by the sacrificial part.


The excess material around the cavity should be removed by cutting it away with a sharp knife.


Exercise care during this and all subsequent steps because the knife can easily slip and deliver a painful cut. We normally stabilize the piece against a metal block, keeping our non knife-wielding hand behind the block.


Note: Larger packages will need to displace a lot of material to create a deep enough cavity. In this situation it may be necessary to trim away the excess material and then repeat the melt/cleanup procedure.


Even though larger packages may not seem to require this type of fixture, die can often nearly fill the package,necessitating good registration in order to get consistent results.


Packages must identify pin 1 in some way. There may be a cavity molded next to pin 1, or one side of the package may be sloped. Quad parts may have a cut corner to indicate pin 1. Any of these methods require us to mark the fixture so users can correctly install packages into the fixture. Incorrectly installing a package will produce a poor seal between the package and fixture, and therefore poor decap results. After removing the excess plastic around the cavity, we identify pin 1 by drilling a small hole near it. However,do not place the hole so close that decap chemicals can flow through it.


The next step only needs to be done for smaller packages, where the etched cavity needs to be < 5mm in diameter. Small holes greatly reduce the flow of the decapsulation chemicals in and out of the cavity, so it is necessary to “back drill” the hole with a larger drill size to assist the flow of fresh chemicals into the etch region. However, drilling the small through hole first creates a problem, because the large drill often forces plastic back up into the smaller hole; and re-drilling can create an off center hole. To avoid this problem, we back drill such a fixture first, before drilling the through hole. The placement of the back drilled hole is not critical; but naturally it cannot go all the way through.


After the fixture is back drilled, the small drill is placed(usually using the binocular microscope) and run all the way through. See Figure 4 for a cross section of an“ideal” back-drilled (and through drilled) etch fixture.


Back Drilled Area Part Cavity


Etch Hole


Figure 4 Profile of a correctly-formed back drilled and top drilled decapsulation fixture.


For small parts, a round hole is all that is necessary for the aperture. However, larger parts may require that the hole be shaped to better match the shape of the die. In this case, the X-Acto blade can be used to cut the plastic to form the etch hole. If a lot of material needs to be removed, cutting from the front and back may be necessary.


10.


After the cavity and etch holes have been created, the next step is to shape the fixture so that it fits into the decapsulation machine. Our decap machine accepts a 1-inch (25mm) diameter disk. Metal shears are used to cut the disk, using the scribed circle as a guide. Other machines may require a different outline. It often is difficult to form a perfect circle, so we often find it necessary to obtain the final shape by grinding the edge with sandpaper. In any case, because the fixture combines the holder and etch mask, alignment in the decap machine is not critical. See Figure 5 for an example of a completed decapsulation fixture.


Note: The examples shown in this paper were chosen because they were large enough to show the construction steps. Fixtures for smaller packages are made in a similar fashion. We have been able to etch 1 mm (.040 inch) diameter holes in SC70 packages using decap fixtures made with the procedure described here.


Figure 5 A completed decap fixture and an example of a decapsulated package using it.


11.


You should always test the decap fixture to make sure that it works for your part. Even with back-drilling,very small parts can take a surprisingly long time to decapsulate. For instance, SOT packages can require 50-60 seconds to expose the die.


Proper characterization of each fixture will ensure consistent results. We normally keep a process book by the decapsulation machine to document the etch time required for each fixture.


The etch hole also may not be large enough to completely expose the die. This can easily be remedied by enlarging the etch hole. Before modifying the fixture, make sure that it is well rinsed with water, to avoid corroding drill bits and cutting blades with residual acid.


At this point you should have a decap fixture that, when properly used, delivers consistent decapsulation results.


Decap Tips And Maintenance


For consistent results, it usually is necessary to clean the decap fixture after each part is decapsulated. The plastic used to fabricate the fixtures is much more rigid than the rubbery material supplied by the decapsulation vendors, so any bit of residual packaging material in the cavity will prevent a good seal. We use acetone to clean the residue. Sometimes it is also necessary to use a cotton swab to help remove residue. Of course, the fixture should also be cleaned before it is put away.


A fixture that has been used a good deal may develop cracks or swell slightly, due to the decapsulation chemicals. If the back of the fixture swells and degrades the quality of the seal required for good decap results, the fixture can be planarized by rubbing it on a


flat piece of sandpaper. The effects of cracks in the etch cavity can be reduced by lightly coating the mating surface with high viscosity ultrasonic couplant2. We have a good quantity of this material left over from a Particle Impact Noise Detector system that we no longer use. We don’t recommend the use of silicon grease because its residues are difficult to remove.


Ultrasonic couplant is water soluble and easy to remove.


If a fixture does not seal very well when direct pressure is applied to parts, an alternative method of sealing the part can usually work. In this approach, a blank rubber decap disk (usually supplied as part of the basic decapsulation kit) is placed over the part, and another,slightly larger, part is placed on top. When pressure is applied, the rubber is forced down around the part,creating a seal. See Figure 6.


Figure 6 Alternative sealing technique.


1 Cole-Parmer® catalog, chemical resistance charts.


Any recent issue.


2. “Multipurpose Ultrasonic Couplant, Water Soluble,Heavy Viscosity”: Automation Industries Inc, Sperry Products Division, Danbury Conn.

Failure Analysis of Plasma-Induced Submicron CMOS IC Yield  Loss by Backside Photoemission Microscopy


Zong Min Wu, Chong Khiam Oh, Soh Ping Neo, Shailesh Redkar


Chartered Semiconductor Manufacturing Ltd


60 Woodlands Industrial Park D, Street 2


Singapore 738406


Backside photoemission microscopy [1-2] was  used to analyze the major yield loss of a  communication product fabricated with  submicron CMOS process --- functional  failures of phase-lock-loop (PLL). The PLL  block was covered by five metal layers and  three of them were bulk metals. Based upon  the backside photoemissions detected on the  capacitor structures within the PLL block and  the ruptures observed at the emission spots on  the polysilicon and gate oxide or of the  capacitor after physical deprocessing, the  failure was proved due to the capacitor gateoxide breakdown. This was believed to be  caused by the plasma-induced-damage during  high-density-plasma (HDP) CVD oxide  deposition after the front-end processes, as  only the lots from one HDP-CVD deposition  equipment have very high percentage PLL  functional failure. Subsequent machine  commonality check did find non-uniform inter  layer dielectric (ILD) thickness from this  equipment, which indicated the non-uniform  plasma intensity occurred during the ILD film  deposition.  This was further confirmed by the  finding of a worn-out gas-shower-head in this  system. The abnormal high density of plasma  created extra charging and caused the PLL  poly capacitor’s gate oxide breakdown due to  the antenna effect. After replacing the gasshower-head, the failure was disappeared and  yield was back to normal. Through this low  yield analysis, we demonstrated an effective  application of backside photoemission  microscopy to fab yield improvement.


Photoemission Microscopy (PEM) is often one  of the first line fault isolation techniques for  failure analysis. However, with the  development of IC design and fabrication  technology, more and more interconnect  metallization are used to boost integration  density and circuit performance. Furthermore,


various new packaging technologies, e.g., flip  chip, lead on chip, etc. are adopted to handle  high I/O counts, especially for ASIC chips. All  of these factors have made it difficult or  impossible to perform fault isolation with PEM  from the front-side of the device. Therefore, for the complex logic chip of more layers of  metallization or advanced package  aforementioned, the need of PEM analysis  from the chip backside became apparent.


On the other hand, with the IC fabrication  going into deep sub-micron, more plasma steps  have been used in both dry-etch and thin-film  deposition processes. Moreover, the plasma  density has also been significantly increased in  process such as high-density-plasma (HDP) CVD deposition. During plasma processing, current can pass through the thin gate oxide as  it is under high electric field stress. As a result  of this current flow, gate oxides are  prematurely broken down or worn out. This  phenomenon is called plasma-induced-damage (PID). The extent of the charging damage will  also be aggravated as more plasma steps have  been increasingly used in deep sub-micro  device fabrication. Recently, there have been  many reports on gate oxide damage due to  plasma processing in the literature [3-6].


Traditionally, the main concern of the PID is  on the dry-etching process such as poly etch, metal etch, contact etch, etc. However, gate  oxide damage that induced by the HDP-CVD  deposition in the backend process of the device  fabrication was also reported [7]. Normally, there are two dominant mechanisms governing  charging damage: one is plasma nonuniformity and the other is topological effects  on the wafer surface. Due to its induced nature, PID related failures are normally not  straightforward and it is sometimes difficult to  conclude the root cause. By using backside  PEM for the analysis of such kind of  challenging failure with success, it is proved  that backside PEM analysis is very effective in  isolating such subtle and critical failures  induced by plasma abnormality for the  complex ASIC chip with multi-metallizations.
