Figure 4. Layout showing the subVt circuit block.


Figure 5. Optical Micrograph showing focused ion beam tool deposited metal strap connection.


A focused ion beam tool was used to re-route the interconnects to modify the circuit design as shown in Figure 5. Since there are two receive channels and three subVt circuits per channel, 12 focused ion beam edits had to be completed for both Vcc and Gnd. We understood the problem from the results of the photon emission analysis and layout verification. We wanted to verify the fix on a die before committing to expensive mask changes and spending time and


money processing wafers. All the reconnections were at the top level metal. Post focused ion beam modified devices showed battery save leakage of 47µA which is nominal with no photon emission from the subVt circuit blocks.


Battery Save Leakage Failure Analysis using Infrared Thermography


The same integrated circuit from a different mask revision had high battery save leakage.  Photon emission analysis did not reveal the source of the leakage in the circuit.  Due to the high density of the circuit a focused ion beam tool circuit isolation method would have been time consuming. Infrared thermography was used as an alternative approach to identify the hot spot [3] as shown in Figure 6.


Figure 6. Optical micrograph of infrared image with hot spot compared with the layout data.


After close verification of the layout with the hot spot identified it was revealed that design rules were violated where several N-wells were very close to the trench of a bipolar.


Electrostatic Discharge Circuit Leakage Failure Analysis


The same integrated circuit had leakage associated with the electrostatic discharge circuits. The electrostatic discharge protection on the pads was one of the sources of leakage. The focused ion beam tool was used to isolate the zener diode from the silicon control rectifier circuit. The silicon control rectifier was also isolated from the bond pad to identify whether the leakage was associated with it as shown in Figure 7.


After focused ion beam cuts, the IV curve confirmed that the leakage was associated with the silicon


controlled rectifier as shown in Figure 8.


The next step was to identify the source of leakage in the secondary electrostatic discharge protection circuit. Devices in the electrostatic discharge


subcircuits were focused ion beam isolated as shown in Figure 9.


Figure 7. Optical Micrograph showing focused ion beam cuts to disconnect zener diode from the silicon controlled rectifier to confirm the source of electrostatic discharge leakage.


Figure 8. IV curve showing the leakage of silicon controlled rectifier after focused ion beam isolation from the bond pad and the zener diode.


After focused ion beam isolation of the two spot zeners in the bias section of the circuit, it was conclusively determined that they are the source of leakage in the secondary protection circuit.


Spot diodes or antennae diodes are added to the circuits to negate the effects of ions collecting on metal and poly layers during processing. With most modern semiconductor processes, ions collecting on gates from interconnect metal that is not attached to a diffusion will induce some shift in device threshold voltages due to charge collection and trapping in gate oxides. In most cases, these metal extensions or antennae will induce threshold shifts proportional to the ratio of interconnect to gate area. In the case of critically matched analog circuits where the interconnect metal is not attached to a junction during any one step of the process, the use of spot diodes effectively eliminates ion induced threshold shifts by


providing a junction to bleed off collected ions. The reverse biased junction of a minimum diffusion is sufficient to prevent ion build up. By using a minimum sized device, noise coupling through the diode is also minimized. An  additional NGC  implant  available  to this graded channel technology was added to the conventional antennae diode  to lower the  avalanche breakdown  voltage  and  thus creating  a “zener  spot diode”. This is beneficial to ESD performance.


Figure 9. Optical Micrograph showing spot zener diode isolation and focused ion beam pad to confirm source of electrostatic discharge sub-circuit leakage.


Figure 10. IV Curve showing the leakage of isolated spot zener diode measured using the focused ion beam pad.


Focused ion beam isolation and focused ion beam pad-out of defective spot zener diode was done as shown in Figure 9.  Post focused ion beam IV curves of the spot zener diode confirmed that the diode was leaky as indicated by 11.4µA at 2.5V as shown in Figure 10. This was also confirmed using photon emission analysis as shown in Figure 11.


Further analysis showed that the spot zener diode,


without the buried layer, was susceptible to leakage.


The analysis followed with cross-sections of “inprocess” and completely processed devices and


imaged using Transmission Electron Microscope and Field Emission Scanning Electron Microscope.


Figure 11. Optical Micrograph showing photon emission site confirming the spot zener diode Leakage.


Transmission Electron Microscope analysis of “inprocess” material showed salicide thickness in the range of 50nm. SEM analysis of spot diode crosssections showed significant over etch of the contacts,penetrating the salicide region and perhaps the PSD/NGC junction as shown in Figure 12.


Design of this spot diode was changed by adding buried layer to fix the diode leakage and generate more robust electrostatic discharge protection.


Figure 12. Scanning electron micrograph of a spot diode.


Summary and Conclusion


In this case study we have discussed fault identification of battery save leakage with different


fail signatures using two different tools namely photon emission and infrared thermography.


Focused ion beam tool was effectively used in the debug of this integrated circuit and also for the design edits and verification.


The use of different failure analysis tools to identify fault location and design verification using focused ion beam helped in cycle time reduction, saved expensive mask change costs, minimized number of wafers processed and time to market is evident from this case study.


For the calculation of the DFT, a free software  library was used called FFTW (The fastest  transform in the West6), which fulfils the  requirements of fast transformation and also  flexible image dimensions.

4. Threshold the power spectrum image to  generate a mask which consists of a certain  proportion (approximately 90%) of the  brightest points in the power spectrum image.  This value has been found to work very well  for most images, although it can be fine-tuned  if necessary.


The resulting mask eliminates the bright spots  that are associated with the periodic  components of the image, and can effectively  maintain the information due to defects, as  explained in  Figure 3.


5. Perform image dilation to extend each detected  spot in the image by approximately one pixel, thereby also including low-level periodic  information that is below the threshold value  of step 4 (Figure 4).

Visualisation


This tool has the effect of making obvious defects  or irregularities immediately visible such as A in  Figure 6, but more importantly, it also clearly  depicts smaller irregularities, such as B, which are  not easily visible in the original image.  Of course  the tool is also employed as a localisation aid in


cases of signature analysis where a fail region or  fail address is being analysed, for which it can very  quickly and efficiently compare the fail cell to the  average image of all the neighboring cells.  The  working tool has several options for visualisation  involving image switching in-place, and also with a  timer for the added convenience of the analyst.  It  could also be applied to SEM images taken by  inline review SEMs, allowing fast offline  inspection of small defects which may be below the  detection limit of optically based tools.


Figure 6  Processed image showing differences, such as A (easy to see in the original image), but  also B (difficult to see in original image).

The original image which was described  earlier (Figure 1) is typical of those taken during  full-wafer baseline yield analysis, in which  electrically identified failure addresses are  inspected after step-by step delayering.


In Figure 7 an example is shown from a full wafer  analysis case in which a DRAM product wafer has  been delayered to the wordline level, also exposing  the bitline contacts.  The spatially filtered image  allows the defect to be instantly noticed and very  clearly recognised.  It is a patterning defect caused  by blocked lithography, and electrically causes a


WL-BL cross fail.  In Figure 8 a further example is  shown, and again the defect is very clearly depicted  in the spatially filtered image.  In this case the  sample is taken from a similar type of analysis, but  deprocessed down to the substrate silicon level, exposing the tops of the deep trenches and active  areas.  In this case, the defect is due to blocked etch  during one of the front-end of line processes, and  electrically causes a cluster of single cell fails in  two areas.


Periodic integrated circuit defects are quickly and  effectively detected by a fully automatic, fine  tunable spatial filtering program on any PC-based  imaging tool in the FA laboratory.

ABSTRACT


The fabrication of semiconductor devices is  handling, processing and test verification  intensive all of which present opportunities for  electrical over stress (EOS) or electro static  discharge (ESD) to occur. Well-documented  models for ESD exist.  These include Human  Body Model (HBM), Machine Model (MM) and  Charged Body Model (CBM), but such is not the  case for EOS and its manifestations. In addition, as device technologies change and reduce in  dimension these geometric reductions create  increases in operating currents and magnetic  fields located on the die surface. When there are  occasions where devices are overstressed  electrically in new device technologies, the  manifestation or evidence of the EOS maintains  the same appearance while physical dimensions  have become much reduced. On occasions, the  manifestation or evidence of EOS in some new  device technologies tends to appear different  from anything we have seen in past device  technologies. The resolution of these new failure  modes is not trivial to analyze. This case study  will detail the diagnostic journey used to resolve  one such new and unique failure, the “Star  Crack”.


Definition of the problem


The EOS problem started in the European  market sector where it was first identified by the  Nice-France FA Lab as a star crack in the  protective overcoat, Figure 1. Cracking in the  protect over-coat is typically an assembly issue.  Therefore, the problem was relayed to the  Philippines assembly site for resolution while  electrical analysis continued in Nice.


In the Philippines assembly site the defect was  described as:


- High functional failure rate at final test.


- Star crack seen on one particular structure  type within the device.


Figure 1. Optical image of Star cracks, “a” and “b”. One of the dummy metal is indicate by the  arrow.


All of the defects were in one module, the ALU, within the device. Figure 2 shows the location of  the ALU module. Figure 3 shows the locations  within the ALU where the defects were found.


Even within the module all defects were only at  the corner of one specific structure, the dummy  metal. Dummy metal, indicated to by the arrow  in Figure 1, is a floating piece of metal used to  ensure uniformity during metal etching and  chemical mechanical polishing (CMP) processes.


Figure 2. Die map showing the location of the  ALU within the device.

The unique structure of the star crack initially  raised speculation that it might be caused by  mechanical damage to the protective overcoat  created during the assembly process. A typical  defect site for mechanical damage is shown in   Figure 4. The defect shown in the optical  micrograph of Figure 4 was inspected by  Scanning Electron Microscope (SEM) to obtain a  higher magnification image of the defect, Figure


5. Notice the defect shape typifies common  mechanical damage in the integrated circuits.


Figure 4. Optical image of a fracture in the  passivation, which is typical of mechanical  damage.


Figure 5. SEM image of a fracture in the  passivation, which is typical of mechanical  damage. Image seen is the defect shown In  Figure 4.


At first glance, the star crack appeared to be  mechanical damage in the passivation caused  by fillers in the mold compound. The Philippine  F/A Team quickly responded by developing a  set of experiments, which were to identify the  root case of the star crack.


The first experiment was to determine if the  passivation thickness was out of specification. Mechanical damage to the passivation, caused  by the package mold compound, can occur if  passivation thickness specifications have not  been met.


Cross sections of the suspected failsite were  made on reference units and failing units. A  SEM image of the reference cross section is  shown in Figure 6 and a SEM image of the  failing device cross section is shown in Figure 7. The results of the cross sections showed the  passivation to be within specification.


Figure 6. SEM cross sectional image of a  reference device. Measurements of the  passivation are: a) 0.928um b) 0.935um and c)


1.153um.


Figure 7.  SEM image of the cross section of a  failing device. The measurements are a)


1.122um b)  0.917um and c) 1.227 .


The cross sections of the reference device and  the failing device confirmed the passivation  thickness was within specification but told  nothing about the location of the defect in  relation to the layers of the device. At this point,


b c


c


more data was required by the Philippine  Assembly F/A Team. To understand the  character of the defect. Optical and SEM images  were taken of the damage passivation at the  failsites.


Figures 8, 9 and 10 are typical examples of the  images obtained during visual review of the starcrack. In Figure 8. the star crack appears to be  fuzzy or worn, not truly cracked. In Figures 9  and 10 the star crack also exhibits the worn look  of Figure 8. The worn look is characterized by a  crystalline appearance along the length of the “cracks.”


The difference in appearance of the star crack  as seen in Figures 1, 8, 9 and 10 as compared  with that of the mechanical damage as seen in  Figures 4 and 5, combined with the knowledge


that the passivation is in specification, lead the  Philippine Assembly F/A Team to speculate the  defect was not surface damage caused by  mechanical defects. The Philippine F/A Team  then sent the unit, shown in Figure 10, to the  United States for focused ion beam (FIB) cross  sectioning through the star crack. The hope was  to determine the level at which the defect  started. SEM micrographs of the cross section, Figure 11., revealed the damage originated at  Metal 2 and ended at the top level dummy  metal.


Figure 11. SEM micrograph cross section of the  star crack showing the crack in the passivation  extending from Metal 2 to the dummy metal.


Using the results derived thus far, the Philippine  Assembly F/A Team attempted to correlate  defects observed at the final test with possible  tool damage created during assembly. During  initial observations there appeared to be a  correlation between the saw used and the high  fallout due to functional fails. The apparent  correlation is shown in Figure 12.

Electrical Testing


While the Philippines Assembly F/A/ Team was  working on potential sources of damage to the  device ether mechanical or electrical which  might be caused by the assembly process, France was looking into the possibility of  process or electrical issues which might have  caused the failure.  Liquid crystal testing shown  in Figure 18, had confirmed the failsite to be at  the end of the top level dummy metal. Careful  deprocessing in France revealed meal bridging  between two metal lines at the Metal 2 level. Figure 19. shows the remnants of the metal  shorts laying on the oxide after the metal has  been removed. A layout of the location shown in


figure 19 is shown in figure 20. The arrows point  to the two lines, which were shorted.


Figure 18. Optical image of a liquid crystal test  showing the locations of hot spots within the  device.


Figure 19. SEM micrograph of metal shorts  found after the removal of the metal lines.


Knowledge of the failsite location gave test  engineers insight into the potential cause of the  failure. Through experimentation with one of the  signals supplied to the device, a race condition  was found which caused the electrical over  stress. By changing the timing of the final test  pattern the failmode was eliminated.

Figure 20. Drawing of the physical layout at the  location of the shorts seen in Figure 19.


The final interpretation of the data concluded  there were no process induced defects. The  heating of the two metal lines that eventually  shorted caused the failmode. The heating  caused the dummy metal to expand rubbing the  passivation against the package. The expansion  and the rubbing motion fractured the passivation  causing the star crack.


From a device reliability stand point, structures  added to the design that should be of no  consequence can cause failures. The dummy  metal discussed here was added to the device  to improve manufacturability. Under the  condition of excessive heating the dummy metal, a non electrical component, contributed to the  failure, adding its own unique appearance to the  failsite.


Collaborative efforts between design, test, product and failure analysis engineering is  required today to resolve complex issues. The  failmode described required the combined  efforts of engineering staff from around the  world. The final outcome of this analysis is that  due to the timing of one signal a race condition  occurred within the device which electrically over  stressed the device. Heating of the metal lines  caused the dummy metal to expand, rubbing the  metal against the package. The expansion of the  dummy metal fractured the passivation, which  caused the star crack. Without the dummy metal  most likely the passivation would not have “cracked”. The only evidence that the defect  existed would have been the metal short. No  star crack would have been seen.


Session 5:


Backside 1


M. Thayer. Advanced Micro Devices, Inc.• Austin, Texas


p 151-159


DOI: 10.1361/cp2001istfa151


F. Beaudoin, R. Desplats, P. Perdu


CNES-THALES Laboratory, 18, avenue Edouard Belin, 31401 Toulouse, France.


D. Lewis


University of Bordeaux 1, Talence, France


Several considerations related to the implementation of the thermal laser stimulation method (OBIRCH,TIVA) in a failure analysis laboratory will be discussed. At the CNES (French Space Agency), we implemented this method on a dual system which includes an emission microscope and a laser-scanning microscope. The amplifier used for amplifying the weak voltage or current variations caused by thermal laser stimulation was shown to be a key factor. The design of such a low noise, high gain and fast voltage amplifier is described. From a 3D finite element ANSYS model of the thermal laser stimulation effect combined with three practical case studies we show that thermal laser stimulation is a rapid and precise method for localizing metallic short type faults in ICs. In order to interpret the thermal laser stimulation signal, a simple CMOS inverter model is also presented.


Fault localization in ICs is an important task for debug analysis and yield improvement. Traditional front-side techniques such as optical microscopy,liquid crystals and emission microscopy are challenged by the use of several metal layers and by the appearance of new packaging technologies. New alternative techniques have recently been developed to tackle fault localization from the backside of the die [1,2]. The thermal laser stimulation method has shown to be very promising for locating current leakage type faults. This method is also known in the literature as Optical Beam Induced Resistance Change (OBIRCH) [3] or Thermally Induced Voltage Alteration (TIVA) [4]. The difference between these two methods lies in the IC biasing method. In the OBIRCH method, the ICs are biased under a constant voltage whereas in the TIVA method, the ICs are biased under a constant current.


In thermal laser stimulation, an infrared laser beam is scanned over the IC front or backside in order to generate localized heat sources and thermal gradients in its metallic elements. The resistance variation of the heated metals will modify the IC’s power consumption. Correlating the induced current


(OBIRCH) or voltage (TIVA) variations with the laser spot location provides information on the location of the resistive type defects.


Before successfully implementing the thermal laser stimulation method in a failure laboratory, several considerations need to be taken into account. How precisely can it locate current related faults? How the weak voltage or current variations generated by laser heating can possibly be detected? And how easily the thermal laser stimulation image of the IC’s power consumption variations can be interpreted?


To answer these questions, we start by presenting a 3D finite element ANSYS model of the thermal laser stimulation method. Then we will describe several considerations relating to its implementation on an infrared laser-scanning microscope. The development of a specific low noise, high gain and fast voltage amplifier will be presented. Using this amplifier, we successfully applied the thermal laser stimulation method to localize defects in three different current failed ICs. Finally, we will present an inverter model for understanding the IC’s response to the thermal laser stimulation.


Thermal Laser Stimulation Model


Model Description


Our first objective was to understand the heating mechanism induced by the infrared laser in order to gain insight into the heating dynamics and the resolution of the thermal laser stimulation method.


The spatial resolution depends on the time delay between the impact of the laser and the induced resistance change. This delay time is essentially governed by the response time of the heating process.


To do so, a 3D finite element (FEM) model with ANSYS (v5.6) of a simple metal line embedded in silicon dioxide was created (Figure 1). The 1μm wide by 0.5μm thick metal line is covered above and below by silicon oxide of 1μm thickness. The width and the length of the whole structure were fixed at 10μm and 120μm respectively, while the silicon substrate was modeled for a thickness of 4μm.


Figure 1 Schematic diagram of the structure modeled in the ANSYS simulation tool.


In this study, we used the material properties of thin film aluminum [5,6], bulk silicon oxide [5] and bulk silicon [5] to perform the simulations. The material parameters are summarized in Table 1. All these parameters were assumed to be temperature independent.

The thermal laser stimulation effect was dynamically modeled through ANSYS Parametric Design Language (APDL) implementations. The two limiting cases for which the laser beam is scanned perpendicular and parallel to the metal line are considered. The corresponding ANSYS modeled structures are shown in Figure 2.


The convection exchange between air and silicon dioxide was neglected (adiabatic boundary condition). All the other structure boundaries as well as the silicon substrate are kept at the reference temperature T0.


Only the case where the laser beam is scanned over the front-side of the IC is considered here. To model the laser beam description in ANSYS, we applied a different power density at each elementary element as a function of the laser beam center. In order to derive an expression for the laser beam intensity, we made the two common assumptions: i) the laser beam is unaffected by the silicon oxide top layer, and ii) the laser beam is represented by a Gaussian distribution.


Figure 2 ANSYS model of a 1μm large metal line for the case where the laser beam is scanned perpendicular to the line (above) and for the case where the laser beam is scanned parallel to the line(below).


Hence, the intensity (I) of the laser beam focused on the surface of the metal line can be described by the following equation:

Where R is the metal line reflectivity, P0 is the laser power, ω0 is the Gaussian beam radius and α is the absorption coefficient of the metal line. The function f(r(x,y),t) describes the laser beam displacement in the transversal or longitudinal cases, and r(x,y) the laser beam position.


Thermal Simulation Results


At the CNES, we implemented the thermal laser stimulation method on the Hamamatsu PHEMOS 1000 emission microscope. This system integrates a laser-scanning microscope that uses a continuous wave infrared laser with a wavelength of 1.3μm and a power of 100mW. The laser spot size is estimated to be approximately 1.3μm when focused. The fastest scanning speed of the laser was calculated to be


1.23m/s. This scanning speed correspond to a 1024x1024 frame scanning time of 2s when a 5x objective is used. Similarly, the slowest scanning speed was calculated to be 0.00768m/s for a scanning time of 16s when an objective of 100x is used.  The model for thermal laser stimulation is based on these equipment parameters.


Therefore, the thermal simulations were performed for a laser beam power of 100mW, a laser beam radius of 0.65μm and for an initial and reference temperature (T0) of 25oC. Both transversal and longitudinal cases were resolved for the fastest and


slowest scanning speed of the PHEMOS 1000 system.


The model first showed us that the metal line rapidly reaches equilibrium and that the permanent regime is obtained at the slowest scanning speed for both the transversal and longitudinal cases.

Self aligned contact wordline-bitline shorts in memory ICs – a  comparative study of a failure mode, its root causes, and simple, but highly effective analysis techniques.


Jeremy D. Russell, Yuh-Juan Chew, Hong-Ming Chen, Christian Giethmann and Zhi-Hong Zhuo


ProMOS Technologies Inc., No. 19 Li Hsin Road, Science-Based Industrial Park, Hsinchu,


TAIWAN, R.O.C.


A systematic approach based on top-down  polishing and voltage contrast is reported for  DRAM WL-BL leakage analysis.  It has the  advantage of very low NDF and obtains high  quality data for process root cause analysis.  It is  compared and contrasted with alternative  techniques, and future developments are discussed.


As geometries shrink towards 0.1 um, a common  feature used to maintain smallest chip size is the  self-aligned (bitline) contact, especially in the  various DRAM and SRAM technologies.  However, shorts and leakage paths between  wordline (WL) and the bitline contact (BL) (Figure  1) are due to defects which are becoming ever  smaller, providing even greater challenges for the  failure analysis which is necessary for process, yield and reliability optimisation.


In this paper, experiences over several years with  this fail type, and the many different manifestations  that occur will be described, and the standardised  approach that has been developed for their analysis  in our laboratory will be discussed.


Figure 1 Cross-section showing the wordlines and  bitline.


Methodology


For this type of failure, a very standardised  approach to analysis has been developed in our  laboratory.  Fails are identified at final wafer test, when fail bitmaps are available, or for back end  and reliability (component) fails, they are  characterised using an engineering tester.  The  normal signature observed for this fail type in our  DRAM technology is a bitline fail within one  segment, due to the wordline voltage affecting  sensing at all addresses on that bitline, while at the  short cell, the read voltage is always the WL  voltage, and so it always fails zero, and is referred  to as the “flip-bit” because it reverses the fail 0/1  sequence along the bitline.  For more marginal fails  this may be the only fail bit.  In other cases the  adjacent WLs also have failing “attached bits”, or  sometimes a weak cross-fail.  In any case, a fail  address is available from the tester that is  descrambled to give a WL-BL address within an  individual segment and block that can then be  counted to for physical location on the chip by FIB  or SEM.


Figure 2  FIB image of bitline contacts after  grounding the wordlines.  The bright contact  confirms the fail address and the presence of   leakage between the BL contact and the WL.


We then proceed to the initial analysis phase for  identification of the short type.  The first stage of  preparation is the decapsulation of the component


BL


Wsix


WL  Poly


Figure 3 BL-WL short due to micromasking.  The  image series is shown at top of WSix, lower WSix,bottom of WSix and polysilicon level. Bottom is a  TEM image - note the overlapping images.


if applicable using standard techniques1 (hot, fuming nitric acid).  Then we delayer to a level at  which the bitlines are visible in the FIB (e.g. by  dilute HF to remove oxide and aluminium upper  layers), then remove the tungsten BL metal using  RCA, for example.  An alternative is to finger  polish.  The failing contact and the presence of a  short are then verified by passive voltage contrast  in the FIB by grounding the WLs2. The failing BL  contact is then brighter than the other BL contacts  under the ion beam since there is a leakage path  between the WL and BL due to the short.  The  shorted BL contact is hence also grounded and  shows up bright under suitable FIB beam  conditions.  Standardised FIB marks are then made  at the fail address (or preferably several on one  chip if possible).


We then proceed to top-down polishing using a  combination of diamond then alumina slurries  depending on the required removal rates at the  different stages of the process, but normally  finishing with 0.05um Alumina / water slurry. The  sample is thoroughly rinsed in a glass beaker of  deionized water and, if necessary in isopropanol (or  acetone) and then quickly blow-dried with nitrogen  to get the sample surface scrupulously clean.  Then, inspections are made in the FE-SEM in order to  obtain a high resolution image, before repeating the  whole polishing step little by little to build up a  top-down slice stack, giving an overview of the  location of the fail and its process side-effects.


Although this technique may appear low-tech and  slow, in practice our experience shows it gives  consistent and clear results and answers to what  type of short is involved.  For a trained technician it  also has a very low NDF rate, and is fast, especially  if more than one fail addresses can be analysed in  parallel on one chip, giving good statistics.


The next step in the analysis flow is to collect any  further information that is required by the  integration and process engineers for root cause  identification, which varies depending on the fail  type, as will be discussed in the case studies which  follow.  Typically, this will involve FIB or polished  cross-sections, TEM, and enhanced decoration of  the polished sections to gain enhanced information  about the integrity of various layers and spacers, etc.


1. Micro masking


This is the simplest type of fail, and is clearly  identified by the fact that the defect in the wordline  stack is over the entire depth, and is seen all the  way to the wordline polysilicon level (Figure 3).  The actual short usually occurs from WSix to


Figure 4 SEM micrographs at top of WSix,polysilicon top, polysilicon middle and polysilicon  bottom showing short.  Voltage contrast can be  observed in the bottom image.


Figure 5 micrograph of shorts due to cap nitride  cracking by top-down polish (top), dry and wet  etching (bottom).


polysilicon, but may  be  open  at the bottom where  the self-aligned contact-etch exposes the sidewall  to a lesser extent.  The spacer then distorts the  bottom of the contact.  The process cause is some  form of defectivity, which can occur at several  process steps, including photolithography, mask  open of WL main etch and various cleaning stages.


2. Polysilicon under etch


This type of fail is similar to the first one, but in  this case, the shorts were extremely thin, and  almost impossible to capture clearly on a FIB  cross-section.  They are also often positioned near  to the centre of the active area and are only present  at the base of the polysilicon.  In the images, there  is clearly no short or problem in WSix or at the top  of the polysilicon, but in the third image, the buried  short material can just be discerned.  Further  polishing until the WL polysilicon is isolated in the  AA recess below the isolation oxide shows the  defect, but also, we observe voltage contrast, especially at the edges of the active area, where the  polysilicon has a brighter edge than the passing  cell.  This voltage contrast is extremely useful to  confirm that there is a buried electrical short during  the polishing process, as the shorts can sometimes  be extremely thin.  In this case, the process root


Figure 6  Micrographs of defect due to a process  marginality.  The last two pictures from good and  bad areas show there is a difference in  constructional measurements that explain the  failure.


cause was related to insufficient WL overetching, leaving residues of unetched material.    After a few  fail sites have been polished, for this type, it is  clearly distinguished from type 1.


3. Nitride cracking


One type of short in earlier technologies was due to  a CMP issue causing cracks in the cap nitride.  These were then filled with contact polysilicon.  This is one example where deprocessing gives  good results, with dry and wet etches selective to  polysilicon, as seen in Figure 5.


4. Process marginalities


This type of short is very marginal, and only occurs  at the corner of the WSix.  It is characterised in the  top-down images by the fact that many contacts  could be marginal in the vicinity of the fail address.  From the first image, it is clear that there is a  significant recess of the AA, as the WL near the BL  contact is still covered by nitride, but the rest of the  WSix is exposed.  This leaves a critical point of  closest contact at the edge of the AA, and in fact, it  is seen that there is a short there.  The short is filled  by the bitline contact polysilicon fill because the  WL spacer is broken through by the bitline contact  etch, then enhanced in size by the post-clean.  In  the rest of the images, it is clear that below the  short, there is no further damage to the structure.  Follow-up random cross-sections indicate that there  is a difference in step-height and cap nitride  thickness, which cause the etching sidewall  breakthrough (Figure 6).


5. Sidewall defects


This type of defect was the one that prompted our  systematic approach to WL-BL short analysis, as it  had the feature of showing very small shorts, which  leads to a high NDF rate with FIB cross-sections.  In fact it led to many cross-sections of the nitride  foot, but no sign of the much smaller short.  Only

Figure 7  Micrographs of short due to sidewall  defect, seen clearly in the second picture and  explanation of process mechanism.


Figure 8  Micrographs after top-down polishing to  the isolated WL, showing voltage contrast  confirmation of the electrical short (top) and a  polysilicon stringer of a few nm at the edge of the  active area.  Once the location is known, it is quite  easy to locate the short in a cross-section, as shown  in the third picture.


Figure 9 Micrograph of shorts due to shallow  isolation trench voids.


Figure 10 Image taken using Dual-beam system  slice and view function.


after top-down polishing is the complete process  root cause clear for this failure type - it is due to  abnormal oxygen content in the WSix anneal step, causing sidewall needle growths, as explained in  the lower part of Figure 7.  Followup analysis by  TEM is normally very effective, because the WSix extensions are sufficiently dense to appear clearly  in TEM


6. Polysilicon stringers


This fail type illustrates the value of voltage  contrast to confirm the electrical short after topdown polishing to the isolated WL.  It is clear in  the upper image (Figure 8) that there is a short  below that point, and on continuing the polish, a  stringer of a few nm at the edge of the active area  was observed.  Once the location is known, it is  also quite easy to locate the short in a cross-section, as shown in the third picture.  The process problem  was related to insufficient WL overetch time.


7. Isolation trench voids


A rare, different route for a short is via a void in the  isolation oxide (e.g. from CMP) near to the active  area, which can become filled by a combination of  the WL polysilicon and contact fill polysilicon.  These would not be seen on a normal cross-section, but in the top-down series’ (Figure 9) second  image, we see there is voltage contrast at the fail  cell when the WL polysilicon is isolated, indicating  a buried electrical short.  This is confirmed later by  further polishing which reveals the WL polysilicon  fills a void which was nucleated by a notch in the  active area, enhanced by a poor oxide fill process.


Discussion


Several different cases have been presented of the  different ways in which a specific circuit structure  can manifest the same electrical failure.  The  physical evidence can be quite different in each  case.  However, we have shown that a systematic  analysis approach can give excellent and highly  reliable results.  It also has the advantage over  other techniques, in that there is a very low NDF  rate, and also excellent information for  determination of the process root cause is obtained.   The alternative, of FIB cross-sectioning is also  employed in our lab, but the experience is that only  a relatively small number of cuts are possible on  one active area, so the chances of missing the short  are too great.  If the short is visible, often only  partial information results, so determination of root  cause is difficult.  Also, FIB cross-sections usually  offer quite poor contrast for subsequent SEM  observation, so in-situ decoration is often used, with the trade-off that it limits the precision with  which the defect may be located.  This further  reduces the number of subsequent FIB cuts that can  be made at the same fail address.


TEM cross-sections are increasingly employed for  failure analysis on our DRAM products because of  its ultimate resolving power, which reaches down  to atomic dimensions under ideal circumstances.


However, in normal FA cases we are approaching  the point where the thickness of the TEM foil is  greater than the width of our active areas.  If we  then have a defect that is a small fraction of the foil  thickness, then overlapping images of very similar  materials, such as silicon, oxide and nitride can  often cause problems for interpretation, or limit the  information which can be seen.  For example, it is  very easy for the TEM micrograph in Figure 3 to  be mistaken for a sidewall defect by internal  customers if careful explanations are not always  given.


An exciting new FA tool is the dual beam FIB, which has been available for a number of years, but  now is available with good SEM resolution3 with  the possibility to make simultaneous slicing and  viewing.  An example taken from a current  machine on a similar circuit can be seen in Figure  10, which shows a cross-section taken during realtime slice and view.  This tool has interesting  possibilities where very fast slices can be taken, and even assembled into animated sequences, which produce very convincing failure evidence.  However, the main limitation of the technique is  that even though SEM resolution is much improved  recently, it is still compromised to some extent, with current systems offering resolution around  3nm.


So, it is our belief that such simple techniques as  polishing still have a place in the FA lab even for  very small geometry devices.  It will also still be  extremely desirable to push SEM resolution as far  as possible, and high performance small sample  SEMs offering resolution down to 0.5nm are now  available4.  Such tools will be increasingly  employed for the clarity of interpretation that is  possible when device sizes are much smaller than  the sampling thickness used by TEM.  In fact it is  expected that different imaging tools such as AFM  will also become increasingly important, and will have to be incorporated into standard FA  techniques.


This systematic, low NDF analysis of SAC shorts  gives unambiguous results, and is compared to  complementary techniques using case studies from  several years of analysis.

A Spatial Filtering Localisation Tool for Failure Analysis of Periodic  Circuits.


Jeremy D. Russell∗


ProMOS Technologies Inc., No. 19 Li Hsin Road, Science-Based Industrial Park, Hsinchu, TAIWAN, R.O.C.


∗ Delegated from Infineon Technologies Inc.


A technique for localisation of defects in periodic  integrated circuit microscope images is described.  It uses a fully automatic and fine tunable algorithm  based on Fourier transform spatial filtering with a  specially developed algorithm.  Applicable to any  digital failure analysis-imaging tool, it produces  fast, automatic results to speed up the FA  localisation function.


For physical failure analysis of regular integrated  circuit structures such as DRAMs, it is often  necessary to see small differences from the periodic  array structure.  This can be difficult or time  consuming by manual visual inspection in many  cases.  One technique which can help to alleviate  this problem is spatial filtering.


The validity of this approach was demonstrated  early on by Luther et al1, using slides of SEM  micrographs and a laser optical bench.  By  manually constructing a mask for the diffraction  peaks formed in the optical path, they demonstrated  that the reconstructed image contains mainly  defects. The possibility of doing this using Fourier  transforms with a dedicated image processing  computer system with specialised software and  hardware was demonstrated later2,3.  However, at  that time, even with dedicated hardware array  processors, Fourier transforms of images with  reasonable size (at least 512X512 pixels is regarded  as a minimum) took more than 30s, making the  application impractical for the FA lab.  However, since then, many efforts have been made to develop  inline inspection tools.  Such systems typically  involve a light-microscopical system, using a  reference image for comparison along with  Fourier-like transforms in an automated sequence  to detect defects before measuring and classifying  them4.  However, although such inline tools exist  which can do this type of inspection, they mostly  involve dedicated hardware and are based on lightoptical systems (apart from some very recent ebeam based systems).  For reasons of flexibility  and ultimate resolution, these are therefore not well  suited to use in the deep sub-micron failure  analysis laboratory.


In fact, as far as the author is aware, there is no  fully automatic software tool available for spatial  filtering in the failure analysis laboratory.  The only  solution that is available at this time consists of  general image processing suites of software, which  can, in principle, be used to perform spatial  filtering.  However, such software systems do not  offer an optimised solution for images of integrated  circuits and usually involve significant resource  overhead.  This overhead is unacceptable for an  analytical tool that is used in a wafer fabrication  facility support lab.  It also requires too high a level  of image processing expertise to be usable by  normal operators; is often interactive in nature and  is hence mainly confined to the domain of research. In this paper a Fourier transform spatial filtering  solution will be described.  Normally, in this  technique the implementation details are dependent  on the specific application; the character of the  images, and what needs to be achieved (noise  reduction, structure averaging, etc).  At the current  time, most microscope-type tools used in failure  analysis laboratories, such as SEMs optical  microscopes, FIB, etc have a control computer, which is a standard PC.  In this work the  application of a fully automatic algorithm that can  be implemented on such PCs is described, enabling  quick, convenient processing of the digital images  that they acquire, leading to faster localisation of  defects with a higher success rate.


The tool consists of a Visual C++ program hosted  by the PC of any digital imaging tool, such as an  SEM or FIB.  Digital images are acquired as  normal, and after saving to the local hard drive, are  then loaded into the processing program either  manually or automatically.  The program then uses  this digital image data in order to perform spatial  filtering, and removes periodic circuit structures,


hence making the defects more visible.  In order to  cut out the spatial frequencies due to the regular  circuit and also imaging artefacts, a patented


algorithm is deployed5.


Figure 1.  Digital image of DRAM circuit after  delayering to Si level.  The box indicates one  regular repeat pattern.


Spatial Filtering


To perform the spatial filtering, the following  process was implemented:


1. Acquire a digital image, leading to a 2-dimensional array of pixel data, f(x,y), where x  and y are integers representing samples from  the real image space at distance intervals of Δx and Δy (Figure 1).


2. Calculate the complex, 2-dimensional, discrete  Fourier transform of the image pixel data,


where N and M are the number of samples in  the x and y directions, and u and v are integers  representing the spatial frequencies, for which  the actual frequency values are derived from  the sampling intervals:


uΔ=Δ 1


vΔ=Δ 1


Layout verification of the die showed that the supply and the ground connections to the subVt circuits were left floating. The subVt circuits were left over from the earlier version of the design. Due to circuit sensitivities to mismatch and sub threshold currents the subVt circuits were performing poorly. These were intentionally left floating since capacitor leakages improved for the new technology and it was hoped that they could be removed in future revisions. These subVt circuits were to account for leakage off a sampling capacitor by restoring the capacitor voltage to its original value. To disable the circuit its supply rails (Vcc and Ground) were floated and its outputs disconnected. During battery save, the inputs pbias and pdvbe as shown in layout Figure 4, are driven to Vcc. Since the Vcc supply rail was left floating it is driven to a Vbe below Vcc through the pbias and pdvbe antenna diodes. Similarly during battery save,ndvbe is driven to ground potential. Since the ground supply rail was left floating, it becomes clamped to a Vbe above ground through the ndvbe antenna diode.


The bsb antenna diodes were probably not a problem since in battery save bsb is driven to ground which turns off its antenna diode. In the battery save state,the subVt is powered up through the antenna diodes such that the Vcc rail is at a voltage of Vcc-Vbe and the ground rail is at ground + Vbe. The magnitude of the battery save current observed is not really accounted for by the power normally dissipated by


this circuit (especially considering the voltage across the circuit is 2Vbe's less than normal). The additional current is most likely due to latchup as a consequence


of diode currents flowing through the n-well and substrate.


• Diffraction-limited performance at the highest  practical numerical aperture


The requirement of only a single laser wavelength (λ= 1064 nm) simplified the optical design, as no  achromatic lenses were needed.  The individual  lenses also had anti-reflection coatings optimized for  this wavelength, contributing to the high overall  transmission.  The objective design contained seven  singlet lenses, which made for straightforward  fabrication and assembly.  Because the silicon


Fig. 1.  Photograph of the immersion objective.  Tapered final lens is indicated.


substrate is an integral part of the optical path, its  thickness was accounted in the design.  The substrate  is manually thinned and polished to a nominal  thickness of 100 μm.  For reasons such as assemblyinduced package warping and variations in the  sample preparation, it is difficult to attain a constant  substrate thickness uniformly across the chip.  Thus  the objective has a correction for spherical  aberrations induced by using a range of substrate  thickness.  The immersion objective was also  designed for use with the diamon window used in  the transparent heat spreader assembly [5].


The immersion fluid itself has a critical role  in the optical path.  It was chosen for the highest


known refractive index, subject to the constraints of  non-toxicity, low vapor pressure, temperature  stability, and reasonable viscosity.  We used optical  coupling fluid OCF-463 from Nye Optical Products, with a refractive index of 1.6 at the target  wavelength [6].  It is the refractive index of the  liquid that effectively limits the ultimate numerical  aperture of the immersion objective.  A numerical  aperture of 1.3 was targeted in the simulations of the  lens designer.  The final thickness of immersion  liquid was chosen to be 100 microns and is the  effective lens-to-sample working distance.


Fig. 2.  Cross-section of the immersion  objective contacting the chip.  The final lens is  machined to a tapered tip to avoid mechanical  interference with the thermal hardware.  (note:  in actual use the lens faces upward)


Thermal-Mechanical   Design Requirements


Several mechanical features were required:


• Internal protection in case the lens is  inadvertently moved into the sample


• Internal lens movements to correct for the  spherical aberrations caused by variations in  silicon thickness across the chip.


• Compatibility with thermal plates used to  control the chip temperature; that is, allowance  for a wide area of visibility when used with the  transparent heat spreader


A photograph of the liquid immersion objective  is shown in Figure 1.  The objective is spring-loaded  to allow it to retract if pushed vertically into the  sample.  A toothed collar is available for moving the  position of three internal lenses for the spherical  aberration correction.  Both these features are  available with the present objective on the IDS2000.


The compatibility with thermal plates required a  major modification to the final lens in the objective.  In high-resolution microscopes, the final lens is


IC Chip


Thermal  Hardware


Final lens with  tapered tip


Final Lens


often a nearly hemispherical aplanatic element, similar to a half-dome [7].  In the case of this  objective, a simple hemisphere would create a large  surface area that would lead to a highly restricted  viewing area on the integrated circuit, as the final  hemispherical lens would mechanically interfere  with the thermal plate.  To avoid this, a taper is  ground into the final lens to give it a conic shape  with the flat near-contact area (Fig. 2).  This allows  much better visibility around the chip.


The objective is mounted upward-looking  in the IDS-2000, and it is moved into the proper  focal position after applying immersion fluid to the  face of the front lens.  The oil is stable for over 24  hours at even elevated temperatures, and its  occasional refilling does not pose an undue  inconvenience to the user.


Fig. 3.  Device images of similar circuitry  acquired with (left) the present 0.85 NA  objective, and (right) the 1.3 NA liquid  immersion objective.  Transistor drains are  resolved much better with the immersion  objective.


Imaging results of the lens are shown in  Figure 3.  The liquid immersion objective displays a  spot size consistent with the design target numerical  aperture (0.5 μm FWHM).  Images of transistors on  a 0.13 μm technology are greatly improved with the  use of this objective, compared with the 0.85 NA  objective.  This allows smaller features, such as  single-leg transistors, to be localized.


Optical probing results are also excellent.  We have acquired excellent waveforms at target  power supply levels for the 0.13 μm process, boasting a signal/noise ratio of greater than 10.  Acquisition time was 2 minutes per waveform with a  tester loop period of 25 μs (Fig. 4).


We have developed the first liquid  immersion objective specifically targeted to through169


silicon laser probing in order to improve known  limitations in spatial resolution.  This objective  achieves a 0.5 μm spot size (FWHM), 33% better  than that achieved with the highest resolution lens  otherwise available, and it is fully compatible with  optical probing of high-power microprocessors.


Fig. 4.  Waveforms acquired from numerous pchannel transistors with an immersion objective  on a 0.13 μm device operating with a supply  voltage of 1.1 V.


Images of microprocessors on a 0.13 μm technology show resolution of individual transistor  drains.  Probing results are excellent, with reliable    5-1 signal-noise levels achieved at supply voltages  of  1.3 V and below.  Based on preliminary results, it  appears very likely that the immersion objective will  continue to enable good transistor probing results  even on the 0.09 μm process technology.

NEW SIGNAL DETECTION METHODS FOR THERMAL BEAM INDUCED  PHENOMENON


M Palaniappan1,4, JM Chin1,4, B Davis2, M Bruce2, J Wilcox2, CM Chua3, LS Koh3, HY Ng3, SH Tan3,JCH Phang3,4, G Gilfeather2


1 Advanced Micro Devices (Singapore) Pte Ltd, 512 Chai Chee Lane #03-06,


Bedok Industrial Estate, Singapore 469028,


Tel: (65) 796-9121, Fax: (65) 449-2360, E-mail: muthu.p@amd.com


2Advanced Micro Devices, 5204 E. Ben White Blvd, Austin, TX 78741, USA,


Tel: (512) 602-5272, Fax: (512) 602-5904


3SEMICAPS Pte Ltd, 55 Science Park Drive, The Faraday, Singapore 118235,


Tel: (65) 779-3735, Fax: (65) 779-3748


4Centre for Integrated Circuit Failure Analysis and Reliability, Faculty of Engineering, National University  of Singapore, 10 Kent Ridge Crescent, Singapore 119260,


Tel: (65) 874-2132, Fax: (65) 776-5031


Thermal beam induced techniques such as Thermally  Induced Voltage Alteration (TIVA), Seebeck Effect  Imaging (SEI) [1] and Optical Beam Induced  Resistance Change (OBIRCH) [2] have been used for  localization of reliability related faults in integrated  circuits over the last few years.  In this paper, we  describe several approaches to optimize the detection  of thermal beam induced phenomenon.  In the first  method, we have improved control of the laser  scanning system to define a specific dwell time at  each pixel.  Secondly, we utilized a voltage source in  series with an inductor to detect the induced voltage  changes as the laser is scanned across the device. Finally, we employed a pulsed laser and a lock-in  signal processing technique to increase the signal-tonoise ratio.


Fault-isolation techniques are critical to the  development and manufacture of large-scale  integrated circuits such as microprocessors.  Examples of such techniques include those  methodologies, which exploit thermal beam induced  phenomena such as TIVA, SEI [1,2] and IROBIRCH [3,4].  These techniques are often effective  in localizing electrical opens and shorts that can be  significant manufacturing concerns.


Thermally Induced Voltage Alteration (TIVA) and  Seebeck Effect Imaging (SEI), reported by Cole et al [1,2], utilize a scanning infrared laser to isolate a


defect within a die by thermally altering the defect’s  electrical characteristics.  The setup uses a constant  current source for biasing and detects the change in  the voltage demand due to the localized heating by  the laser.  This technique requires the use of time  constant delay to amplify the change in voltage for  effective detection.  However, integrated circuits  such as microprocessors require high current biasing  that is often beyond the specification limits of  commercially available current sources.  In addition, biasing in the constant current mode may not turn on  all the operation modes of certain mixed signal  integrated circuits.


Like the TIVA and SEI techniques, Infrared Laser  Optical Beam Induced Resistance Change (IROBIRCH), reported by Nikawa et al [3,4], map the  infrared laser position to changes in the device’s I-V  characteristics which result from localized laser  heating of a defect. In this case, a constant voltage  source is implemented for biasing and the resultant  change in current is monitored.  However, Cole et al


[1] have reported that this method is less sensitive  than TIVA implementation.  Moreover, detection of  current change is difficult to implement in general.


In this paper, the authors report on the development  of new signal detection methods that have  successfully overcome the difficulties reported in  these techniques.  Specifically, hardware  improvements were incorporated into the system, which allowed more precise control over the incident  laser flux.  Furthermore, we developed a new  detection circuit to sense minor voltage fluctuations  by using a voltage source in series with an inductor.


System Configuration


A block diagram of the major system components is  shown in Figure 1.  The scanning optical microscope (SOM) used for this work is the SEMICAPS SOM  1005, an integrated multi-laser NIR scanning optical  microscope system that was designed for OBIC, TIVA [1], SEI [2], OBIRCH [3,4], SCOBIC [5], LIVA [6], and other related techniques.


Figure 1  System block diagram.


For this work, only the 1340 nm Nd:YVO4 laser was  used.  At this wavelength, localized heating through  the silicon backside is possible.  The system is  equipped with a laser attenuator and an optical  chopper for pulsing the laser beam up to a frequency  of 4 kHz.  The laser scanning microscope uses a Ge  diode photo-detector to produce the reflected images.  Typical image raster scan rates of 1.6 to 12 seconds  per frame are used for a 512 by 512 pixel image  resolution.  A motorized turret with 5 objectives and  an accurate tri-axis motorized stage are used to for  focusing and accurate positioning of the device under  test (DUT).


This system is designed for high resolution and  maximum power delivery to ensure maximum range  and sensitivity for the detection of the laser induced  phenomena.   Through use of a 500 mW laser source  and the 100X 0.85NA NIR objective, more than 30  mW of laser power is available at the DUT with a  spot size of less than 1 μm (based on the full width  half maximum (FWHM) criterion).


For TIVA experiments, a Keithley 2420 source  measurement unit provides the bias for the DUT in  the constant current mode and an Ithaco 1201 voltage  amplifier with variable time delay operating in the  ac-coupled mode detects the voltage variations.  For  OBIRCH, a Hewlett Packard E3614A voltage source  was used to provide the constant voltage source.  Various signal detection and processing modes can  be implemented with the system including the use of  a signal generator to pulse the laser beam together  with a lock-in amplifier for signal processing.  The


entire system is controlled through a fully integrated  software interface.


Experiments


Device Description and Sample Preparation


A test chip consisting of a NMOS test device with a  test diode tied to the gate was used for analysis. The  test chip was fabricated using a sub-micron process  technology and was mounted in a flip chip package  for the experiments.  The backside silicon substrate  of the test chip was polished to a thickness of 100 μm with a tolerance of +/- 5μm using mechanical  grinding and polishing techniques.  An anti-reflective  coating [7] was applied to maximize the transmitted  intensity of the laser light through the silicon  backside.


Dwell Time Control


In order to define a specific dwell time at each pixel, a point mode scan control was implemented with a  common signal generator to ensure synchronization  between the laser scanning and the signal detection  and processing systems.  The range of the pixel dwell  time was selectable from 75 μs to infinity, with the  minimum time being limited largely by the response  time of the piezoelectric scanners within the laser  scan module.


The next three figures visually illustrate the results of  implementing dwell time control where the reflected  backside, conventional TIVA and 1 ms dwell time  TIVA images are shown in figures 2 a, b and c, respectively.  A comparison of the latter two figures  highlights the improvement resulting from the  incorporation of dwell time control. Specifically, use  of conventional TIVA on the test structure resulted in  a distorted tail at the location of the diode junction  whereas the image having controlled dwell time at  each pixel was well defined due to synchronization of  the detected signal with respect to the laser scan.


Figure 2a  Reflected backside image of the test diode.


Figure 2b  Conventional TIVA image.


Figure 2c  TIVA image using a pixel dwell time of 1  ms.


Improving Bias & Detection: Voltage Source with  series Inductor


In order to overcome the limitations of the constant  current source, we have developed a new circuit  configuration for bias and detection using a voltage  source in series with an inductor (schematically  illustrated in Figure 3).  The resistance shown in the  circuit is the internal resistance of the supply which is  negligible.


Figure 3  Voltage source in series with inductor for  bias and detection.


When a scanning laser beam irradiates an integrated  circuit with a short circuit, the localized heating  changes the resistance of the short thereby changing  the IC power demand.  In this configuration, the  change in the power demand of the test diode due to  thermal excitation would result in a voltage change  across the transistor since the inductor would be  effective in restricting instantaneous changes in  current.   An analysis of the effect of this detection  circuit is theoretically described below.


The change in the power demand dP across the diode  shown in Figure 2 is provided in the following  equations

By incorporating a series inductor into the bias  scheme, variations in power demand are reflected  proportionally as changes in the voltage across the  test diode.  The overall effect is similar to use of a  constant current source as in the case of the  conventional TIVA methodology.


Figure 4 highlights the results of the new bias and  detection system.


Figure 4a shows the reflected backside image of the  test transistor and for a baseline comparison, Figure  4b shows the signal obtained utilizing a conventional  TIVA type detection system.  The remaining  photographs (Figure 4c – 4e) show results from the  new bias and detection system with 1.3 mH, 2.7 mH  and 5.4 mH series inductors, respectively.


Figure 4a  Reflected backside image of the test  transistor.


Figure 4b  Conventional TIVA image of the test  transistor.


Figure 4c  Voltage source in series with inductor  detection using 1.3 mH inductor.

detection using 5.4 mH inductor.


It is clear from figure 4 that the same type of contrast  as the conventional TIVA setup can be achieved with  this configuration.  It is also noted that as the  inductance value is increased, the closer this setup  approximates the constant current mode of the  conventional TIVA setup.  Furthermore, the images  from the new configuration are better defined with  features that were not observable from the traditional  TIVA setup.


Pulsed Laser and Lock-in Detection


The authors have also implemented a pulsed laser  and a lock-in signal processing system as proposed in


[2] and implemented in a previous technique [5].  A  lock-in amplifier is used for signal processing to  increase the sensitivity and signal to noise ratio of the  collected voltage transient.  The signal generator is  used to generate a reference frequency for the laser  pulsing and the lock-in amplifier.  The output of the  lock-in amplifier is fed into an image acquisition  system that also controls the X and Y position of the  laser beam.  Thermal beam induced images are then  constructed by making the image brightness level be  proportional to the output voltage of the signal  processing module.  Thus, the pixel intensity is  proportional to the integrated transient signal.


Figure 5 shows the results of pulsed laser beam and  lock-in amplifier detection technique.  As with the  previous example, the first two figures (Figure 5a, 5b) represent the reflected backside and conventional  TIVA image, respectively. The image in Figure 5c  illustrates the higher sensitivity achieved through use  of the new technique.  Note that the image has both a  higher signal to noise ratio and better contrast and


that more details are observed in the image due to the  selectivity of the lock-in amplifier.  Since the  constant current supply has limited bandwidth, the  laser beam was pulsed at lower frequency (3.5 KHz).  This was achieved by selecting a sufficiently high  pixel dwell time to accommodate the longer  processing time needed for signal processing by the  lock-in amplifier.


Figure 5a  Reflected backside image of the test


transistor.


Figure 5b  Conventional TIVA image.


Figure 5c  Thermal beam induced image using a


pulsed laser of 3.5 KHz and lock-in signal acquisition  technique.


We have described new approaches to improve the  signal amplification and detection of thermal induced  phenomenon.  The synchronized dwell time control  method eliminates the tail associated with the  asynchronized conventional TIVA detection method.  An inductor in series with the voltage source converts  the power change to a voltage change effect similar  to the use of a constant current source.  Finally, by  employing a pulsed laser and a lock-in detection  system, images with a higher signal to noise ratio and  increased sensitivity were achieved. Recommended  future development is to model and simulate the  series inductor with voltage source to see the change  in voltage across the DUT

CNC Milling and Polishing Techniques for Backside Sample  Preparation


Romain Desplats, Felix Beaudoin, Philippe Perdu


CNES-THALES Laboratory- 18 avenue Edouard BELIN - 31401 Toulouse Cedex 4 – France Email: Romain.Desplats@cnes.fr


An increasing number of analysis techniques requires  access to the backside silicon of a functional device.


For backside sample preparation of packaged devices, CNC milling tools can perform both package  opening and circuit preparation.  They offer good versatility in terms of type and size of packages –from ceramic to exotic plastic molding.  They are suited for precise silicon thinning as well as polishing.  Finally, the automation and software control of the process offer good reproducibility of chip opening and preparation.


For some applications, the silicon substrate needs to be thinned as closely as possible to the circuitry with a uniform thickness (less than 100 microns).  Bent silicon surfaces are challenging for backside sample preparation.  This is the case of C4 packages or large  plastic TSOP packages.  Conventional approaches would cut off the top of the bent surface.


From small flat surface to large bent silicon dies, we will detail our technique for thinning silicon to a uniform thickness with extreme precision.  Finally,we will characterize the final surface roughness which plays an important role in backside techniques.


The main challenge of failure analysis is locating the root cause of circuit failures.  This requires gaining access to the heart of the device.  In order to benefit from the panel of tools and techniques for this task,package opening and circuit preparation must be done without altering the functionality of the device.


Backside laser based techniques and Atomic Force Microscopy (AFM), which are the upcoming tools for defect localization and failure analysis, impose severe restrictions on backside preparation techniques


[1].  Not only do they require opening a wide variety of packages of different types and sizes but also


thinning and polishing of backside silicon with extreme precision.


In this paper we will address the practical issues of silicon thinning and polishing with the CNC milling and polishing tool.  At each stage, we will precisely measure the surface quality and curvature of the device.


After a review of CNC milling and polishing techniques, we will address the challenge of large silicon dies where the surface is not flat but “hill”shaped to see if it is possible to limit and recover this  bending.  Finally, we will present our developments for addressing this problem with a precise and reproducible approach.


3D Confocal Microscope for Backside  Surface Measurements


To characterize the silicon surface after silicon thinning or silicon polishing, we use a STIL 3D confocal microscope.  It is an optical measurement instrument that performs 3D imaging of surfaces.


One can perform optical surface reconstruction with any confocal microscope.  However, this particular kind of microscope is well-suited for topographic characterization.  Its principles are based on modified  confocal microscopy techniques.  A white light beam is initially dispersed in its constitutive wavelengths before striking the surface of the sample.  The light spectrum spreads across all focal planes associated with each wavelength.  A slit then collects the light whose peaks of intensity are located at different wavelengths.  A spectrometer finally translates these results into an altitude term, Z, of the studied surface.


This process, reproduced at any point of the sample surface, yields a three-dimensional image of the surface (see Figure 1).


Figure 1.  Principle of operation of the STIL confocal  microscope.


Contrary to the traditional confocal microscope, this technique makes it possible to study the height variations of very complex surfaces while being free from the corrections of the source-sample distance during a measurement.  We used a variety of objectives with different numerical apertures to characterize silicon surface bending and roughness,for example.


For the transversal case, the temperature profile was extracted at the instant when the peak temperature was reached in the metal line. It is plotted in Figure 3 as a function of the laser beam position (left side) and the elapsed time after heating (right side). The metal line is physically situated between 1μm and 2μm. For the fastest scanning speed, the peak temperature is shifted of 0.25μm from the line center. However, for the lowest scanning speed, the metal line has time to reach equilibrium and the peak temperature is obtained at the center of the line. We can also see from Figure 3 that the metal line is rapidly cooled down by the silicon substrate. For the fastest scanning speed, the cooling is delayed by approximately 2μm due to the slow cooling in the silicon oxide.

The metal line resistance was calculated using ANSYS for the transversal case at the slowest scanning speed. It was found that the resistance change, ΔR, could be calculated from the average temperature along the metal line at a given time(Tavg(t)) according to the following analytical model:

The excellent correspondence between this simple analytical model and the ANSYS calculation results can be explained by the small temperature variations in the metal line’s cross-section upon laser heating. It is sufficiently small to not perturb the electric field along the metal line.

The knowledge gained from the thermal laser stimulation model is manifold:


• Rapid thermal equilibrium and rapid heat dissipation are achieved upon and after laser irradiation


• The hottest temperature occurs at the laser spot center


• The thermal spreading is limited to 30μm


• Metal lines characterized by a small crosssection (S) will produce a higher resistance change upon laser irradiation (see equation 3)


All these conclusions demonstrate that the thermal laser stimulation method is able to precisely detect metal type short defects when a current flows within them. The difficulty remains now in detecting the weak variations in power consumption upon a thermally laser induced resistance change.


Thermal Laser Stimulation System


As mentioned previously, CNES acquired Hamamatsu’s PHEMOS 1000 system which incorporates an emission microscope and a NIR laser-scanning microscope (Figure 6). Our objective was to unite emission microscopy and thermal laser stimulation, two important backside failure analysis techniques, on the same tool. The challenge was to implement the thermal laser stimulation method on this system as well. Since the power of the infrared laser (λ=1.3μm) is only 100mW, we opted for the development of a low noise, high speed and high gain specific amplifier (Figure 7).


The Hamamatsu OBIRCH acquisition card and software was used to acquire and visualize the


thermal laser stimulation images of the power consumption variations. A superposition of these thermal laser stimulation images on the laserreflected images is done to obtain precise localization of the defect. The reflected images are taken with the infrared laser-scanning microscope. In addition to the Mitutoyo NIR 5x, 20x and 100x objectives, we acquired Hamamtasu’s 50x OBIRCH objective to obtain high-resolution images. The resolution can be furthermore increased by the use of the laser scanner zoom of 2x and 4x.


Figure 6 PHEMOS 1000 emission microscope from Hamamatsu.


Figure 7 Low noise, high speed and high gain specific amplifier.


Specific Amplifier


The general scheme of the amplifier is shown in Figure 8. It consists of three stages: i) input stage, ii)amplifying stage and iii) output stage. The ultra-low noise input stage was designed to pick up the week voltage or current variations caused by laser heating in order to adapt them to the amplification stage.


When the IC is biased with a constant voltage(OBIRCH), the current variations caused by laser heating are picked-up by the current to voltage converter. For the constant current IC biasing method(TIVA), a voltage follower picks up the voltage variations. Both the current to voltage converter and the voltage follower where designed not to affect the device under test.


After filtering the continuous voltage component, the voltage variations are then amplified with a variable gain amplifier stage. The amplifier gain is controlled either manually or by computer. Finally, the last stage of the amplifier is designed to adapt the voltage variations to the imaging system.

Figure 8 General scheme of the specific amplifier.


More specifically, the amplifier response time was designed to accommodate the fastest scanning speed of the Hamamatsu scanning laser microscope which is 1.23m/s. It corresponds to a 1024x1024 frame scanning time of 2s when a 5x objective is used. In order to detect a 1μm line under this scanning speed,we need a bandwidth of at least 1MHz. We therefore designed our amplifier to have a constant bandwidth two to three times higher than this minimal requirement.


The main characteristics of the specific amplifier are resumed below:


• Constant bandwidth from 40Hz to 3MHz


• Current to voltage converter gain of 2000x


• Voltage follower gain of 1x


• Variable gain from 100x to 2000x (manual or software controlled)


• Powered by batteries to avoid noise


• Enclosed in a metallic box to avoid electromagnetic noise


Experimental Setup


At the CNES we opted to implement both the OBIRCH and TIVA methods to perform thermal laser stimulation. From experience, the thermal laser stimulation results seem to be independent of the biasing method. However, from a system point of view, the noise level, thus the sensitivity, will depend on the biasing scheme. The constant current or constant voltage source noise, or instabilities,detected by the amplifier will be more or less important depending on the IC resistance. For current failed devices characterized by a high resistance, it is more appropriate to use a voltage source. For low resistance in-line test structures such as metallic lines and vias using a current source will reduce the noise associated with the biasing scheme. The current source response time should be rapid enough to provide a constant current.


When a constant current source is used, the amplifier is mounted in parallel to the IC as can be seen in Figure 9. In this case the output voltage of the amplifier (Vout) is given by:
