Thermal Laser Stimulation Case Studies


The success of the thermal laser stimulation implementation on the PHEMOS 1000 system will be demonstrated through three case studies. More specifically, these examples will underline the ability of the thermal laser stimulation method to precisely localize metallic shorts. In all these cases, traditional emission microscopy failed to directly localize the defects due to their non-emissive nature.


The first example illustrates the ability of this technique in locating current leakage type faults from the backside of failed BICMOS ICs. The failed devices presented at certain I/Os a leakage current several hundred micro-amps higher than the expected 10μA. Thermal laser stimulation was performed from the backside due to the presence of four interconnection layers above the suspected defective structure.


The failed devices were powered up using a filtered voltage source. A shunt resistance was placed in series with the I/O presenting a high leakage current.


The amplifier was then mounted in parallel with the shunt resistance. The voltage follower of the amplifier picked up the voltage variations at the shunt resistance.


Figure 11 Superimposed thermal laser stimulation image acquired with a 20x objective (above) and SEM image of the located tungsten micro-bridge(below).


From the thermal laser stimulation image of the voltage variation superimposed on the laser reflected image, the current leakage source could be precisely localized on one of the open drain PMOS structures(Figure 11). SEM analysis after delayering at the thermal laser stimulation location showed the


presence of tungsten micro-bridge between the drain and the source electrodes of this output PMOS transistor.


In the second case study, the thermal laser stimulation method was successful at localizing a short in a failed device characterized by a high current leakage (2mA). This failed device was biased under a 5V constant voltage. The current to voltage converter of the amplifier was placed in series with the failed IC as shown in Figure 10.


Upon thermal laser stimulation of the full device,only one sensitive point was detected. A thermal laser stimulation image superimposed on the laser reflected image done under a 200x magnification of this sensitive area is shown in Figure 12. After FIB crosssection at the thermal laser stimulation sensitive area,a metal-1 to metal-2 short was found as can be seen on the SEM image shown in Figure 12. This polymeric-metallic defect was created during the plasma etching process.


Figure 12 Superimposed thermal laser stimulation image acquired with a 50x objective and a laserscanner magnification of 4x (above) and SEM image of the thermal laser stimulation located polymericmetallic defect (below).


In the last case study we illustrate the sensitivity of the thermal laser stimulation method to localize highly resistive type shorts. The failed devices presented a 2mA leakage current under a 3V bias. In this case we powered up the devices with a low noise voltage source in order to minimize the noise. Again,the current to voltage converter of the amplifier was placed in series with the failed devices as shown previously in Figure 10.


The thermal laser stimulation image as well as its superposition on the laser reflected image are shown in Figure 13. Here we can clearly see the current path created by the defect. We can also see that the imaged voltage variations change sign in the middle of the current path (indicated by an arrow in Figure 13). We suspect this sign difference in the imaged voltage variations to be defect induced. FIB cross sectioning was performed at this location. The SEM image of the cross-section is shown in Figure 14. We can clearly see a metallic short between metal-2 and metal-3. This defect was induced by a bad etching procedure that resulted in a parasitic via.


Figure 13 Thermal laser stimulation image of the voltage variation acquired with a 20x objective(above) and superimposition on the laser reflected image (below).


Figure 14 SEM image of the thermal laser stimulation located metallic parasitic via.


Interpretation


The interpretation of the thermal laser stimulation signal is not always straight forward since the IC can affect the voltage or current variations. This was well illustrated in the last case study where the imaged voltage variations changed sign at the defect location.


On the left side of the current path shown in Figure 13, the laser induced imaged voltage variations are negative (black) whereas on the right side they are positive (white). According to our experimental setup, the imaged positive voltage variations correspond to an increase in resistance upon laser heating. Knowing that the current to voltage converter of our amplifier inverses the signal, the positive voltage variations can easily be understood since the temperature coefficient of resistance of aluminum is positive (see equation 6). However, the physical model of thermal laser stimulation fails to explain the observed negative voltage variations on the right side of Figure 13.


The answer lies in the IC’s response to thermal laser stimulation. In order to gain more insight into this IC effect we simulated with Spice, a resistive defect in a simple CMOS inverter. The modeled inverter is shown in Figure 15. The resistive defect, here represented by a resistance, was introduced in parallel to the PMOS transistor of the first CMOS stage.


Figure 15 CMOS inverter modeled in SPICE


The thermal laser stimulation of the resistive defect was simulated in SPICE by varying the resistance value. In this model we are mainly interested in the two current paths formed by the resistance R and the transistor Q.  We first plotted the ratio of the absolute current in the transistor Q over the absolute current in the resistance R as a function of the resistance R value. We can essentially identify three regions in Figure 16. In the first region (I) and in the third region (III) the current variation in the resistance R dominates. Whereas in the second region (II) the current variation is mostly dominated by the transistor Q. We conclude that the thermal laser stimulation signal is strongly influenced by the location and the value of the resistive type defect in the IC.


Figure 16 Relative contribution of the absolute current in the resistance R and the transistor Q of the CMOS inverter shown in Figure 15.


In order to understand the sign change in the last case study, we plotted the total current variations in the inverter as a function of the resistance change. As can be seen in Figure 17, the sign of the current variation strongly depends on the resistance value.


Furthermore, the physical model would predict a current decrease upon a resistance increase when a voltage bias is applied to the CMOS inverter. This is the case only for a small range of resistance value.


Figure 17 Total current variations in the CMOS inverter shown in Figure 15.


In the case study shown in Figure 13, the metal lines interconnected by the parasitic via are respectively connected to the gate of two different transistors.


These transistors being at different logic states, it is


easily conceivable in the view of the CMOS inverter model that the induced power consumption variations are different from one side to the other side of the defect.


The IC effect can also lead to a thermal laser stimulation signal increase at non-defective metallic structures.  This is illustrated in Figure 18. In this case only the consequence to the intentionally FIB made defect causing current leakage appears on the thermal laser stimulation image.


Figure 18 Superimposed thermal laser stimulation images of non-defective metal structure (above) and non-defective via (below).


Other physical effects such as the so-called Seebeck effect can furthermore complicate the understanding of the thermal laser stimulation images. Upon laser heating, a stack composed of two different metals will generate a Seebeck voltage that will translate into power consumption variations. Non-defective IC structures such as via can also show up in the thermal laser stimulation image. This is also illustrated in Figure 18 where we can see non-defective via. In conclusion, a good knowledge of the IC’s layout is sometimes needed in order to identify which one of the thermal laser stimulation signals are defect related.


We have described several considerations related to implementation of the thermal laser stimulation method (OBIRCH, TIVA) in a failure analysis laboratory. From the 3D finite element ANSYS model and the three case studies we have shown that the thermal laser stimulation method can rapidly localize current related defects such as metallic shorts with a very good precision. This is possible since there is no spatial and temporal shift between the “hot spot” and the laser spot center and since there is no significant spatial extension to the thermal laser stimulation effect. We have also shown that the interpretation of the thermal laser stimulation images is not always straightforward due to the IC response and to the influence of other effects such as the Seebeck effect.


Finally, we have shown that the thermal laser stimulation method can be implemented with emission microscopy on a dual system. Based on our experience, emission microscopy and thermal laser stimulation is a winning combination for fault localization.

Liquid Immersion Objective for High-Resolution   Optical Probing of Advanced Microprocessors


Travis Eiles


Intel Corporation, Santa Clara, California


Patrick Pardy


Intel Corporation, Hillsboro, Oregon


The paper details a critical innovation for scaling  optical probing to access the small feature sizes on  advanced silicon process technologies.   By using the  liquid immersion principle to increase the numerical  aperture of the microscope objective, a focused laser  spot size of 0.50 μm is achieved for the first time. The liquid immersion objective is the first known  application of the immersion principle to backside  probing. This system improvement allows optical  probing to be used in geometrically scaled processes  that would not be accessible without it, and thus will  extend the usefulness of laser probing for at least  one more generation.


Optical probing based on the Schlumberger IDS2000 has proved to be critical in the design debug  and analysis of Intel microprocessors since the 0.25 μm process technology. Demand for  microprocessors has pushed the rapid development  of the 0.13 μm process technology to the present [1], and the 0.09 μm process technology to the near  future [2].  Excellent optical probing results are now  obtained when probing transistor drains fabricated in   the 0.18 μm process generation [3].  In this process  technology, source/drain diffusions are  approximately 0.5 μm long (that is, the distance  between gate edges in a stacked transistor device).  The laser is focused onto these diffusions with a  microscope objective designed for a numerical  aperture of 0.85.  This objective yields a spot size of  approximately 0.75 μm (full-width at half-maximum [FWHM] of the intensity profile).  Though this spot  size is greater than the length of the diffusions, the  beam overlaps the drains sufficiently for good signal  recovery and minimal crosstalk. Imaging is also  reasonably good for localizing even nearly  minimum-sized transistors.


To extend the life of optical probing to  process nodes with smaller geometries without the  need for specially designed-in probe points, we  developed a liquid immersion microscope objective


with numerous customized characteristics.  It has  been used on the most advanced CMOS processes  available to date (0.13 μm process), and allows  excellent imaging of transistor features and  acquisition of high quality waveforms at low power  supply voltages.


Problem Statement


Spatial resolution in optical imaging systems is  limited by the Rayleigh criterion for the resolution  limit (FWHM):


Δx ~ λ/2(NA)


where  λ is the wavelength of the light and NA is the  numerical aperture.  The numerical aperture is equal  to nsinΘ, where Θ is the angle subtended by the  aperture of the incident light and n is the refractive  index of the medium in which the rays are incident  at angle Θ.  Improving spatial resolution in an  optical system is done by either reducing the  wavelength or by increasing the numerical aperture. Shrinking device dimensions in integrated  circuits are made possible by improvements in  optical lithography, which are driven in large part by  decreasing the wavelength of the exposing source.  However, optical probing relies on transmitting the  laser light through the silicon substrate.  Therefore  the wavelength cannot be less than about 1.0 μm due  to strong absorption at photon energies greater than  the Si bandgap.  For optical probing, improvement in  resolution can be made only by increasing the  numerical aperture.  Since sinΘ cannot exceed unity  and is already ~0.90 in the highest resolution  objectives available, there is not much to be gained  in increasing the marginal angle.  The greatest  benefit is gained by increasing the refractive index  of the medium through which the marginal rays  travel.  This has been done historically through the  use of liquid immersion objectives, and more  recently solid immersion lenses [4].  We have  chosen a liquid immersion objective over the solid  immersion variety due to its compatibility with  variable sample thickness and chip cooling  hardware, as well as its greater simplicity.


Optical Design Requirements


The objective was designed to meet several  requirements:


• Compatibility with the existing laser scanning  microscope (LSM)—that is; size, entrance  pupil, illumination, and infinity correction.


• High transmission


• High magnification (100x)


CNC Milling and Polishing


CNC milling and polishing of the samples was performed with the Chip Unzip tool from Hypervision.  It is a software driven CNC, (Computer  Numerical Control) three-axe milling tool (Figure 2)which can perform a variety of backside sample preparation processes on many types of IC packages.


Its versatility is steins from the use of 5cm long and thin carbide and diamond milling bits that allow material removal in a controlled area. The carbide bits have a diameter of 3.175mm whereas several diameters ranging from 0.794mm to 6.4mm are available for the diamond bits. The diameter is chosen according to the area that needs to be milled.


These bits allow processing of the center of plastic or  ceramic packages with surrounding vertical pins.


CNC milling tools make it possible to access the backside silicon while keeping the functionality of packaged devices.  This approach is necessary for techniques such as backside emission microscopy.


Other key points of CNC milling are control and reproducibility.  This requirement for backside techniques leaves a narrow area of maneuver for device milling and thinning.  For example, thinning silicon from 300 microns to less than 50 microns is difficult to achieve manually.  The process must be automated thus gaining in both control and reproducibility.  As we will demonstrate later in this paper, CNC control allowed us to develop a specific approach for bent silicon surfaces as is the case for large dies in plastic packages (Figure 3).


Programming the milling tool was accomplished using SmartCAM Production Milling from SDRC,Inc.


Figure 2.  Chip Unzip® CNC milling tool.  The


process can be monitored on an additional camera  screen.


Figure 3.  Detailed view of the milling process using  a custom ball shaped bit.  The spindled cooling water  is projected away by the rotating bit.


Decapsulation


Prior to silicon backside thinning, the devices first need to be decapsulated.  For package decapsulation,the Chip Unzip uses a high-speed air spindle with diamond bits (80 or 100 grit) to process ceramic packages and a low speed spindle with carbide bits to  process plastic packages (Figure 4). Packages are set into a metallic fixture using a low melting temperature wax. This metallic fixture is then rigidly fixed into the Chip Unzip tank. A coolant fluid stream that wets the area being milled continuously cools down the milling process.


Since the x-y movement of the bit has a good planarity, it is necessary for the packages to be parallel to this plane.  This is not always possible to


achieve for large packages or for packages mounted on a PCB.  The presence of a tilt relative to the x-y plane was resolved by the development of software macros at the CNES allowing variable z milling.


This improvement was described in an earlier publication [2].


Figure 4.  Chip Unzip spindle, high speed (left), low  speed (right)


Silicon Thinning


Die attach resin removal and silicon thinning are done with the high-speed spindle using diamond bits(400 grit).  For small dies, the main concern is the die  planarity.  If the die is tilted, we correct the angle with the variable Z milling software macro mentioned above.  The diamond bit follows the tilt and thins the die parallel to its surface. Figure 5 shows the different stages of package opening, lead frame opening and silicon thinning. We thinned down the silicon  to a residual thickness of 50 microns without affecting the device functionality.


Figure 5.  Backside silicon thinning.  The plastic package and lead frame opening are also visible.


Silicon Polishing


Final polishing is a key issue in backside failure analysis, especially for optically based techniques.


Without an optical-grade finish, the effectiveness of


techniques such as photon emission and TIVA is significantly reduced.  Polishing is challenging since it is necessary to polish away the micro-grooves in silicon generated by the milling bits during the silicon thinning process.


Figure 6.  CMP fine polishing of the surface.  The rotating bob bit is held by our added spring activated bit in order to optimize the pressure on the surface of the circuit.


The chemical mechanical polishing (CMP) process implemented on the milling machine significantly improves the optical quality surface.  This process uses the low-speed spindle, a cotton bob and a colloidal silica solution in NaOH The pH of the solution is set to 11.2. However we found that it could not completely remove the grooves created by the milling process.  In order to improve the surface quality we used a 10µm polycrystalline diamond paste prior to CMP polishing.  This additional step significantly improved the silicon surface uniformity as can be seen in Figure 6.  We measured an r.m.s.


roughnesses as low as 4.2 nm when polishing with the diamond paste preceding the CMP step.


Large-Area Silicon Thinning and  polishing Techniques


Silicon Die Bending Limitations


For larger dies in plastic packages, we encountered significant silicon bending.  This bending also appears before silicon thinning with parallel lapping tools on C4 plastic packages [3].  In order to thin silicon to uniform thickness, this bending must be accounted for in the milling and polishing process.


Our initial hypothesis was that the bending was caused by relaxation after decapsulation.  We thought  that the curvature of the die could be corrected if the package were to be maintained in a flat position.  To test this hypothesis, we glued a device in a Thin181


Small-Outline Package (TSOP) to a metallic plate prior to decapsulation.  The die size was 16 mm x 8 mm and had a thickness of approximately 300 microns.  Optical and topographic images of the sample after decapsulation are shown in Figure 7.  As  can be seen in the optical image, the TSOP package is fixed rigidly to the metallic plate.  We measured a height difference of more than 20 µm from the center  to the edge of the die.  The bending of the silicon die thus occurs during the molding process.


Figure 7.  Optical and 3D confocal image of a TSOP


package after decapsulation.  The grooves on the


silicon made during plastic milling are visible.


Silicon thinning of large bent silicon dies results in a non-uniform silicon thickness.  The silicon die showed in Figure 7 was thinned to a residual thickness of 80µm at the center. The silicon surface after thinning is shown in Figure 8. After silicon thinning the surface was flat and only the milling grooves created by the diamond bit could be observed.


We measured the silicon thickness with Hamamatsu’s PHEMOS 1000 laser confocal microscope.  We found a height difference of approximately 20µm between the silicon die center and the corners.  This result confirmed that silicon bending is not affected during thinning and that the die thickness becomes non-uniform.


Having a flat surface is often sufficient for backside techniques such as emission microscopy.  If the surface has some curvature, it is important to have a constant silicon thickness to insure the same optical path at any point of the surface.  The ideal, therefore,


is to compensate the bending in order to have both a flat surface and a uniform thickness.


Figure 8.  3D confocal microscope image of the


TSOP package silicon surface shown in Figure 7


after thinning.


Tentative Compensation for Die Bending Using  Thermal Annealing


Our second hypothesis involved thermal annealing.


We attempted to heat up the packages to 80oC for approximately one hour in order to relax the stress on  an 80µm thick silicon die.


We found that the bending was indeed modified by thermal stress. In certain cases a height difference of a few microns could be recovered on the die’s corner as can be seen in the height profiles shown in Figure


9. However, in other cases, the bending was accentuated after the thermal stress. In conclusion,our experience showed that thermal annealing did not  assure silicon-bending recovery.


Figure 9.  3D confocal microscope profile image


performed at the center of the silicon die before


(above) and after (below) thermal stress.


Therefore, for laser probing and several other analysis techniques such as radiation testing where a thin silicon of uniform thickness is mandatory, this bending must be taken into account.  At the CNES we have developed a new approach to thin the silicon  surface along its curvature in order to keep the silicon  thickness constant during milling.


Silicon polishing


Since the surface is flat after thinning, there is no specific issue for polishing large dies. A small area of  the surface after a 10 microns polishing step and a CMP polishing step is shown in Figure 10. The root mean square surface roughness was measured with the 3D confocal microscope to be 6.7 nm.  This very low surface roughness makes possible to appreciate that CNC polishing is indeed surprisingly good and that it responds to the need of backside analysis technique such as TIVA and OBIRCH.


Figure 10.  Silicon surface roughness after polishing  step with a 10µm polycrystalline diamond paste and  ChipUnzip CMP.  The RMS surface roughness was  measured to be 6.7 nm.


CNC Development for Bent Silicon  Surface Thinning


In an increasing number of applications, it is necessary to thin down the backside silicon of ICs to a constant thickness of less than 100 microns.  Such applications include backside laser probing and backside radiation studies.  For backside irradiation studies done at the CNES, we had to thin down the silicon substrate of large memory devices to less than  50µm!


The difficulty in thinning devices to a uniform thickness comes from curved surfaces.  This is especially true of large plastic packaged chips,including C4 [3]


A simple example of this is a heavily doped silicon IC.  If the silicon bending is 20 microns, it is difficult  to appropriately thin the silicon to less than 50 microns.  To achieve this on the full surface, the center of the device will have to be thinned to only 30  microns!


We responded to the challenge posed by our irradiation group by developing a specific milling approach which tracks the curvature along the thinning process.  The different stages in our approach are:


1. Measure points on the surface curvature


2. Model the 3D surface


3. Calculate the milling path


4. Execute 3D silicon thinning


5. Execute 3D silicon polishing


Measurement of the Silicon Surface Curvature


Figure 11.  Backside silicon surface curvature


measurement with our additional Z measurement tool  in Chip Unzip.


In order to thin silicon and maintain a constant thickness, it is necessary to account for the curvature of the surface in the milling process itself.  The first step requires measuring the relative surface heights at  various points in order to generate a model of the surface (see Figure 11).  Typically, the curvature the chip is smooth with a single maximum near the center of the die.  We have found that it is usually sufficient to measure 9 points: one at the maximum height and eight on the periphery of the die (see Figure 12).  For a more complex curvature, a higher number of points are necessary.  We can measure the points, either with the CNC milling tool or with the 3D confocal microscope.  The advantage of the latter is an extremely fine resolution in all directions.  The surface is therefore accurately modeled.


In our experience, the surface curvature is limited to a single hill shape where the summit height and position varies from one circuit to another.


Therefore, 9 points are sufficient to accurately model  the 3D curvature.


Figure 12.  Height measurement points for a die with  a single global height maximum


Surface Curvature 3D Model


With only 9 points, it may appear difficult to accurately model backside surface curvature.


However, SPLINE functions give an excellent fit to this type of bending.  Using only three points,SPLINE functions approximate the best curve using these three sets of coordinates (see Figure 13)


-15,000-10,000-5,000 0,000


-8,0


-6,0


-4,0


-2,0


0,0


2,0

6,0


8,0


Figure 13.  3 point SPLINE model of a curvature.


SPLINE curves give a satisfactory approximation of  the silicon curvature.


We implemented this approach in a mathematical software.  With Mathematica, the surface is modeled and a 3D matrix is calculated (see Figure 14).  This approach was validated by comparing the generated models with surface 3D measurements done on a STIL microscope.


Figure 14.  Silicon surface Curvature 3D Modeling done with Mathematica (height difference is 45 microns).


3D Griding Tool Correction


Once the surface has been modeled, it is important to  account for the shape and size of the milling bit.  If the rotation axis of the bit follows the surface model curvature, the edge of the bit chips out the real silicon  bending.


-0,11-0,09-0,07-0,05-0,03-0,01 0,01


Z


Surface Drill


Figure 15.  2D modeling for the bit path in order to  accurately track the circuit curvature.  Computing of  this new spline curve involves a series of calculations  which take the shape and size of the bit into account.


The path of the bit must be offset by the radius of the  bit to provide the appropriate milling path.  In a two dimensional representation, the new 3D path for a flat  bit will look like an even smoother surface (see Figure 15).  This smoothness is especially noticeable at the surface maximum


3D Milling Path Modelization


Chip Unzip uses SmartCam production milling software to create a model of the milling path.  It takes into account all the parameters such as bit dimensions, type and milling path.  For regular silicon thinning, milling is done in a plane (2D) and,at the end of each plane, the height, Z, is decreased(relative to the device active area) to machine another  layer of silicon.


Here we used the same approach, but this time the milling path is in 3D.  Using surface modeled in Mathematica®, we generate the milling path for one 3D “pass” and then we copy this “pass” at different lower heights Z in order to remove more silicon (see Figure 16).  In this first part, the bending was


modeled only along one direction (along the length of  the TSOP packages)


Figure 16.  3D milling path.  The modeled path takes  into account the curvature correction due to the bit  diameter and shape


3D Milling Results


To demonstrate the accuracy of our results, we modeled an exaggerated bending on an already bent device.  The original bending of the circuit was measured to be 20µm (height difference between the die center and the corners).  The bending we wished to achieve at the end was 100µm.


The results were extremely satisfactory.  The expected bending was obtained and the surface roughness after milling was acceptable.  We did not obtain a staircase shaped surface as one could expect(see Figure 17 and Figure 18).  Our CNC milling tool  tracked accurately the curvature and did not add indentation while milling in the Z direction.


Figure 17.  14µm by 4µm 3D silicon thinning with a 3,175mm diameter bit.  Measured bending after thinning : 100µm as modeled.


Figure 18.  3D confocal microcope image of the silicon surface after milling (100 µm bending is confirmed).


3D Milling Path Optimization


When we started addressing bent silicon surfaces, we found that the milling path and direction played a significant role.  For flat silicon surfaces, the bit mainly moves horizontally (x,y) and we can consider that the side of the bit (which removes the silicon)chips out a constant quantity at each depth of the thinning process.  For bent surfaces, the direction affects the process.  When the bit moves up along the  curvature, its side is solicited.  When going down, it is the bottom of the bit.  As a result, the silicon surface is stepped down.


To avoid this problem we modified the milling path in order to only move moves in an upward motion(Z) along the bending (X or Y).


A second optimization involves criss-crossing the surface in order to remove the grooves created by the side of the bit sideways along the milling path(Figure 19).


Figure 19.  Top view optical image of the bent surface after milling with a small cylindrical bit.


Tiny grooves are visible before the surface was crisscrossed.


Bit Size Selection


If one looks attentively at the above picture, the two bottom sides of the curvature are flat.  This is due to the cylindrical shape of the 3.175mm bit.  A larger diameter bit contributes to a larger contact area with the silicon, thus reducing the surface grooves created by the bit.  However, if the bit diameter is too large, it  also mills material outside the silicon die when the bit  is milling the die edge.  For TSOP packages, IC pins could be cut off in the process.


We next tried with a smaller diameter bit.  The main disadvantage is: more steps must made to cover the circuit (i.e.  more time).  Thus, a compromise has to be made between the size of bit and the excursion on  the periphery of the milling area.


Ball Shaped Bits For Bent Surfaces


In order to improve the surface that resulted from a smaller diameter bit, the use of a spherical bit was investigated.  A first thinning trial with the spherical bit showed unexpected results.  As can be seen in Figure 20, the ball shaped bit left a high number of grooves on the silicon surface along the milling direction despite a very narrow pitch between successive milling paths.  It appears that the bit was too perfectly round for a flat silicon surface.  To avoid this problem we flattened the bottom of the diamond spherical bit.


This solution gave the advantage of the rounded bit for smoother edges and flat bottom for better roughness.


Figure 20.  3D silicon thinning of a bent surface with  a ball shaped bit.


3D Bent Surface Thinning and  Polishing Results


To implement our developments on the CNC milling and polishing tool, we took the case of a complex 45 micron bending on a 14 x 4 mm2 backside memory die. Thinning was performed in two stages. First,rough thinning in three passes of 20 microns each followed by two fine passes 5 microns below. Rough  thinning was done along a serpentine path (see Figure 16) while the two fine passes consisted in two crisscrossed paths in each quarter of the circuit (see Figure 21). The bit only moves in an upward motion in order to have the best surface finish, as detailed in the milling path optimization paragraph.


Figure 21. The advanced fine thinning is done in  each of the 4 quarters of the die separately. Milling is


done in two criss-crossed paths starting at each


corner, going up only along the X and Y axes until it  reaches the top of the bent surface.


Milling is performed with a flattened, ball shaped bit along the appropriately calculated paths (Figure 22).


A 3D measurement of the complex resulting surface is represented in Figure 23. The 45 micron bending between the corner and the top of the bent surface accurately matches the expected surface. In addition,the slight 5 micron bending along each side of the circuit is also visible showing the precision of the approach.


After CMP polishing with our added rotating spring tool (cotton bob bit), the resulting roughness is 4.2 nm (Figure 24), which is slightly better than our results for a flat silicon surface.


Figure 22.  Results of 3D complex bending obtained  with a “flatten” ball shaped bit.


Figure 23.  3D confocal microscope image of the


silicon surface after milling along a complex 3D


surface with a with a “flatten” ball shaped bit.


Figure 24.  Silicon surface roughness after polishing.


The RMS surface roughness was measured to be


4.2 nm


CNC milling tools are adapted to silicon thinning and  polishing of packaged devices for backside application.  However, when thinning large dies, one must account for the curvature of the Silicon die.


Because it is necessary to have a constant silicon thickness for laser probing and for backside irradiation studies, we have developed a technique that accounts for the surface curvature.  Briefly, the technique involves the following stages:


• Characterizing the surface profile.


• Modeling the 3D surface using a set of quadratic polynomials.


• Creating the 3D milling path according to the milling bit diameter and shape.


The authors want to thank Benoit Bayol from Centech for his technical support on the Chip Unzip,Alexandre Douin at the CNES for his help on the modelization of the 3D milling path .and Tony Brown from Hypervision for providing the spherical milling bit.

Contactless Failure Analysis of Integrated Circuits Via Current Contrast Imaging with Magnetic Force Microscopy


W. Mertina, R. Webera, F. Seiferta, E. Kubaleka, G. Zimmermannb, C. Boitb


aGerhard-Mercator-University Duisburg, Department of Materials for Electrical Engineering D-47048 Duisburg, Bismarckstr. 81


bInfineon Technologies AG, Failure Analysis, D-81605 München, Balanstr. 73


One approach for the measurement of circuit internal currents is the measurement of the magnetic field caused by a current flow through a conducting line.


Due to its inherent superior spatial resolution in the topography modus magnetic field measurement techniques based on Scanning Force Microscopy seem to be a good basis for advanced measurement techniques for circuit internal failure analysis.


Magnetic field measurement can be done by Magnetic Force Microscopy (MFM) using commercial and reproducible magnetic probes. In this paper the state-of-the-art of current measurement via MFM and a real failure analysis of an actual IC where standard failure analysis techniques failed will be demonstrated.


For failure analysis of modern ICs it is often not sufficient or even impossible to make only circuit internal voltage measurements. It is often desired to perform circuit internal current measurements. For example:


• In the growing number of mixed signal devices or analog devices the voltage swing is in the order of mV and therefore, at the limit of the Electron Beam Test (EBT) technique [1, 2]. The corresponding current swing however is in the order of hundreds of µA which is a relatively large alteration.


• The knowledge of the power consumption of circuit internal blocks obtainable by measuring the local supply current of these blocks would often provide an important aid for detecting faulty blocks [3].


• Some failures show only anomalous current (e. g. in combination with IDDQ testing [3]).


• One major problem is the detection of leakage currents.


Therefore, circuit internal current measurements are of growing importance.


However, in contrast to the maturity of circuit internal voltage measurement techniques, like EBT


[1], Laser Voltage Probing [4], or the Scanning Probe Voltage Measurement Technique [5, 6]


contactless circuit internal current measurement techniques are at their beginning. Several methods for circuit internal measurement of currents have been developed during the last decade.


One possibility of making contact current measurements is the use of so-called picoprobes [7].


The conducting line of interest is opened by laser cutting or a focused ion beam technique. Then both ends of the line are contacted by the tips of the picoprobes in series with an ammeter. However, this procedure is very time consuming, destructive and offers insufficient spatial resolution.


A better method of current measurement employs the use of contactless methods. Current flow through a conducting line induces an increase in temperature around it. By measuring the temperature change one can derive the current flowing through the conducting line. Several techniques based on temperature measurements exist [8] like Liquid Crystal Thermography [9] or Scanning Thermal Microscopy[10, 11]. The problems with these techniques are either insufficient spatial resolution or insufficient temporal resolution.


Another technique for acquiring current measurements is to determine the voltage drop along an interconnection line, e. g. with the EBT, and dividing this voltage drop by the resistance of the interconnection line. Such experiments have been done [12], however, not circuit internal but circuit external. The problem in using this technique circuit internal is that the exact resistance of the interconnection line must be known. This problem can circumvented by using a separate resistor within the current path, which however, needs an extra IC area (design for testability).


A third and most promising method for measuring circuit internal currents contactless is to measure the magnetic field around the conducting line which is generated by current flow through the line. There are several interactions of probes with a magnetic field.


These are based  on the Lorentz force, the induction principle, and on the interaction with a magnetic material.


Magnetic Force Microscopy, which belongs to the Scanning Force Microscopy (SFM) [13] techniques,is based on the interaction between a magnetic material and a magnetic field. Due to it’s inherent superior spatial resolution in the topography mode,techniques based on SFM seem to be a good basis for advanced measurement techniques for circuit internal failure analysis of micro and nanoscale devices and circuits.


Based on SFM two principle techniques are available to measure this magnetic field. Either by measuring the magnetic field via Magnetic Force Microscopy(MFM) [14] where a topography sensor is also used as the magnetic field sensor [15] or by measuring the magnetic field via a bifunctional probe consisting of a separate magnetic field sensor (e. g. magneto resistive sensor, Hall sensor, or SQUID sensor) and a topography sensor [16], both implemented into a SFM. The latter method has the same problems which also occur at hybrid systems like e. g. reliability and reproducibility.


This paper will review the state-of-the-art of current measurement via MFM and will demonstrate a practical failure analysis of an IC.


One of the options available for probing deep submicron circuits is to limit probing to only DUT locations where no other interconnects are near the one being probed.  In many cases this is impractical or unachievable.  Alternatively, a focused ion beam (FIB)can be used to deposit an enlarged test pad above the desired test point, but at the expense of added load capacitance and processing time [3].  This option is also undesirable when working with silicon-on-insulator structures with low-k dielectrics due to damage from gallium implantation by the FIB [4].  Numerous solutions have been proposed to eliminate the limitations of optical imaging and wire or needle probing for sub-micron ICs.  One of the most successful has been the electron-beam probing technique [5].  This system combines imaging using a scanning electron microscope (SEM) with low-loading sampled electronbeam probing.  Such methods are very expensive,require complicated preparation for the DUT(measurements must be performed in a vacuum system), require repetitive waveform stimulation, and have other limitations when the IC feature size is less than 0.25 μm.  Another alternative is to use the FIB as


an imaging beam for positioning needle probes. This approach is expensive, requires the DUT to be placed inside the FIB, and therefore does not have the advantages of a standard probe station platform.


Until recently it was not believed that mechanical probing could extend much beyond 0.5 μm.  This is undesirable as users of probe stations often have considerable investment in customized add-on fixtures or instruments used to hold and stimulate samples, heat or cool the DUT, provide shielding for high-precision measurements, and provide special DUT environmental conditions.  In addition, traditional probe stations are well configured to stimulate devices and mechanical probes are still useful when microelectronic devices have dimensions large enough to be imaged by probe station microscope systems.  A solution to this problem has been found through the application of Atomic Force Microscopy (AFM) technology to electrical probing[6,7].  The use of AFM technology has resulted in a technique that has extended the usefulness of mechanical probing well below 0.5 μm.  In this paper we will be describing an AFM based submicron probing technique with simultaneous probing capability.


Atomic Force Microscopy


Atomic Force Microscopy is a class of scanning probe techniques that are powerful tools for visualizing


nanometer scale structures on surfaces [8,9].  Fig. 2 shows a schematic of a typical AFM, consisting of a micro-machined cantilever with a sharp tip at its end and a deflection sensor.  In one mode of operation, the probe tip is lowered onto the substrate surface, with a computer monitoring the vertical deflection of the cantilever.  Once contact between the probe tip and sample surface has been established, the probe tip is moved across the surface of the substrate in a raster pattern, while cantilever deflection is recorded.


Variations in substrate height (such as interconnects)cause the cantilever to deflect up or down.


Fig. 2: Atomic force microscope motion sensing using an optical-lever deflection measurement system.  The micro-machined Si probe is metalized for electrical contact measurements.


In the AFM deflection sensing system shown in Fig.


2 a laser impinges on the top of a reflective cantilever,the underside of which contains a micro-machined tip.


A topographic image of the surface beneath the tip is generated by associating the tip deflection with the tip location.  The very sensitive position sensing system means that cantilever displacements of less than 0.1 nm can be measured.  It also enables precise control of the AFM imaging contact forces.  Contact forces in the 1-50 nanonewton range can be achieved utilizing feedback control in the deflection sensor system.  This low contact force is several orders of magnitude less than the contact forces exerted by needle probes.


The extraordinary resolution of an AFM derives from its micro-machined tip.  The AFM probe tip is normally micro-machined as the integral part of a micro-fabricated silicon (Si) or silicon nitride (Si3N4)cantilever. Scanning electron microscope (SEM)pictures of two AFM tips are shown in Fig. 3.  The


Cantilever


Probe Tip Laser


Source


Position Sensitive Detector


single crystal fabrication process results in a tip whose final diameter can be less than 10 nm.  Nanometer scale tips are needed to obtain the resolution required for imaging and material characterization of deepsubmicron IC structures.


Fig. 3: Atomic force microscope micro-machined cantilever and various tip structures. Top is a standard commercially available Si tip that is metalized for electrical contact measurements. Bottom is a custom solid metal tip.


Figure 3 shows two AFM probe tips used for electrical circuit probing.  Standard commercially available Si probes can be metalized for electrical contact measurements.  For reliable and low ohmic contact electrical measurements solid metal probe tips have been developed as shown in the bottom photograph in Fig. 3.


The AFM technique has been used extensively in microelectronic device characterization and failure analysis. Despite its success in many device characterization and failure analysis applications, AFM technology has not been widely applied to probe station based testing.  This is because most AFM systems were designed to obtain resolutions well below 5 nm and consequently were constructed as specialized highlystable independent systems that are not compatible with probe stations.


Leading Edge Probes for Multi-Probe Measurements


In this paper, we describe AFM technology that has been designed and applied to the imaging and electrical probing of multiple close proximity points on packaged parts and wafers on traditional probe stations.  This enables users to image the surface of the DUT with better than 50 nm resolution and reliably obtain electrical contact with structures (interconnects and via plugs, for example) that are less than 0.25 μm.  In this


application, the AFM probe operates in a relatively high contact force mode and uses a conductive AFM tip to enable signals from the DUT to be captured in realtime.  Its surface topography imaging capability can relatively easily and quickly (under one minute) obtain images of deep sub-micron structures on an IC with sufficient resolution to easily identify features for electrical probing.


Electrical probing often requires the simultaneous measurement and stimulation of more than one node at a time.  Micro-machined probes that have been produced to date are not suitable for this task.  Fig. 4 shows two standard commercially available probe tips in a close proximity-probing situation and demonstrates how the large overhang of present micro-machined probes prohibits the probe tips from coming into very close proximity. The problem becomes even more challenging when more than two nodes need to be probed simultaneously.  This probe spacing would not allow the probing of an individual memory cell or even allow the simultaneous probing of the input and output of a state-of-the-art  logic gate.


Fig. 4: Diagram showing the closest approach of two standard micro-machined probe tips.  The closest approach between the two probe tips as shown above is approximately the length of the 10 micron scale marker.


To address the problem of very close proximity probing, we have developed a micro-machined probe in which the probe tip is the leading point on the probe as shown in Fig. 5.  With these leading edge probes, it is theoretically possible to bring the probe contact points to within a small fraction of a micron. These probes are machined out of silicon and have a metallic coating to provide a conductive path. Figure 6 shows two of these


Solid Metal Tip


probes being used on a probe station.  The probe tips are the most forward protruding point on the probes and allow the simultaneous probing of two close proximity circuit nodes.  The contact resistance of these leading edge probes has been measured to be less than 100 Ω.


Further, the geometry of these probes is such that up to four probes can be brought into close proximity. Figure 7 shows an optical micrograph of two leading edge probes in close proximity.  Besides having probe tips at the leading edge of the probe the probes also have tapered geometry that minimizes interference between adjacent cantilevers and allows for at least 4 probe tips to be used in close proximity.


Fig. 5: Diagram showing the closest approach of two custom micro-machined leading edge probe tips.


Theoretically the probe contact points could be less than 0.25 microns apart.  The scale marker on the right hand side is 2.5 microns long.


Fig. 6: Two leading edge AFM probes mounted on a typical probe station.  Note that the probe fits under the optics of a standard probe station and can be used in conjunction with standard needle probes.


With  four probes in close proximity  up to four point parametric testing using any desired combination of stimulus and sense AFM probes may be used.


Figure 8 shows a four-probe AFM system configured


on a standard probe station. Theses probes are a significant advance over present micro-machined probes which generally do not allow multiple close proximity circuit nodes to be probed.


Figure 7 Optical micrograph of two probes in close proximity on a sample surface. The probes have a tapered profile to minimize the problem of interference between probes.


Fig. 8: Four-point dc parametric probing within a submicron footprint using a four probe AFM system and the custom leading edge probe tips shown in Fig. 5.


Sub-Micron Probing


When an AFM probe is mounted on a probe station the effects of vibration are noticeable and the imaging capability will not be “metrology quality.”  However,even though image resolution is typically 50 nm, this is far better than necessary to discern two 0.18 μm


interconnect lines from each other to place the probe tip for electrical measurement.


An image of a 0.25 μm geometry serpentine test structure, taken using an optical microscope, is shown in Fig. 9.  It is clear that when using only optical


imaging, it is impossible to position mechanical probes.


An example of an AFM image of the same 0.25 μm serpentine test structure is shown in Fig. 10.  The 12μm x 12 μ m images show the higher resolution


provided by the AFM, and the ability to easily discern sub-0.1 μm surface details. The imaging capability of


the AFM far exceeds that obtainable from an optical microscope. An additional advantage is that the AFM surface image can also be used for probe positioning.


In Fig. 10 two probes have been used simultaneously to image the same region.  Once the images of the surface have been captured, they can then be used for positioning of the probes for contact electrical measurements.  To avoid collisions between probes the images are taken sequentially with only one probe in contact with the surface at a time.  Only when the final probe points have been identified for both probes do both probes contact the sample.


For accurate placement, several non-idealities of piezoelectric positioners have to be taken into account.


For example, piezoelectric positioners suffer from hysteresis, creep and limited dynamic response.  This is accomplished using a numerical model for the voltage versus position response of the piezoelectric and calculating applied voltages to yield the desired position.  This approach can yield an probe placement accuracy of better than 75 nm.  With typical probe station drift and vibration it is possible to place probes within 2 microns of each other.  Although under more ideal conditions it is possible to do significantly better.


Fig. 9: An optical microphotograph of a 0.25 μm serpentine test structure.  Only a small ripple in the edge gives any indication of interconnect features,making probe placement virtually impossible.


The lateral resolution of the AFM probe is determined by the radius of the probe tip, typically less than 50 nm and is adequate for imaging and probe placement on sub-0.18 μm devices.  The vertical


resolution of the probe imaging system is dependent on the cantilever deflection sensing mechanism and is thus much more sensitive.  This is very useful when probing extremely flat surface features, such as polished devices.  Fig. 11 shows images of a lower level metal vias fabricated in a silicon-on-insulator technology [4].


Fig. 10: The top figure is 12.3 um x 12.3 μm AFM image of the 0.25 μm serpentine test structure shown in Fig. 9. The bottom figure is a 12.8 um x 12.8 μm AFM image taken with a second independent probe.


The surface was unlayered in order to expose the vias connecting to cell transistors.  The topography difference between the dielectric and via surfaces is estimated to be 35-45 nm.  The probe could be placed


on individual vias and the contact-body diode characteristics measured.  This enabled verification of open circuit contacts during a Failure Analysis test.


This measurement could not have been achieved using standard wire probing and optical placement due to the small variation in surface topography.


Integrating lower resistance copper plated interconnects with low-k dielectric films in a siliconon-insulator technology offers faster circuit operation for higher performance microprocessors [10].  The electrical characterization of submicron copper interconnects with low-k dielectrics such as Dow Chemical’s SiLKTM poses challenges not present with SiO2 or with fluorinated silicon glass dielectric films


[11].  When attempting to perform contact electrical probing, the high porosity/low modulus characteristics of these low-k dielectric films require precise control of the probe tip contact forces.


Fig. 11: AFM probe images of vias of a silicon-oninsulator technology. The device has been unlayered in order to expose the vias connecting to the cell transistors (from [4]).


Conventional needle probe techniques, even when using FIB deposited probe contact pads, present concerns when used in conjunction with low-k dielectric films.  The intrinsic inability to control tip contact forces with conventional needle probe techniques results in damage to the sub-micron copper interconnects and deformation of the underlying low-k dielectric.  Even probes guided by external piezoelectric motorized movement can not prevent deformation of the copper interconnect or damage to the dielectric film.  Focused ion beam assisted probing


techniques applied to silicon-on-insulator designs incorporating copper interconnects and SiLKTM dielectric processing present other challenges for electrical measurements.  Apart from the issues of FIB beam charging and the floating body effect associated with SOI designs [4, 11], FIB tungsten deposition used to form probing pads can result in gallium implanted into the top surface of SiLKT M  low-k films, this producing a conductive layer.


The AFM probing technique described in this paper eliminates probe contact damage and avoids the problem of FIB beam implantation of gallium when applied to electrical probing of sub-micron copper interconnects with underlying low-k dielectric films.


Fig. 12 demonstrates AFM imaging of a dual damascene copper interconnect test structure.


Electrical probing using multiple AFM probes was performed in order to detect opens indicating a defect.


The test structure consists of different level copper lands that are linked together by copper sperentine interconnects and surrounded by a SiLKTM dielectric film.  Following the deprocessing of the low-k dielectric film to expose the top level of the copper via test structure, one probe of a two-probe system is placed on one link and another on an adjacent link to detect the source of any resistive or open circuit site(s).


During these measurements, the close spaced copper segments surrounded by the low modulus of SiLKTM were not damaged or smeared even after repeated AFM contact cycles [11].


Fig. 12: Copper interlevel via test structure in SiLKTM dielectric film .   Two probes were used to examine this structure for opens.


Probing for opens and shorts is a relatively low frequency measurement.  By using close proximity buffering electronics the usable frequency of this technique can be extended to at least 3 GHz.


A new contact probing system designed for surface visualization and multiple node close proximity probing of sub-micron integrated circuits was described.  This analytical-probing tool, based on atomic force microscopy technology, extends the capability of traditional probe stations to deep sub-micron integrated circuit technologies. Higher magnification probe images of interconnect structures indicate better than 50 nm image resolution.  The vertical force of the probe during imaging and contact for electrical measurements could be maintained to less than 1 micronewton so that the probe does not damage the circuit.  A new type of probe was introduced where the leading point on the probe is the probe tip.  In combination with a tapered cantilever this allows up to 4 probes to be placed in close proximity on a sample surface.  With this advance most of the electrical measurements that are now possible with mechanical probes will be possible with sub-micron integrated circuits.


The authors thank Terry Kane at the IBM Advanced Semiconductor Technology Center for providing the low-k dielectric/SOI images and measurement results.

Terence Kane, Patrick McGinnis, and Brett Engel


IBM Advanced Semiconductor Technology Center Hopewell Junction, New York


As logic technologies ramp to 0.13μm and beyond,integrating lower resistance dual damascene copper plated BEOL interconnects with low k (k = 2.65)processing with Dow Chemical’s SiLKTM dielectric and with silicon-on- insulator (SOI) technology offers even higher performance (> 1GHz) RISC microprocessor operation [1,2]. Such low k dielectric films dramatically reduce the line-to-line capacitance over conventional silicon dioxide dielectric films as well as FSG (fluorinated silicon glass) dielectric films.


Electrical characterization of submicron copper interconnects with SiLKTM offer challenges not present with SiO2 or with FSG dielectric films.


Similarly, deprocessing such low-k dielectric films require approaches very different than for silicon dioxide dielectric films or even FSG films.


Conventional tungsten contact probe techniques and even FIB deposited pads for submicron probing present concerns as applied to low-k dielectric films.


Alternative techniques in electrical probing employing atomic force microscopy (AFM) surface imaging may provide a solution. This technique combines the capability to precisely position multiple probe tips within a small footprint and to perform electrical measurements on features as small as 0.10μm. The low total input capacitance loading with this technique is applicable to AC waveform characterization for higher speed RISC microprocessor designs.


Integrating lower resistance dual damascene copper BEOL interconnects with low k dielectric films and silicon-on- insulator technology permits even faster performance circuit operation.


A significant reduction in total interconnect line capacitance can be realized by combining SiLKTM organic dielectric with copper interconnections yielding an effective k of 3.0 [1, 2].


Electrical characterization of submicron copper interconnects embedded in low k dielectric films presents challenges in planar deprocessing of the low k films to expose the copper interconnects.


Manufacturing decisions governing the integration of low-k dielectric films using CMP processing dictate whether polishing endstop materials for copper dual damascene interconnects are used and whether hardmask materials on top of the porous low-k dielectrics are implemented [8, 9,10]. Based on these decisions, planar deprocessing of low-k dielectric films to expose the copper interconnect can vary and may involve multiple, different processing steps,including RIE (reactive ion etching), wet chemistry removal, and mechanical polishing, among others.


Another hurdle to electrical characterization of these emerging technologies that integrate low k dielectric films with copper interconnects is the limitation of conventional probe stations to optically image the typical feature sizes (0.18µm and smaller) employed.


In addition, the high porosity/low modulus characteristics of these low k dielectric films require precise control of the probe tip contact forces. The intrinsic inability to control tip contact forces with conventional tungsten tip probing techniques results in damage to the copper interconnects and deformation to the underlying low k dielectric film.


Without a precision sensitive detector to precisely control the tip contact forces, even probes guided by external piezo-electric motorized movement cannot insure against deformation of the copper interconnect or damage to the low modulus dielectric film.


Focused ion beam (FIB) assisted probing techniques on copper interconnects with silicon-on-insulator designs incorporating SiLKTM dielectric processing present other challenges for electrical measurements.


Apart from the issues of FIB beam charging and the“floating body” effect associated with partially depleted SOI designs [11,12,13,14,15], FIB CVD tungsten deposition to form probing pads can result in implantation of gallium into the top surface of


SiLKTM low k films [17]. This produces a conductive layer inimical to discrete submicron device operation and electrical characterization.


The AFM technique eliminates probe contact damage and avoids FIB beam implantation of gallium into the low k dielectric film. The lower input capacitance of this AFM active contact probing technique as compared to alternative active contact probing techniques will be described.


Deprocessing Low K Dielectric Films


The requirement to planar deprocess/unlayer BEOL structures, including low k dielectric films, to expose the embedded copper interconnects for subsequent electrical characterization is probably the most challenging task facing the characterization failure analyst today.


The manufacturing decisions that formed the strategy of the BEOL dielectric strategy also dictate the deprocessing/delayering options available.


CMP (chemical-mechanical polishing) processing common to current technologies with multiple metallization levels demands fabrication decisions for low- k dielectric implementation as well as for dualdamascene copper structures. For CMP, material selection of endstop materials such as silicon nitride must be made as well as whether hardmask materials on top of the porous low-k dielectrics are used as protection from such common CMP effects as scratching or dishing.


The same manufacturing considerations involved in device manufacture with regards to low-k damage during RIE etch, wet etch strip, or clean processing face the failure analyst during deprocessing.


Selectivity of etch stop during RIE/wet etch deprocessing is very important. Poor selectivity of etch stop materials can cause undercutting of copper interconnects and damage which compromises further analysis. Figure 1[10] shows some possible features that might be introduced during low-k BEOL manufacturing that would represent challenges to the


failure analyst during deprocessing of the die.


Figure 1 [10] Challenges of Integrating Low-k Dielectric films with Dual Damascene Copper interconnects


Specifically, some of these deprocessing challenges of low-k dielectric films include:


(1) planar delayering without scratching, dishing or allowing polishing particles/slurry to become embedded in porous, low k dielectric layers;


(2) selective endstop RIE (reactive ion etch) removal of low k film without undercutting copper interconnects/metal lands;


(3) effects of conventional wet etch deprocessing on porous, low k dielectric films


AFM Probing


Using atomic force microscopy (AFM) for surface imaging provides a precise control of probe contact forces with high spatial image resolution. The principle of the  “optical lever” associated with AFM technology is well documented [3,4,5,6] for precise control of the tip contact forces. These low contact forces (on the order of 1-50 nanonewton) are especially applicable for probing submicron copper features surrounded by the low modulus of SiLKTM.


Figure 2 depicts the AFM deflection system composed of a laser source, position sensitive detector, cantilever, and probe tip.


Figure 2 [4]: Principle of AFM “optical lever”


The laser impinges on the top surface of a reflective cantilever, the underside of which contains a micromachined tip. This system mimics an “optical lever” as very small changes in cantilever deflection can result in large changes in laser position at the detector. Repeatable contact forces are controlled by a position sensitive detector.


The low contact forces of the AFM tip allow multiple contact cycles on submicron copper interconnect features without damage to the copper feature or to the underlying low modulus SiLKTM film.


Independent experiments verified this through repeated two point AFM probing.


Using a dual damascene test structure (shown in Figures 4 and 5), the IV plots of fifteen individual probe contact cycles were superimposed and plotted in Figure 3. The resistance was calculated from each IV curve and was found to be 168 Ω .  The resistance remained the same during successive AFM probe contacts.


Another advantage of AFM is that it enables high resolution imaging of features as small as 50nm in a scanned field of view 38µm by 38µm.


One example of AFM imaging over a wide field of view to detect a resistive site is described in Figures 4 and 5. Here a long test structure consisting of different level copper lands that are linked together


by copper via interconnects and surrounded by the SiLKTM dielectric film.


Figure 3: IV Plots of multiple AFM probe contact cycles of dual damascene interconnect test structure in non-failing areas.


Following the requisite deprocessing of the low k dielectric film to expose the top level of this copper via test structure, one probe is placed on one link and another on an adjacent link to detect the source of the resistive/open site(s).


Figure 4 is a lower magnification AFM image of this long copper test structure showing the individual copper line segments linked to underlying copper lines with dual damascene copper interconnects .


Figure 5 is a higher magnification AFM image that details the site of a resistive element identified by AFM two point probing. Here, the close spaced copper segments surrounded by the low modulus of SiLKTM were not damaged or smeared even after repeated AFM probe contact cycles. Figure 6 shows the higher than normal electrical resistance  (348Ω) obtained at the site shown in Figure 5. This plot is an overlay of repeated probe contact measurements with no apparent change in resistance. This technique is equally successful in measuring distributed resistive paths in such structures.


Figure 4: A Long  (>38µm) copper interlevel via test structure in SiLKTM dielectric film.


Figure 5: AFM DC probing detected the location of a resistive element in dual damascene via test structure embedded in SiLKTM dielectric film.


Figure 6: Electrical IV plot of the resistive site shown in Figure 5 as measured by AFM probing.


An example of high resolution AFM imaging is provided in Figure 7. A single probe scanning over a region of interest (in this case an SRAM cell). The image in Figure 7 shows multiple FET devices.


Figure 8 shows a two-probe AFM system. As many as four probes can be manipulated within a 2 μm by 3μm footprint area and permit simultaneous probe contact and electrical measurements up to four distinct FET devices within an SRAM cell shown in Figure 7.


Principle of Scanning Force Microscopy


Scanning Force Microscopy (SFM) is a Scanning Probe Microscopy based technique [17] and is the most common type of it. It is based on the interaction of a very sharp probe tip and the device under test(DUT). The tip is located very close to the DUT surface (typical distances h are few nanometers to several tens of nanometers) or is in contact with the DUT surface (Figure 1a). By means of piezo scanners it is possible to raster-scan the tip in x, y, and z direction. The interaction is related to forces F between the tip and the DUT surface. Due to different distance-dependent tip/DUT force interactions, the tip is attracted to or repulsed from the surface of the DUT which results in an optically detected bending of a cantilever where the tip is fixed to. This detected interaction product is processed further and is used either as the measurement result or for the readjustment of the tip.

Figure 1: Principle of Scanning Force Microscopy


a) Fundamental arrangement of a Scanning Force Microscope.


b) Fundamental distance dependence of the most important forces in a Scanning Force Microscope for large distances between DUT and probe tip.


Figure 1b shows the principle distance dependency of the three main forces which can occur during the test of ICs. These are the atomic and van-der-Waals forces (Fig. 1b shows only the van-der-Waals force),the Coulomb force, and the magnetic force [18, 19].


Atomic and van-der-Waals forces are used for mapping the topography (topographic mode). There are two operating modes. In the so called contact mode the tip is in contact with the DUT (The topographies shown in this paper are made in this mode.). If the working distance between the tip and DUT surface is within a few nanometers the operating mode is called noncontact mode. In the topographic mode a spatial resolution of nanometer and even subnanometer is possible [19].


If the probe tip is retracted to a constant distance h(typically 50 nm) from the measurement point on the DUT surface, additional to the van-der-Waals force long-range forces like the Coulomb force


Fel


become dominant. By using a conductive tip this Coulomb force allows voltage measurements (voltage contrast mode). This force and therefore the bendingΔhel of the tip is now caused by the voltage difference between the probe and the test point on the DUT [5, 6].


Additional to this Coulomb force magnetic forces(


Fmag ) become apparent at this distance. This magnetic force interaction between a magnetic field caused by the DUT and a ferromagnetic tip leads to Magnetic Force Microscopy [19, 20].


Principle of Magnetic Force Microscopy


The Magnetic Force Microscopy (MFM) probe consists of a cantilever with an extremely sharp tip at its end which is now coated with a 50 nm thick(typical) ferromagnetic material like Cobalt-Chrome with the magnetization


M (Fig. 2). One common model describes the tip as a single magnetic dipole m with a magnetization component only in the zdirection mz [19].

magnetic dipole


Figure 2: Magnetic Force Microscopy probe


a) Fundamental probe arrangement.


b) Simple model for the magnetic behaviour of the probe.


In this model it is assumed that the ferromagnetic coating of the tip interacts only with the z-component Hz of the magnetic field of the DUT. This leads to a bending of the cantilever Δhmag which is now proportional to the magnetic force interaction which itself is proportional to the derivation of the magnetic field in z-direction:


Δh


m Hz


mag


mag,z


∝


= − ⋅ ⋅μ ∂∂0


By raster-scanning the probe tip in xy-direction the normal component Hz of the magnetic field is measured. Since the magnetic force is a long-range force it interacts not only with the apex of the probe tip but also with its side walls. This limits the spatial resolution to about 10 - 20 nm [21, 22]. In the model shown in Figure 2 this behaviour will be considered in that way, that the magnetic dipole is not at the apex of the tip but somewhere in the tip.


Because the probe tip and the cantilever are mechanical parts they offer a resonance behaviour if the cantilever oscillates. This resonance behaviour limits the frequency response and therefore the temporal resolution to the resonance frequency or multiples of it. This resonance frequency depends on the geometry of the cantilever/probe tip arrangement and is in between 10 kHz and 200 kHz [23].


The sensitivity depends on the spring constant of the cantilever, the magnetic moment of the ferromagnetic coating of the tip, and on the virtual position of the magnetic dipole within the probe tip. The sensitivity increases with increasing magnetic moment,decreasing spring constant, and decreasing distance of the magnetic dipole to the apex of the tip. Tips where a specific ferromagnetic particle is attached can measure magnetic fields as small as 10-8 Tesla


[24]. Standard probes are less sensitive.


Principle of Circuit Internal Current Measurement via Magnetic Force


Microscopy (Current Contrast


Imaging)


One approach for the measurement of circuit internal currents is the measurement of the magnetic field Hext caused by a current flow is through a conducting line. From Ampere’s law it is known that each current flow through an interconnection line induces a magnetic field around the line:

Due to magnetic force interaction between this magnetic field Hext,N and the ferromagnetic probetip the tip will be attracted or repulsed from the test point leading to a bending ( Δhmag ) of the cantilever described by Equation 1. Therefore, Δhmag is proportional to the current is . Again, the probe tip interacts only with the normal component Hext,N ofthe magnetic field. By using a lock-in amplifier the measurement result is represented as magnitude and phase. As can be seen in Figure 3b the distribution of the absolute value |Hext,N| (magnitude) shows aminimum and two maxima. At the position of the


minimum the phase changes by 180°. This means that these outstanding points are the middle of the conducting line where the current flows through. By using this information there is a possibility to localize the conducting line where the current flows through.


Three types of current measurements can be realized.


Time domain measurements can be done by positioning the probe tip at a specific test point and measuring the magnetic field versus time. By rasterscanning the probe across distinct lines or areas either line scans or two-dimensional measurements can be performed. In all cases the cantilever bending is measured at every point. Two dimensional measurements are also known as Current Contrast Imaging (CCI) [26]. An example of a CCI measurement is shown in Figure 4. Figure 4a shows the topography of all three parallel 2 µm wide and spaced conducting lines. The current of 500 µA with a frequency of 14 kHz (which was the resonance frequency of the used probes) flows only through the center of all three conducting lines.

From Figures 4b,d it can be seen that the amplitude distribution shows a minimum and two maxima.


Furthermore, Figures 4c,e show a phase change of 180°at the same test points where the minima in 4b,d


are. Compared to Figure 3b this means that this minimum and this phase change indicates the middle of the center conducting line. Please note, that the black line in Fig. 4d is not the conducting line itself as might be suggested. Compared to Fig. 4b it is obvious that this black line comes from the false color representation. That means, that the difference between the highest and the lowest measured value is so high that small measured values are represented as a wide black line and not as a small black line.


The physical limits of the CCI technique regarding spatial and temporal resolution as well as sensitivity are based in principle on the same limitations as with a MFM.


Figure 5 shows the result of a current measurement on a 100 nm wide conducting line. Depicted are 200 line scans at the same position. The current amplitude was choosen to be 70 µA in order to avoid destruction of the test structure, the frequency was 14 kHz. This linewidth is the smallest on which current measurements have been demonstrated up to now. Please note that the current carrying conducting line is in this case not in the center of the image (at 10 µm) like in Fig. 4 but shifted to the right side (at 15 µm). Again, please note, that the wide dark line in Fig. 5a has nothing to do with the actual line width.

In Figure 6, measurement of a 1.7 µA current on a 2 µm wide conducting line with standard commercial MFM probes is shown. The frequency was again 14 kHz. Due to the very small current level the signal is very noisy.
