The smallest currents measured up to now are 1 mA DC and 1 µA AC using a specially prepared MFM probe [26].


The highest detectable current frequency is limited by the resonance frequency of the cantilever or multiples of it. A current measurement with a frequency of 66 kHz is demonstrated in [26].


This technique of measuring the magnetic field around a wire was originally used for a calibration procedure of magnetic probes in MFM [15, 27, 28].


Then it was used for current probing in electronic devices [25, 26, 29].


These measurements demonstrated the possibility to measure circuit internal currents using MFM with high spatial resolution and high sensitivity. In the following, a case study of a circuit internal failure analysis via CCI based on MFM is presented.


Problem


During electrical testing in powerdown mode all devices of a new test IC design in a 0.35 µm technology with 4 metallization layers failed for high current consumption (75 mA at 1 V). Pin leakage measurements showed a short between a VDD supply pin and ground (VSS). The signal of the failing VDD pin is distributed to different sectors of the IC by a 500 µm wide supply conducting line, as shown in the optical image of a locally decapsulated IC (Fig.7).


Figure 7: Optical image of a locally decapsulated device. The white line highlights the VDD distribution line across the chip which is connected to the failing pin.


A first failure localization with Liquid Crystal Thermography (LCT) showed an increased heat dissipation along the VDD supply conducting line as seen in Figure 8. During this measurement the IC was cooled to below 0°C to reduce overall IC warming.


Figure 8: Liquid Crystal Thermography image of the area of interest of the failing IC.


At a position close to the IC border where the supply line is stitched to lower metallization levels (area A)the LCT signal disappeared. Optical inspection revealed no suspicious features in this area. At this point the analysis could have been continued by


electron beam testing. This however would have required the depassivation of several layers of metal or the deposition of a large number of FIB probe pads to further follow the electrical signal. Instead we decided to investigate whether the failure localization could be done without further time consuming IC preparation by using CCI.


IC preparation


Although CCI can be performed on passivated devices and does not need additional preparation steps of the IC itself, the current design of MFM probes is limiting the test access to the IC surface in cases where a locally opened IC has to be investigated. Figure 9 shows the problem which occurs when a recessed IC surface has to be inspected.


Clamp


Probe tip


Pins Holder


Bondwires Package


Figure 9: Limited test access to the die due to the large dimensions of the probe.


Due to the fact that the probe tip does not reach vertically into the cavity of the packaged IC the carrier and the holder comes into contact with the bondwires at the rim of the cavity thus preventing complete test access. Fortunately as shown in Figure 10 the IC was only bonded at three sides. Due to the missing bondwires, test access was realized via this fourth side by sawing the package without limiting the function of the IC.


Figure: 10: Improvement of the test access by cutting the fourth side of the package.


By doing so it was possible to reach the surface of the failing IC. In order to increase the testable area the


rims of the package were partially removed. The testable area is shown in Figure 11. It is obvious that not the complete area is testable. However, the lower part, where the LCT signal disappeared, can be reached by the MFM probe.


Testable area


Figure 11: Testable area after preparation.


Experimental results


The following measurements have been performed with a standard MFM system which was modified extensively for current measurements. The first set of measurements was checked to determine whether the current on the conducting line located by LCT had the same amplitude at different test points along the line. Figure 12 shows the corresponding positions of the line scans.

That means that no leakage current from this line is detected up to MP 4.

Further measurements have been made in the area around the end of the conducting line (area A in Figure 8). Several linescans have been made. The most important were MP 5 and MP 6 as indicated in Figure 14.

Figure 14: Positions of line scans at the end of the

The corresponding amplitude and phase measurements of points MP5 and MP6 are shown in Figs. 15a-d. The amplitude distributions in both points are comparable (Figs. 15a,c) to each other,signifying a comparable current amplitude in both test points. In the phase distributions (Figs. 15b,d)however, the phase contrast is contrary, i.e. there is a phase shift of 180°. This means that the directions of the currents are contrary and it can therefore be concluded that the current path is divided into two paths (indicated by arrows in Fig. 14).


As for the LCT measurements optical inspection revealed again no suspicious features in this area (see Fig. 16a). However the seal ring, a VSS metal line which surrounds the active parts of the whole chip in several metal layers, is crossing the suspicious area in the appropriate way to explain the measured current distribution by a short of the VDD supply line and the VSS seal ring possibly at a lower metallization level.


Figure 15: Verifying the directions of the current at MP 5 and MP 6.

c) Amplitude distribution at MP 6


d) Phase distribution at MP 6


In order to verify this assumption the chip was polished parallel to the surface and controlled by optical inspection after each removal of one metal level. Figure 16a shows a light microscopy image of the area of interest before polishing. Figure 16b shows a zoomed area after removal of the uppermost metal levels. A short between the power supply conducting line and the seal ring caused by a design error is visible. For comparison a corresponding good area on the chip is shown in Fig. 16c, where a gap exists providing the necessary isolation between the VDD line and VSS sealring.


Figure 16: Verifying the failure located with CCI.


a) Light microscopy image of the area of interest.


b) Image of the failed area.


c) Image of a good area.


Conclusion and Outlook


We have successfully demonstrated the applicability of the Current Contrast Imaging (CCI) technique based on Magnetic Force Microscopy for function and failure analysis of modern multi-metal layer ICs.


We have performed circuit internal contactless current measurements on a real IC and localized a short between two metal lines in an intermediate metal layer, where the conventional LCT technique was not successsful.


We demonstrated the state-of-the-art of CCI. Current measurements on 100 nm wide conducting lines have also been demonstrated as well as measurements down to 1.7 µA AC.


For circuit internal measurements the failed IC was prepared in order to improve the test access. Based on CCI measurements the current of the incoming VDD supply line was equally distributed on two branches of the VSS seal ring. Sample delayering by surface parallel polishing and optical inspection revealed a short between the VDD supply line and the VSS seal ring in an intermediate metal layer caused by a design error.


Despite the successful failure analysis in the present case, the use of CCI was not straightforward. The largest hindrance was limited test access to the die inside the cavity due to the large dimensions of the probe and its holder. This problem is inherent to all cantilever based probes. A solution of this problem could be the use of tuning fork [30] or needle sensor


[31] based test systems. In both probe arrangements the probe tip reaches vertically into the cavity of the packaged IC. Experiments have demonstrated the possibility of current or voltage measurements with both systems [32, 33].

Multiple probe deep sub-micron electrical measurements using leading edge micro-machined scanning probes


Kenneth Krieg1, Douglas Thomson1,2and Greg Bridges1,2


1MFI Technologies, San Jose, CA 95134


2Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering, University of Manitoba


A contact electrical probing technique based on atomic force microscopy is described which has the capabilities for imaging and real-time electrical signal measurement of multiple close proximity circuit nodes in deep sub-micron integrated circuits. Similar to traditional wire probers, the probing technique described operates on a standard probe-station and is outfitted with a conductive atomic force microscope micro-machined tip for measuring surface topography.


The probe scanning system is capable of imaging and probing features smaller than 0.18 micron and is able to control and maintain the contact force to less than one micronewton, minimizing circuit damage. The probe system we describe can support multiple probes that can be simultaneously configured on the same probe station, enabling four-point parameter measurements of devices in a 2 micron square area.


The needle or wire probe and associated probe station are a mainstay in integrated circuit (IC) and device characterization and failure analysis.  The probe station provides a functionally flexible and mechanically stable platform for mounting the probe cards and manipulators needed to stimulate the device under test (DUT).  Viewing of the DUT and placement of the needle probes at the desired internal test points is typically performed with an optical microscope mounted on the probe station.


This traditional needle probe and probe station system has sufficed for many years to stimulate and measure signals from the internal nodes of ICs.


Unfortunately, the size of the circuit elements comprising present day ICs has decreased to well below the optical resolving limit and thus cannot be observed using conventional probe station optical microscopes.


For example, two 0.25 μm devices positioned 0.25 μm


apart cannot be resolved with an optical microscope.


Since the dimensions of microelectronics features are too small to be imaged with optical systems, present day probe station systems are no longer capable of the spatial resolution needed to place probes on the desired measurement points of a DUT.


In addition to the above imaging problem, for device geometries below 1.0 μm, the tip diameter of a needle


or wire probe pose significant electrical contact


limitations [1].  For sub-0.5 micron circuit elements reliable and isolated contact with a specified interconnect line becomes almost impossible.  For example, Fig. 1 shows a photomicrograph of a typical etched-wire probe located over an IC with three  0.5 μm


Fig. 1: Typical etched wire needle probe near three 0.5μm interconnect lines (top) showing the difficulty in


imaging and making reliable contact to a trace inside a 1 μm deep probe-point window (bottom) without shorting to an adjacent trace or damaging the trace due to high contact forces.


wide interconnect lines having 0.5 μm spaces [2], using


a probe station equipped with a Mitutoyo 2X VM ZOOMTM microscope and a 50X objective.  This demonstrates that when using an etched wire probe it is very difficult to obtain reliable electrical contact with


microscope


optical path


0.5 µm traces


Needle probe with


0.1 µm tip radius


only one of the metal traces.  The situation worsens considerably when the circuit feature size is less than


0.5 μm. Additional problems can arise when probing


small geometry structures or delicate low-k dielectric materials due to possible mechanical damage of the DUT from the contact pressure of the needle probe.


Figure 7: AFM single probe imaging generated image of submicron FET devices within the SRAM cell.


Figure 8: Two-probe AFM DC probing system simultaneously contacting submicron SRAM FET discrete devices.


AFM scanning over wide areas of SiLKTM to image a copper feature for electrical characterization does not appear to affect the measured electrical values. An experiment was performed to determine if AFM scanning over a surface area with the low modulus of SiLKTM results in any observed interaction or material pickup at the probe tip. Figure 9 shows no indications of material pickup after repeated scans.


Figure 9: SEM image of AFM probe tip after repeated scans over SiLKTM


Higher probe tip resistances (50 – 100 ohms)associated with the AFM contact tips can potentially affect the electrical measurements. The net effect of these higher tip resistances can be compensated by utilizing a standard parametric analyzer unit in combination with a precision resistance R box.  In this way, the effects of these higher tip resistances can be minimized during subtle, low voltage/low current measurements such as FET subthreshold VT characteristics.


In comparison, alternative techniques such as SEM


specimen current or FIB voltage contrast imaging is less effective in applications where copper interconnects are embedded in these low k dielectric films. Due to SiLKTM charging in the SEM (even with low accelerating voltages), the technique of specimen current is less effective as a method of isolation.  In addition, prolonged e-beam irradiation(> 10 minutes) is reported to cause changes between the oxide hardmask and the low k dielectric polymer,including cracking [17].


FIB Assisted Probing


FIB assisted probing techniques successfully applied to conventional silicon dioxide dielectric films or even FSG (fluorosilicate glass) dielectric films is not as effective when applied to low k dielectric films.


Gallium from the FIB beam can interact with the porous low-k dielectric film forming a conductive layer as well as introducing an electrical leakage path, thus hindering discrete device characterization measurements.


Similarly, the technique of FIB voltage contrast imaging to pinpoint resistive/open sites is also affected. Dwell time during FIB voltage contrast imaging of a copper interconnect test structure results in charge buildup in the surrounding SiLKTM low-k dielectric, obscuring the resistive/open region of interest.


The effects of gallium implantation into the top surface of the SiLKTM to form a conductive layer is easily verified in cross-section by SEM or FIB. In work reported by H. Bender and R.A. Donaton [17],distinct contrast effects were observed during FIB cross sectioning of SiLKTM.  The bright contrast shown in Figure 10 [16,17] was linked to the creation of a conductive path near the top surface of the SiLKTM layer due to FIB gallium ion implantation.


Figure 10 [16, 17] is a FIB cross section of contact holes milled by FIB through a multi-layer stack of SiO2 and SiLKTM. Figure 10(B) is a high magnification view of Figure 10(A) showing the detail of this multilayer structure of oxide and


SiLKTM. The arrows in photo (B) identify the oxide layers. The SiLKTM layers are labeled.


Figure 10: FIB cross section of contact holes milled by FIB through multiple-layer stack of SiO2 and


SILKTM


AC Active Probing


The low contact forces associated with the AFM technique as well as the capability for pinpoint probing on features as small as 0.10 µm make it very useful for AC waveform measurements in high performance SOI CMOS technologies with copper interconnects and low-k dielectric films.


Conventional active probe contact techniques (either using etched wire probes or needle type probes)exhibit higher total input capacitance when accounting for the effects of the FET tip mounting device, probe arm capacitance effects, among other factors.


In addition, in order to measure submicron features,these conventional probe techniques require FIB


deposited tungsten pads that introduce an additional 150 to 200 femto-farads of capacitive loading [7].


AFM probes offer a total input capacitive loading of 80 femto-farads. This difference means that narrower copper linewidth interconnects can be directly probed with minimal effect on the AC signal to be measured and without resorting to FIB deposited tungsten probe pads [7].


Most importantly, AFM probing does not cause damage to the submicron copper interconnects and deformation to the underlying low-k dielectric films.


The AFM active probe tip offers the same capability of probing features as small as 0.10 µm as well as measuring waveform signal speeds > 2 gHz [3,6,7].


Figure 11 shows an AFM active probe on a deprocessed die in a package.


Figure 11 AC active waveform probing of a deprocessed die in a package.


AFM AC Active probing involving a Tektronix TDS7404 Digital Oscilloscope with an input bandwidth of 4 gHz and sampling rates of 20 gigabytes per second permit waveform measurements with nanovolt resolution and subpicosecond edge timing. The flip chip packaged die is first backside thinned. The AFM non-contact active probe tip obtains the AC waveform measurement during ATE stimulation of the DUT.


Figure 12 shows an AFM scanned image of a fully functional, backside-thinned microprocessor with multilayer copper interconnects and low-k dielectric films.


Figure 12: AFM scanned image of a fully functional,backside thinned die. The large rectangle regions are Rx regions (active device regions).


The AFM technique for DC contact probing and for AC waveform signal measurement is more suited for copper interconnects embedded in low modulus SiLKTM dielectric films as compared to conventional active probe techniques [3,6,7].


The controllable, low contact forces  (1 to 50 nanonewtons) associated with this technique means that the same submicron feature can be repeatedly probed without damage to the copper interconnects or deformation to the underlying low k dielectric film.


The added capability to image and contact features as small as 0.10µm in size for both DC probing and AC waveform signal measurement applications is a significant improvement that overcomes limitations with conventional techniques. Simultaneous, multiple AFM probing capability permits SRAM cell electrical characterization within a 2µm by 3µm footprint.


High speed AC active probing capability enables characterization of high performance 0.18µm SOI CMOS technology with copper interconnects and low-k BEOL dielectric.


The authors thank H.Bender and R.A. Donaton of IMEC and to ASM International for permission to


use the FIB cross sectional photograph identified as Figure 10 in this paper.

Session 7:


Backside 2


D. Bodoh • Motorola, Inc .• Austin, Texas


p 227-235


DOI: 10.1361/cp2001istfa227


D. Patrie, G. Haller


ST Microelectronics, 13106 Rousset, France


P. Poirier


Laboratoire de Microelectronique ISMRA PHILIPS (LAMIP), 14079 Caen, France


P. Jacob


EM Microelectronic-Marin SA, CH 2074 Marin Switzerland


EMPA Swiss Federal Institute for Materials Testing and Research, Ueberlandstr. 129, CH-8600 Duebendorf, Switzerland


Emission microscopy and thermal laser stimulation(OBIRCH, TIVA) are two key methods for backside failure analysis. They are both dedicated for localizing current leakage faults in ICs. The complementary relationship of these two techniques is illustrated through six practical case studies.


Thermal laser stimulation was able to precisely and directly localize defects such as shorts in the IC’s metallic elements that where not readily detectable by emission microscopy. The case studies also illustrate the ability of thermal laser stimulation to detect and physically localize defects in the IC’s polysilicon layers and silicon substrate.


Failure analysis of integrated circuits requires more and more the aid of non-contact testing through backside. This trend is due to the widespread use of flip-chip and lead-on-chip packaging and to the increasing complexity of circuit layout. Several new optical or electromagnetic based techniques have recently emerged to tackle the challenging task of fault localization through the IC’s backside [1,2].


However failure analysts still have to choose which one of these techniques are the most appropriate.


The objective of this paper is to present what we feel is a winning combination for backside fault localization: backside emission microscopy and thermal laser stimulation. Emission microscopy is a well-known technique that has recently been improved for backside failure analysis [1]. Thermal laser stimulation is a relatively new method for


locating current leakage type faults. This method was proposed by Nikawa and Tozaki under the name of OBIRCH [3] and by Cole et al. as TIVA [4]. It consists of scanning an infrared laser beam on the IC’s backside in order to generate thermal gradients.


Precise localization of defects is obtained by imaging the IC’s power consumption variations caused by these thermal gradients.


The complementary relationship of emission microscopy and thermal laser stimulation is illustrated in this paper through several backside and a few front-side examples.


Emission Microscopy


Principles of Emission Microscopy


As mentioned previously, emission microscopy is a very well known technique in failure analysis. It has been shown to be efficient in detecting a wide range of leakage defects in MOS oxides, forward bias junctions, reverse bias junctions, saturated MOS,bipolar transistors, etc. The application of this technique to backside failure analysis is quite straightforward. Nevertheless, the absorption of the substrate must be taken into account. Higher quantum efficiency cameras in the near infrared are needed since only photons with a wavelength higher than about 950nm will be transmitted through the backside


[1].


Emission Microscopy System


At the CNES (French Space Agency), we acquired a Hamamatsu PHEMOS 1000 system to perform


emission microscopy (Figure 1). Throughout this study we used the Hamamatsu’s front side illuminated CCD camera. Front-side imaging is done with a white light camera whereas for backside imaging through the silicon substrate an infrared light is used. In this latter case, the circuit is observed with the CCD camera through a NIR filter. An overlay of the circuit image with the emission image is done to precisely localize the emitting structures.


Figure 1 PHEMOS 1000 emission microscope from Hamamatsu.


Thermal Laser Stimulation


Principles of Thermal Laser Stimulation


The thermal laser stimulation method is a relatively new technique for locating defects in the IC’s metallic lines and vias. It consists of scanning an infrared laser over the front-side or backside of the device under test. Upon laser beam irradiation,localized thermal gradients are generated in the IC’s metallic elements. This temperature increase results in a variation of the metal resistivity (Δρ) according to:


( )0


TCR −


(1)Where ρ0 and αTCR are respectively the resistivity and the temperature coefficient of resistance of the metal,T the temperature upon laser heating, and T0 the initial temperature of the metal.


In the OBIRCH method, the devices under test are biased with a constant voltage source. Upon thermal laser stimulation, or laser heating, the current varies according to:


( ) SICIC VRR


(2)Where ΔRIC is the thermally induced resistance variation, R is the IC’s resistance and VS the voltage.


In the TIVA method, a constant current (IS) is applied and we observe a voltage variation upon thermal laser stimulation. These voltage variations are related to the thermally induced resistance variation (ΔRIC) by:


IC I


V Δ=


(3)Throughout the same metallic element, we expect identical heating and resistance change. If a defect is present within that structure, both heating and resistance change will differ from the non-defective part of the metallic element. Therefore, detecting the current or voltage variations at the pins of the device under test and correlating them with the laser spot location provides precise localization of defects such as voids within these elements. Furthermore, the heating and the resistance change of smaller metallic elements are favored compared to larger ones [5].


This makes thermal laser stimulation a powerful method to detect and precisely localize metallic shorts causing abnormal current consumption.


In our laboratory we implemented the thermal laser stimulation method on the Hamamatsu PHEMOS 1000 emission microscope as well since it incorporates a NIR laser-scanning microscope. Our goal was to combine on the same tool these two predominant techniques for backside fault localization. The laser-scanning microscope of the PHEMOS 1000 system uses a continuous wave infrared laser with a wavelength of 1.3μm and a power of 100mW. Due to this relatively low laser power, we opted for the development of a low noise voltage amplifier (Figure 2). The amplifier was designed to be adapted to the biasing method. Its first stage consists of a voltage follower and a virtual mass current to voltage converter. Either one can be selected trough a manual switch. To amplify very small voltage variations (TIVA method), the voltage follower is implemented in parallel to the device.


However, to amplify very small current variations(OBIRCH method), the current to voltage converter is inserted in series with the device under test.


Figure 2 Low noise, high speed and high gain specific amplifier developed at the CNES.


For device biasing, either a constant voltage or current source can be used to perform thermal laser stimulation. In some cases it is simply more appropriate to use a current source while in other cases a voltage source is better suited, depending on the noise associated with the biasing setup. The same requirements described for the specific amplifier applies to the voltage or the current sources. They both need to have a fast response time and very low noise.  Furthermore, the biasing scheme was designed to allow emission microscopy and thermal laser stimulation testing under the same experimental setup.


Hamamatsu’s OBIRCH acquisition card and software is used to acquire the thermal laser stimulation images. For precise localization, the thermal laser stimulation image is superimposed in false color on the laser-reflected image. Since the scanning microscope uses an NIR laser, the laser-reflected image can be taken either from the front or backside of the IC. In addition to the Mitutoyo NIR 5x, 20x and 100x objectives, we acquired the Hamamtasu 50x OBIRCH objective to perform high-resolution thermal laser stimulation and acquire laser reflected images. The resolution can be increased by the use of the laser scanner zoom of 2x and 4x.


Emission Microscopy vs. Thermal Laser Stimulation


As mentioned in the introduction, we intend to demonstrate the complementary relationship of emission microscopy and thermal laser stimulation through several practical cases. Since emission microscopy effectiveness has been proven over the years, here we will concentrate on what thermal laser stimulation can bring to emission microscopy. As we will demonstrate through the following case studies,thermal laser stimulation is truly a complementary method to emission microscopy for directly localizing several types of current related defects.


More specifically the presented examples will illustrate the following cases:


1. The emission and thermal laser stimulation located defects are non-correlated.


2. The emission and thermal laser stimulated located defects are linked but not at the same location.


3. The emission and thermal laser stimulated located defects are linked and at the same location.


Emission and Thermal Laser Stimulation are Non-correlated


The two first case studies underline the efficiency of the thermal laser stimulation method to localize


metallic shorts that were undetectable by emission microscopy.


Case study #1


The failed CMOS IC is characterized by a high current leakage of 2mA under a 5V constant voltage.


To perform emission microscopy and thermal laser stimulation, the devices were biased with a filtered constant voltage source. For thermal laser stimulation, the current to voltage converter of the amplifier was placed in series with the IC. After a relatively long acquisition time, no emission was observed. Using the thermal laser stimulation method, one sensitive point could be detected as can be seen in the superimposed thermal laser stimulation image of the full device shown in Figure 3. FIB cross sectioning at the located defect was performed. From the SEM image shown in Figure 4, a polymericmetallic defect shorting metal-1 to metal-2 can be seen.


Figure 3 Thermal laser stimulation image acquired with a 20x objective.


Figure 4 SEM image of the thermal laser stimulation located polymeric-metallic defect.


Case study #2


In this case study we performed backside failure analysis on several failed 0.35μm BICMOS ICs. The failed devices presented at certain I/Os a leakage current several hundred micro-amps higher than the expected 10μA. From electrical characterization, the BICMOS devices output PMOS transistors were found to be defective. Liquid crystal and other frontside FA techniques failed to localize the defect due to i) the normal 85mA consumption current of the BICMOS devices and to ii) the presence of four interconnection layers. Backside failure analysis was performed though a silicon thickness of 290μm. The silicon substrate did not need to be thinned down due to its low doping concentration. Package decapsulation was performed with the Chip Unzip tool from Hypervision.


We first performed backside emission microscopy to localize the current leakage source in these 30x400μm2 PMOS structures. The devices were biased under constant voltage. After a short integration time,several emission points could be detected on each device. However, by superimposing the emission image with the reflected image, we found that the observed emission points in the vicinity of the PMOS structures did not correlate with the defect location(Figure 5). These emission points were found to be from the normally active saturated bipolar transistor.


Figure 5 Backside emission image of the full PMOS structure at 20x.


For backside thermal laser stimulation, the failed devices were powered up using a filtered voltage source. A shunt resistance was placed in series with the I/O presenting a high leakage current. The amplifier was then mounted in parallel with the shunt resistance. The voltage converter of the amplifier picked up the voltage variations at the shunt resistance.


The obtained superimposed thermal laser stimulation image is shown Figure 6. The thermal laser stimulation method could successfully localize the current leakage source from the IC’s backside. SEM analysis after delayering at the thermal laser stimulation location showed the presence of a tungsten micro-bridge between the drain and the source electrodes of this output PMOS transistor(Figure 7).


Figure 6 Backside thermal laser stimulation image done with a 20x objective.


Figure 7 SEM image of the tungsten micro-bridge.


Emission and Thermal Laser Stimulation are


Correlated but not at the Same Location


The two following case studies illustrate the ability of the thermal laser stimulation method to directly localize the current related defect. The observed emission coming from these devices is only a consequence of the defect. In one case, layout information was available and the approximate defect location could be traced back from the emission points. However, in the other case, no layout information was available and the defect location


could only be found with the thermal laser stimulation method.


Case study #3


Several failed CMOS devices presented a 2mA leakage current under a 3V bias. The failed devices were powered up with a voltage source in order to perform emission microscopy. The obtained emission image acquired under the 0.5x macro lens is shown in Figure 8. Two distinct regions in the superimposed emission images can be identified. It was found that the observed emission comes from saturated transistors which are activated by the defect.


Furthermore, the saturated transistors from these two regions are not connected at the design level. Using the Knight’s layout tool, the equipotentials associated with the two emission regions were highlighted. The IC layout image is shown in Figure 9.


Figure 8 Enlarged emission image acquired with the


0.5x objective.


Figure 9 Knight’s layout showing the two noncorrelated equipotentials.


We can see that the two non-related equipotentials are more or less superimposed in one area of the circuit. Mechanical probing, FIB device modification and voltage contrast analysis were performed in this


area to precisely localize the failure site. Even though successful, this analysis was quite time consuming.


Other failed devices were subjected to thermal laser stimulation for comparison. They were biased under a constant voltage source and the current to voltage converter of the amplifier was placed in series with the devices. The thermal laser stimulation image as well as its superimposition on the laser reflected image is shown in Figure 10. Here we can clearly see the current path created by the defect. This current path corresponds to the two shorted equipotentials(circled out in Figure 9). We can also see that the imaged voltage variations change sign in the middle of the current path. This location corresponds exactly to the failed area identified previously from the emission images. FIB cross sectioning was performed at this location. The SEM image of the cross-section is shown in Figure 11. We can clearly see a metallic short between metal-2 and metal-3 as observed in the other cases resolved with emission microscopy.


Using the thermal laser stimulation method, several hours of fault isolation were saved.


Figure 10 Thermal laser stimulation image of the voltage variation acquired with a 20x objective(above) and superimposition on the laser reflected image (below).


Figure 11 SEM image of the thermal laser stimulation located metallic parasitic via.


Case study #4


Backside failure analysis was performed on a failed space dedicated CMOS IC. Conventional front-side techniques failed to localize the defect due to the presence of metal filling over the suspected failed area. The device exhibited an abnormal leakage current of 60mA when one of its blocks was activated. Using the Chip Unzip tool, the packaged devices were decapsulated and the silicon substrate was thinned down to a thickness of 200μm. A laser reflected image of the area of interest obtained through the silicon substrate is shown in the middle of Figure 12.


For backside emission microscopy, the device was biased with a voltage source. The obtained superimposed emission image is shown on the left side of Figure 12. As can be seen, the emission comes from a saturated transistor characterized by an abnormally high current.


Due to the high leakage current involved, a shunt resistance was placed in series with the device and the biasing voltage source. The voltage follower of the amplifier was then mounted in parallel with this


shunt resistance. The thermal laser stimulation image is shown on the right side of Figure 12. From this image, we could localize the defect to be within a different structure than for the emission. This thermal laser stimulation located defect explains the emission image. It is most likely the cause of the transistor saturation.


After delayering at the thermal laser stimulation sensitive spot, we observed the defect to be located at the polysilicon gate level. SEM observation after plasma etching of the polysilicon showed a polysilicon spike within the substrate, thus shorting the gate to the substrate. (Figure 13). This substrate breakdown was most likely generated by an ESD/ EOS type damage.


Figure 13 SEM image of the ESD/ EOS induced polysilicon spike.


The emission and thermal laser are correlated and at the same location


In the two last case studies, we observed both emission and thermal laser stimulation signals at the defect location. However, the emission and thermal laser points where found to be slightly shifted in position.


Figure 12 Laser reflected image acquired with a 20x objective from the IC’s backside (middle). Magnification of


the emission image and the thermal laser stimulation image are shown respectively on the left and right side.


Case study #5


Backside failure analysis was performed on several failed NPN bipolar transistors with multiple emitters since metallic layers are obscuring the structure’s sensitive area. After package decapsulation with the Chip Unzip tool, backside emission microscopy and backside thermal laser stimulation were done through a 290μm thick silicon substrate.


The failed emitters contact electrodes are made of doped polysilicon. A collector current leakage of approximately 100μA was measured under a collector to emitter voltage of 10V. The devices were biased with a filtered voltage source and the current to voltage converter of the amplifier was mounted in series with the device ground pin. The collector current was fixed to 100μA for both emission microscopy and thermal laser stimulation testing.


The obtained emission image is shown in Figure 14.


We can clearly identify a localized emission point near one of the emitter’s corners, which coincides with the base-emitter junction of the NPN transistor.


For comparison, thermal laser stimulation was performed at the same location. The superimposed thermal laser stimulation image is shown in Figure


15. The intensity level of the thermal laser stimulation signal was adjusted to localize the most sensitive point. By comparing Figure 15 with Figure 14 we can see that the defect is located at the same emitter’s corner. However we can observe a slight difference in the position between the two points. For all the studied transistors, the thermal laser stimulation points were located in the vicinity of the emission points.


Figure 14 Magnification of the emission image done with a 100x objective.


The thermal laser stimulation signal can be explained by the presence of a melted spike in the polysilicon emitter. Upon laser irradiation, the spike is


preferentially heated which induces a higher resistance change. The larger current flowing in the spike also contributes to the increase in the transistor’s power consumption at the spike location.


Figure 15 Backside thermal laser stimulation image done with a 50x objective and laser scanner zoom of 4x.


We also observe emission at this area since the critical field is preferentially reached at the end of the melted polysilicon spike. Emission therefore occurs at this location when the junction is under reverse bias. This hypothesis also explains the slight position difference from the emission and the thermal laser stimulation located points. TEM analyses are currently under way to analyze the melted polysilicon spike origin.


Case study #6


Several CMOS Grounded Gate NMOS (GGNMOS)protection structures were tested for their robustness to electrostatic discharge (ESD). The structures were submitted to 100 consecutive CDM (Charge Device Model) stress cycles at –2kV. The protection structure exhibited a leakage current of 2μA after the CDM stress compared to a few nano-amps before.


Both emission microscopy and thermal laser stimulation were used to localize the ESD induced defects. The device was biased using a constant voltage source and the current to voltage converter of the amplifier was mounted in series between the circuit and voltage source ground.


One of the several ESD induced defects was found in the latchup guard ring of the protection structure. The superimposed emission and thermal laser stimulation image are respectively shown in Figure 16 and Figure


17. The defect is most likely a melted silicon spike in the p-n junction of the latchup guard ring since it is


characterized by a strong thermal laser stimulation signal. Emission is observed at the tip of this silicon spike due to the lower breakdown voltage at this location. This would explain the large position difference between the emission and the thermal laser stimulation located points. To confirm this assumption, we are currently performing complete delayering and FESEM inspection on the silicon surface.


Figure 16 Superimposed emission image acquired under a 100x objective.


Figure 17 Thermal laser stimulation image acquired with a 50x objective and laser scanner zoom of 4x.


All the presented case studies demonstrated the ability of the thermal laser stimulation method to localize current leakage faults. Two questions still need to be answered: How precisely can we localize the defect from the thermal laser stimulation images?When we observe both emission and thermal laser stimulation signals from the same area, which one should be trusted to precisely localize the defect? The first question was answered with a 3D finite element


ANSYS model of the thermal laser stimulation method which was described elsewhere [5]. It was found that thermal laser stimulation could rapidly localize defects with very good precision since: i)there is no spatial or temporal shift between the “hot spot” and the laser spot center and ii) there is no significant spatial extension to the thermal laser stimulation effect. These findings lead us to answer the second question. We believe that thermal laser stimulation can precisely physically localize the defect with greater precision than emission microscopy. A physical defect such as a metallic spike will generate emission only through mechanisms such as high field and no direct localization of the defect can be obtained from the emission signal.


Furthermore, when backside emission microscopy is performed, the emission point is not necessarily at the same location as the observed front side emission one. This is partly due to the silicon substrate which filters out wavelengths shorter than 1μm. The observed emission is also influenced by the silicon CCD cameras which have higher quantum efficiency in the visible range (40 % at 700nm) than in the near infrared range (5 % at 1µm). This is illustrated on a failed bipolar ESD protection structure. The relatively low doped substrate (1015 At/cm3) was thinned down to 300μm to allow backside emission microscopy. The front side and backside emission images obtained on the same device are respectively shown in Figure 18 and Figure 19. By comparing these two images, we can observe a position shift of approximately 10μm between the front side and the backside peak emission intensities.


Figure 18 Front side emission image acquired for a 600s acquisition time with a 20x objective.


Figure 19 Backside emission image acquired for a


600s acquisition time with a 20x objective.


Precise localization is important since it makes physical analysis much simpler. Especially when time-consuming techniques such as TEM are required to reveal tiny defects like melted spikes. Our next objective is to understand the fundamentals of the thermal laser stimulation induced mechanism in order to gain insight in the physical cause of the located defect. This would allow us to relate the thermal laser stimulation signature to the physical defect thus bypassing most of the complex physical analysis techniques.


Emission microscopy and thermal laser stimulation are two key methods for backside failure analysis.


The complementary relationship of thermal laser stimulation to emission microscopy was illustrated trough several case studies. They essentially illustrated the ability of the thermal laser stimulation to:


• Localize current related defects from the frontside as well as from the backside of silicon ICs.


• Directly localize defects in the IC metallic elements which resulted in no or indirect emission.


• Localize non-metallic defects in the IC polysilicon layers and silicon substrate.


• Precisely localize physically the defect generating a thermal laser stimulation signal.


In the light of the presented case studies, the thermal laser stimulation technique is an essential addition to emission microscopy as well as to the other techniques already available in a failure analysis laboratory.


The authors want to thank J. Aznar and J-Y. Bernard for their work on the development of the CNES thermal laser stimulation amplifier and B. Bayol from Centech for Chip Unzip backside preparation.


[5] Beaudoin F., Desplats R., Perdu P. and Lewis D.


This conference


p 237-241


DOI: 10.1361/cp2001istfa237


CMOS Front-End Investigation over Large Areas by   Deprocessing from the Back Side


Frank Zachariasse


Failure Analysis Section, Philips Semiconductors MOS4YOU,  Gerstweg 2, 6534AE Nijmegen, The Netherlands


In this paper a method is presented to remove the  silicon substrate from the back of a CMOS chip  altogether, whilst leaving gate oxide and silicide  intact.  This is achieved by grinding the chip until it  becomes transparent and then selectively etching  the remaining silicon away. The procedure  developed is very fast: A 50 mm2 die can be  prepared in under an hour, with the gate and  silicide of every transistor intact.


The method is especially valuable when front-end  process features need to be examined over a large  sample area.  Information can thus be gained with  less effort than by using front side deprocessing or  cross sections.


Several case studies are used to illustrate the  effectiveness of the technique and its benefits over  deprocessing from the front.  These include  accurate measurements of gate length (+/- 2 nm) over the entire chip in a 0.18 µm CMOS  technology, and applications to investigation of  silicide formation.


For deep submicron CMOS processes, the control  of gate length as a function of orientation and  position on the reticle is an important issue  affecting yield and Idd performance of the process.  The failure analyst, faced with the need to  understand polysilicon critical dimension (CD) and  its spatial distribution could, until now, use cross  sections or deprocessing from the top of the IC.  However it is difficult to establish two-dimensional  variations in gate length from cross-sections.  Similarly, top side deprocessing is time consuming  and can suffer from artefacts as the poly is attacked  to a small but significant degree during  deprocessing.  In addition, measuring a  deprocessed sample from above can hide effects  such as gate tapering.


Similarly, it is not possible to uncover titanium  silicide by deprocessing from the front side, because this silicide is etched rapidly by HF based  etchants used to remove inter layer dielectric (ILD).


Front-end defects and process issues, such as  missing silicide or an incorrect poly width can also  be revealed with cross sections.  It is then of  importance to understand whether the feature  suspected of causing the fault is indeed exclusively  present on failing sites.  Making multiple cross  sections can provide this information but is time  consuming.  It is shown in this paper how back side  inspection can simplify the work needed to  characterize a process in these cases.


Sample Preparation Method


The following steps are used to prepare a sample  with all silicon removed from the backside, but  with all titanium silicide and gate oxides kept  intact:


1) The sample is glued top side down onto a piece  of microscope slide, 0.8 mm thick, using an  epoxy glue [1].  The glue is chosen specifically  because it is not dissolved when etching the  sample.


2) Three additional ‘dummy’ pieces of silicon  wafer, of the same thickness as the sample of  interest, are similarly glued down.


3) The glue is cured on a hot plate.  Glue  thickness is minimised by holding the samples  between the jaws of a croc-clip during the  curing process.


4) Crystal wax, with a melting point of around  150 °C, is used to bond the 4 samples to a flat  pyrex sample holder disc, some 40 mm  diameter and 8 mm thick.


The resulting ‘stack’ is shown in Figure 1.


The silicon is now thinned by lapping with  successively finer grades of diamond impregnated  lapping film.  The sample can be measured at  intervals with a micrometer to judge progress.   The  hand-polishing process allows one to compensate  for any deviation from planarity during the  grinding. As the thickness is reduced to around  10 µm, the silicon starts to become transparent, and  planarity can be judged by examining the sample  through a back-lit microscope.


Figure 1. Schematic diagram of the sample holder assembly viewed from top (left) and side (right).


When the sample has been thinned until the


remaining silicon is a uniform yellow-brown, the


pyrex disk is heated on a hot plate to melt the wax


and remove the sample. The remaining silicon is


etched away in a boiling mixture of 60 ml


tetramethyl ammonium hydroxide (TMAH, 25 %


solution) plus 20 ml isopropyl alcohol (IPA). The


IPA helps to control the temperature by reducing


the boiling point of the solution to around 80 °C.


Zubel and Kramakowska found that the addition of


IPA to a TMAH etch mixture also helps to obtain a


smoother etch surface [2].


The etch rate of silicon in this etchant is around


0.35 µm/min.  The solution is highly selective


against titanium silicide (etch rate <2 nm/min) and


gate oxide (etch rate < 0.1 nm/min).  An important


feature of the etch mixture used is that the etch rate


of silicon (100) and (111) crystal planes is similar.


This means that high aspect ratio holes (such as


active areas, surrounded by STI) are etched clean.


This would not work if one were to use, for


example, potassium hydroxide, which is known for


very slow etching in the (111) direction.


The high selectivity to oxide and silicide is the key


to the speed of this technique.  Because the etch is


so selective, the thickness and taper of the silicon


before etching are not critical in obtaining a


uniformly etched, large area sample.  Thus, to


produce a sample that will be uniform after etching,


it is sufficient to polish it to a taper of around 3 µm


across the sample, which may be achieved


significantly more easily and rapidly than the


planarity needed for front-side parallel lapping over


a similar area.


One can trade off the total etch time against the


additional grinding time required to carefully thin


the silicon to below 10 µm thickness with a fine


polish.  With practice, the silicon can be lapped


down to 2 µm uniformly over a 50 mm2 sample, and then etched for 6 minutes to clear the  remaining silicon.


After etching, the sample is rinsed well in deionized water, and dried under a nitrogen flow.  It  is important not to attempt to rinse the sample with  solvents since this leads to contamination.  For  examination in a scanning electron microscope, the  edges of the glass slide are well covered in  conductive tape to eliminate charging. Although  similar methods using hydrazine have been  described previously [3], the use of TMAH over  hydrazine may be preferred for safety reasons.


Applications


Accurate Gate Length Measurements


The titanium silicide and gate oxide of a sample, prepared as described above, may be removed with  a 20 s dip in 7:1 buffered HF.


A typical view is shown in Figure 2. The contact  plugs and poly gates are easily visible. Adjacent to  the poly gate is an oxide layer some 20 nm wide.  This oxide layer has also been etched and the  resulting trenches show up as black lines.  This  high contrast feature helps in accurately measuring  the gate length.


This step makes it possible to measure the  variations in gate length on a single transistor,  and  to repeat this measurement over large areas across  the sample.  This is important because gates are not  usually of perfectly uniform length, so that limited  measurements from a cross section can give a  misleading result.


Pyrex sample holder Dummy silicon


piece


Microscope slide Sample


Figure 2. Typical view of a 0.18 µm CMOS


transistors from the back side, after additional


removal of silicide and gate oxide.


This method was used to investigate a product  yield problem due to gate length variation.  It was  found that certain samples in a 0.18 µm CMOS  technology dissipated excessive currents on powerup.  However, the problem was only seen for a  particular memory block, and only when laid out in  a particular orientation on the wafer.  Similar  blocks, rotated 90 degrees, did not show the effect. The problem was caused by an astigmatism in poly  patterning, that affected only the word line drivers  in memory blocks of one orientation.  All other  gate dimensions were within specified limits.  Samples, prepared from the back side, helped to  accelerate the investigation by allowing gate  lengths to be measured accurately in various  functional blocks within the same sample. This  phenomenon would have been difficult to measure  from cross sections, because only certain structures  in particular directions were affected.  Viewing  from the back side was a more suitable method in  this case, because it made it possible to collect a  statistically sound data set and allowed us to draw a  correct conclusion about the root cause.


In order to collect consistent data not only of the  gate length but also of its spread along the width of  the gate, an image processing program was used to  measure the gate length.  The program measured  along each scan line of a high resolution SEM  image and collected these data into a frequency  distribution. Figure 3 is a typical example for the  case presented. Used in this way, the method could  also lend itself to studying transistor gate length  matching.


Word line driver NMOST Gate length distribution

Figure 3. Distributions of gate length in two


perpendicular directions on a single device, suffering from astigmatism in the exposure tool.


Large-area Investigation of ‘Missing’ Silicide


Fleuren and Zhang discussed the application of the  back side method in investigating silicide and  contact integrity. [4].   Further examples where the  ability of the method to look at extended areas  comes into its own, are given below.


A TEM cross section was made of a particular  failing contact on a 0.18 µm CMOS device.  It was  found that titanium silicide had failed to form at  that location (Figure 4).  However, this data alone  was not sufficient to conclude that the root cause  had been found. The question remained whether  lack of silicide automatically led to a failing  contact, or whether other similarly failing contacts  in fact showed the presence of silicide.


One option would have been to make a number of  cross sections of similar failing sites and nonfailing sites, but this would have been labourintensive. Instead back side investigation allowed a  rapid check, by being able to compare known good  and bad sites in terms of the quality of the silicide  under the contact.


Figure 4. TEM cross-section of two contacts. The  top image shows a failing site, with silicide missing  under the contact.  The bottom image shows a nonfailing site for comparison.


Figure 5 and Figure 6 show typical views from the  back side.  Several failing sites could be examined  on each sample, speeding up the analysis.  In this  way it was possible to show that :


1) All the failing contacts were missing silicide


2) While the fraction of all contacts with missing  silicide was constant across the wafer, the  circuit failure rate by this mechanism peaked  strongly towards the wafer centre.


Only little additional effort was thus needed to be  able to conclude that the lack of silicide was a  necessary, but not sufficient condition to explain  the contact failure.  The root cause was ultimately  found in contact formation processing.


Figure 5.  Overview of contacts from the back side.  Known failing contact marked by circle.


Figure 6.  Detail of the area shown in Figure 5.  The bottom of the contact plug is visible because  silicide was not properly formed.


Similarly, in a different analysis, a failing gate was  found by cross-sectional analysis to lack silicide on  top of the polysilicon (Figure 7).  The failure is  similar to that described by Kane et al. [5].  In this  case, a similar back side preparation was made, but  back side grinding was deliberately extended until  the gates were reached from the back.  The TMAH  now removed the remaining polysilicon, but  selectively left the silicided polysilicon areas.   In  this way, a sample was rapidly prepared showing  that no gates could be found with missing silicide  except those of known failing sites (Figure 8).  A  root cause was thereby established.


Figure 7. TEM cross section showing absence of


titanium silicide on polysilicon on the left hand


gate.


Figure 8.  Back side sample processed to reveal  titanium silicide on polysilicon


Investigation of ESD and Gate Oxide Integrity


Similarly, gate oxide damage can be viewed as  shown in  Figure 9.  Here, an ESD (electrostatic  dischrge) tested sample has been prepared from the  back side.  A hole in the gate oxide has allowed the  poly to be removed locally.   The fact that the gate  darkens through the removal of poly allows such  gates to be rapidly  identified even when large  areas need to be searched.  This is not so easily  accomplished when performing deprocessing from  the front side.


Figure 9.  Typical views of a CMOS IC


deprocessed from the back side.  Top : An ESD  event has damaged a gate oxide in the marked gate. Bottom: Detail view, showing damaged gate oxide


As in all engineering disciplines, the choice of the  right tool for a particular failure analysis job can  drastically cut the time and effort needed to reach  the correct root cause conclusion.  This paper has  presented a back side preparation method together  with a range of application examples.  The main  features of the method (namely rapid, uniform  deprocessing over a whole die, and the ability to  examine gate lengths, titanium silicide and contact  footprints) make it an ideal choice for certain  analyses.  It has been demonstrated how the  features of the method led to  analysis results in a  direct way.


I would like to thank Xiao-Mei Zhang, Gidi  Fleuren, Karel Sauer and Gerben Boon for useful  discussions and for their co-operation on the  analyses presented here.

New Techniques for the Identification of Defects in Multi-layer  Flip-Chip Packages


Steve Hsiung, Kevan Tan, Joe Luo


LSI Logic Corporation, Fremont, California


Two novel techniques to identify continuity failures  in multi-layer substrates of flip-chip package are


discussed.  The first technique uses the custom


designed and fabricated Package Substrate Probe


Fixture (PSPF™).  The fixture eliminates the


traditional method of soldering directly to the


package solder balls.  This ensures that failures are  not heat cured and the solder ball as well as the Ball  Grid Array (BGA) pad are not detached from the


package substrate during physical analysis.  Also


employed are beam-based systems that include both  Focused Ion Beam (FIB) and Electron-beam (Ebeam) to detect Capacitive Coupling Voltage


Contrast (CCVC) images.  Voltage contrast imaging  augments traditional optical inspection techniques  using bright and dark field microscopy.


Contrasted to the traditional wire-bonded packages, the silicon die in flip-chip packages is flipped over  and attached to the package substrate using solder  reflow techniques.  LSI Logic, for instance, has


introduced Flip-Chip Plastic Ball Grid Array (FCPBGA) packages with lead counts in excess of 1700 (Figure 1).


Figure 1  Multi-layer flip-chip construction.


The substrate structures in flip-chip packages vary


depending on the substrate vendors.  The substrate


could have as many as eight layers with each layer


being a Vdd, Vss or I/O plane.  With its complex


construction and ever-increasing pincounts (Figure 2) failure analysts face an array of challenges to identify  defects in such multi-layer substrates.


International Technology Roadmap for  Semiconductors 2000


3500


1998 1999 2000 2001 2002 2003 2004 2005 2006


Figure 2  ITRS pincount roadmap for highperformance packages.


In this paper, two novel techniques to identify


continuity failure in multi-layer flip-chip substrate


packages will be introduced.


The custom designed and fabricated Package


Substrate Probe Fixture (PSPF™) is used for


measuring resistance between layers in multi-layer


substrate in flip-chip packages without damaging the  packages’ solder balls.  Two case studies will be


presented.


Capacitive Coupling Voltage Contrast (CCVC) in


beam-based systems is applied to package-level


failure analysis.  CCVC is used to isolate and localize  I/O metal trace defects in multi-layer substrates of


flip-chip packages.  Sample preparation, setup and


case studies will be discussed in both FIB and e-beam  systems.


Package Substrate Probe Fixture


To test substrate continuity between layers in flipchip packages, traditionally a failure analyst must


first remove the silicon die.  Then a probe wire is


soldered to the solder ball of the failing pin.  A


micro-probe needle is then used to measure the


resistance from the solder bump and its associated


solder ball (Figures 3, 4, and 5).


If the resistance measured is defect-level high, then


the top layer of the package substrate is removed (this  entails removing the solder bump pads that lie just


beneath the removed die).  This process is typically


achieved by parallel lap on a mechanical polishing


wheel.  Another resistance probe measurement is


taken.  Again if the resistance measured is high,


another layer is removed and the resistance


measurement taken.  This is repeated on subsequent  layers until the layer having the defect is found.


Package Substrate


Solder ball


Probe point


Underfill material


Solder bump


Signal path


Figure 3  Schematic drawing showing resistance


measurement between a solder bump and its


associated solder ball.


Figure 4 Photograph of a wire being attached to the  solder ball of a flip-chip substrate.


Figure 5  Photography depicting traditional resistance  measurement technique.


However, problems can arise using this testing


method.  When attaching the probe wire with solder, the defect may be heat-cured if it is physically close  to the solder ball.  Furthermore, the subsequent


removal of upper substrate layers by parallel-lap can  put undue tension on the bottom soldered probe wire.  This tension can result in the detachment of both the  solder ball and the BGA pad from the substrate


(Figure 6).


Figure 6  Photograph of a solder ball detached from  the BGA pad due to shear forces created by probe


wire and parallel lapping process.


The least disruptive analysis approach is desired to


test continuity.  Creating a fixture that does not need  heating to form a strong electrical contact between


the probe wire and the solder ball was the best


solution.  Further, a fixture would allow the package  substrate to be easily attached and unattached.  This  would allow for easy manipulation of the substrate


for layer removal steps.  The target solder ball would  then not be subjected to any undue external forces


during the layer removal procedure.


Substrate Probe Fixture (PSPF™) consists of an


adjustable x-y table on the top and a single pogo pin  on the bottom (Figure 7).  The adjustable x-y table


allows the user to drive to the failing solder ball.  The  package substrate is held onto the table with the use  of a sliding push plate that is secured by a


thumbscrew.  The push plate accommodates


substrates of various dimensions (30mm to 60mm).  The pogo pin contains a compressible spring for


making a strong but non-destructive contact to a


single solder ball on the underneath surface of the


substrate (Figure 8).


To use the PSPF™, the package substrate is secured  in the fixture. The analyst drives to the target solder  ball and makes contact with the pogo pin.  Continuity  is checked between the pogo pin and the probe needle  from the top of the substrate.  Resistance is measured  from solder bump pad to its related solder ball


(Figure 9).  Sequential deprocessing steps follow


until the discontinuity layer is determined.


Figure 7  Photograph of PSPF™ with a sample flipchip package substrate loaded.


Figure 8  Photograph of the PSPF™ pogo pin


connected to the targeted solder ball.


Figure 9  Photograph of PSPF™ depicting its typical  usage.  The terminus of the DMM is the pogo pin and  the probe needle from the top of the package


substrate.


Case Study I


Background


A flip-chip package with an 8-layer microlaminate  substrate was received from reliability stress test as  an open electrical reject.  Time Domain


Reflectometry (TDR) analysis showed that pin R8  was open in the substrate (Figure 10).


Figure 10  TDR waveforms that showed pin R8 was  open in the substrate.


With a polishing grinding wheel, the silicon die was  ground down to the underfill-substrate interface to


expose the solder bump pads. Then the substrate was  placed in the PSPF™.  Resistance measurements were  taken first from the solder bump pads of R8 and T9, target and control respectively. Then, solder mask


was removed by plasma etch.  Next, with substrate


drawing provided by the design engineers, layers of  the substrate were sequentially removed by parallellap.  As each layer was removed, the resistance was  measured between the I/O and its associated solder


ball (Table 1).  The first layer was skipped because it


is a power plane.  For the failing pin R8, the


resistance measured was infinite from top layer down  to 6th layer.  In the 7th layer a resistance value in the  range of good continuity was measured.  From these  observations, it was concluded that the open occurred  between 6th and 7th layers of the substrate.


With this finding, a second part that failed the same  pin R8 was cross-sectioned directly along the via


between 6th and 7th layer of the substrate.  Visual


inspection revealed an open via (Figure 11).

Case Study II  Background


Two pins AL23 and AM23 were confirmed to be


open in an 8-layer substrate by TDR (Figure 12 and  Figure 13).


Pin AM23 and AL23 is a daisy chain pair.  A daisy  chain die simply consists of traces needed to


complete a substrate circuit, which allows testing for  package substrate trace electrical continuity.  Pin


AM24 is an adjacent pin used as a control.  After the  silicon die was removed, the part was put on PSPF™


so the probing could be made through the solder


bump pads to the solder balls, which confirmed the  discontinuity.


After the solder mask in substrate was etched, the


substrate was parallel-lapped sequentially.  In each  step, the resistance between the conductor metal and  its associated solder ball was recorded (Table 2).


Figure 12  TDR waveforms characteristic of a


package substrate discontinuity showed for pin


AL23.


Figure 13  TDR waveforms that showed pin AM23  was open in the substrate.

The resistance of AM23 went from 20.9Ω (a resistive  open) to 0.4Ω after the first layer was removed.  The  resistance for AL23 went from infinite to 0.4Ω after  the first layer was removed.  From this it was


concluded the defect was likely in the Via 1 of the


package substrate.  A second unit failing with similar  TDR characteristic waveforms was cross-sectioned


through the Via 1 layer.  A malformed via between  layers 1 and 2 was found (Figure 14).


Figure 14  Photograph of a void in the via between  the 1st layer and 2nd layer.


Voltage Contrast in Beam-based  System


Package-level failure analysis has traditionally been


performed by visual inspection.  Conventional


observation methods for package defects involve


direct bright and dark-field microscope optical


inspection.  The trace length of I/O lines, as long as


9mm, complicates the task of detecting micro-cracks  by optical inspection.  Furthermore, even if a microcrack is observed, its causality is not always certain.     As I/O routing becomes increasingly dense in multilayer substrates in flip-chip packages, a method of


pinpointing defects is needed to augment and


eventually supplant current techniques.  A new


application of beam-based voltage contrast is made


for package substrate defect localization and


isolation.


Focused Ion Beam (FIB) systems have long been


employed for micro-machining and micro-surgery of  silicon die. Using the FIB both as a mill and a metal


trace depositor, an operator can open up desired


layers of a semiconductor device, severing and


jumpering I/O lines as needed.  Another application


of the FIB is to detect voltage contrast (VC).  This


application takes advantage of the surface charging


from the FIB’s impinging positive Gallium ion beam  and the resulting secondary electron emission.


Analogous to FIB voltage contrast imaging is SEM


voltage contrast, where the SEM impinges negatively  charged electrons.  By supplying a signal to a device, another flavor of VC imaging is achieved –


Capacitive Coupling Voltage Contrast (CCVC).  In


an elctron-beam system, an electron beam is rastered


on a device sub circuit; its surface gets charged by  the impinging negatively charged electrons.


As semiconductor device packages move to everincreasing densities and miniaturization, new


approaches to their failure analysis is needed.


Employing beam-based VC to multi-layer, high pincount flip-chip packages is a novel technique that  requires little additional capital spending for a


modern failure analysis lab.


FIB and E-Beam Setup


The Schlumberger AMS-1000 and IDS-5000 are


employed for FIB and e-beam systems, respectively.  A pulse generator is used to provide a square wave


input signal and an oscilloscope to measure the input (Figure 15 and 16).


The sample is mounted in an off-the-shelf load


module.  Once the sample is loaded, the analysts can  focus FIB image with the FIB’s focus tools or SEM  image with the e-beam’s focus tools.  When a sharp  image is achieved, the square waveform from the


waveform generator is input.  Brightness and contrast  are adjusted in the FIB or SEM image tool to acquire  a desired image.


Figure 15  Photograph of e-beam based system set-up  for CCVC imaging.

Figure 16a  Schematic of beam-system, load module, function generator, and oscilloscope.


Load Module DUT

side


Figure 16b   Schematic of load module and DUT  viewed from the side.


To inspect a particular metal trace, the upper layers of  the package substrate need to be removed.  This is


achieved by mechanical grinding on a polishing


wheel.  It is important to stop grinding before the


subject metal trace is completely exposed – this


ensures that a deprocessing artifact does not confuse  the analysts.  Furthermore, a thin layer of organic


material is needed for CCVC imaging.  To


incrementally remove layers of package substrate an  O2 asher may be used.


Case Study III


A flip-chip package with daisy chain die and an 8-


layer microlaminate substrate was received from


production test as an open electrical reject.  TDR


analysis and subsequent PSPF™-aided delayering


showed an open in Layer 2 of the substrate at the I/O  pin AK39 (Figure 17).  Optical inspection of the I/O  trace, compared to the I/O trace schematic, could not  find any anomalies.  Pin AM 37 is an adjacent pin


used as a control (Table 3 and Figures 18 and 19).


AK39 (Ω)


AM37 (Ω)


Table 3  PSPF™ resistance measurements for two I/O  pin AK39 and AM37.


Figure 17  TDR waveforms characteristic of a


package substrate discontinuity of pin AK39.


Figure 18  Substrate drawing of I/O routing for AK39  highlighted in red.


Figure 19  Optical inspection of the I/O trace AK39  did not reveal any anomalies.


The sample was mounted in the FIB system for


CCVC imaging.  A 4kHz 2Vpp square-wave pulse


was input to the failing pin.  Navigating the length of  the I/O trace, VC was noted.  The VC from the


toggling signal stopped abruptly at a location along  the length of the I/O trace (Figure 20).


The sample was loaded into the e-beam system for  CCVC imaging.  A 4kHz 2Vpp square-wave pulse


was input to the failing pin.  The VC from the


toggling input signal was noted to stop at the same


location of the I/O trace (Figure 21).


A micro-crack was observed by optical inspection in  the I/O trace at the location where VC was observed (Figure 22).  The micro-crack was the root cause of  the open failure.

Case Study IV


A 4-layer microlaminate flip-chip substrate was  returned from the field.  The unit failed shorting  between I/O traces.  TDR analysis and subsequent  PSPF™-aided delayering showed an I/O short in  Layer 2 of the package substrate (Figure 22 and  Table 4).


Figure 23  TDR short waveforms of AB38


R AB38-AC37


(Ω)


Table 4  PSPF™ resistance measurements for two I/O  pin AB38 and AC37.


was input to the failing pin.  Driving the length of the  I/O trace, CCVC was noted, the two I/O traces


toggled together (Figure 24).


The sample was then loaded into the e-beam system  for CCVC imaging.  VC was noted and similar to the  FIB CCVC image, both lines toggled together


(Figure 25).


Optical inspection of the I/O trace revealed dendrite  growth at the location where VC was observed


(Figure 26).  The dendrite growth caused the I/O


traces to short.

traces toggle together.


Figure 26  Photograph of dendrites.


Case studies have proven that the Package Substrate  Probe Fixture (PSPF™) is an innovative and more  efficient way to measure resistance in multi-layer


substrates of flip-chip packages. Not only does it


avoid heat-curing or damaging substrate, but it can  also help analysts localize and isolate defects.


Capacitive Coupling Voltage Contrast (CCVC)


imaging in beam-based system augments traditional  inspection techniques.  Moreover, this new


application of beam-based imaging required little


capital investment, instead drew upon equipment


already available in most modern FA labs.
