Session 8:


Case Histories 1


S. R. Sheu • UMC • Hsinchu City, Taiwan


p 253-257


DOI: 10.1361/cp2001istfa253


Shamsul Mohamed, Caroline Francis, Mohd Firoz Ismail, Lam Boon Yew


Advanced Micro Devices


Penang, Malaysia


The widespread usage of portable electronic  equipment had significantly increased the demand of  chip scale packages (CSP). With this rapid growth, the reliability of chip scale packages has become a  significant issue. The root causes of board level  failure whether it is due to solder joint integrity, package/board design parameters, package material  or assembly issues need to be weed–out in the  planning stage itself.


However, due to high demand and rapid change in  board design, suppliers and manufacturers of CSP  components, some of these issues could not be caught  at this stage. Thus, failure analysis becomes one of  the major factors in taking corrective action and  improving productivity in general.


This paper outlines an optimal approach for board  level Chip Scale Package (CSP) failure analysis, where the chip and Printed Circuit Board (PCB) are  analyzed as a single unit to determine the root cause  of board level failures.  A technique using a  combination of cross-section and parallel polishing is  described in detail. This technique was specifically  developed to inspect key aspects of solder joint  fatigue, which are


• Solder joint height


• Pad dimensions on both package and PCB


• Heating profiles or reflow


• Substrate warpage


• Intermetallic compound (IMC) thickness


• Solder joint voids


Failure Analysis Approach


In board level failure analysis, an analyst will face various obstacles such as


• Determination of failure origin


• Accessing the site of failure


• The inability to inspect both the CSP


package and PCB together for failures


related to pad dimensions


• Measurements of pad dimension on CSP


package and PCB (X-section measurements  of non-circular pad will give erroneous


data)


So, in order to define corrective action in limited time  frame, an optimal failure analysis flow is very  essential. The failure analysis approach is a  combination of cross section and parallel polish  technique that allows for inspection of various  aspects of board and package failures in a single  sample preparation. The analysis flow is illustrated in  Figure 1.


Figure1 Flow chart illustrating the failure analysis  matrix developed for CSP board level failure.


The process of determining failure site starts by  tracing the overall loop of electrical connectivity on  the CSP package and PCB. The resistance value was  then measured from various test point and  comparison was made to good boards. Once the  electrical isolation completed, package and board  deprocessing process will be carried out to detect the  existence of substrate and interconnect crack.


Initial analysis using CRT X-ray system offer better  images of solder joint failure before the destructive  analysis is conducted. The images collected from the  x-ray can predetermine an anomaly such as distortion  and misalignment occurred on the solder joint. With  additional application of a "pseudo-laminography" tool, clearer images of abnormality will be detected  such as in Figure 2.


(a)                                    (b)


Figure 2  X-ray radiography of solder joint defects. A  sample was parallel polished to allow for solder joint  and package alignment inspection. (a) Photo of solder


joint distortion/abnormal diameter. (b) Photo of


solder joint misalignment at PCB pad


The sample was then cross-sectioned at the failure  site. The purpose of this process is to inspect whether  the solder joint fatigue failure occurred at CSP  package or PCB side. In addition, this will give a 3D  view of the failure site (Refer to Figure 3, 4 and 5).


Figure 3  The 3D view of failure site. Arrows point at  a defective solder joint that occurred at CSP package  side.


Figure 4  Front view of the defect observed on failing  sample in Figure 3. Solder joint crack can be


observed at the failing solder joint.


Figure 5  Inadequate solder coverage was observed at  PCB pad of the failing solder joint.


Samples prepared using the failure analysis matrix  above would give a clear indication of the nature of  solder joint failures. Further detailed investigation  can then be carried out with the same sample.   This  includes


• Intermetallic compound (IMC) thickness  measurement


• Measurements of package parameters such  as pad size, solder joint height, that  contribute to solder joint fatigue


Based on the previous investigations, package pull  test, solder joint shear test and dye penetration test  can then be carried out to determine the root cause of  failure.


Experimental


Fine pitch ball grid array (FBGA) package was  selected for this analysis. Samples of printed circuit  board (PCB - double sided) with FBGA package  assembled on it were tested for temperature cycling  stress (Refer to Figure 6). All PCB were FR4 board  and consist of 8 layers of metal with laser-drilled via  and non-solder mask defined (NSMD) copper pad.


Prior to temperature cycling, the test boards were  thermal conditioned and humidity conditioned to  stimulate handling and storage prior to use. All  boards were subjected to two-chamber temperature  cycle test, with 10 seconds transition time and one  hour cycles time and the boards were tested every  250 cycles.


Figure 6  Overview of the FBGA samples mounted  on printed circuit board.


Experimental Results


The failures were observed after 750 cycles and 1000  cycles read point. The result from electrical fault  isolation showed the failures were originated from  solder joint


External inspection on all failing boards did not  observed any mechanical or gross damages. For two


(2) of the failing boards, the FBGA were sheared off  from the board. All solder balls broke at the package  side (Refer to Figure 7), thus showing good wetting  and IMC bonding at board side. The failure was not  induced by insufficient heat during reflow that could  cause single drop or cold solder joint.


Figure 7  The footprint of the PCB showing all the  solder balls together with copper pad being ripped off


from the FBGA, indicating good IMC bonding.


Consequently, based on electrical isolation, x-ray  parallel polish and cross-section analysis, the  following observation could be made which are  illustrated in Figure 8, 9 and 10.


• The failing solder balls are non-symmetrical  in shape


• All the solder joint crack occurred at the  FBGA package side


• The crack propagation occurred at the bulk  solder region, not at the intermetallic  compound (IMC) layer.


• Lower than expected standoff height of the  solder joint


Normally, for solder joint failure caused by  coefficient of thermal expansion (CTE) mismatch, two cracks location will occur. The initial begins at  the upper right hand corner and propagates near the  interface between the CSP package and the solder  joint. The secondary crack could initiates (later) at  the lower left hand corner and propagate near the  interface between the PCB and the solder joint.


In addition, the location of maximum stress and strain  hysteresis responses (i.e due to thermal cycling  stress) basically at the corner solder joint. However, for this experiment neither the characteristic was  observed, thus eliminating the CTE factor in root  cause determination.


Figure 8 X-ray micrograph showing solder ball


distortions with no formation of severe voids.


Figure 9 Cross-section analysis detected nonsymmetrical shape of the failing solder balls, lower


than anticipated standoff height and larger solder


mask clearance on PCB.


The thermal fatigue failure of the solder joint is also a  function of solder material, metallization on  package/board and formation of intermetallic  compound at the joint interface. Fatigue crack  generally initiated at bonded edges and may  propagate along the interface or curve into the solder. Hence, in many cases the excessive IMC thickness  may lead to early than anticipated solder joint failure.  Although the analysis result on all the failing samples  showed that the crack occurred at bulk solder ( Refer  to Figure 10), further investigation was then carried  out to inspect the intermetallic region. Besides  investigating the thickness of intermetallic region, the  standoff height of the solder joint and solder mask  clearance were also taken into consideration. This  will provide an additional data to improve the overall  reliability of solder joint.


Figure 10  SEM micrograph showing the cracks


propagation along bulk solder. The IMC layer


consists of gold (Au), Nickel (Ni) and Tin (Sn)


elements.


The IMC analysis and measurement were focus on  AuSnNi layer since in previous experiments  excessive thickness of the layer lead to crack  propagation at the IMC region thus causing a massive  failure. The result from this experiment however  indicating the AuSnNi layer on all the failing samples  was only reached the maximum thickness of 2.5um at  1000 cycles T/C stress (Refer to Figure 11).


Figure 11  IMC measurement on failing samples


(Case A) showed low thickness of AuSnNi as


compared to previous experiment (Case B and C) The failing boards were also parallel polished in  order to obtain various dimension such as solder  mask opening, copper pad diameter, solder mask  design clearance and copper trace width.


Besides providing the measurement data, parallel  polish analysis showed the following characteristics


• The solder mask opening on the PCB was  found to be larger than expected, thus  causing  in larger clearance between the pad  and solder mask for the FBGA package (Refer to Figure 12)


• Solder mask misregistration was observed  at the failing FBGA location (Refer to  Figure 13).


Figure 12  SEM micrograph showing the 3D


overview of the failing site after combination of cross  section and parallel polishing technique. The solder


mask opening on the PCB was found to be larger  than expected.


Figure 13  Top view photo of parallel polished and xsectioned board. Solder mask misregistration can be  observed on a failing board.


Discussion of Findings


Based on cross section and parallel polishing  analysis, it can be seen that the area wetted on the  board side is much larger than on the package side. This difference in the attachment area significantly  reduces standoff height of the solder ball and will  adversely affect the solder joint fatigue life. All these  were contributed by printed circuit board design that  is not optimized for solder ball with low diameter (<0.4mm).


For all the solder joint failures, the crack propagation  occurred at the bulk solder region. Therefore, it is  basically indicated that the solder ball does not facing  an issue regarding the bonding strength of IMC layer.


The major cause of solder joint cracking during  thermal cycling stress is due to temperature change. This will cause strains in the solder joint due to  thermal expansion mismatches between various  materials resulting in fatigue of solder. In this  retrospect, package properties, the design of solder  pads play the major role in determining how the  fatigue behavior of solder joints will affect CSP  component. Additional factors like nickel/gold and  nickel palladium finishes has been found to be more  brittle and promote solder joint cracking.


The failure analysis approach as described in the  experiment allow investigation of the above factors in  a single sample preparation and readily arrive at the  root cause solution in the minimum time.


The authors would like to thank Rodzaki Saad (AMD  Penang), LK Suresh, Viswanath Valluri, Ranjit  Gannamani, Dhiraj Bansal, Steve Sue and  S.Gowrishankar of AMD Sunnyvale, California in  the co-development of the technique and for  providing technical advice.

3. Flip Chip and Chip Scale Package Reliability  Modelling: Dr. Jean-Paul Clech, Chip Scale  Review - November 1998, pp 1-10


4. PBGA Package: Brian Sloth Bentzen, SMT In  Focus - November 2000, pp 1-6


5. Substrate Requirement For Chip Scale Packaging: Dr. Rao Mahidhara, Chip Scale Review - January 1999, pp 1-9

IC failure by electrical overstress (EOS)


Fan Wang, Hongxue Zhang, Petri Ronkainen, and Pasi Välimäki


Nokia Networks, IP Mobility Networks,


Kaapelitie 4, 90630 Oulu, Finland


In this paper, an IC failure case by EOS is presented. The optical microscopy, SAM, and X-ray were used  for the non-destructive analysis. The decapsulation, SEM, FIB were used for the destructive analysis. It  was found that IC’s presented in this case were  electrically overstressed (EOS), which caused the  uncontrollable overheating. The EOS symptoms can be  various, such as electromigration, intermetallic  compound formation, delamination on die surface, circuit track damages, and wire bonding broken. The  latch-up testing and deprocess technique were used to  simulate the failures and it was found that the failures  in this case was due to latch-up. The results show that  failure symptoms of EOS are various and their  identifications require different failure analysis  techniques and tools.


According to the field return data analysis in the  semiconductor IC industry, it is well documented that  the failure caused by the electrical overstress (EOS) damage can amount to 25 % [1].  EOS is a wide  spectrum electrical characteristic of stress events, which also includes transients on systems, electromagnetic pulses, and lightning. The electrostatic  discharge (known as ESD) is just a subset of EOS [2]. For IC, EOS events are commonly associated with the  overvoltage and overcurrent, which subjected in IC  life-time. The EOS generally refers to events that  encompass time scale in the microsecond and  millisecond ranges. Whereas the ESD events are  commonly in the time scale of 100 ns range. There are  no qualitative or quantitative EOS standards. According to the published work, EOS-related failures  in semiconductor devices can be classified, according  to their primary failure mechanisms, into thermally  induced failures, electromigration, and electric-fieldrelated failures [3].


The electrical overstress also can cause the  intermetallic formation with the under layers. This  phenomena were also observed in this case, showed in  Figure 8. The phase reaction of alloys can be observed  by the bonding pad appearance and the contrast  changes. The EDS (energy dispersive spectroscopy) element analysis results were used to confirm the  intermetallic formation. Figure 9 showed the spectrum  of the marked points in Figure 8. In Figure 9(a) (corresponding to the spectrum 1 in Figure 8), the  element Au was found to exist in this point, which  indicates the diffusion and the reaction between the  bonding wire Au and the bonding pad material. In  Figure 9(b) (for spectrum 2), the Au element was also  detected. The element Ag came from the bonding pad  plating layer.


Figure 7 The SEM image of wire bonding pad showing  the symptom of electromigration.


Figure 8 SEM image of the damaged bonding pad with  the EDS analysis points marked.


Spectrum 2


Spectrum 1

Figure 9 The EDS spectrums for the marked points in Figure 8. (a) is for spectrum 1 and (b) is for spectrum 2.


In the areas near the bonding pads, the damages of


circuits were clearly observed (Figure 10).


Figure 10 Damaged circuit on die surface.


Focused ion beam (FIB) was used to make further


investigations on these damaged areas. The passivation


in the interested areas (Figure 10) was removed by FIB


and the metal interconnects under the passivation was


revealed. It is clear that the interconnects have been  damaged (Figure 11). Figure 12 showed the crosssection of another damaged area where metal  interconnects have been damaged too.


Figure 11 Cross-section picture by FIB for the


damaged in Figure 10.

Figure 12 Passivation layer removing by FIB, showing  the damaged metal track.


To simulate the failure mechanism, the ESD (HBM) and latch-up testing were carried out for the new  functional samples. For ESD testing, the HBM pulses  were stressed to the certain pins (pin 1-6), and at the  same time, the curve tracer was used to observe the I/V  characteristics. It was found the devices were failed at  the voltage from 8 kV to 14 kV. For the latch-up  testing (at room temperature), it is showed that the  overvoltage about 18 v to induce the latch up.


All the testing samples were examined by SAM. Figure  13 showed the SAM picture. No. 1-3 samples were  carried out for the ESD testing, and sample No.4 was  for the latch-up testing. It was observed that the ESD  testing samples (No. 1-3) were normal from SAM  examination. But the latch-up testing sample (No. 4) showed the delamination in the testing pins area.


Figure 13 SAM picture for the ESD testing (No. 1-3) and the latch-up testing (No. 4) samples.


The No. 4 sample was decapsulated using the same  method as described above. The opened package was  observed that the encapsulant plastic could not be  etched off completely, which is the same symptom as  the failed samples (Figure 14).


Figure 14 SEM picture for the decapsulated sample  No. 4. The plastic can not be etched off completely. This sample was also deprocessed by concentrated  hydrogen fluoride acid (49% HF) for about 1.5  minutes. Figure 15 showed the SEM picture of  delayered circuit at the same location of Figure 14. The  picture showed the server damages of the underlayer  circuit. It is the typical lateral overstress damage, caused by latch-up.


Figure 15 The SEM picture of the delayered sample  No. 4, at the same location as Figure 14.


The same failure symptoms in failed samples can be  repeated for more latch-up testing. It gave more  evidences that the EOS in this case caused by latch up.


It is concluded that the IC’s presented in this case were  electrically overstressed (EOS), which caused the  uncontrollable overheating. The persistent overheating  resulted in the acceleration of electromigration, the


90 μm


formation of intermetallic compounds and also  delamination on the die surface. All these in return  caused the circuit track damage, and bonding wire  broken. From this case study, it is showed that failure  symptoms of EOS are various and their identifications  require different failure analysis techniques and tools.

1. Electrostatic discharge in semiconductor  devices: an overview: J. E. Vinson and J. J. Liou, Proceeding of the IEEE, 86, 399-418 (1998)


2. ESD: a pervasive reliability concern for IC  technologies: C. Duvvury and A. Amerasekera, Proceeding of the IEEE, 81, 690-702 (1993)

p 265-271


DOI: 10.1361/cp2001istfa265


James E. Vinson, Ph.D.


Intersil Corporation


Electrical overstress (EOS) is a common failure cause  for many of the electronic circuits today. The Failure Analyst has no difficulty identifying EOS as the cause of the failure. The difficulty comes from determining the source of the EOS event so it can be  eliminated. This paper describes two case studies looking a gross EOS damage and very mild EOS damage. Close cooperation between the customer and  vendor was required to determine the source of the EOS events.


Electrical Overstress (EOS) is a failure cause where excessive electrical stimulus (current or voltage) is applied to a unit resulting in failure.  The stimulus comes in the form of operator error or as a result of the environment where the part is used. An example of an operator error is a part being plugged into a socket backwards reversing the power and ground pins. This condition applies a forward bias to the isolation diodes causing large currents to flow. The result is a fused bond wire and damaged die metal.


An environmental EOS example is electrostatic discharge (ESD). This type of event is caused by charge buildup in or around the test device. The charge is then discharged through the device causing damage.


The types of damage observed from EOS are characterized by 5 damage mechanisms: thin film fusing, filamentation, junction spiking, charge injection, and dielectric rupture. [1, 2] The first three are current induced damage mechanisms and the last two are voltage induced damage mechanisms. One or  more of these damage mechanisms may be active in a  failure. The current induced damage mechanisms are illustrated in Figure 1. The voltage induced damage mechanisms are illustrated in Figure 2. It is important  to understand these failure mechanisms and also know whether current or voltage is the source of the damage observed.


EOS, by nature, is an event that occurs at a point in time. An event takes place triggering the failure. This  type of failure is different from a wearout mechanism  that degrades with time. Wearout mechanisms have an activation energy associated with them because a degradation mechanism is taking place over time.


This is usually a chemical process. EOS events are


not associated with an activation energy. These events are probabilistic in nature.


EOS damage in most cases is very distinctive in its appearance. It usually is determined from a visual inspection of the device. A typical optical view of EOS damage is shown in Figure 3 and 4. Clear evidence of excessive current is shown. Metal lines have been fused open and device junctions shorted.


This type of damage is produced from a high current pulse. The main cause of damage is the Joule heating  in the structure. The flow of current from the EOS event dissipates energy along its path. The energy causes a temperature rise based on the thermal mass and thermal conductivity of the structure. Eventually the melting points of the materials are exceeded resulting in the damage observed.


Figure 1. Current induced damage mechanisms.


In contrast, wearout related mechanisms do not show this type of damage. In most cases the result of a wearout or infant mortality failure cannot be seen until the failing element is located and its layers removed to expose the failure site. An infant mortality failure is similar to a wearout related failure  but the time to failure is shortened because of a manufacturing related defect. The defect allows that device to fail earlier. In some wearout related cases,such as ionic contamination of the sample, no observable damage is found on the die. The charge state of the oxide in a transistor is the only thing that  is affected.


Figure 2. Voltage induced damage mechanisms.


Figure 3. Extensive EOS Damage Example.


The extent of the EOS damage varies over a wide range. Figure 3 shows extensive EOS damage. In contrast, Figure 4 shows very mild EOS damage. In Figure 4 only a single transistor was damaged.  The same source of the event can cause both of these failures. The more severe had more energy available to cause damage. This can come from the event lasting longer or having a higher magnitude. It is difficult to determine the source of an EOS event and  put safeguards in place to prevent its reoccurrence.


Locating and correcting the source of an EOS event is the most difficult task when implementing corrective action for a failure. This is further complicated if the event does not occur on a regular basis. It is much easier to track down an event that affects multiple lots or occurs on a periodic basis.


Many times the failure analyst does not have the luxury of periodic EOS failures. There are times that  a single part is subjected to an EOS event and the source must be identified. Efforts to determine the source of these low occurrence failures are hampered  because of cost. Their randomness and low frequency  of occurrence require large sample sizes for experiments to identify the causes. The added cost may prevent an accurate determination of the failure source. A supplier may learn to live with the yield loss because it is more economical not to fix the EOS  anomaly.


Figure 4. Mild EOS Damage Example.


This paper illustrates the failure analysis and subsequent work necessary to identify a root cause for the EOS damage observed on two operational amplifiers (op-amps). The first op-amp, OP1, has a massive EOS event that caused significant destruction of the circuit. The second case is an opamp, OP2, where the only element damaged is a thin film resistor and the only visual damage is a change in color of the affected resistor. These two extremes illustrate the steps and involvement necessary from the customer and vendor to arrive at the source of the  EOS events.


Electrical Signature


Failure analysis is necessary to correctly identify and classify a failure as electrical overstress. The first bit of evidence comes from the electrical test data taken to determine that the part is an electrical reject. This data is taken in the form of a set of parametric test results and a set of functional tests. The results typically contain DC measurements of key parameters including input leakage current and supply current. Additional tests may include continuity to determine if a pin is open or shorted.


This data is the first clue that a problem has occurred  in the device. A pin with extremely low or high input  impedance is a possible sign of EOS. This type of results possibly indicates a shorted device or a fused conductive trace respectively. Both are possible evidence of an EOS event.


The two op-amps in question showed a clear sign of EOS. They both had one or more pins that were electrically open. These pins failed continuity. The continuity test was the first sign that the part failed.


In addition to this signature, OP1 showed other pins with degraded traces when compared to a good unit.


Neither op-amp passed functional testing.


Comparison between a failing and passing unit is a good method to locate the failure site(s).


Visual Evidence


Once the electrical data is obtained and analyzed it is  time to open the package and do visual inspections of  the failed device. Figures 5 and 6 show the observed damage on the two op-amps.


Figure 5. Massive EOS Damaged Op-amp, OP1.


Figure 5 shows the numerous damage sites caused by the massive EOS event. Much of the damage is associated with the output, negative input, and the positive supply rail. EOS damage produces the


distinctive melted and burned metal traces. This is caused by the large amounts of energy dissipated in the circuit by the event. Joule heating melts the metal  allowing it to fuse as is seen.


Figure 6. Mild EOS damaged op-amp, OP2.


Figure 6 shows none of the classical signs of EOS.


There is no disturbed metal traces nor any burn marks. The only noticeable evidence is the suspected  resistor has a slightly different color. At this point EOS may not be the primary choice for a possible failure cause. The NiCr resistor could be oxidized or chemically corroded. The only problem with this theory is the lack of byproducts from corrosion and the lack of any anomaly in the passivating oxide to allow egress to the resistor. Resistors in the same location do not show signs of discoloration. It is unlikely that a wafer fabrication defect would affect a  single resistor on a die.


Cause of Failure


The major cause of failure for both parts was the continuity reject. For OP1 the continuity-open failure  was traced to a fused metal line on the output as shown in Figure 7. This also accounted for the functional failure. Since the output was electrically open no signal could be transmitted to the package pin. A complete section of the line was fused open.


This requires a large amount of energy to melt and vaporize this much metal. The section of metal fused occupies a volume of 8.4E-9 cm3. The amount of energy necessary to raise this volume of aluminum from room temperature to the vaporization point is 245μJ. [3] For comparison purposes, a 2000 volt ESD event contains 200μJ of energy. During the event most of this energy is dissipated in the 1500-ohm series resistance of the source so only about 2% (4μJ) of the energy reaches the part.


Op-amp OP2 failed because of the open resistor shown in Figure 6. This resistor was connected to the  negative input. When this resistor became open the feedback path was severed and caused the output to


swing high and stay high no matter what the input signal was. Probing on the opposite side of the resistor shown a normal trace indicating that the remaining elements in the input path were not damaged by the event.


Figure 7. Fused output line.


Knowing what caused the circuit to fail is not enough. The analyst must trace down the root cause.


In this case the source of the EOS event must be determined. This task can prove to be difficult. The next section describes an approach to determining the  source of the EOS event.


Monster Hunt


Know Your Enemy


EOS events produce damage from excess voltage or current. Figures 1 and 2 show the five damage mechanisms caused by EOS. It is important to modify ones thinking when considering EOS events and the damage they produce. Assumptions about what are and are not significant impedances are no longer valid. Currents can be in the multiple amperes  and voltage in the 10’s to 100’s of volts. These conditions may exist only a short time (<milliseconds) but they can have serious consequences.


The current induced damage mechanisms shown in Figure 1 are the most prevalent among EOS damage.


Metal lines are fused and melted, junctions are damage, and many times the metal from a contact migrates into the silicon shorting out the junction.


The first of these damage mechanisms, thin film fusing, has the ability to do a lot of secondary damage caused by the sudden interruption of the flowing current. The interruption induces a large voltage pulse. This voltage is caused by the EOS source inductance.


The EOS current is flowing through this source inductance. Just like any inductor it does not want the  current to stop flowing. In many cases this current is


in excess of an ampere. Fusing events take place very  rapidly. The time to open the metal line once fusing threshold is reach can be very short. If the metal line fuses in 0.1 μs, the source inductance is 10 μH, and the line is carrying 1A of current the induced voltage  will be 100 volts. This voltage is a decaying sine wave. The voltage will increase with decreasing fuse time and increasing inductance. For very fast events,peak voltages in excess of 500 volts have been measured.


This voltage pulse produces secondary damage by creating additional breakdown paths where the EOS current can flow. These additional paths may not be able to carry the large current. They can also rupture gate oxides and other dielectrics creating permanent shorts on chip. Where there is an opened metal line one must look for the secondary damage caused by the voltage pulse created after fusing occurred. The large currents flowing in an EOS event may also give  rise to large generated voltages based on the series resistance. A 2-ohms trace carrying 5 amps will produce an additional 10 volts of generated voltage.


Monster Identification


The first step in identifying the EOS Monster is to get  as much information from the person that experienced the failure. The types of information needed are the circuit and board configuration used when the failure occurred, operational conditions during stress, last time the part was known good, and  the events that have transpired from that last know good point. The schematic should show the failing component in the circuit and its relationship to the other components and the outside world. Good cooperation between the analyst and the customer is a  must. There cannot be “finger pointing” if the cause of the EOS event is to be found.


OP1 was configured as a unity gain non-inverting opamp with its output going to a cable harness off the board. In a unity gain non-inverting configuration the  output is directly tied to the negative input. The signal input to the op-amp comes directly to the positive input of the op-amp and is contained on the board.


The next step in the analysis procedure is to look for patterns in the damage observed on the die. The patterns should be based on the circuit and boards schematic obtained from the customer or design group. When looking at these schematics it should be  noted that pins connected through an impedance is less likely to be the source of EOS. The impedance will impede the current flow and provide protection.


Pins that art directly connected are the ones most likely to have damage.


A partial schematic for OP1 is shown in Figure 8.


Based on this damage it is believed that the initial current path was caused by a positive voltage with


respect to V+ being applied to the output pin. This is based on the observation that the output metal line was fused and the current appears to flow up to the positive supply rail through Q75. The current traveled from the output bond pad through the aluminum interconnect to the emitter of Q70 (the large output transistor). The base-emitter junction of Q70 was in breakdown during the event. This is the large output transistor and could handle the current pulse better than Q75. The current then traveled from  the base to collector of Q70 in forward bias conduction to the base of Q75. The base-emitter junction of Q75 was found shorted by aluminum.


This is a smaller transistor and could not dissipate the  large energy without shorting.  A large current flowed in this junction to case filamentation/junction spiking of the base-emitter diode. Once the current reached a critical level the output metal line to the bond pad fused. A large section of the line was blown  away as shown in Figure 7. To blow this large of a section of metal indicates a very large current pulse and a very short duration. It was estimated that the current needed to be between 1 and 2 amperes to cause this level of damage.


When the output metal line fused open the current would quickly decrease to zero. The parasitic inductance in the EOS source causes the output voltage to increase very rapidly and to very high levels. Because the op-amp was designed for unity gain operation, this voltage is felt at the negative input, Pin 2. It is believed that this higher voltage pulse caused the damaged observed around Pin 2(Negative Input) and in the elements associated with Pin 2 as shown in Figure 5. The breakdown path is shown in Figure 8 starting with the IN- Pin.


Figure 8. Damaged areas of OP1.


OP2 had less damage to consider making the possible  scenarios more difficult to determine. The only thing present in OP2 was the open NiCr resistor. Color change in the NiCr resistor indicates that the chromium was oxided leaving a higher resistance


film. Electrical testing showed that the other elements  connected to the NiCr resistor were functioning correctly. The EOS event must have entered the negative input pin. The lowest breakdown path was to the negative supply rail so this is the path the EOS  current traveled. No damage was seen in metal line or  other elements so a limited amount of energy was present in this event. Additionally, the event was very  fast in duration and rise time. A slower pulse would blow the NiCr resistor in the center of the resistor rather than damaging the entire area.


Experimentation


The next stage of the hunt is experimentation. This stage develops an assumption about the type of event  that causes the damage. Estimates of the event include voltage or current levels, energy delivered to the circuit as well as the duration of the event. Once a  set of conditions is assumed the proposed event is applied to a good unit and the resulting damage is compared to the failure. Modification of the event parameters is done and the event applied to another unit until the damage signature of the failure is matched. A close match does not imply that the event  was just like the one used in the experiment but it does provide an estimate of the general requirements of the event. Many types of events can produce similar results.


OP1 required a large energy pulse to cause the massive amount of damage done. In addition, some level of inductance was needed to cause the voltage spike that damaged the negative input. It was assumed that the inductance in a test lead was sufficient to cause the voltage spike so no additional inductance was placed in the stress circuit. The voltage level for the stress was placed at 25 volts.


This voltage level is just above the breakdown voltage of the output to the positive supply rail. The part was wired in a non-inverting unity gain configuration just like it was on the board. The output  was momentarily contacted with the test lead. A Tektronix curve tracer was used to supply the voltage  pulse through a 3-foot test lead. The pulse duration was measured to be between 10 and 50 milliseconds.


The lead inductance was measured to be 2μH. The part did not fail in the manner observed. The only damage observed was in the transistors. Two of the transistors in the current path were shorted.


Insufficient current flowed to cause the output metal to fuse open. A second attempt was made with the voltage set to 350 volts and with a series resistor to limit the peak current to 2.5 amps. The damage observed with this set of conditions was similar to the  failure in that the same areas of the circuit were damage. The difference was in the level of damage.


This unit has more severe damage than observed in the failure. A reduction in the voltage level or a higher series resistance would decrease the amount of


energy deposited into the op-amp and reduce the amount of damage closer to that observed on the failure. No further experimentation was necessary.


This experiment showed that a high voltage pulse on the output pin could damage the op-amp. The current  flowing in the output to V+ rail would fuse the output  metal line causing a high voltage transient on the output. The unity gain configuration would feed this pulse to the negative input breaking down the input transistor causing secondary damage to input stage.


With this information the customer was able to determine the probable cause of the failure. The test station where the parts were discovered as EOS failure had an ungrounded test cable that could charge to significant voltages. When the board was connected to this cable the cable would discharge into the board damaging the components on the board  including this op-amp.


OP2 was more difficult to determine the source of its  EOS event. The first step in evaluating the EOS failure was to simply apply a constant voltage to the negative input and ramp this voltage until the input resistor fused open. The lowest voltage level that would produce a failure was found to be a positive voltage on the negative input. The resistor fused at about 17 volts. The appearance was different from the failed resistor. A distinctive line was present in the center of the resistor showing the location of the open. The failing resistor was completely blown and showed no line in the center. This result showed two things: 1) more energy was needed to completely fuse the resistor and 2) the energy must be delivered very quickly so the entire resistor heated adiabaticly.


In this paper, we presented one case study concerning  such failures.


Non-destructive analysis


The failed IC was packaged in PQFP (Plastic Quad  Flatpack) with 44 I/O, which have totally lost the  functions. The failed IC was visually examined first  and the symptom of overheating (brown color) was  observed on the package surface (Figure 1).


Figure 1 External visual picture of the packages.


Scanning acoustic microscopy (SAM) examination was  followed and it has been noticed that the package  contains two dies and the delamination happened on  the interfaces for the both of the dies (as shown in  Figure 2). It was also observed that most packages  showed a black area in the right-up of the packages (corresponding to pin 1-6) under SAM, which is  corresponded to the colored area (trace of overheating) on the package surface. The size of the darkened areas  under SAM is roughly related to the extent of coloring  on the package surface: the more serious the coloring  on package surface, the bigger the black area is. This  result has shown the fact that the properties of  encapsulant material have been changed due to over  heating.


Figure 2 The SAM picture of the packages.


The failed samples were also checked by X-ray  microscopy. Figure 3 showed the delamination above  the die surface, which consistent with the SAM result. Figure 4 showed the wire bondings were broken.


Figure 3 The X-ray picture showing the delamination  above the die surface.


Figure 4 X-ray picture showing the wire bonding


broken.


Destructive analysis


The plastic packages were opened with the mixed  sulfuric/nitric acids in a volume ratio 1:2. Etching  temperature was 65 °C and etching time was about 2  minutes. From Figure 5, it was observed that the  seriously colored areas (or black areas in SAM picture  Figure 2) were unable to be opened, which indicates  the heavy carbonization of encapsulant polymers.


Figure 5 The picture of the opened packages.


The carbonized encapsulant is the typical evidence of  EOS damage. Scanning Electron Microscopy (SEM) examinations were also carried out for the opened  packages to investigate more detailed symptoms of  EOS damage.


Figure 6(a) and (b) showed the ball bonding wires  cracking, deformation and broken, which indicates the  displacements of wires relative to the die during  operations. This relative displacement is mainly due to  the thermal expansion with delaminated interfaces.


80 μm


Figure 6 Bonding wires were deformed and broken.


Figure 7(a) showed the SEM picture of one wire  bonding. A zigzag pattern can be observed on the wirebonding pad. Figure 7(b) was the higher magnification  image of the pattern in Figure 7(a). The path was ended  by a small hole. This is the typical sign attributed to  electromigration, which occurs in high current  circuitry.


A similar experiment was tried to that used for OP1.


A curve tracer was connected to deliver the pulse via  a probe lead. This level of pulse was still too slow to  provide adiabatic heating of the resistor. Further work was tried using a transmission line pulse (TLP)tester. This piece of test equipment produces a current pulse with a very fast rise time (< 2ns) and a variable pulse width. The tester is based on charging a length of coaxial cable to a defined voltage level and the allowing this cable to discharge into a DUT.


The current pulse produced is rectangular in shape with a pulse width proportional to the length of the cable used to produce it. An exact match was found when the cable was charged to 250 volts. This produced a peak current of 0.5 amps. The resistor fused in 55ns.


This result does not imply that a cable assembly damaged the part. What is does imply is a very fast pulse with a fast rise time and about 0.5 amperes of current was required to produce this damage. Further  work by the customer tracked one possible cause to a  checkout bench used to test boards. An oscilloscope was present on this bench in close proximity to the boards being tested. It was found that a high electric


field was being radiated from the cathode ray tube(CRT) of the oscilloscope. The CRT was inducing a charge on the parts. When test instruments were touched to the boards a discharge occurred. It is important to remove charge generation sources away from areas where parts are being handled. [4] These include CRT’s for computer monitors and test equipment as well as insulators like cups, paper,synthetic clothes, and plastics. All of these can charge and induce a charge on the parts creating an EOS event.


Obstacles to Identification


There are a number of obstacles that must be overcome to be successful in determine the source of EOS events. The key thing to remember is that EOS events are extremes in voltage and current. A part will respond differently to this type of stimulus. The analyst must change the way they think about the part  to see what areas will break down and allow conduction that would normally not be present.


The next obstacle is not pointing fingers at each other  and trying to assign blame without knowing what cause the damage. It is easy for a vendor to tell the customer it is their fault for overstressing the parts.


The customer may state that they have all the safeguards in place and could not have overstressed the parts. Neither of these arguments gets to the root cause. Customer and vendor must work together to determine what occurred and correct the problem.


The real root cause may be a combination of part sensitivity coupled with a procedure used by the customer. Both parties are required to correct this type of flaw.


Another common problem is looking for the wrong cause. This is related to assigning blame described above. A customer may not be willing to accept that they produced an EOS event and looks at the vendor for a manufacturing related problem. The customer looks at their process flow and sees all controls in place and no signs of a problem. They do not understand EOS events are random in nature and may  not be easily found.


The last obstacle is also related: the lack of information. Sometimes the failure analyst just does not know enough about the part that was damaged to  understand how it operates and what internal dependences are present. This is where a relationship  with the vendor is required. The vendor knows the most about the part and the customer knows the most  about how the part is being used. Both parties are required to share information to reach a satisfactory conclusion.


A failure analyst does not have to go on a monster hunt to determine the root cause of an EOS failure


but it can be time-consuming process. It requires an understanding of the damage mechanisms caused by EOS and what types of events generate the observed damage. This is coupled with a close relationship between vendor and customer to share information about the part and the system where the failure was found. Experimentation is used to determine the class  of events that produced the damage. From these results, one can track down the root cause of an EOS  failure.

[2] J. E. Vinson and J. J. Liou, Proceedings of the IEEE, Vol. 88, No. 12, 1878-1900 (2000)


[3] J. E. Vinson, Proceedings of the 24th International Symposium for Testing and Failure Analysis,  (1998)


[4] Kirk, W. J. 1993 EOS/ESD Tutorial Notes, B1


- B23 (1993)


Session 9:


K. N. Hooghan • Lucent Technologies. Allentown, Pennsylvania


p 275-280


DOI: 10.1361/cp2001istfa275


Q. S. Wanga, C.-C. Tsaoa, J. Fernandeza, E. Deleniab, and T. Lundquista


a: Schlumberger Probe Systems, San Jose, California b: IBM, Almanden, California


Results of experimental studies are presented which address a concern that gallium staining from FIB imaging during backside editing might degrade IR navigation as well as signal acquisition during probing of flip chips by such techniques as Picosecond Imaging Circuit Analysis (PICA) and Laser Voltage Probing(LVP). Although optical transparency does depend on gallium implantation dose, Ga staining is, however, not necessarily a limitation to the implementation of photon optical tools in the debug laboratory. Comparisons are made on the results from devices under the following conditions: with and without an anti-reflective (AR)coating, with and without XeF2 enhancement during FIB etching, and with confocal laser scanning microscope (CLSM) imaging and CCD-based IR microscope imaging.


1. Introduction


The advent of flip-chip packaging for complex ICs has required the design debug strategy to employ elegant photon optical tools such as an IR-CLSM. IR is required because the heavily doped silicon used for most IC devices is not transparent to shorter wavelength photons [1,2,3]. Furthermore, the value of FIB (focused ion beam) edits to the design debug strategy has been extended to these flip chip devices [4, 5].  After the FIB editing of a flip chip device the question necessarily arises, “Does the IR image still provide adequate navigation and edit placement capability?”  If Ga+implantation results in decreased transparency, either because of damage caused by the high energy impact of the Ga+ beam (~30 keV) or because of optical property changes due to the quantity of p-dopant [2,3], then IR image quality would be degraded and navigation capability limited.


To provide productivity to FIB editing, the backside of the silicon device is usually thinned to ~100 μm [4,5](this is also the preferred thickness for imaging and navigation with λ = ~1000 nm IR) [2,3]. Local trenches up to (~0.5 x 0.5 mm2) are created with chemically assisted FIB etching [4,5,6] or larger local trenches up to 2 x 2 mm2 are created with laser chemical etching(LCE) to obtain a remaining silicon thickness of ~10μm.  Smaller local chemically assisted FIB trenches may then be formed to allow access past active regions—critical FIB operations [4,5]. Gallium implantation occurs not just during local FIB trenching,


but also during FIB surface imaging that may be necessary to achieve positional accuracy as well as to provide before-and-after documentation. The question of whether or not Ga+ implantation unfavorably affects IR imaging is, therefore, valid. Of course the deposition of conductors and their over-spray results in opacity.


To address the concern, experiments have been performed to investigate the effectiveness of optical tools in the presence of Ga staining of various degrees.


The setup and tools used for the experiments are briefly described in Section 2. Results are presented in Section


3. In Section 4, some discussion of the results concludes the paper.


2. Experimental Tools and Setup


Commercially acquired, digital signal process (DSP)flip chips were used for these studies. The samples were prepared with a global, ultra-thinning process to a remaining silicon die thickness of about 10 μm [7].  For comparison, samples with and without SiOx AR coating(~125 nm thermally evaporated silicon in atmosphere)were prepared [8]. To determine the critical dosage defined as the dose at which the reflection raises to a level at which image information is totally lost, several 25 x 25 μm2 areas on the previously thinned die were exposed for various time periods utilizing a 250 pA beam.


The 30 keV FIB imaging and milling experiments were carried out in a Schlumberger IDS™ P2X PLUS FIB instrument. The chemical delivering jet, at ~300 μm from the ion-sample interaction areas, delivered XeF2 to the flip chip devices [6,9]. Different chemical gas fluxes were obtained by monitoring chamber pressure while adjusting the temperature of the XeF2 crucible. It was expected that the critical dose would depend on the amount of gas flux at the sample surface.


Additional FIB work was also performed in a Schlumberger IDS OptiFIB FIB instrument in which a FIB column is coaxial with the column of an optical microscope [10].  The dual column configuration permits simultaneous FIB and optical imaging.  All FIB operation areas in the IDS P2X PLUS were also imaged using the optical microscope in the IDS OptiFIB. In OptiFIB a reflecting objective of 0.36 NA is used as microscope objective lens. A cooled, high-resolution(1280 × 1024) CCD is used as image sensor. All IDS OptiFIB images were taken using λ = 850 ± 35 nm. For


comparison purposes we also imaged some FIB operation areas with a CLSM, which provides supreme image depth discrimination. CLSM imaging may therefore be more sensitive to gallium staining.  The CLSM used for image comparison has an optical objective of 0.85 NA and utilizes laser of λ = 1064 nm for imaging.


3. Experimental Results


Extensive experiments have been performed to investigate the effects of gallium staining on the IR imaging under various conditions. These conditions represented real application situations including differences in sample preparation (with and without AR coating) and in working environments involving no enhanced chemistries and those involving XeF2. For better and clearer comparisons, detailed results were grouped into subsections.


3.1 Effect of XeF2/FIB Exposure


Using a 250 pA FIB beam, an array of sample areas of 625 μm2 were subject to gallium ion bombardment for 10, 20, 60, 120, 180 and 240 seconds. The chamber pressure was also varied from low 10-6 Torr range to 1×10-5, 2×10-5 and 4×10-5 Torr, respectively, with the addition of XeF2 gas. The processed samples were then examined under the microscope in OptiFIB column.


The image brightness was quantified and the image degradation was evaluated. Figure 1 shows a typical series of optical images demonstrating the effect of gallium staining on an AR coated sample in the presence of XeF2. As the ion dose increased it is clear that the gallium staining affects the image quality in different ways: initially the FIB box became brighter and then with increasing ion dose became dark presumably due to absorption.


For the AR coated samples, the results of XeF2 pressure dependence are summarized in Fig. 2 where normalized reflection from the gallium impinged area is plotted against ion dose at various XeF2 gas pressures. Because of the material and geometry complexity of the underlying circuitry, the surface reflectivity of the gallium stained area is normalized by the brightness of the area immediately outside the FIB box with the equivalent material and geometry.


For the silicon surface without the SiOx AR film applied, the gallium stained area became opaque and no feature was identifiable inside the FIB box (Fig. 3a)even at the lowest XeF2 pressure (1×10-5 Torr) and with the least ion dose (0.4 mC/cm2).  On the other hand the absence of XeF2 resulted in a brighter gallium stained box as the ion dose increased.  The circuit feature was still not resolvable for the non-AR surface bombarded by gallium ions (Fig. 3b).


Figure 1 Optical images of a series of gallium staining run on AR coated silicon in the presence of 4×10-5 Torr XeF2.  Pictures of (a)-(f) represent exposures of 250 pA gallium ion for 10, 20, 60, 120, 180 and 240 seconds respectively.


Figure 2 Plot of normalized reflection from the gallium ion impinged area as a function of ion dose at various XeF2 gas pressures.  Sample is AR coated with SiOx film. Those data points, which have normalized reflection greater than 2.0, are not shown because of intensity saturation of the CCD sensor.


Figure 3 Optical images of non-AR silicon stained by 250 pA at various lengths of time (a) 10 sec. exposure with XeF2 (1×10-5 Torr), and (b) 240 sec. exposure without XeF2.


Examination of Figs. 1 and 2 leads to the following observations.  First of all, the image became brighter, or the light reflection became stronger, with increasing ion dose until a maximum was reached. The IR imaging is almost totally impeded near the maximum brightness,which may be due to a build up of metallic gallium on the silicon surface. This observation is true regardless if AR coating and/or XeF2 gas were applied. When the ion dose was further increased, the image brightness was decreased, i.e., the light reflection from the surface was reduced. This may have resulted from a silicon surface that was severally damaged by the high dosage of gallium ions. The increased roughness of such a damaged surface would cause light scattering resulting in less light reflected back to the camera directly, and,therefore, darkened optical images. Secondly, the image is brighter at a higher XeF2 gas pressure for a given ion dose. In other words, the maximum image brightness,occurred with the least ion dose and the highest XeF2 gas flux. This is not surprising and can be understood to be a direct result of the chemically enhanced dielectric etching effect. A third observation one could make is that the dose, at which the Ga staining started to adversely affect IR imaging, appears to decrease with increasing chemical etching.


3.2 Effect of AR Coating


Comparisons have also been made to study the effect of a thin layer of SiOx AR coating. The effects of AR film in the presence of XeF2 are discussed in previous section and illustrated in Figs. 2 and 3. The layer of AR film “protects” the silicon surface from image degradation caused by heavy ion bombardment. The“shielding effect” of AR film is also true for the case of gallium staining without chemical. Figure 4 plots the normalized reflection against ion dose for the samples with and without AR coating. It is clear that the surface becomes shiny and poor for imaging at a much lower ion dose for a sample without AR coating than for an one with AR coating.


Figure 4 Normalized reflection from the gallium ion impinged area as a function of ion dose without XeF2 gas. AR data is plotted on the right Y axis and the nonAR is plotted on the left Y axis.


For the AR coated surface, the presence of XeF2 accelerated image degradation dramatically even at relatively low pressures. However, the reduction rate in critical dose is less rapid with high XeF2 pressure than with the low pressure. On the other hand, the critical dose for the non-AR coated sample had a much weaker dependence on XeF2 pressure


Figure 5 Critical ion dose as function of XeF2 gas pressure for samples with and without AR coating.


Figure 5 indicates that one can generally obtain a good IR image at a much higher ion dose, especially at low XeF2 gas pressure, if the samples were pre-coated with a thin layer SiOx. Without the protection of SiOx film it was impossible to extract useful optical information on the gallium stained area even at low ion dose (< 5 mC/cm2).


It was also noticed that the boundaries of FIB etched areas for AR coated samples are more clearly defined than those for non-AR coated samples, especially at high ion doses. This is attributed to the “protective”


nature of the AR film against impacts of gallium ions from the tail portion of the focused ion beam of finite current distribution.  Since the XeF2 gas flux covers a much larger area than the FIB box, silicon outside the box can also be removed by the ions from the beam tail if the silicon surface is directly exposed to XeF2 spontaneous erosion.


3.3 Optical Imaging Versus FIB SE emission


At first there seems that there might be some correlation between the IR microscope image and the secondary electron (SE) emission.  (Secondary electron emission is the basis for highly accurate endpoint determination in FIB device editing [11]).  This relationship is somewhat demonstrated in Fig. 6.


Figure 6.  The FIB SE signal during 250 pA FIB milling with XeF2 chemistry on an AR coated sample.


Also shown are the optical images of the “stained”operation areas generated in various stages.


The interpretation of this SE trace is that the SE signal remained low for small doses, presumably because the SE emission was limited due to charging of the nonconductive AR coating. While the AR coating remains intact the IR imaging shows little degradation.


When the AR coating was etched very thin to a level that SE electrons can be replaced by tunneling through the remaining oxide thickness at a dose of ~3.2 mC/cm2, the SE trace increased dramatically [12].


Figure 7(a) shows the FIB image of this area; the scratches due to mechanical thinning before applying the AR coating is the proof that only the AR coating is removed, that is, no true surface cleaning has yet occurred. On the silicon surface enhanced optical reflectivity is due to the transferred Ga buildup and XeF2. As mentioned earlier, the IR microscope “sees”only brightness. There is negligible transmission through the silicon to allow imaging.


Once the AR coating and any residual interface oxide was removed the SE emission decreased to that of Si at a dose > 6 mC/cm2. At 6mC/cm2, the scratches are no longer seen, but a layer of silicon still remains as evidenced in Fig. 7(b).  Note especially the typical bright edge around the bottom of the operation area;this is due to remaining SiOx still enhancing the SE emission.


Figure 7.  FIB images of the FIB operation areas showing the silicon surface topography at different stages of imaging.  Image (a) corresponds to the optical image at a dose of 3.2 mC/cm2 in Fig. 6, and image (b)is related to the optical image at a dose of 6 mC/cm2 in Fig. 6.  Note the machined scratches evident in (a) after removal of the AR coating and in (b) note the bright edge around the bottom of the etched area indicative of remaining SiOx.


Due to extraction fields weakening as well as solid angle limitations on the SE emission, the SE trace gradually decreased with increasing dose.


Changes in the relationship between FIB SE emission and photon imaging, is suggested when Fig. 6 is compared to Fig. 8. Figure 8 essentially compares SE emission to photon reflectivity as a function of Ga dose without XeF2 chemistry enhanced milling.  It is readily seen that, as would be expected, the time to reach the SiOx/Si interface took greater Ga dose. However, the peak in SE emission no longer correlated to maximum reflectivity. The explanation must relate to the lack of enhanced reflectivity due to XeF2. The final observation was that, as expected, with increased dose both phenomena changed more slowly.  (Note that the Si overlaying the first SiO2 ILD is not breached.)


Figure 8.  FIB SE signal during 150 pA FIB imaging without XeF2 chemistry enhanced milling.  The optical brightness within the FIB operation area was monitored in real time (trace with circles).


3.4 CLSM Versus CCD-Based Images


In order to examine the response of CLSM on the gallium-stained surface, a number of CLSM images were taken and compared with CCD-based images. The sample images are shown in Fig. 9.  Because of the confocal configuration, CLSM has much narrower depth-of-focus (DOF) than the CCD based imaging system (~2 μm versus ~20 μm) enabling CLSM to acquire optical information free from interference from neighboring layers.


The CLSM image focused on silicon surface in Fig.


9(a) clearly shows the “damage”, caused by exposure to the gallium ion beam.  The CLSM image of diffusion layer in Fig. 9(b), which is about 10 μm below the silicon surface, shows no sign of image degradation.


The gallium box was drawn in the picture for illustration purpose.  For comparison the same area is examined using OptiFIB microscope and shown in Fig.


9(c). Because of large DOF in the CCD-based imaging system relative to silicon thickness, all the optical information within the 10 μm stack come into focus including strong surface reflection by gallium implantation. This results in a less tolerance to gallium staining than CLSM. For CLSM imaging tool the“critical dose” defined and applied in Fig. 5 is estimated to be larger then 40 mC/cm2 for the case of gallium staining on AR coated device.


4. Discussion and Conclusion


Gallium ion implantation resulting from local FIB trenching and imaging hinders through-silicon IR imaging if the ion exposure of the device is too high.  In


Figure 9. After a 2 nA FIB beam with no chemistry injection, imaged for 30 seconds, CLSM image focused


(a) at the surface of the die and (b) at the diffusion layer, both with the same field of view. (c) IDS OptiFIB image of the same device area under the same conditions.


practice this means that deep trenching with a Ga+ FIB makes IR navigation, IR assisted probe placement and IR signal acquisition essentially impossible.  Further the addition of XeF2 to enhance FIB operations further hinders through silicon IR imaging—less ion dose results in opacity. Extensive experiments have been conducted to investigate the effect of gallium staining on IR imaging. These experiments were so designed to simulate real application situations. The results of the experiments indicate that there exist ranges within which good IR imaging quality can be obtained and,therefore, the value and productivity of optical tools can be retained on devices that experienced FIB editing.


It is found that applying a thin layer (~125 nm) of AR coating during sample preparation greatly increased the critical Ga+ dosage as well as improve the IR imaging.


For no XeF2 flux imaging time can be increased > 5X with the AR coating over that of having no AR coating.


Quantitative results indicate that with ~250 pA beam current, surface damage from Ga+ implantation is not an issue for < 60 second exposures. With XeF2 flux, IR image degradation occurred at reduced ion doses, but this reduction is not a strong function of XeF2 flux.


Therefore, minimal ion beam exposure is required to ensure good optical imaging from the backside of the die for IR navigation. Based on the results, it is important not to exceed the critical ion dose (~30 mC/cm2 for AR coated samples) without any chemicals.


The critical imaging times are reduced with the introduction of XeF2.


Furthermore, the short depth of focus of a CLSM,which enables selective imaging of different layers,reveals the degree of surface damage caused by Ga+.


However, the relatively long depth of focus of other IR microscopes such as the Schlumberger IDS OptiFIB would not be sensitive to this information.


In conclusion, Ga staining is a concern. Yet it must be emphasized that it is not necessarily a limitation to the implementation of photon optical tools in the debug laboratory. Further study is needed to look into surface topography and chemical composition on the galliumimpacted area to help build a model to understand how the gallium impact/staining affects optical and imaging properties. Another study suggested is to investigate the effect of gallium staining on optical probing(active/passive waveform probing and other optical based failure analyses) after FIB imaging/editing.

Water Vapor Enhancement for Elemental Analysis Using Focused Ion Beam Secondary Ion Mass Spectrometry (FIB-SIMS)


T.L. Shofner, F.A. Stevie, and J.M. McKinley


Agere Systems, Orlando, FL, USA


J. Lomness


University of Central Florida, Orlando, FL, USA


Secondary Ion Mass Spectrometry (SIMS) with a quadrupole detector has been used on FIB systems but it needs yields enhancement to become an effective tool. By introducing water vapor to the area of analysis, the secondary ion yield can be significantly increased for certain metals.


As semiconductor technology size continues to shrink, the demand for elemental analysis from smaller areas increases. The work of Chabala and Levi-Setti led to the development of scanning microprobes that used a Ga+ liquid metal ion source(LMIS) to produce 20 nm lateral resolution for secondary ion mass spectrometry (SIMS) images.[1]Ga+ beams are now capable of 5 nm resolution, but the secondary ion yield from Ga+ bombardment is similar to that of Ar+ and is often too low for many semiconductor materials.[2-4] It has been shown that by introducing cesium onto the sample the SIMS sensitivity for As, using a gallium source, can be increased by orders of magnitude.[5] In our study, a similar approach has been taken with the FIB-SIMS by adding water vapor to the area of bombardment via an injection needle near the sample surface as shown in Figure 1.


Figure 1. Schematic of FIB-SIMS with water vapor enhancement


The addition of a water vapor source to FIB systems has become common. The water vapor increases the sputtering rate of carbon containing materials such as polyimide, photo resist and diamond while concurrently decreasing the sputtering rates of other materials, such as Al and Si. This provides FIB analysts with the ability to selectively remove carbon-based materials. The mechanism responsible for the lowered sputtering rate is oxidation. By adding the water vapor to Al, for example, an oxide layer is formed on the surface and being sputtered away by the Ga+ ions, reducing the net amount of surface material that is removed. Conversely, when H2O encounters a carbon based material it will form a volatile product that is easily desorbed from the surface, thus increasing the rate of material removal.[6]


It may seem counter intuitive that by reducing the sputtering rate, the SIMS yield could be increased.


However, it is the oxidized layer that is being sputtered and as the metal oxygen bonds break the metal is left with a positive charge while the oxygen becomes negatively charged due to its high electron affinity. As a result more metal atoms are ionized than would be the case without the oxide. Without oxygen enhancement the sputtered metal atoms tend to remain neutral.


The scope of this paper is to show the effects of water vapor and the presence of the gas injection needle on ion yields from different metals. The materials selected for this study are common to the semiconductor industry as contaminants or constituents and are as follows: Ti, Co, Cu, Fe, Ni,and Al.


Experimental Procedure


The instrument used was the FEI 800FIB with FEI SIMS III quadrupole attachment. The samples were purchased from Geller Microanalytical Laboratory and consisted of Ti, Co, Cu, Fe, Ni, and Al standard foils.


The experiment consisted of two parts. First, a determination of any effects caused by the insertion of the needle was made. This was done by monitoring the count rate as the needle was inserted and removed. Second, with the needle inserted, the count rate was observed while opening and closing the valve that allows water vapor into the chamber near the sample surface. All experiments were preformed at 350 pA beam current, 0% beam overlap, and 0.2 µs dwell time. With the sample set at eucentric height, the needle is positioned 50-75 µm above the sample surface and 50-100 µm from the sample center. The quadrupole is approximately 1.5 cm above the sample and at 45 degrees to the ion column. The water vapor temperature is set to 35°C.


Figures 2 through 13 show the data collected for the foils.


Figure 2. Effect of needle on Ti foil SIMS yield


Figure 3. Water vapor on Ti foil SIMS yield


Figure 4. Effect of needle on Co foil SIMS yield


Figure 5. Water vapor on Co foil SIMS yield


Figure 6. Effect of needle on Cu foil SIMS yield


Figure 7. Water vapor on Cu foil SIMS yield


Figure 8. Effect of needle on Fe foil SIMS yield


Figure 9. Water vapor on Fe foil SIMS yield


Figure 10. Effect of needle on Ni foil SIMS yield


Figure 11. Water vapor on Ni foil SIMS yield


Figure 12. Effect of needle on Al foil SIMS yield


Figure 13. Water vapor on Al foil SIMS yield


From the results it is seen that Al and Ti have much higher count rates before the water vapor is added to the system and both experience greater increases after the water vapor is added. An explanation of this requires a look at the ionization potentials of these metals as shown in Figure 14. In general, the lower the ionization potential the higher the secondary ion yield. The results support this. To evaluate the results after water vapor enhancement the oxidizing characteristics of the foils must be considered. Both Ti and Al are highly reactive with oxygen after the surface oxide is removed, explaining the significant increases seen in the yields when the valve is opened.


On the other hand Cu, Fe, Co, and Ni would require increased temperatures to oxidize more readily although an increase in the count rate is still observed.


Figure 14. Ionization potentials [7]


The data indicate that in order to obtain enhanced count rates it is necessary to first overcome the


detrimental effects of the needle. Typical overall net enhancement (increase due to water vapor and decrease from needle) was by a factor of 2, and as much as an order of magnitude in the Ti case. The results were consistent in all but the case of Al. In this case the insertion of the needle actually increased the count rate. Also, for Al, after opening the valve the rate increased and continued to increase even after closing the valve, unlike the other cases studied.


To fully understand this mechanism would require more experimentation into the oxidation process of Al. One possible explanation is that while the valve is open, most of the ions being counted by the detector are formed just at or above the surface while an oxide layer continues to grow at the surface of the sample. Immediately upon closing the valve the sputtering of the newly formed oxide layer continues,giving an increased count rate for a short time. This scenario indicates that the oxidation rate was greater than the sputtering rate. The field disturbance from the insertion of the needle is still being investigated.


Future work should include varying the sputtering rate in order to find an equilibrium point where sputtering and oxidation are occurring at similar rates in order to confirm the mechanism behind the Al results. In order to compensate for the disturbance in extraction field due to the needle insertion, a series of experiments investigating applied voltages to the needle are being planned. As FIB tools can also be equipped with an oxide deposition gas (TEOS), it is hoped that experiments into SIMS enhancement while the oxide deposition gas is present will be explored. For less reactive metals, the ability to increase the specimen temperature would also be beneficial because the oxidation rate would be increased.


In conclusion, the results of these preliminary studies show a strong need for further work in the area of FIB-SIMS. Although EDX is now available on the FIB dual beam tools, SIMS still has the advantage of resolution. FIB-SIMS will only detect secondary ions from the small spot being sputtered. The EDX will receive signal from a interaction volume dependent on the electron beam energy and material being examined, therefore losing the surface sensitivity and lateral resolution of the SIMS.[8]SIMS is one of the most sensitive elemental analysis methods. If the Ga+ beam secondary ion yields can be increased by a factor of ten, the FIB-SIMS will be of significant use for small area analysis.
